European Economy Institutional Papers are important reports analysing the economic situation and 
economic developments prepared by the European Commission's Directorate-General for Economic and 
Financial Affairs, which serve to underpin economic policy-making by the European Commission, the Council 
of the European Union and the European Parliament. 
 
 
DISCLAIMER 
 
The views expressed in unofficial documents should not be considered as representative of the European 
Commission’s official position.

=== PAGE 15 ===
FOREWORD 
 
xiii 
The feared scenario of prolonged disruption in global energy supply has largely materialised. Yet 
its economic impact has so far remained manageable. Economic activity has held up better than 
expected, with positive momentum set to persist, even though inflation has stayed high and is 
expected to prove more persistent than previously assumed.  
Europe continued to benefit from a strong global investment cycle, even as domestic investment 
remained weak. External demand, particularly in sectors linked to the rapid build-up of data 
centres, helped cushion the adverse impact of higher energy costs and tighter financing conditions. 
Consumption also held up, supported by continued, albeit decelerating, employment growth and 
continued real wage gains. 
Moreover, the EU economy benefits from buffers to withstand further shocks, including a still 
robust labour market and sound private-sector balance sheets. However, aggregate strengths 
conceal important vulnerabilities. Younger workers are among the first to feel weakening labour 
demand; inflation disproportionately affects lower-income households, which have fewer savings 
to draw on and spend a larger share of their income on food and utility bills; and, despite still-
healthy overall profit margins, some industrial sectors face considerable cost and non-cost 
competitiveness pressures. Importantly, private-sector financial strength coexists with a gradual 
deterioration in public-sector positions, a vulnerability now laid bare by surging interest rates. 
The baseline outlook remains one of moderate expansion amid gradually easing price pressures. 
The recent decline in sovereign yields from their highs and the partial easing in commodity prices 
may provide some welcome breathing space. Still, in an external environment that remains highly 
uncertain, sound public finances are essential to preserve confidence, contain financing costs and, 
above all, retain the capacity to act should the outlook worsen. 
Rising sovereign yields across the EU have emerged as a potentially more consequential challenge. 
The increase in long-term rates reflects a global repricing of bonds amid renewed inflationary 
pressures and rising public and private borrowing needs. Within the EU, widening spreads for 
Member States facing elevated borrowing needs and deteriorating debt dynamics, point to   
markets differentiated appraisal of fiscal sustainability. 
Already in spring, the policy message was to stay the course on public finances and avoid costly 
blanket fuel subsidies that would merely delay the necessary adjustment in energy demand. 
Renewed tensions in financial markets reinforce the importance of that message. 
Sound public finances are not only a requirement for sustained and balanced growth; they are also 
key to safeguard intergenerational equity by ensuring that today's fiscal choices do not unduly fall 
on the shoulders of future generations. 
 
 
 
                              Director General 
                              Economic and Financial Affairs 
 

=== PAGE 16 ===

=== PAGE 17 ===
CONTINUED GROWTH AMID HIGH INFLATION 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
1 
Almost eight months after the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 
energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz—the world’s main oil 
artery—remain severely disrupted. Damage to the region’s energy 
infrastructure means that supply will recover only gradually, even if the 
Strait fully reopens. After easing over the summer amid a fragile 
ceasefire and hopes of a lasting peace agreement, Brent prices 
exceeded 100 dollars in October and at times even surpassed the late 
April peaks. Re-routing of flows, together with a contraction in global 
demand and the release of strategic reserves, helped contain the 
increase in crude oil prices. However, freight and insurance costs rose 
sharply, while bottlenecks in refining capacity have added pressure on 
refined product prices, with diesel prices doubling year-on-year. Gas 
markets are also tight, with TTF prices trading above 95 EUR/MWh in 
late October—more than three times their late February level. Futures 
point to gradual easing ahead, but oil product and gas prices remain 
well-above pre-war levels throughout the forecast horizon and low 
levels of strategic reserves and gas storage suggest that buffers to 
absorb further shocks are thin (Box I.3.1). Global headline inflation 
closely mirrored the energy market developments and, after easing 
over the summer, it surged again in September.  
In response to inflationary pressures, central banks across the globe, 
including the US Federal Reserve, have started, or are expected, to raise 
policy rates, lifting short-term interest rates. The increase in interest 
rates has been steeper at the long end of the yield curve. At the cut-off 
date of this forecast, the 10-year US Treasury yield was at around

European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026

5.1%, up from 4.4% at end-April, and the 30-year yield exceeded 5.4%. 
Long-term yields in other advanced economies, notably the UK and 
Japan, also increased. Beyond intensified short-term inflationary 
pressures, this reflects the large supply of long-term debt issued both 
by official and private sectors, while central banks have turned net 
sellers of sovereign bonds (Box I.4.1). Meanwhile, equity markets have 
been volatile, amid rebounding oil prices and concerns about the 
profitability and financing of AI companies, which weigh on technology 
shares.  
The global economy continued to expand in the first half of 2026, and 
the composite PMI still signals strong momentum in the third quarter. 
Merchandise trade also grew robustly, by 6% y-o-y in the first eight 
months of 2026, led by advanced, and emerging Asian economies. In 
the US, AI-related investment continued to expand vigorously, and large 
technology companies have further scaled up their investment plans. 
The Southeast Asian economies have also stepped-up investment, 
partly to meet rising demand from the AI boom. These developments 
have generated trade spillovers beyond the semiconductor industry and 
US-Southeast Asian trade, reflecting the geographically dispersed 
supply chains and the broad range of high- and mid-tech inputs 
required to build and equip data centres. Chinese exports also surged, 
with rising evidence of an ongoing reconfiguration of trade flows in 
response to US-China trade tensions (Box I.2.1). Global growth 
(excluding the EU) is forecast at 3.2% in 2026 and 3.6% in 2027 and 
2028, with the 2027 pick-up largely driven by emerging market 
economies. Global trade (excluding the EU) is forecast to grow by 4.1% 
in 2026 and 5% in 2027, before moderating to 3.8% in 2028 as AI 
investment growth slows. 
At 1.3%, the rate of expansion of the EU economy over the first three 
quarters of the year was higher than expected in spring. The upward 
surprise was largely driven by a strong rebound in exports, while 
consumption held up broadly as expected and investment came in 
slightly weaker. Reflecting these developments on the demand side, 
after a prolonged period of weakness (Special Issue 1.), activity in 
manufacturing experienced a positive upswing, retail trade and services 
held steady, while construction activity broadly stagnated. By contrast, 
agricultural production was weak in the first half of the year and 
suffered further in the third quarter on the back of this summer’s 
heatwaves and droughts (Box I.5.1). Overall, these developments lift 
the EU growth forecast for 2026 to 1.4%—the same rate as in 2025. 
Economic activity is expected to expand by 1.4% also in 2027 and by 
1.5% in 2028. Growth in the euro area is expected to reach 1.2% this 
year and next and move up to 1.3% in 2028. Potential output growth in 
2026 in the EU is estimated at 1.4% and is expected to decrease over 
the forecast horizon and beyond. These estimates do not include any 
adjustment for the potential impact of AI on TFP (Box I.10.1).  
In October, euro area inflation rose to 4.0%, from 3.8% in September. 
Inflation was driven by energy prices, which surged by almost 21% y-
o-y. Unprocessed food inflation also edged up to 4%, likely reflecting a 
delayed pass-through of higher energy and fertiliser costs, alongside 
disruptions to crops caused by the summer’s extreme weather events. 
Energy inflation is projected to moderate next year, as falling retail fuel 
prices more than offset the gradual rise in electricity and gas, due to 
the delayed transmission of high wholesale prices. By contrast,

unprocessed food price pressures are expected to intensify in the near 
term and gradually spill over to processed food prices along the supply 
chain. Moreover, higher energy costs are likely to feed through to the 
consumption basket (Box I.7.1). Still, second round effects are expected 
to remain contained against a backdrop of decelerating wages, while 
aggregate profit margins edge up marginally. Overall, headline inflation 
in the EU is expected to peak above 4% between the end of this year 
and the beginning of 2027. Annual inflation is set to reach 3.3% in 
2026 and 3.2% in 2027, before falling back to 2.3% in 2028. Euro area 
inflation is expected to follow broadly similar dynamics. As in the 
previous inflationary episode of 2022, lower income households are 
more exposed to high food prices and rising household energy bills, 
given the higher share of these essential items in their consumption 
basket (Special Issue 2.).  
Despite renewed inflationary pressures, medium-term market-based 
inflation expectations remained well anchored, reflecting markets’ 
confidence in the ECB’s ability to restore and ensure price stability. The 
euro area policy rates were raised by 25 bps. in both June and 
September. At the cut-off date of the forecast, markets expected three 
to four further increases, with the deposit facility rate peaking at 
around 3.4% by mid-2027. Longer-term interest rates had risen more 
markedly, broadly in line with developments in global bond markets. 
The 10-year German government bond yield, the euro area benchmark, 
stood at around 3.4% in September, compared with around 3.0% at the 
time of the Spring 2026 Forecast cut-off. The increase reflected both 
expectations of a higher path for short-term interest rates and higher 
term premia, partly associated with sustained government borrowing 
needs. Bond markets appeared to increasingly differentiate across 
sovereigns, reflecting differences in fiscal positions, with sovereign 
spreads widening across the EU. French government bond yields saw 
significant increases. Higher policy rates and long-term yields weigh on 
credit demand, while banks have also tightened their lending 
standards, adding to the restraint on credit supply.  
The high-rate environment is set to weigh on residential construction, 
which is expected to broadly stagnate this year and recover only 
modestly over the next two years. In a context of continued excess 
demand, pressures on housing prices have continued to build up, 
raising social cohesion issues to greater prominence in some urban 
regions. By contrast, non-residential construction, including public 
investment, is projected to keep expanding this year under impulse 
from the Recovery and Resilience Facility, but the momentum is set to 
weaken over the forecast horizon. Equipment investment, by contrast, 
is projected to keep expanding at a healthy pace this year, also 
supported by the rebound of manufacturing since spring, but 
decelerate in 2027. Other investment, which largely includes intangible 
assets, is set to keep expanding steadily over the forecast horizon, 
though at a slower pace than in the US, also reflecting weaker AI 
investment across Europe. Overall, investment is set to decelerate from 
a yearly growth rate of 2.6% in 2025 to an annual average of around 
1.5% this year and next, before edging up towards the end of the 
forecast horizon. The combination of subdued investment growth and 
solid corporate balance sheets leads to continued excess of corporate 
saving over investment, consolidating the sector’s position as a net 
lender (Box I.5.2).

European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
4 
The EU labour markets are expected to employ more than 800,000 
additional workers in 2026, even if employment growth is projected to 
remain subdued relative to output growth. The implied elasticity of 
around 0.25–0.3, below its long-run average of close to 0.5, points to a 
less job-rich economic expansion than in previous years. Cyclical labour 
productivity growth is thus projected to return to around 1%. Despite 
adverse demographic trends (Box I.6.1), labour force growth is still set 
to increase over the forecast horizon, with activity rates nudging up to 
66.7% by the end of the forecast horizon. The unemployment rate is 
expected to remain close to record lows, slightly above 6%, throughout 
the forecast horizon. Stability in the unemployment rate, nonetheless, 
masks an increase in unemployment among younger cohorts. A rising 
share of the youth cohort is also working fewer hours than they would 
like. While these developments point to some emerging weaknesses in 
the labour market, it is too early to establish a link with potential 
labour-market effects of AI adoption. Wages are projected to grow by 
3.8% this year and 3.7% next year—marginally higher than expected in 
spring—and decelerate more notably in 2028. This implies positive real 
wage growth over the forecast horizon, though below productivity 
growth.  
Over the forecast horizon, real disposable income is, however, projected 
to expand at a weaker pace than in spring, given the important increase 
in the cost of living. Private consumption growth is set to soften to 
1.3% this year, weaken marginally to 1.1% in 2027 and move back to 
1.3% in 2028, as decelerating inflation allows for stronger expansion 
of household purchasing power. The forecast for consumer spending is 
also conditional upon a small drop in the household saving rate, to 
14.1% in 2028, still well above the pre-pandemic average of around 
12%. Wealth effects play a marginal role in supporting consumption 
(Box I.4.2). After expanding at a projected rate of 1.9% this year, 
government consumption growth is expected to decelerate to just 
above 1% over the two years.  
Following a strong rebound in the first half of the year, export growth 
is set to attain 3.2% this year and 2.8% in 2027, well above the growth 
rates expected in spring. It would then decrease to 2.3% in 2028. The 
strong global AI and electrification investment cycle supports the 
improved outlook for goods exports, with firms across the EU supplying 
power, cooling, and chip-making equipment. Imports are also seen 
higher, but still projected to decelerate over the forecast horizon, from 
3.5% in 2025 to 2.3%. Re-alignment of export and import growth 
implies an almost neutral contribution of net external demand over the 
forecast horizon starting this year. The external balance of goods and 
services is affected by the negative terms of trade effect of this year’s 
surge in energy prices and is set to decline from 3.5% of GDP in 2025 
to 3.1% this year, before moving up again to 3.4% by 2028. The 
services surplus (around 2% of GDP) accounts for most of the overall 
surplus, although total goods trade flows remain about twice as large 
as services trade flows.  
Government deficits are set to rise markedly this year, as a result of 
increasing defence spending, rising interest expenditure, and some new 
measures to shelter households and firms from high energy prices. The 
EU aggregate general government deficit is set to increase from 3.1% 
to 3.6% this year, followed by further small increases in both 2027 and 
2028—with a similar trend expected in the euro area. While fiscal

policy is overall slightly contractionary in 2027-28, and temporary 
measures to mitigate the social and economic impact of high energy 
prices are expected to be phased out, interest expenditure is set to rise 
steadily, from 1.9% of GDP in 2025 to 2.4% of GDP in 2028—the 
highest ratio for more than a decade. Although cohesion policy 
spending is set to increase gradually, the overall level of EU-funded 
expenditure is expected to decrease in 2027 as the RRF comes to an 
end. This contributes to the contractionary fiscal stance in 2027. Public 
investment remains stable as a percentage of GDP at historically high 
levels, bolstered by the continued rise of defence spending. The debt-
to-GDP ratio is set to increase in the EU from 82.6% of GDP in 2025 to 
85.6% in 2028. In the euro area, it is projected to increase from 88.6% 
of GDP in 2025 to 91.3% in 2028. 
Risks to the outlook remain closely linked to geopolitical developments 
and persistently elevated uncertainty. Although the conflict in the 
Middle East has outlasted initial expectations, military action has 
eased. Lower oil and gas prices following the US mid-term elections 
could, if sustained, materially lower the inflation outlook for 2027 and 
2028, while marginally lifting economic activity in 2027. Closer to 
Europe, however, Russia’s protracted war of aggression against Ukraine 
and rising hybrid threats continue to undermine the security 
environment, with potential disruptions to supply chains, higher 
operating costs and weaker confidence. Trade policy uncertainty also 
remains elevated, although deeper relations with EU partners such as 
the UK and Canada could provide some offset. Equity markets remain 
vulnerable to a correction given high valuations and market 
concentration, particularly in the United States. Renewed increases in 
long-term interest rates could add to fiscal pressures and weigh on 
growth. On the upside, faster AI adoption could boost productivity and 
potential growth, particularly in European manufacturing, while 
stronger investment in 
defence, infrastructure and industrial 
modernisation could support activity. Finally, more frequent and 
extreme weather events could continue to weigh on output and raise 
prices, through production losses and damage to natural assets and 
infrastructure. Faster implementation of structural reforms to address 
long-standing bottlenecks to EU competitiveness could unlock stronger 
growth. Finally, resolute progress in the energy transition to reduce 
Europe’s dependence on imported fossil fuels would further boost 
economic resilience.

PART I 
Economic outlook for EA and EU 
 
 

=== PAGE 24 ===
1. SETTING THE SCENE 
  
8 
Energy supplies from the Middle East remain disrupted, contrary to the market 
expectations of a few months ago. In March, following the first US and Israeli strikes on Iran, 
oil prices surged sharply. Yet markets priced in a short-lived conflict, reflecting the expectation that 
a prolonged disruption to shipping through the Strait of Hormuz would be economically untenable 
for both Iran and the United States. A ceasefire reached in June collapsed in July, and renewed 
hostilities have pushed back expectations of an imminent resolution. At the cut-off date of this 
forecast, speculation about renewed strikes after the US midterm elections had added further 
upward pressure on prices. The market assumptions underpinning this forecast therefore reflect 
energy prices close to their peaks so far this year, particularly for natural gas. 
Even so, the energy shock delivered by the conflict has proved less severe than feared 
in the pessimistic scenarios prevailing in spring 2026. The increase in crude prices was 
limited by the re-routing of export flows, a gradually more porous blockade, a stronger-than-
expected contraction in global oil demand and the exceptional release of strategic reserves. 
Refined product prices have risen more markedly than crude, as supply disruptions have 
compounded existing refining bottlenecks. Gas prices also increased markedly, as gas markets 
offered less scope to offset the disruption through alternative supply routes and sources. 
Moreover, the slow pace of storage replenishment over the summer contributed to a surge in 
European gas prices ahead of the winter season. 
Inflation has rekindled, mainly driven by energy, and is now beginning to broaden to 
food. Headline inflation rose markedly from 1.9% in February to 4% in October, largely 
on account of energy. Beyond energy commodity prices, the exceptionally hot and dry summer 
of 2026 in much of Europe also contributed to the surge in energy inflation, as low water levels 
constrained hydropower generation and cooling water availability for nuclear plants, just as 
electricity demand for cooling surged. Disruptions to agricultural production are already putting 
upward pressure on unprocessed food prices, while the developing El Niño phase and renewed 
disruptions to grain exports from Ukraine via the Black Sea are likely to add to upward pressure on 
food import prices in the coming quarters. 
A consequential adverse development over the summer has been the marked rise in 
long-term interest rates. At the cut-off date of this forecast, the 10-year Bund yield was 
around 60 basis points above its level in February, after having risen by as much as 90 basis 
points in mid-October. Yields have also increased in the United States and other advanced 
economies. This repricing reflects a confluence of factors, including shifts in central bank policy 
expectations, increased supply of private and public debt, and concerns about fiscal sustainability. 
Equity markets have remained volatile, with frequent concerns about stretched technology 
valuations, but, overall, they continue to trade at elevated levels. 
Still, economic activity in the EU held up better than expected in the first half of the 
year, buoyed by a strong global investment cycle. In the United States, AI-related investment 
continued to expand vigorously, and large technology companies further scaled up their investment 
plans. South-East Asian economies also stepped-up investment in semiconductor manufacturing 
and other technology-related capacity, partly to meet rising demand from the AI investment boom. 
This generated moderate but notable trade spillovers beyond traditional semiconductor trade and 
US–Southeast Asian trade, reflecting geographically dispersed supply chains and the broad range 
of high- and mid-tech inputs required to build and equip data centres. EU producers appear to have 
benefited chiefly as suppliers of power, cooling and semiconductor-manufacturing equipment, 
rather than of computing hardware itself. It was mainly thanks to strong export growth that EU 
GDP growth was somewhat stronger than projected in the Spring 2026 Forecast. 
Strong private-sector balance sheets and still-tight labour markets continue to have a 
stabilising effect, though their cushioning impact may be eroding. Employment has 
continued to expand, and unemployment remains at historically low levels. Household saving rates 

=== PAGE 25 ===
Economic outlook for EA and EU 
 
9 
are still above pre-pandemic averages, offering protection against the erosion of real incomes, 
while corporate indebtedness has declined in recent years. These buffers have so far helped the 
private sector absorb economic shocks and contain the drag on growth. Aggregate figures may, 
however, conceal underlying fragilities. Younger workers are often more exposed to a weakening 
labour market, even while aggregate employment remains robust. Lower-income households have 
more limited financial buffers and devote a larger share of their income to energy and food; the 
high cost of living is increasingly a source of public concern, fuelling social discontent and 
undermining social cohesion. Finally, while aggregate corporate margins look healthy, they are at 
the lower end of their historical distribution in many Member States, and some industrial sectors 
continue to face considerable competitive pressure. 
The macro-policy mix is likely to provide less support than previously expected. In spring, 
monetary policy could plausibly be expected to look through a temporary rise in inflation. With 
energy prices remaining elevated for longer and their pass-through broadening, the expected 
monetary policy stance has become less accommodative than previously assumed. Fiscal policy 
also faces tighter constraints. Fiscal space had already been reduced by the legacy of successive 
crises and rising interest expenditure. Increasing pressure from bond markets is adding to interest 
expenditure at a time when defence spending still needs to be ramped up and demands from 
households and firms for protection against surging energy costs are intensifying. 
The exceptional volatility of energy and financial markets makes this forecast 
particularly conditional on developments up to its cut-off date. Averaging market prices 
over a ten-working-day period – as is done to form the assumptions underpinning the European 
Economic Forecasts – provides only limited smoothing when volatility is pronounced. Moreover, the 
cut-off date for the technical assumptions (29 October) preceded the US midterm elections by 
almost a week. Subsequent developments have materially changed the picture. A lower perceived 
risk of renewed strikes against Iran has contributed to sharp declines in energy prices, with Brent 
crude and TTF gas spot prices falling by around 20%. Financial markets have also repriced 
significantly. Concerns that public opposition to data centres expansion in the US and elsewhere, 
alongside tighter regulation of frontier AI models could weigh on investment have added to 
volatility in AI-related equity valuations. These developments have supported bond markets, 
pushing yields down, including at shorter maturities. While the dust has yet to settle, these 
developments, if sustained, would point to upside risks to growth and downside risks to inflation, 
as discussed in more detail in the risk assessment. More broadly, the exceptionally uncertain 
external environment means that forecasts are increasingly exposed to events unfolding almost as 
soon as they are finalised. 
 

=== PAGE 26 ===
2. INTERNATIONAL ENVIRONMENT 
  
10 
The global economy continued to expand in the first half of 2026, with growth pace 
estimated to have remained steady in the third quarter. Global economic activity is 
estimated to have expanded by around 0.8% q-o-q in 2026-Q2, up from 0.7% recorded in 2026-
Q1 and matching the growth rate recorded in 2025-Q4 (Graph I.2.1). Growth in the advanced 
economies accelerated further to 0.7% q-o-q in 2026-Q2 from 0.5% in 2026-Q1 and 0.3% in 
2025-Q4, supported by strong activity across the US, Japan, UK and EU. Growth in emerging 
market economies (EMEs) was at 1% q-o-q in 2026-Q2, after slowing from 1.1% q-o-q in 2025-
Q4 to 0.9% q-o-q in 2026-Q1. Recent survey data point to continued global expansion in the third 
quarter, with the Global Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI) rising from 51.8 in April to 
54.3 in September 2026, matching the value last achieved in May 2023 (Graph I.2.2). The services 
PMI increased significantly, from 51.2 to 54.5 over the same period, while the manufacturing PMI 
strengthened to 53.0 in September, its highest level since February 2022, just before Russia’s full-
scale invasion of Ukraine.   
 
 
US real GDP growth remained robust throughout the first three quarters of 2026. 
Household consumption has remained strong so far in 2026, while investment in AI-related 
equipment and intellectual property products continued to expand robustly. A one-off increase in 
tax refunds in the first half of 2026 (nearing 20% compared to the same period last year) 
provided an additional tailwind to domestic demand, even as weak government spending after the 
post-shutdown rebound in 2026-Q1 weighed on growth. Ongoing repayment of the IEEPA tariffs, 
which were struck down by the US Supreme Court in February 2026, helped to further strengthen 
healthy corporate sector balance sheets. Exports continued to increase at a robust pace, while the 
strength of domestic demand was increasingly reflected in imports (notably of AI-related goods), 
resulting in a further widening of the trade deficit. Employment growth remained subdued, but the 
unemployment rate edged down by 0.2 pps. to 4.2% in September, partly due to a shrinking labour 
force. Overall, economic activity in the US is forecast to expand by 2.3% in 2026.  
US economic growth is set to ease in 2027 and 2028. Private consumption growth is 
expected to moderate, as employment growth is set to remain weak and household saving rates 
low. Higher living costs and tighter financing conditions are also forecast to weigh on consumption 
and non-tech investment. In 2027, continued robust technology-related business investment is 
projected to offset the drag from higher energy prices and lingering headwinds from trade policy. 
Booming demand for AI-related equipment and components is set to keep imports strong, keeping 
the growth contribution of net exports negative. In 2028, AI-related investment growth is expected

In other advanced economies, domestic and external headwinds are set to constrain 
economic expansion. In the United Kingdom, higher energy prices are weighing on real incomes, 
while a softening labour market keeps precautionary saving elevated, restraining consumption. 
Higher borrowing costs further limit room for fiscal support, with the new budget keeping energy 
relief targeted, temporary and financed by a time-limited bank levy to preserve fiscal headroom. 
Public investment is nonetheless expected to provide some offset. Growth is projected at 1.3% in 
2026 and 1.2% in 2027, rising to 1.4% in 2028 as inflation returns towards target and real 
income growth resumes. In Canada, escalating trade tensions with the US and uncertainty around 
the USMCA (US–Mexico–Canada trade agreement) review are weighing on exports, business 
investment and employment. As a net energy exporter, higher energy prices as well as increased 
government spending provide offsets. Growth is projected to accelerate from 1.1% in 2026 to 
1.6% in 2027 and 1.9% in 2028. Japan’s economy expanded in the first half of 2026, with resilient 
exports and public spending offsetting weak private consumption and contracting investment. 
Growth is expected to remain subdued in the second half of this year as high inflation weighs on 
private spending. Growth is forecast at 0.9% in 2026 as a whole, before moderating to 0.7% in 
2027 and 0.6% in 2028, with private consumption remaining lukewarm despite rising real wages. 
Public spending is expected to remain elevated, supporting domestic demand.  
China’s economic activity picked up in the third quarter of 2026, with real GDP growth 
reaching 4.5% y-o-y, up from 4.3% in the second quarter. Growth over the first nine months 
reached 4.6%, at the lower end of the government’s 4.5% to 5.0% target range. The expansion 
nonetheless remained narrowly based. Industrial production rose by 5.3% over the first nine 
months, with high technology and equipment manufacturing again the most dynamic segments. 
Household consumption was subdued, and real retail sales remained flat in the same period. Fixed 
asset investment contracted by 7.2% in the same period, dragged down by the ongoing property 
correction, declining investment by both state-owned enterprises and private firms, and more 
limited infrastructure spending. The contribution of net exports to growth declined, as export 
growth slowed while import growth picked up, even though crude oil import volumes fell markedly 
as China drew on its large stockpiles in response to disrupted Gulf supply. 
The growth outlook in China has decreased marginally. Real GDP growth is forecast at 4.5% 
in 2026, 4.4% in 2027 and 4.3% in 2028. The broader picture remains one of weak domestic 
demand: subdued household consumption, a weak labour market, cautious private investment 
amid high indebtedness, and a property sector that continues to weigh on activity, leaving growth 
reliant on policy support and external demand. While the conflict in the Middle East adds to 
external risks, China’s large strategic reserves, domestic energy production and relatively 
diversified energy mix provide significant buffers against disruptions to energy supplies. Overall, 
China is expected to remain on a fragile but only mildly decelerating growth path, with increased 
sensitivity to weakening external demand and weaker-than-expected policy support.  
India grew more than expected in 2026-Q2, but the pace is set to moderate. Real GDP 
growth accelerated to 8.6% y-o-y in 2026-Q1 and remained robust at 7.8% in 2026-Q2. Resilient 
domestic demand remained the main driver and provided a buffer in the face of the external 
shocks. It is set to continue benefitting from a large and young labour force, a growing domestic 
market and competitive wage costs. That said, high-frequency indicators point to some moderation 
in activity, both in manufacturing and services. Rising food and energy prices are set to weigh on 
private consumption and could also dampen the recent strengthening of private investment. 
Overall, projected GDP growth is expected at 6.9% in 2026, slowing to around 6.7% in 2027 and 
accelerating again in 2028. Key downside risks relate to a less supportive external environment 
and potential effects of below-average rains, including in the context of El Niño, on agriculture and 
rural demand.  
Growth across EMEs is set to weaken in 2026, amidst widening divergences across and 
within regions. In Türkiye, the economic fallout of the energy shock and rising domestic political 
tensions have stalled the disinflation process, with inflation persisting at around 31%. Fiscal and 

=== PAGE 28 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
12 
monetary policy remain tight. Growth is expected to slow to 2.8% in 2026, in a context of weaker 
domestic demand and a negative contribution from net exports, before recovering somewhat in 
2027 and 2028. The economic outlook across the countries of the Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC) 
has also weakened markedly as the prolonged conflict in the Middle East disrupts shipping through 
the Strait of Hormuz and constrains hydrocarbon production and exports, while non-oil activity 
remains comparatively resilient. Consistently with indications from the energy futures market, a 
steep rebound recovery is expected in 2027 as energy flows are assumed to normalise. However, 
recent attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure and the temporary shutdown of the East-West 
pipeline highlight substantial downside risks for these economies. Several economies in Sub-
Saharan Africa are facing a combination of economic and humanitarian pressures from the Middle 
East conflict and renewed disruptions to Black Sea shipping, with high energy and food prices. 
Regional growth in Sub-Saharan Africa is nonetheless expected to hold up, supported by stronger 
export revenues and economic activity in a few resource-rich economies, which help offset weaker 
growth in countries heavily dependent on energy and food imports. In Latin America, the wider 
impact of the conflicts is similarly uneven. In Brazil, growth is projected to stabilise at around 2%, 
with higher energy and commodity prices partly cushioning the drag from high interest rates and 
reliance on imported fertilisers. In Mexico, growth is expected to pick up toward its potential growth 
rate of 2% as external and domestic uncertainty slowly dissipates. In Russia, growth is expected to 
weaken further in 2026 and 2027, with only a marginal recovery in 2028. The impact of higher oil 
prices on economic activity is expected to remain limited as the dire fiscal situation and an 
overleveraged corporate sector absorb much of the windfall oil revenues. In parallel, continued 
elevated war-related fiscal spending and ongoing Ukrainian drone attacks on refineries add to 
underlying inflationary pressures, where the resulting drag from high interest rates is set to 
continue weighing on the economy. 
Global headline inflation largely followed energy price developments. After increasing 
from 3.3% in March to 4.0% in May, global inflation eased to 3.8% in July before rising to 4.6% in 
September, surpassing its spring peak. This renewed acceleration reflects the energy shock 
associated with the conflict in the Middle East. Inflation in advanced economies followed a similar 
pattern, rising from 3.1% in March to 3.7% in September, despite a temporary easing over the 
summer. In the US, headline inflation climbed from 2.4% in January to 4.2% in May, fell to 3.4% in 
July and rebounded to 3.7% in September, amidst renewed energy price pressures and persistent 
services inflation. Inflation also temporarily eased during the summer in other advanced 
economies (e.g., Canada, the EU, and the UK) while it picked up in Japan. In EMEs, headline inflation 
rose from 3.3% in March to 5.5% in September, reflecting the much higher share of energy in their 
consumption baskets. China remained an exception as inflation fell from 1% in March to 0.5% in 
July before edging up to 0.9% in September, with core inflation reaching 1%.  
Global financial conditions have tightened since the spring, reflecting changing 
monetary policy expectations and rising long-term borrowing costs. Renewed inflationary 
pressures have led some central banks to pause easing or resume tightening. The US Federal 
Reserve raised its target rate range to 3.75–4% in September, with most Federal Open Market 
Committee (FOMC) participants expecting at least one further hike in 2026. Other advanced 
economies, including the euro area, Australia and Japan, have also raised policy rates since the 
spring. By contrast, some emerging economies, including Brazil, have continued to ease policy, 
reflecting differences in domestic inflation dynamics and the scope for monetary easing. Long-
term government bond yields have risen across major advanced economies. The US 10-year 
Treasury yield reached around 5.1% at the end of October, up from 4.4% at the end of April, while 
the 30-year yield exceeded 5.4%, compared with around 5% at the end of June. Similar pressures 
have affected the UK, Japan and the euro area. Beyond inflation and expectations of higher policy 
rates, rising real yields, term premia and government borrowing needs have contributed to the 
increase. Fiscal concerns have further differentiated sovereign borrowing costs, particularly in 
highly indebted economies, while strong corporate bond issuance to finance AI investment has 
added to long-term financing pressures. 
Risk asset markets have become more volatile, although corporate credit conditions and 
emerging market sovereign financing have remained relatively resilient. Equity markets 

=== PAGE 29 ===
Economic outlook for EA and EU 
 
13 
experienced periods of volatility as geopolitical tensions and concerns about stretched AI 
valuations weighed on technology shares. Pressure returned in the autumn due to higher oil prices 
and questions about the profitability and financing of AI investment. Corporate bond spreads 
widened but remained narrow by historical standards, while issuance in both investment-grade and 
high-yield segments remained robust, increasingly supporting AI-related capital expenditure. 
Foreign capital inflows into emerging market equity markets weakened, although investors 
continued to purchase emerging market sovereign debt. The US dollar appreciated modestly since 
the spring against a broad range of major advanced-economy currencies, including the euro, 
sterling and Japanese yen, supported by higher US yields and a more hawkish monetary policy 
outlook. 
Overall, global GDP growth is expected to slow down in 2026 before rebounding in 2027 
and stabilising in 2028. Global growth (excluding the EU) is forecast at 3.2% in 2026, slightly 
above 3.1% projected in spring, reflecting resilient activity in the first half of the year despite 
geopolitical tensions, trade-policy uncertainty and climate-related disruptions. Growth is expected 
to pick up to 3.6% in 2027, 0.1 pps. above the Spring 2026 Forecast, driven by a stronger 
expansion in EMEs, particularly in the Middle East, as energy markets normalise in line with the 
assumptions underpinning the forecast (see below). Emerging Asian economies are also expected 
to maintain robust high growth, supported by sustained AI-related demand. This acceleration in the 
EMEs is expected to offset the drag from tighter financial conditions and subdued activity in 
advanced economies. In 2028, global growth is projected to remain at 3.6%, with growth in 
advanced economies and EMEs holding broadly stable. In advanced economies, the drag from 
tighter financing conditions and a maturing AI investment cycle is expected to be offset by a 
recovery in real household incomes as energy prices normalise, together with sustained public 
investment in infrastructure and defence. In EMEs, continued domestic demand in emerging Asia, 
the lagged effects of monetary easing and ongoing investment in infrastructure and energy are 
expected to sustain activity, partly compensating for the fading impetus from the recovery in the 
Middle East. 
Despite the headwinds, global merchandise trade continued to expand at a strong pace 
thanks to tech-related products. Merchandise trade increased by 6% y-o-y during the first 
eight months of 2026 compared with the same period in 2025 (Graph I.2.3), with advanced, and 
emerging Asian economies driving the expansion and recording double-digit growth in both exports 
and imports. Strong trade in electronic components linked to the AI investment cycle more than 
offset the negative effects of the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle East. Despite accounting 
for only around 15% of global merchandise trade, AI-related goods contributed almost half of 
global goods trade growth in 2025, and their contribution increased further in the first half of 
2026. The strength of global trade is especially noteworthy given that trade had already expanded 
rapidly in the first half of 2025, partly reflecting the frontloading of shipments ahead of the 
anticipated increases in US tariffs. US trade-weighted tariff rates have been hovering around 
10.5% since February 2026, but the revenue-implied effective rates were around 3 pps. lower. This 
gap reflects implementation and collection lags, as well as changes in import patterns, including 
substitution and trade re-routing. Global services trade remained buoyant in the first half of 2026, 
supported by strong merchandise trade activity and continued robust demand for digitally 
deliverable services. 

=== PAGE 30 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
14 
 
 
Disruptions at key trade chokepoints led supply chain pressures to increase markedly in 
2026. The Global Supply Chain Pressure Index (GSCPI) of the Federal Reserve Bank of New York, 
measured in standard deviations relative to its long-term average, increased from -0.12 in 
November 2025 to 1.84 in April 2026, its highest level since the summer of 2022, before easing 
to 1.2 in August but edging up again in September. The increase reflected the reduction in traffic 
through the Strait of Hormuz, repeated typhoon-related disruptions at major Asian ports and the 
historically low water levels in European rivers. The outlook for supply chain disruptions remains 
highly uncertain: disruptions to traffic through the Strait of Hormuz have intensified in the last few 
weeks, while risks have also increased around the Bab el-Mandeb Strait, the Red Sea and the Black 
Sea. Against this backdrop, global shipping costs have remained volatile. The Freightos Global 
Container Freight Index reached its highest level since January 2025 in July before easing as the 
seasonal peak in container-shipping demand faded (Graph I.2.4.). Nevertheless, in October it 
remained about 47% above its 2025 average, partly reflecting persistent port congestion and 
higher fuel costs. Dry-bulk freight costs also surged in 2026, amidst weather-related disruptions 
and tightened vessel availability, pushing the Baltic Dry Index to a five-year high in September. The 
index subsequently corrected markedly but remained about 47% above its 2025 average. 
The trade-weighted average US tariff rates have increased over the summer, and 
further hikes may be expected. Following the expiry of the temporary 10% tariff regime 
imposed under Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 in July 2026 (which were introduced for 150 
days after the Supreme Court struck down the IEEPA tariffs in February 2026), the US 
administration re-established the tariff regime under an alternative legal basis. Following 
investigations conducted under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974, the US imposed 10% or 
12.5% tariffs on most trading partners, on the grounds that they had failed to take sufficient 
measures to prevent forced labour in supply chains. Changes in country-level trade-weighted 
average tariff rates have remained relatively limited, mostly within 1 pp., except for Brazil and 
Canada. Brazil faced a large tariff hike from July, while certain Canadian imports are subject to 
additional 50% tariffs since August, imposed under a new legal basis (Section 338 of the Tariff Act 
of 1930). This prompted retaliatory measures from Canada. By contrast, the trade-weighted 
average tariff rates for the EU edged down. Overall, the US trade-weighted average tariff rate 
increased from 10.3% in April to 10.6% by September. Trade policy uncertainty remains elevated. 
Further tariff rate increases cannot be ruled out as the US administration has repeatedly stated its 
intention to restore effective tariff rates to levels broadly comparable with the “Liberation day”’ 
IEEPA tariffs.  
Despite lingering trade policy uncertainty, global trade is projected to expand at a 
robust pace over the forecast horizon, supported by strong demand for AI-related 
goods. As major US and global technology companies continue to massively scale up their

and the assumed easing of supply chain constraints are set to provide additional global trade 
momentum, further offsetting the negative impacts of protracted geopolitical tensions. Global

in 2028 as the pace of global AI investment growth is projected to slow after several years of 
exceptionally buoyant expansion. Systemic trade frictions, risks to vital shipping corridors and 
continuous tariff pressures are expected to lead to an increasingly fractured and uneven regional 
trade performance.

Box I.2.1: China’s export and import performance amidst escalating US-China trade 
tensions
China’s merchandise trade developments so far 
point to a new record surplus in 2026. In 2025, 
China’s exports in value terms rose by 5.5% to a 
record USD 3.77 trillion, while imports were broadly 
unchanged at USD 2.58 trillion. The trade surplus 
expanded by 19.8%, to USD 1.19 trillion, exceeding 
USD 1 trillion for the first time. This imbalance has 
been 
supported 
by 
China’s 
manufacturing 
competitiveness, aggressive industrial policy and cost 
advantages, against the backdrop of subdued 
domestic demand and weak import growth. In 2026, 
nominal exports accelerated substantially, growing 
by 36.3% y-o-y in September, to an all-time high of 
USD 447.2 billion. In the same month, the surplus 
widened by 50.5% y-o-y, to USD 133.5 billion, the 
fifth consecutive month above USD 100 billion (see Graph 1). At USD 938.1 billion, the surplus accrued 
so far this year puts China on track to surpass its 2025 record, further widening its already substantial 
contribution to global external imbalances. 
China’s export dynamism reflects swift adaptation to US trade policy changes, supported 
by trade re-routing and supply chain reconfiguration across Asia. In 2025, US importers front-
loaded purchases ahead of the new tariff, temporarily supporting Chinese exports to the US. At the 
same time, an increasing share of Chinese goods reached the US via “connector” countries, particularly 
in Asia. This was accompanied by a gradual relocation of supply chains, as foreign multinationals 
adopted a “China +1” strategy – retaining operations in China while shifting part of their production to 
at least one other country, primarily in Asia. Chinese exporters also diversified their reach into emerging 
economies.  
These developments altered the geographical 
configuration of Chinese exports. By destination, 
the US share fell sharply, from 14% in 2024 to 10.2% 
in the first nine months of 2026, while intra-regional 
trade, particularly with East Asia and ASEAN, gained 
prominence. Until the outbreak of the Middle East 
conflict, exports to Sub-Saharan Africa, South Asia, 
and the MENA region (including the Gulf Cooperating 
Council states) also accounted for a growing share of 
total shipments (see Gaph 2). 
The evolution of direct trade flows between 
China and the US overstates the extent of their 
economic decoupling. This is because several 
economies in Asia, but also in Africa and Latin 
America act as connector hubs by taking over final 
assembly and late-stage production. Evidence in Lovely and Wan (2026) shows that Taiwan, Vietnam 
and Mexico have increased their shares of US imports, while simultaneously sourcing a growing share 
of their inputs from China. The same study finds that the Chinese value added embodied in total US 
imports has remained broadly stable despite the decline in bilateral trade. Thus, while China’s export 
destinations have become more geographically diversified, Chinese inputs remain deeply embedded in 
goods ultimately absorbed by the US market.

Trade re-routing towards the EU market does 
not appear to be part of the ongoing global 
reconfiguration of Chinese trade. The share of 
China in total EU imports has been on a steady 
upward trend for several decades, up from just 
around 3% in the early 2000s to 9.5% on average in 
the first eight months of 2026. Growth of import 
volumes (1) from China in 2025 and 2026 does not 
suggest a structural break from long-standing trends, 
though some sectors have seen strong acceleration. 
Notably, chemical import volumes have expanded at 
rates above 20% since 2021 per year, while vehicle 
imports accelerated from a roughly 20% average 
annual pace in 2021-2025 to more than 30% in the 
first eight months of 2026 (see Graph 3).  
Technology and “new energy” products are increasingly important drivers of China’s export 
growth to the EU. China’s export growth in 2026 is concentrated in a relatively narrow group of 
products, notably AI-related and semiconductors, as well as “new energy” products: electric vehicles, 
solar panels and batteries. Together, AI-related and “new energy” products accounted for around 58% 
of China’s export value gain in the first four months of the year. Rising prices account for part of the 
surge in China’s export values, particularly within AI-related trade where semiconductor price increases 
have been an important driver. Overall, the recent acceleration in China’s exports reflects a combination 
of stronger EU demand, higher prices and continued gains in market shares in selected technologically 
advanced manufacturing sectors. 
Following the flat profile in 2025, Chinese imports rebounded strongly in 2026, reflecting 
the country’s heavy import dependency for AI-related investments.  September imports rose 
by 31% y-o-y to an all-time high of USD 313.7 billion. The composition of import growth points to 
strong demand for specific supply chain inputs: imports of electrical machinery and machinery and 
equipment - two of the largest contributors to import growth in the first nine months of the year -  
increased by 47% and 48%, respectively. These gains have been associated with AI-related inputs such 
as memory chips and other electronic components. China’s role in the AI supply chain is concentrated 
less in the highest value chips and more in the electronic components, electrical and mechanical 
equipment used to build data centres. China thus remains a significant importer of higher value 
memory chips, which tend to be upstream inputs to this supply chain. The increase in AI-related imports 
therefore largely reflects China’s position as a downstream integrator in the global AI supply chain.  
China’s import acceleration this year also reflects higher energy prices and a surge in gold 
purchases, while domestic demand remains weak. Energy and commodity purchases have 
contributed to the higher import bill, reflecting both higher prices and increased stockpiling, motivated 
by energy security considerations. Precious metals have been the fastest-growing import category, 
more than doubling between January and September. The prevalence of gold coins and bars rather 
than jewellery in this import surge points to investment and store-of-value motives. Overall, China’s 
import rebound appears to be driven primarily by supply chain requirements, higher commodity prices 
and specific investment assets, rather than a broad-based recovery in domestic demand. 
These recent developments build on longer-standing structural drivers. China has steadily 
upgraded its export structure towards more complex, knowledge-intensive products and moved 
upstream in global value chains, increasingly supplying inputs to production abroad (Growth Lab at 
Harvard University, 2024; ADB, 2026). Prolonged domestic deflation has driven the substantial real 
 
(1) 
EU imports from China are better analysed in volume terms, as falling import prices (unit values) since 2023 
mean that import values understate the magnitude of expansion.

effective depreciation of the renminbi since 2020, strengthening Chinese producers’ cost advantage. 
Sustained, state-supported investment in manufacturing has also created excess capacity that weak 
domestic demand cannot absorb, increasing the incentive to sell output abroad. At the same time, 
policies promoting the substitution of imported components with domestic alternatives have increased 
China’s domestic value-added content and strengthened its position in global value chains. Together, 
these developments reinforce China’s manufacturing competitiveness, while fuelling growing concerns 
over the global distribution of production capacity. 
 

=== PAGE 35 ===
3. ENERGY AND COMMODITY MARKETS 
  
19 
After subsiding in early summer, oil prices have been on the rise since July and are now 
back to the highs reached just after the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle East. The 
closure of the Strait of Hormuz, following US and Israeli strikes on Iran in late February, disrupted 
global oil supplies and pushed prices markedly higher. Prices eased over the summer, as supply 
was partially redirected and market concerns over the immediate shortfall abated, before rising 
again following the breakdown of peace negotiations on 8 July. Front-month Brent futures prices, 
commonly used as a proxy for spot prices, increased from an average of USD 84/bbl in July to USD 
88/bbl in August (see Graph I.3.1). Following renewed military confrontation, attacks on tankers 
and Houthi strikes against Saudi energy infrastructure, prices surged to 100–110 USD/bbl in 
September and October, close to their peaks in late April. Despite the prolonged disruption to 
shipping through the Strait of Hormuz, the increase in crude prices has remained relatively 
contained. This partly reflects efforts to maintain and reroute deliveries, including through 
increased use of pipelines and overland bypasses, ship-to-ship transfers and, for some cargoes, 
the passage through the Strait under US military escort. Alternative sources of supply and 
continued releases from strategic oil reserves, coordinated by the International Energy Agency 
(IEA), further cushioned the supply losses. At the same time, higher prices and supply disruptions 
have weighed on oil demand, limiting the pressure on prices. The IEA reported contractions of 5 
mb/d y-o-y in 2026-Q2 and 3.7 mb/d y-o-y in 2026-Q3, and projected that global demand would 
fall annually by 2.6 mb/d to 102.4 mb/d. China in particular registered a strong reduction in oil 
demand following supply disruptions, partially thanks to a rapid shift to electric vehicles. For 2026 
as a whole, OPEC expects oil demand to decline relative to 2025 in both China and OECD Europe 
by 0.06 mb/d and 0.19 mb/d respectively. By contrast, demand is expected to increase in the US by 
0.17 mb/d. 
  
 
Futures indicate that market conditions are expected to gradually ease over the 
forecast horizon. Markets still price average Brent futures at around 118 USD/bbl in the fourth 
quarter of 2026, before they decline rapidly to around 88 USD/bbl in the final quarter of 2027, 
substantially above the assumptions in spring of 76 USD/bbl. Subsequently, they are expected to 
ease towards around 80 USD/bbl by the fourth quarter of 2028. The oil futures curve has 
remained in backwardation since the outbreak of the war, reflecting expectations that supply from 
the Middle East would normalise relatively quickly. However, as the conflict persists and the 
prospect of a resolution is repeatedly pushed back, the anticipated easing of supply constraints is 
continually deferred.

The price of oil paid by refineries has risen well above the spot price benchmark. The 
spread between Dated Brent (a key benchmark for oil prices at delivery in Northwestern Europe) 
and front-month Brent widened to around 30 USD/bbl in the beginning of October but narrowed by 
end-October, up from a pre-war narrow band of +/- 2 USD/bbls. (Graph I.3.2) The increase reflects 
a significantly higher premium to immediate physical availability. Yet, even the Dated Brent does 
not capture the full cost of delivery to buyers, but only the cost of crude itself. Shipping costs, 
which typically make up a small share of the oil price, have soared. By mid-October, the cost of 
shipping crude from the Middle East Gulf to Asia on a large tanker had risen to over 37 USD/bbl, 
more than five times its pre-war level, while shipping from West Africa to Europe on a Suezmax 
cost more than 22 USD/bbl, more than four times higher (IEA, 2026). This implies that the front-
month Brent price is increasingly a weak proxy for the total effective cost of imported oil. 
High feedstock prices combined with bottlenecks in refining capacity have pushed up the 
prices of oil products to historically high levels. With much of the Gulf’s refining capacity cut 
off from international markets and refineries elsewhere often left to process less suitable 
substitute crudes, the supply of key refined products is constrained. This is further compounded by 
disruptions to Russia’s refining capacity in the context of the ongoing conflict (Graph I.3.3 and 
Graph I.3.4). Refining margins—the difference between the cost of crude oil and the market value 
of the refined petroleum products produced from it—have consequently surged, with the Brent 3-
2-1 crack spread (a benchmark measuring the profit margin from refining three barrels of crude oil 
to two barrels of gasoline and one barrel of diesel) rising to USD 57/bbl in September, more than 
230% above its February 2026 level (1). Although it has eased somewhat since then, it remains 
very high. In October, the wholesale price of diesel averaged USD 193/bbl, more than double its 
level a year earlier. Jet fuel prices rose by a similar magnitude, while petrol reached USD 136/bbl—
up more than 70% y-o-y. Futures prices point to a decline in refining margins over the forecast 
horizon, with some seasonal fluctuations. However, the 3-2-1 crack spread is projected to remain 
around USD 24/bbl in the fourth quarter of 2028, about 30% above its 2024–25 average. This 
persistence suggests that refined product prices are being driven not only by crude supply 
shortages, but also by persistent constraints in refining capacity. Gasoline and gasoil/diesel futures 
likewise remain in backwardation, reflecting expectations of an easing of current supply pressures. 
Nevertheless, prices are expected to remain above their pre-conflict levels throughout the forecast 
horizon.

the loss of supply. Under the IEA’s March 2026

Global LNG supply remains tight as exports from the Gulf face difficulties bypassing the 
closure of the Strait of Hormuz. LNG supply is less flexible than oil supply and more 
geographically concentrated, making it particularly vulnerable to disruptions along key shipping 
routes. Unlike crude oil, which can be partly rerouted through pipelines and overland bypasses, LNG 
exports depend on specialised liquefaction facilities and LNG carriers, with limited scope for 
alternative transport routes. Qatar alone accounted for around 20% of global LNG exports before 
the conflict, virtually all of which transited the Strait of Hormuz. Following the outbreak of 
hostilities, LNG production was curtailed, disrupting deliveries to international markets. Additional 
supply from other producers and new capacity coming on stream elsewhere has only partly offset 
the shortfall, as existing facilities have limited scope to increase output in the short term and new 
capacity takes time to ramp up. 
 
 
European TTF gas spot and futures prices have increased notably since spring. The

85 EUR/MWh in October, closing the month just above 100 EUR/MWh (see Graph I.3.6). Futures are 
significantly higher than in spring, particularly for this year: on average, they are around 110%

spring. A steep drop is expected by mid-2027 as more global LNG supply comes online. In 2028, 
the TTF gas benchmark is expected to average 47 EUR/MWh. The premium of JKM—the main 
benchmark price for spot LNG delivered to Northeast Asia—over TTF, which signals the incentive to 
divert flexible cargoes to Asia, remained narrow in October. Both benchmarks rose together to 
levels last seen in late 2022, so Europe is competing directly with Asia for cargoes. EU gas storage 
stood at 75% at end-October, about 15 pps. below the five-year average and the lowest level for 
the season since records began in 2011 (compared to 83% in 2025, see Graph I.3.7). The low 
aggregate storage ratio must be seen against the backdrop of materially lower gas demand—
down almost 20% over the past 5 years. At the same time, the aggregate storage level conceals 
local vulnerabilities, as low storage in parts of the network can constrain the ability to meet 
demand locally, with resulting supply pressures propagating across interconnected markets (see 
Box I.3.1). 
Electricity prices also surged well above expectations in spring, pushed up by higher gas 
prices and weather-related shocks. The surge in wholesale electricity prices partly reflects the 
impact of last summer’s extreme weather events. Severe heatwaves boosted electricity demand 
for cooling, while widespread droughts hindered hydropower generation and curtailed cooling 
water availability for nuclear plants. This has strengthened the spillover from high gas prices into 
electricity prices in some European electricity markets. The German day ahead baseload prices—
the benchmark for the region—increased from an average of 106 EUR/MWh in July to 
167 EUR/MWh in October, which is 85% above the price in October 2025. The outlook is for 
sustained high prices throughout winter, peaking in January 2027 at around 210 EUR/MWh, some 
90% above January 2026 average prices. The outlook continues to point to moderation, in lockstep 
with developments in the gas market, although likewise expected to remain above pre-war levels 
over the forecast horizon.  
 
 
 
Differences in electricity futures remain substantial across the EU, with Germany and 
Italy showing significantly higher prices throughout the forecast horizon. In October, 
wholesale electricity prices (front-month baseload) averaged 220 EUR/MWh in Italy—45%, 35% 
and 30% above the corresponding prices in the integrated Iberian market (MIBEL), France and 
Germany respectively. Other sub-regional benchmarks traded in this range. The sharp differences 
reflect, to a large extent, past investment in renewable energy and/or nuclear, both of which reduce 
the reliance on gas for power generation. Over the forecast horizon, Italian wholesale electricity 
prices trade on average around 60% above corresponding Iberian futures.

Industrial metals prices broadly increased 
year-on-year, amid diverging developments 
between metals linked to construction and 
those 
linked 
to 
AI 
and 
the 
broader 
electrification investment cycle. Prices have 
been retreating for iron ore in recent months, and 
its futures curve is in backwardation over the 
forecast horizon (see Graph I.3.10). Aluminium 
prices have eased in recent months and futures 
are slightly tilted towards backwardation, as 
weakness in construction is partly offset by 
higher power costs for this electricity-intensive 
metal, supply disruptions linked to the Middle 
East conflict and robust demand from other 
sectors. 
Copper 
is 
benefitting 
from 
both 
electrification and AI investment, with gains year-
on-year and in recent months, although its futures curve is essentially flat over the forecast

upside, reflecting anticipated battery demand. Disruptions to Gulf supply of sulphur, a by-product 
of gas processing and oil refining, add to cost pressures, as it is used to produce the sulphuric acid 
needed for copper leaching and lithium refining.  
Agricultural production is facing higher input costs, adverse weather conditions and 
supply disruptions in key producing regions. Beyond the surge in energy prices, agricultural 
production is being hit by a sharp rise in fertiliser prices, driven by the disruption of supply from 
the Gulf—a key global producer—as well as higher gas prices. After surging in March and April, the 
World Bank Fertiliser Index eased back to pre-crisis levels and is now slightly below the level of the 
previous year. This composite headline glosses over some heterogeneity, as urea prices have fallen 
markedly since spring while phosphate prices stayed considerably firmer. Producer prices in the EU, 
however, have followed different dynamics, and while the pre-summer spike was more contained, 
current prices are still well-above previous year’s level. The rise in fertiliser prices compared to last 
year is expected to have a lagged impact on food prices next year, as farmers switch crops or 
reduce application rates, with an impact on yields. Logistical constraints in the Black Sea—a major 
global agricultural hub—linked to Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine continue to weigh on 
trade, especially of grains. Finally, this summer’s extreme temperatures and droughts have 
reduced European production, while the strengthening El Niño is weighing on the output of major 
staple commodities. The World Meteorological Organization warned that the current El Niño event 
could be unprecedented in strength. It is expected to peak towards the end of 2026 and persist 
until the first quarter of 2027. A strong El Niño increases the likelihood of significant shifts in 
temperature and rainfall patterns in many parts of the world, but especially around the tropics. In 
line with past episodes, this could lead to failed harvests and global food shortages that would 
weigh heavily on many developing economies and also exert additional upward pressures on some 
EU food imports.

World food commodity prices have been 
rising since early 2026, and farm-gate

earlier (see Graph I.3.11). The increase was driven 
by cereals, vegetable oils and sugar, while the

nonetheless stood 19.1% below its level a year

in the euro area, with a larger weight on more

further decline between May and July this year. It then rebounded by around 2.4% m-o-m in both

dairy prices. 
Futures point to elevated food commodity prices in 2027 and some easing towards 
2028. Curves for most traded products have shifted up since spring. Wheat futures are 10% 
above the spring assumptions for 2026-Q4 and 4% above for 2027, and the curve is broadly flat, 
implying a 2027 average around 8% above 2026. Soya meal, cocoa, sugar and coffee show a 
similar upward shift with flat curves, while maize and rice are in backwardation, with declines 
mainly in 2028. Futures are, however, an imperfect guide to EU food prices. They do not cover 
meat and dairy, which dominate EU farm-gate prices and depend more on feed, input costs and 
weather. Higher energy costs, fertiliser prices still well above last year’s levels and the lagged 
effects of the summer’s heat and drought are set to push farm-gate prices up in the coming 
quarters.
