Box I.3.1: Oil reserves, gas storage and the resilience of energy markets
Oil reserves and gas storage support market resilience, with oil reserves acting as an 
emergency buffer, while gas storage provides flexibility in normal operations. Strategic oil 
reserves are a buffer against severe supply disruptions: when released, they can temporarily offset a 
physical shortfall in supply. By contrast, gas storage is part of the normal functioning of the European 
gas market, providing flexibility to accommodate seasonal demand and other short-term fluctuations 
in supply and demand. Gas storage therefore helps the system absorb shocks over time, reducing their 
impact on prices and on security of supply. This box sets out these differences and their implications 
for the resilience of energy markets going forward. 
The crude oil market is deep, liquid and globally integrated, allowing supply to adjust to 
disruptions. Although the global oil market is commonly treated as a single entity, it comprises several 
connected markets, notably for crude oil and for refined products. Crude oil production is geographically 
diversified, and crude grades are to some extent substitutable as refinery feedstock, albeit with 
technical constraints and significant costs. Moreover, though rerouting can be costly, as freight rates 
become significant when transit risk increases, a large and flexible tanker fleet allows crude to be 
redirected towards markets where it is most highly valued.  Crude prices therefore adjust within a 
comparatively liquid global market, and crude trades within a narrower price range than other energy 
commodities like gas or coal. However, the oil market is not immune to volatility: price swings are 
inherent to the low responsiveness—or inelasticity—of both supply and demand to price changes in 
the short run. Extraction and transport infrastructure is fixed in the near term, so new supply takes 
years to develop. This responsiveness has nonetheless increased: before the shale oil revolution, crude 
supply was concentrated in low-cost but capital-intensive, long lead-time sources, with limited scope 
to expand output quickly in response to price (1). US shale oil, with its much shorter investment cycle, 
was the first large-scale source of such flexibility. Today a broader set of flexible, marginal producers—
not concentrated in shale alone—limits price increases at the margin, narrowing that range further.  
The supply of refined products is less flexible, constrained by greater segmentation, 
logistical frictions and refining capacity. Among fuels, refined products trade as at least three 
interdependent but distinct markets—gasoil, gasoline, and kerosene—each further segmented by 
regional product specification, blending requirements and delivery infrastructure. These markets are 
therefore thinner and less liquid than the market for crude, limiting the scope for trade to arbitrage for 
local shortages. This relatively low depth and liquidity in the downstream market compounds with 
greater logistical frictions. Bringing new refining capacity online typically takes several years. Moreover, 
although existing refineries can process a range of crude grades and adjust their product mix, doing 
so can entail significant losses in efficiency or yield, and this flexibility is particularly limited when 
refineries are operating close to full capacity. A disruption to refining capacity can therefore create a 
persistent shortage of products even when crude supplies remain available, with adjustment relying 
instead on trade, inventory drawdowns, changes in refinery utilisation and, ultimately, demand 
adjustment. Demand elasticity plays a role too: it is probably less inelastic than once estimated and its 
elasticity has likely increased further in the medium term as alternatives to the internal combustion 
engine become more widespread (2). 
 
 
(1) 
Empirical estimates confirm a substantial rise in the response of US crude supply to price since the shale 
revolution. Newell and Prest (2019) estimate that the aggregate supply response is around thirteen times larger 
than in the pre-shale era. 
(2) 
Better-identified estimates—using tax variation to separate price effects from income effects—put gasoline 
demand elasticity at -0.25 to -0.37 across the US, Japan and the EU (Coglianese, Davis, Kilian and Stock, 2017; 
Levin, Lewis and Wolak, 2017; Knittel and Tanaka, 2019; Aklilu, 2020), and diesel demand elasticity at around 
-0.3 across the OECD (Liddle and Huntington, 2020). Over a longer horizon, electric vehicles provide a further 
channel of adjustment: increases in gasoline prices stimulate EV adoption around 4–6 times more strongly than 
equivalent increases in electricity prices (Bushnell, Muehlegger and Rapson, 2022). 
 

=== PAGE 42 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
26 
 
Box (continued) 
      
(Continued on the next page) 
Strategic oil reserves play an important role 
in smoothening short-term supply disruptions. 
Reserves held by IEA members through the 
Agreement 
on 
an 
International 
Energy 
Programme (3) and by EU Member States under the 
Oil Stocks Directive are typically released as 
emergency drawdowns in response to a supply 
disruption.  EU Member States should have 
emergency stocks equivalent to 90 days of net 
imports or 61 days of inland consumption, 
whichever is higher. The obligation of holding stock 
equivalent to 90 days of net imports applies to all 
net-importing IEA members. Though the US became 
a net exporter in 2020, it still holds substantial crude 
reserves (the Strategic Petroleum Reserve, SPR), 
generally released as the US contribution to wider 
IEA collective action. Recent IEA coordinated releases happened in 2011 (Libyan Civil War, 60 million 
barrels), in 2022 (Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, 60 million barrels, of which 30 million from 
the SPR), and, at far greater scale, in 2026, following the closure of the Strait of Hormuz, when IEA 
members committed to release 400 million barrels - the largest coordinated release in IEA history 
(Graph 1). The release is still ongoing: in October, the G7 called for the remaining volumes under the 
March commitment to be released more quickly, with a substantial share of diesel front-loaded. While 
this was not a separate 100-million-barrel release on top of the original 400 million barrels, total 
releases may exceed that amount as some countries go beyond their initial pledges.  By historical 
standards, reserves are now quite low, especially in the US, raising concerns about the capacity to 
absorb persisting supply disruptions (4). 
Still, the composition of strategic reserves may not be well aligned with the current 
configuration of the global oil market. The reserve architecture was established largely as an 
insurance mechanism against the exceptional supply disruptions of the 1970s, when trade in refined 
products was limited and refining capacity concentrated next to final markets. Also, trade was mostly 
concentrated between the Middle East, North America and Europe, whereas Asia is by now the largest 
importer. That reserve architecture of the 1970s still carries over today: reserves remain weighted 
toward crude globally, in particular in the US and Japan. The composition in terms of crude and products 
is more balanced in Europe, and the EU average for emergency stocks is about 60% oil products and 
40% crude. This adequacy, however, should be assessed against two further developments: an 
extended decline in European refining capacity, and a widening mismatch between where products are 
produced and where they are consumed, as regional refining specialisation has increased (5). In 
 
(3) 
Reserves are held and released under arrangements that vary considerably across countries. Three broad 
approaches can be distinguished: (1) government stocks, owned directly by the state and financed through the 
public budget (e.g. the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve), which can be released through government-directed 
sales or loans; (2) agency stocks, held by a dedicated public entity on behalf of either government or industry 
which can be released with government authorisation; and (3) mandated industry stocks, held directly by private 
operators under a minimum stockholding obligation set by governments, with release permitted by temporarily 
lowering that obligation (e.g. the model existing in several European Member States). 
(4) 
Reported reserve volumes can overstate what is genuinely available to cushion a shock. Part of the constraint 
is technical. Crude reserves held in underground storage, including the geological formations used for the 
largest strategic holdings, cannot be withdrawn at a constant rate as volumes decline, so that the system 
approaches operational limits well before physical volumes reach zero. The operational limits are harder to 
define for mandated reserves, as a share of what is reported as commercial inventory constitutes working 
capital rather than a genuine buffer: the minimum volume of product that must remain in pipelines, storage 
tanks, and distribution networks for those networks to continue functioning at all. Therefore, reserves cannot 
be drawn down to the reported total without impairing the operation of the system that holds them. 
(5) 
Reserves held under a mandated stockholding obligation, whether of crude or refined product, are considerably 
more difficult to track, since they may be commingled with the ordinary commercial inventory that operators 
hold for operational reasons. Tracking and composition concerns are accordingly more pronounced in the 
refined-product segment, which—unlike crude—cannot as readily be redirected or substituted across 
specifications.

Box (continued) 
      
(Continued on the next page) 
particular, Europe faces a structural oversupply of petrol and a structural undersupply of diesel and, 
especially, jet fuel.  
The growing role of LNG means Europe's gas market is now more affected by global demand 
and supply conditions. Gas markets have historically been more geographically segmented. Global 
LNG supply has grown rapidly over the past decade, while long-distance pipeline trade has stagnated 
or declined, notably after the sharp decline in Russian pipeline exports to Europe which followed 
Russia's invasion of Ukraine. This has increased the scope for gas to be redirected across regions and 
strengthened the links between regional gas markets. Competition for flexible LNG cargoes has driven 
greater convergence between global price benchmarks, such as the Japan-Korea Marker (JKM), the 
benchmark for Asia, and the Dutch Title Transfer Facility (TTF), Europe’s benchmark. 
Gas storage is an integral part of the European gas system. It performs several functions: it 
accommodates seasonal and short-term fluctuations in demand, provides physical flexibility by 
allowing gas to be stored close to consumption centres, including in less-connected parts of the 
network, and reduces the import and regasification capacity otherwise required to meet peak demand. 
It also provides intertemporal flexibility, allowing market participants to secure gas ahead of periods 
of anticipated tightness rather than relying on spot-market purchases at times of stress. These 
functions are particularly important in a market characterised by a physical network with finite capacity 
and limited connectivity, a supply base increasingly reliant on globally traded LNG rather than fixed-
capacity pipelines, and the predominance of hub-based spot pricing (6). Storage levels follow a 
pronounced seasonal pattern, typically filling from a spring low of around a fifth to a third of capacity 
to above 90% ahead of the winter heating season, before being drawn down again over the following 
months. Storage volumes do not fall to zero even at the point of maximum depletion: a portion of the 
gas in each facility—known as cushion gas—must remain in place to maintain the pressure needed 
for withdrawal. As storage levels decrease, the rate at which gas can be withdrawn declines well before 
the physical stock is exhausted. 
Low storage compounds network constraints and can give rise to persistent supply-demand 
imbalances. In the gas market, meeting demand depends not only on the total volume of gas 
available, but on whether it can be delivered to the right location at the right time. In the absence of 
binding constraints, both supply and demand respond to price in the ordinary way, so that even a 
sizeable imbalance is absorbed through a moderate price adjustment, shared between buyers curbing 
demand and sellers releasing more gas. Under extreme congestion, however, this mechanism breaks 
down: the volume that can physically be delivered may be constrained irrespective of price incentives, 
and demand at the point of use may also be relatively unresponsive to price in the short term. 
Importantly, gas also feeds into industrial processes and electricity generation, which contributes to 
lower short-run demand elasticity (7). As congestion becomes binding, the price increasingly reflects 
scarcity at the constrained location rather than the aggregate balance between supply and demand. 
With neither side responding materially to price at that margin, even a modest local imbalance can 
 
(6) 
This configuration reflects both the legacy of the pipeline era, from which Europe inherited substantial 
underground storage capacity, and the particular geology of North-Western Europe, which is well suited to 
underground gas storage. The alternative model, adopted by major Asian importers, relies instead on long-term 
contracts that fix price and volume in advance, complemented by sufficient import capacity to receive 
contracted volumes when required. This latter model tends to be less flexible and more costly to maintain, since 
import capacity must be sized to meet peak rather than average demand. 
(7) 
Evidence on short-run price elasticities suggests important differences across users. For residential consumers, 
Favero and Grossi (2023), using monthly billing data for natural-gas customers in Italy, estimate price 
elasticities of around −0.23 to −0.51. By contrast, Alpino et al. (2025), using the staggered expiry of fixed-price 
contracts to identify energy-price shocks for Italian industrial firms, find virtually no short-run response initially; 
gas demand only adjusts later, with the response particularly limited for gas-intensive producers. Their results 
also indicate that subsequent reductions in gas use reflect both input substitution and reductions in output. 
More broadly, Emiliozzi and Favero (2025) find that gas demand remains close to unresponsive during the first 
year following supply-driven price shocks, consistent with technological and contractual rigidities. 

=== PAGE 44 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
28 
 
Box (continued) 
      
(Continued on the next page) 
therefore require a disproportionately large price adjustment (8). Storage is critical to the system's 
ability to absorb such constraints. Low storage can reduce withdrawal capacity and leave the system 
more exposed to local infrastructure bottlenecks, while imports, particularly pipeline supplies, cannot 
always be increased or redirected at short notice. The resulting adjustment may take the form of 
extreme prices, demand curtailment, or both. The contrasting price responses to broadly comparable 
tightenings of the European gas flow balance in the first and fourth quarters of 2021 illustrate how 
the storage position affects the market's ability to absorb shocks (9).  
Local price pressures are transmitted across European markets via a de facto single, hub-
based pricing system. European gas trades predominantly against one reference price, set by the 
TTF. Its influence extends beyond the physical infrastructure that underpins it. This dominance reflects 
the geographical centrality of the Dutch infrastructure, its dense entry points and storage capacity, and 
is reinforced by its elevated degree of financialisation (10). As a result, other European virtual trading 
points (NBP, PSV, THE, PEG, ZTP) largely track TTF prices. Under hub-based pricing, scarcity at any point 
of the network shows up in the common reference price. This is an efficient feature of hub-based 
pricing, but tensions arise when the network is interconnected enough to transmit the marginal price 
signal, but not enough to relieve local demand-supply mismatches. This tension between a highly 
integrated financial architecture and an imperfectly connected infrastructure is mitigated by adequate 
storage, which reduces the risk that localised demand-supply imbalances are transmitted to the whole 
market. This transmission holds only as long as the network is not fully saturated: when it is, trading 
points can decouple, as occurred for much of 2022 (Fulwood, 2024), and the local shortage is no longer 
shared across the market, but borne locally. Such decoupling signals a severely stressed market, in 
which the network can no longer absorb further shocks. Where transmission does hold, it is not confined 
to Europe. Given the size of the European gas market, a rise in the TTF pulls up other benchmarks such 
as the JKM, as flexible LNG cargoes go to wherever prices are highest, as happened in 2021–22. 
Private incentives to store gas may fall short of the value of storage for the system, thus 
creating a market failure. Some operators have an incentive to store gas only to the extent that 
the expected summer-to-winter price differential—or seasonal spread—justifies the cost and risks of 
holding it. Other operators are bound by contractual supply obligations and may face penalties if they 
fail to meet them. This is generally reflected by the continuous injection in summer, regardless of the 
price configuration. However, while the costs of holding gas are generally borne by the individual 
operators, the benefits of adequate aggregate storage are collective: a larger buffer reduces the 
probability of severe price volatility and limits the consequences of a supply disruption for the wider 
market. The individually optimal level of storage for market operators—utilities, gas suppliers, traders 
and large industrial consumers—may therefore result in an insufficient level of storage at system 
level. This is a standard market failure problem that creates a rationale for public intervention. 
 
(8) 
Second-order responses can further amplify the effect: as the price rises in response to the constraint, operators 
holding gas have an incentive to delay releasing it in anticipation of a still higher price ahead, while buyers 
facing the same signal bid more aggressively to secure supply before being rationed—reinforcing the very 
price rise that prompted it. The resulting price reflects the cost of securing the marginal deliverable unit at that 
moment, rather than the average scarcity of gas supply across the market. Oliver et al. (2014) formalise this 
logic for US pipeline networks, showing that as congestion between two points rises, the scarcity value of 
transmission capacity itself drives a wedge between their spot prices—and that storage mitigates this effect 
by allowing intertemporal substitution of transmission services.  
(9) 
In 2021-Q1, a tightening of the flow balance of around 202 MMcm/d (relative to  2019-Q1) left TTF prices 
largely unresponsive, as European storage was almost full at the start of the 2020-21 winter. Weak injections 
over the following two quarters left storage at around 77.5 Bcm by end-September—roughly 8 Bcm below the 
pre-2019 average—eroding the buffer available to absorb further shocks. By 2021-Q4, a broadly comparable 
tightening of the flow balance, around 238 MMcm/d, coincided with a far sharper price response, as the 
depleted storage position left the market little slack to absorb it (Fulwood, 2022).  
(10) This in turn increases the risk of financial contagion. Market participants that have hedged their positions can 
face substantial demands for additional collateral as prices move sharply against those positions. Firms can 
therefore face acute liquidity pressure even where their underlying physical supply position remains adequate. 
A related channel operates in the opposite direction: because the relevant futures contract settles by physical 
delivery rather than in cash, positions held by participants without a genuine physical stake must be closed out 
ahead of expiry, and the closing out of a short position, as prices move against it, can itself add to upward price 
pressure independently of any change in the underlying physical balance. 

=== PAGE 45 ===
Economic outlook for EA and EU 
 
29 
Box (continued) 
      
Mandatory filling targets help internalise the system value of storage, though enforcement and 
compliance is not uniform across Member States, which can lead to gas aggregate storage levels 
falling below adequate levels – as indeed currently the case (Graph 2). This provides a rationale for a 
robust coordinated governance framework, with mandatory filling targets helping to ensure that 
adequate storage levels are maintained to safeguard the smooth functioning of the gas market and 
the security of gas supply. 
Ultimately, both oil reserves and gas storage 
are key in market resilience, underscoring the 
importance of ensuring their adequate level, 
composition and governance framework. The 
role of policy is to ensure that strategic reserves are 
of appropriate size and composition, and promptly 
accessible and usable, while allowing prices to fulfil 
their role in allocating scarce supply and encouraging 
adjustment in demand. In this respect, policy 
measures at the time of a supply disruption should 
avoid suppressing the price signal. Inadequate 
storage in the European gas market amplifies 
potential network congestion, which can in turn 
prevent the orderly functioning of the market even 
under normal global supply conditions. The role of 
policy is therefore to ensure storage levels remain 
adequate through appropriate governance, making 
use of existing import and network capacity. The price 
signal remains key to allocate scarce LNG supply across regions and–under normal congestion–within 
the European gas market. At extreme degrees of congestion, however, prices stop performing this 
function, making measures such as the EU's 2022 price cap (11) or joint purchasing arrangements 
(including the EU Energy Platform's AggregateEU mechanism) become relevant policy 
considerations (12). 
In the long term, energy transition remains key. Building new refining capacity in response to a 
temporary disruption is not necessarily warranted, just as expanding gas import and network capacity 
is difficult to justify given the expected long-term decline in gas demand. For refining specifically, this 
assessment rests on the assumption that current bottlenecks remain temporary rather than structural, 
and that the oil market retains its globalised character rather than fragmenting along regional lines. 
More broadly, in an international environment set to remain geopolitically challenging, reducing 
structural dependence on imported fossil fuels through a continued shift towards domestically 
produced low-carbon energy is not only a climate objective but also a matter of economic security and 
macroeconomic stability. A massive shift towards electrification, meanwhile, brings its own challenges: 
electricity networks require substantial investment to be repowered, expanded and digitalised to absorb 
increasing volumes of electricity and handle more volatile supply conditions.  
 
(11) This is the Market Correction Mechanism, in force from 2023 until 31 January 2025 and never activated, which 
could be triggered by a TTF front-month price above €180/MWh and at least €35/MWh above an LNG reference 
price computed across different European delivery points, both sustained for three consecutive working days. 
(12) The two differ in kind: the former intervenes directly on the price signal itself, the latter coordinates demand

4. FINANCIAL CONDITIONS IN THE EU 
  
30 
The European Central Bank (ECB) and most 
other EU monetary authorities embarked on 
a new path of monetary policy tightening 
since the Spring 2026 Forecast. The ECB’s 
deposit rates had remained unchanged at 2.0% 
following 
the 
completion 
of 
the 
previous 
tightening cycle in June 2025. As inflationary 
pressures mounted, the ECB raised its policy rates 
again, by 25 bps. last June and by a further 25 
bps. in September. At the cut-off date of this 
forecast, markets expected the ECB to tighten 
further, with the deposit rate rising by around 75 
bps. by the end of summer 2027, to around 3.3%. 
This is some 70 bps. above the level implied in 
the Spring 2026 Forecast (see Graph I.4.1). 
Financial markets were also pricing in further 
policy rate increases in most non-euro-area Member States.  
Euro area inflation expectations remain 
anchored, reflecting markets confidence in 
the ECB’s ability to restore price stability. 
While shorter-term inflation expectations have 
increased, according to both markets and 
consumers, 
markets’ 
longer-term 
inflation 
expectations have remained broadly unchanged 
at close to 2%. The gap between short- and 
medium-term inflation expectations is consistent 
with markets’ expectation of a waning inflation 
shock (see Graph I.4.2.) 
Long-term sovereign bond yields have 
increased over the summer. In September, 
yields reached their highest levels in the post-
COVID-19 period. The 10-year German Bund yield 
peaked at 3.65% at the end of September before 
gradually declining to close to 3.3% by the cut-off date. This is still 30 bps. higher than at the time 
of the Spring 2026 Forecast. The US equivalent yield displayed a similar pattern, rising from 4.4% 
in spring to a peak of 5.3% at the end of September, before falling back to 4.9% at the end of 
October. Yields have also increased since spring in other advanced economies, including the United 
Kingdom and Japan (see Graph I.4.3).

The repricing of government bonds reflects 
a combination of interconnected factors. 
According to a model-based decomposition (see 
Box I.4.1), the drivers of rising long-term rates 
include the effect of the short-term rise in 
inflation and the expected monetary policy 
response, as well as the widening gap between 
supply and demand of bonds, especially at long 
maturities. The large volume of expected 
government 
and 
corporate 
long-term 
debt 
issuance is increasing the supply of bonds that 
needs to be absorbed by investors. At the same 
time, central banks have turned net sellers of 
sovereign bonds, while traditional institutional 
investors have given way to more price-sensitive 
buyers of long-term bonds. Investors also seem 
to increasingly differentiate between sovereigns 
based on their fiscal risks. 
Real interest rates are expected to increase 
over the forecast horizon. Rising nominal long-
term interest rates come against the background 
of well-anchored inflation expectations, largely 
implying higher long term real rates compared 
with the Spring 2026 Forecast (see Graph I.4.4). 
Meanwhile, real short-term rates have declined 
since spring as inflation outpaced policy rates. 
However, 
they 
are 
expected 
to 
increase 
considerably over the forecast horizon as inflation 
eases while policy rates remain elevated. 
Within the Euro area, sovereign spreads 
widened for some countries, notably for 
France. As the yield of the French 10-year bond 
increased to around 5% at the end of October, 
the French spread exceeded 160 bps., double its 
level at the end of August and up from 65 bps. in 
early May (see Graph I.4.5). The widening of 
French sovereign yield spreads can be associated 
with 
market 
perceptions 
of 
uncertainty 
surrounding the country's medium-term fiscal 
outlook and the broader policy environment 
ahead of the forthcoming national elections. In other highly indebted euro area countries, the 
increases in sovereign yield spreads have been more contained. These changes can be largely 
attributed to higher sovereign credit risks, with the credit default swaps (CDSs) having increased 
significantly since mid-September for most of those countries (see Graph I.4.6).

Banks surveyed in the latest Euro Area Bank Lending Survey (BLS) for the third quarter 
of 2026 reported tightened credit standards, with further tightening ahead. For 
corporations, credit supply remained restrictive, with banks continuing to tighten lending standards. 
Heightened risk perceptions and lower risk tolerance, amid ongoing geopolitical uncertainty and 
volatility in energy markets, contributed to tighter credit standards. For households, credit 
standards remained unchanged for housing loans but tightened further for consumer credit. Risk 
tolerance and risk perceptions continued to have a small tightening impact on credit standards for 
households. For the fourth quarter, banks expect credit standards to tighten further for all types of 
loans.  
Banks also reported a net decline in demand in the third quarter of 2026 by both 
corporates and households. Banks reported a net decrease in loan demand for firms, driven 
primarily by the impact of the general level of interest rates but also by weaker demand for fixed 
investment and a shift towards alternative sources of financing. Demand for housing loans 
declined sharply in the third quarter, driven by rising interest rates, declining consumer confidence 
and worsening housing market prospects. At the same time, demand for consumer credit continued 
to decline slightly. For the fourth quarter, banks expect loan demand to decline further for 
households, and in particular for housing loans. For firms, banks expect loan demand to remain 
broadly unchanged. 
Bank lending to the private sector remains 
robust amid rising financing costs. Since 
spring, the cost of borrowing for both non-
financial corporations (NFCs) and households 
have increased slightly (see Graph I.4.7). The 
composite cost of borrowing for corporations in 
the euro area, as measured by the Commission’s 
composite 
credit 
cost 
indicator 
for 
NFCs, 
increased from 3.61% in March to 4.03% in 
September. The composite credit cost for 
households increased from 3.88% in March to 
4.15% in September. Over the same period, the 
annual growth rate of lending to the private 
sector continued to increase and reached 4.3% in 
September, up from 3.4% in March. The increase 
was supported by a robust growth in loans to 
NFCs, which recorded annual growth of 4.2% in September.

EU equity valuations adjusted to higher 
interest rates but continued to show robust 
performance. European equity prices declined 
from mid-August to early October on the back of 
rising sovereign yields and the rebound in energy 
prices adding to inflationary pressure. However, 
better than expected outturns on economic 
activity in the EU, robust corporate results and 
expected profitability gains from AI adoption 
continued to be supportive for equity markets. 
Overall, European equities are trading higher than 
in spring (some +6% for the Eurostoxx600 since 
early May, slightly overperforming the US’s 
S&P500 
over 
the 
same 
period). 
Sectoral 
differences persist, with the strong performance 
of the energy, commodities and technological 
sectors. The banking and financial sectors also performed well recently, being driven by strong 
earnings and the expanded net interest margins resulting from higher current and expected 
interest rates. By contrast, corporations exposed to consumers continue to underperform relative to 
other sectors (see Graph I.4.8). 
The euro NEER has weakened by 3%. Between early May and the cut-off date of this outlook 
update, the euro weakened by about 5% against the US dollar, by more than 7% against the 
Chinese renminbi and by 6% against the Japanese yen. The depreciation of the euro NEER was led 
by a weakening against major currencies, while the euro strengthened against a number of 
emerging market currencies. The depreciation of the euro against the dollar over this period was 
driven by higher real rates in the US, expectations of tighter monetary policy by the FED, and the 
more favourable US growth outlook. In August, the US dollar moved in reaction to news about 
domestic fiscal and monetary policies. It weakened in mid-August amid fiscal and debt concerns 
but later recovered after FED Chair Warsh’s speech, which signalled the FED’s commitment to its 
inflation target and the FED actual rate hike in mid-September. More recently, the depreciation of 
the euro appears to have reflected both a downward revision of expectations for monetary 
tightening in the euro area and an increase in perceived fiscal and political risks in some euro area 
Member States, as indicated by widening long-term government bond spreads. 
House-price growth eased in the first half of 2026 as financing conditions tightened. In 
2026-Q2, house prices were 4.7% higher than a year earlier in the EU and 4.0% higher in the euro 
area in 2026-Q2, down from 5.4% and 5.0% in 2025-Q4. Price growth nonetheless remained in 
double digits in nine Member States, as at the end of 2025, while prices fell in a few others (2). The 
EU house prices are forecast to increase by 4.4% in 2026 and 4.0% in 2027. Continued housing 
price growth is intensifying affordability pressures which, in terms of macroeconomic impact on 
consumption, are offsetting the positive wealth effects from rising house prices (see Box I.4.2.).   
Overall, financial conditions have tightened further since the Spring 2026 Forecast. The 
tightening reflects an upward shift in market interest rates across maturities. As longer-term 
inflation expectations remained unchanged, real long-term interest rates turned higher since 
spring, while short-term real rates are expected to increase over the forecast horizon amid 
expectations of abating shorter-term inflation and still high policy rates. Financial conditions are 
thus expected to tighten further throughout the forecast horizon. This may slow down the current 
dynamism in bank lending to corporations, which currently stands firm despite rising funding costs, 
reported tightened credit standards and declining demand. EU equity valuations adjusted to higher 
interest rates but continued to show robust performance overall. 
 
(2) Annual growth in 2026-Q2 was highest in Portugal (16.5%), Bulgaria (15.5%) and Lithuania (14.3%), and above 10% 
also in Slovakia, Croatia, Spain, Romania, Latvia and Hungary. Prices fell in Finland (-2.7%), Luxembourg (-2.2%) and 
France (-0.8%).

Box I.4.1: What is driving the recent surge in long-term interest rates? 
Long-term interest rates have increased since late February in major economies and are 
now at historically elevated levels. In the US, the 10-year treasury yield increased by close to 130 
bps from its end-February levels, before partly retreating in October. At the cut-off date of this 
publication, 10-year US yields were above 5%, about 100 bps higher than in February. In the euro area, 
average yields rose by a similar magnitude over the same period – and have decreased too over recent 
weeks, though less markedly and with large variation across Member States. By end-October, 10-year 
risk-free rates (1) were above 3% in the euro area. These dynamics reflect a confluence of factors, 
including shifts in central bank policy expectations, surging supply in private and public debt and 
concerns about fiscal sustainability. However, there is considerable uncertainty, and no consensus, 
about the relative importance of these drivers.

Box (continued) 
       
(Continued on the next page) 
expected path of short-term rates and a term premium. (3) For the purpose of identifying the drivers of 
the increase in long-term yields, the present analysis further decomposes expected short-term rates 
into the expected real interest rates and inflation, while the term premium is decomposed into credit 
risk, inflation risk and a residual item. 
Higher inflation compensation accounts for about half of the rise in long-term euro area 
risk-free rates over the past eight months. As regards euro area risk-free rates,  increases in 
average expected inflation linked to the energy price shock are estimated to have added close to 35 
bps to 10-year yields, accounting for around half of their total increase since February. (4) The increase 
in projected inflation is concentrated in the first two years, as long-term inflation expectations have 
remained anchored. Moreover, the inflation risk premium has not moved much since February, 
suggesting that the uncertainty related to longer-term inflation outcomes, and the compensation 
required by bond investors for this risk, have not changed noticeably.   
About a quarter of the increase in long-term 
risk-free rates reflects higher expected short-
term real rates, mostly due to a higher 
estimated natural rate. An upward shift in the 
expected path of real short-term interest rates added 
an estimated 45bps to 10-year US yields, and 
approximately 20bps to aggregate euro area yields. 
While real short-term interest rates also reflect the 
expected reaction of monetary policy to higher 
inflation, the PRISM model suggests that their 
expected rise is mostly structural in nature and 
related to an increase in the estimated natural 
interest rate (5) in both regions (Graph 2). This 
increase can be understood in the present economic 
context as the consequence of (i) large investment 
needs (linked to artificial intelligence, national 
defence and energy security and transition) outstripping available or projected savings (including from 
traditionally surplus countries such as China and Gulf oil exporters); (ii) improved long-term growth 
prospects, potentially linked to AI-driven productivity gains. (6) (Investment in defence and in the energy 
transition, which plays an important role in Europe, is set to add less to potential growth when 
compared with AI, though it improves strategic autonomy and sustainability.) 
A significant share of the rise in long-term rates reflects a higher term premium: the extra 
compensation investors demand for holding long-term bonds. Specific imbalances between the 
supply of and the demand for long-term sovereign bonds are reflected in “other term premia”. (7) 
Several major economies have large stocks of government debt on increasing trajectories, with large 
fiscal deficits adding to them. In such a context, it has proved challenging to issue additional long-term 
sovereign bonds without investors demanding additional compensation, particularly as highly rated 
corporations have also increased their bond issuance, notably to fund capital investment in AI 
infrastructure. At the same time, central banks have been reducing their holdings of long-term 
government bonds, while other traditional institutional investors such as pension, insurance and 
sovereign wealth funds have partly retracted from the sovereign bond market, giving way to

The term premium is the additional compensation required by investors for holding longer-term bonds instead

(5)

(7)

premia” may also have been affected by other factors, including a tilting to the upside of the 
uncertainty surrounding the expected path of real short-term rates. Ultimately, the result has been an 
upward adjustment which has contributed to increase 10 year yields by approximately 50 bps in the 
US, and between approximately 10 and 25 bps in the four largest euro area economies (Graph 3). 
Perceived credit risk has followed heterogenous dynamics across euro area countries, 
related to their fiscal situation. This factor, which can be related to the level of sovereign debt 
ratios and to the fiscal policy stance, has remained relatively stable in Germany, Spain and in several 
other euro area Member States. However, it has increased markedly in France, particularly since 
September, and in Italy.

Overall, the rise of bond yields is driven by common factors, but their importance varies by 
country, with credit risk as the main source of heterogeneity within the euro area. While the 
results shown in this box are based on a reading of market data for yield, CDS and inflation swap 
curves, they are also subject to modelling choices which may affect the details of the decompositions. 
Still, a number of conclusions that are both general and robust can be drawn. One is that there has 
been close co-movement in the long-term sovereign yields of the US and euro area countries. Another 
is that all the main yield drivers – be they real, inflation or credit risk-related – have tended to move 
in the same direction of pushing up long term rates. A third conclusion is that the magnitude of their 
impact has differed across monetary areas and Member States. The increase in short-term inflation  
expectations has been markedly more important in the euro area while the effect of real factors has

Box I.4.2: Household wealth and household consumption 
Household wealth, defined as the difference between the value of households’ assets and 
liabilities, is a key determinant of private consumption. Together with current and expected 
income, it shapes the resources households expect to have available over their lifetime and, in turn, 
their consumption path. Analysing developments in household wealth, its relationship with consumption 
and how this relationship has evolved over time is therefore important for assessing the consumption 
outlook and, more broadly, GDP prospects, given that private consumption is the largest component of 
aggregate demand.  
Sector accounts provide a comprehensive 
framework for analysing developments in 
aggregate household wealth. While household 
financial accounts provide information on assets, 
liabilities, transactions and other flows by instrument, 
non-financial accounts contain data on saving, 
investment and non-financial assets.  The changes in 
balance sheets obtained from financial accounts can 
be divided into changes arising from transactions and 
other changes. Financial transactions cover net 
acquisitions of financial assets and net incurrence of 
liabilities, while “other changes” reflect revaluations 
due to changes in market prices and exchange rates 
(as well as other volume changes such as 
reclassifications). Households’ non-financial assets 
mostly include dwellings (and the land underlying 
dwellings) owned by households, but also other non-
financial assets owned by employers and own-
account workers (1). Changes in non-financial assets 
can also be decomposed into changes arising from 
transactions (net investment in non-financial assets, 
mainly housing) and other changes (notably, 
revaluations in house prices). Based on sector 
accounts, Graph 1 shows how different factors have contributed to the average annual growth rate of 
real household wealth in the euro area from 2000 to the second quarter of 2026, distinguishing six 
broad phases.  
House price developments have been a key driver of changes in euro area household net 
wealth, reflecting the large share of housing in household balance sheets (at above 60% on average 
in the EU). In the expansionary period leading up to the 2008 financial crisis, real wealth grew strongly 
(5.2% per year, on average, between 2000 and 2007). This growth was mainly driven by rising prices 
of non-financial assets, particularly house prices, and, to a lesser extent, by the acquisition of financial 
assets. Between 2008 and 2013, declines in asset prices, together with moderate inflation, reduced 
household wealth in real terms, despite continued saving (which resulted in net acquisition of financial, 
and to a lesser extent, non-financial assets). In the two subsequent periods, covering the 2013-19 
economic rebound and the COVID-19 shock, nominal wealth grew faster than inflation. Rising house 
prices supported this growth, as did investment in financial assets, particularly during the pandemic-
induced surge in savings. During the inflation surge of 2022-23, however, inflation significantly eroded 
the value of household assets and more than offset the positive effects of asset accumulation and 
valuation gains. As a result, household wealth declined by an average of 2.5% in each of those two 
years. As inflation moderated between 2024 and 2026-Q2, real household wealth recovered again, at 
an average annual rate of 2.7%, somewhat below the rates observed during the pandemic and the 
 
(1) 
Since the household sector includes employers and own-account workers, household non-financial wealth 
includes some productive assets belonging to them. In any case, housing wealth represents close to 95% of 
non-financial wealth.

previous expansionary period (4.5% and 3%, respectively). Aggregate euro area developments mask 
substantial heterogeneity across countries and even within countries, with sharper house price cycles 
associated with larger movements in household wealth. 
As high-wealth and higher income households have lower propensity to consume out of 
wealth, the distribution of wealth is relevant for assessing aggregate consumption. Carroll 
et al (2017) and Ampudia et al (2018), among others, show that wealthier households and those with 
higher income have lower propensity to consume out of wealth. Similarly, Garbinti et al (2026) find 
heterogeneous marginal propensities to consume across households in the euro area, with lower 
propensities among high-wealth households. Distributional aspects therefore play a key role in 
determining aggregate wealth effects on consumption and the transmission of shocks and policies 
operating through household balance sheets. In this context,  Beach et al (2025) link the weak economic 
recovery following the Great Recession in the US to a decline in the propensity to consume out of 
wealth. They argue this can be explained by the fact that wealth gains increasingly accrued to higher-
income households, whose propensity to consume out of income is lower.  
Changes in aggregate household wealth have occurred alongside shifts in its distribution 
across households. Data from the ECB Distributional Wealth Accounts, available from 2009 onwards, 
point to an increase in wealth inequality, as measured by the Gini index, during the years 2009-13, 
which was partly reversed in subsequent years (see Graph 2). Overall, the increase in the Gini index 
between early 2009 and early 2026 has been modest, although with some variation across 
countries (2). But disparities in wealth accumulation—especially housing wealth—have been more 
pronounced across wealth quintiles. In particular, the lowest quintile saw its housing wealth decline in 
nominal terms during this period —a development that mirrors the fall in home ownership rates 
observed in most euro area countries in this group, particularly among the younger (and with lower 
wealth) cohorts (3). The remaining wealth quintiles recorded nominal increases exceeding 25%, with the 
largest gains concentrated at the top of the distribution (see Graph 3). Overall, wealth accumulation—
including both financial and non-financial assets—was also particularly modest in the lowest net 
wealth quintile and driven by a decline in net liabilities (in the remaining quintiles the value of total 
assets outweigh, on average, that of liabilities). In real terms, the lowest wealth quintile no longer 
shows the weakest growth in wealth, because inflation reduced the real value of their net liabilities. 
By contrast, inflation eroded wealth in the remaining quintiles (4). At the same time, real housing wealth 
became more concentrated, with gains recorded only in the top quintile and, marginally, the fourth 
quintile of the net wealth distribution. As a result, the ratio between average housing wealth in the 
fifth and the first quintiles has shown a substantial increase since early 2009 onwards, and, 
particularly, in the current decade (see Graph 2).  
 
 
(2) 
According to the Gini index, since 2010 wealth inequality has increased most in Finland, Spain, Greece and Italy, 
while it has declined somewhat in Luxembourg, Slovakia, Cyprus, Belgium and Austria. At the same time, the 
countries with comparatively large increases in the Gini index were also those that, in general, showed lower 
inequality in the early 2010s. For Italy and Spain, which have information covering the pre-2008 period, the 
increase in wealth concentration is observed over the extended period starting in the early 2000s. 
(3) 
See Ciccolini et al (2025) or Pedro Gete (2025).  
(4) 
In the first quintile, households had, on average, negative net financial assets throughout the period analysed 
(that is, the value of their liabilities exceeded that of their financial assets). The second quintile showed negative 
financial wealth in 2009, but it progressively recovered from early 2014, turning positive by mid-2018. This 
upward trend came to a halt by mid-2023, and since then it has shown some decline.

Overall, observed trends in the euro area are consistent with a weakening link between 
aggregate wealth and consumption in the euro area over recent decades. The ratios of 
consumption to financial wealth and to non-financial wealth have followed a downward trend since 
late 1990s onwards (from 2012, in the case of financial wealth) and, excluding the pandemic period, 
are currently at historically low levels (see Graph 4). The consumption-to-income ratio is also at low 
levels relative to historical standards, but only from the pandemic period onwards.

between consumption, income, housing wealth, net financial wealth and interest rates in the euro area 
suggest that the link between housing wealth and consumption weakened after the financial and 
sovereign debt crisis relative to the period 1999-2007. The estimated marginal propensity to consume 
(MPC) out of housing wealth has declined from moderate pre-crisis levels (around 0.5 cents per euro 
on average over the period 1999-2007) to values that are around and no longer significantly different 
from zero from 2013 onwards (see Graph 5). This shift is consistent with the above-mentioned 
increased concentration of housing wealth among wealthier households, who typically display a lower 
MPC out of wealth. By contrast, the lowest wealth quintiles, which generally exhibit a higher MPC out 
of wealth, include a rising share of tenants who do not benefit from rising house prices.  
Regional heterogeneity may reinforce this effect. Since 2010, house prices in euro area capital 
cities have outpaced national averages by around 20–30% (see Höynck et al, 2025), despite some 
post-pandemic realignment. Crucially, these capital regions have much higher rental shares than other 
parts of their respective countries. While rising house prices increase homeowners’ wealth, they tend 
to translate into higher housing costs for renters. Given the different MPCs out of wealth across the 
wealth distribution, the negative impact of rising house prices on renters’ spending is likely to partially 
offset the positive impact on homeowner spending, thereby weakening the link between aggregate 
housing wealth and consumption. 
The marginal propensity to consume out of financial wealth is higher and comparatively 
stable. As can be seen in Graph 5, the estimated MPC out of financial wealth is somewhat higher than 
for non-financial wealth. This is consistent with the different nature of the two types of wealth: 
financial assets are generally more liquid, while housing wealth mainly provides housing services, 
particularly in the euro area context.  Stability in the estimated MPC out of financial wealth mirrors the 
absence of major shifts in the distribution of financial wealth (5). 
Estimated wealth effects on consumption in the euro area are modest, particularly 
compared with estimates for the US. Before the financial crisis, in the euro area an additional euro 
of housing wealth was associated with around half a cent of additional consumption (see Graph 5), 
while the estimated response to financial wealth was somewhat higher but remained comparatively 
low (6). Since then, the estimated housing-wealth effect has fallen to values close to zero, whereas the 
financial-wealth effect has remained broadly stable. These findings suggest that changes in household 
wealth, including those driven by asset-price fluctuations, have had a limited direct effect on aggregate 
consumption in the euro area. This may have constrained the boost to consumption from rising asset 
prices, but it also leaves consumption less exposed to a reversal in valuations. In the current context 
of elevated stock-market valuations, a correction in equity prices may therefore have a more limited 
direct impact on euro area consumption, particularly taking into account that they tend to be 
concentrated in households with lower marginal propensity to consume. Moreover, the marginal 
propensity to consume out of these assets tends to be lower than that out of other financial assets (7). 
Similarly, policies to increase housing supply and improve affordability could put downward pressure 
on house prices dynamics, with a potentially limited direct effect on consumption through the housing-
wealth channel. This needs to be weighed against the benefits of improved access to housing, 
particularly for households currently excluded from homeownership or facing high housing costs. 
 
(5) 
The elasticity of consumption to financial wealth becomes statistically non-significant in the period 2013-19, 
partly reflecting greater estimation uncertainty due to the limited size of the sample, while the magnitude of 
the coefficient itself does not decline significantly with respect to the pre-crisis period. Meanwhile, the marginal 
propensity to consume out of income has remained broadly stable throughout the period analysed. 
(6) 
For the US, see, for example, the already cited paper by Beach et al (2025), where the marginal propensity to 
consume out of total wealth is above 2.5 cents per dollar throughout the period 1990-2019, above that 
estimated here for both financial and non-financial wealth.  
(7) 
An important adjustment in equity valuations may nonetheless be accompanied by episodes of financial stress, 
which may impair credit flows and thus have important indirect consequences on the EU economy.

5. ECONOMIC ACTIVITY 
  
43 
The 
EU 
economy 
expanded 
above 
expectations in the first three quarters of 
2026. After growing more than expected in the 
first half of the year, real GDP increased again 
above expectations in the third quarter, by 0.5% 
in the EU and 0.4% in the euro area. Acquired 
growth for the first three quarters of the year 
was lifted to 1.3% for the EU and 1% for the euro 
area - 0.5 pps. and 0.3 pps., respectively, higher 
than implied by the Spring 2026 Forecast (see 
Graph I.5.1). The majority (14) of the 20 countries 
providing a flash GDP estimate on 13th 
November (3) 
reported 
higher-than-expected 
acquired growth for 2026-Q3; most prominently 
Germany, Ireland, the Netherlands and Italy. A 
strong positive surprise observed for Denmark 
also contributed significantly to the better overall outcome. For 2026-Q3 quarterly growth was as 
strong or higher than projected in Spring in 16 Member States (4). France is a notable exception, as 
real GDP contracted slightly in the third quarter following a decline in the first and stagnation in 
the second.  
Private consumption expanded as expected. 
Private consumption rebounded (0.4%) in the 
second quarter after a weak first quarter (0%) 
(see Graph I.5.1). The dynamics in private 
consumption growth in 2026-Q1 and Q2 are 
partially driven by net spending on travel: in Q1 it 
was a drag, while in Q2 it rebounded and gave a 
significant boost. The domestic portion of 
consumption increased by a modest 0.2% in both 
quarters (see Graph I.5.2), only slightly better 
than 
expectations. 
According 
to 
available 
information 
from 
national 
sources, 
private 
consumption expanded further in 2026-Q3. 
Growth of real household disposable has slowed 
down since mid-last year, due to weaker 
employment and real wage growth, but also drag 
from other income and transfers. The household 
saving rate averaged 13.9% in 2025-Q4 and 
2026-H1, 0.6 pps lower than the average over 
2023-Q4 – 2025-Q3. While still higher than in the 
pre-COVID period (12%), the decrease supported 
consumption in recent quarters. 
Government consumption increased in line with expectations. An important driver of the q-
o-q increase in 2026-H1 were transfers in kind via market producers. Comparing to a year ago, the 
increase also reflects operational costs related to increased military spending and the purchase of 
cybersecurity services that resulted in additional expenditure in the course 2025-H2.

Investment growth was subdued, dragged by residential investment. Investment 
rebounded in 2026-Q2 after retreating in 2026-Q1, driven in particular by non-residential 
construction (see Graphs I.5.1, I.5.2). According to available information from national sources, 
investment was weak in 2026-Q3, pointing to an overall weaker-than-expected growth in the first 
three quarters of the year. Housing continued to be a drag, after a depressed 2024 and generally 
weak growth throughout 2025 (5). Housing supply has been slow to respond to housing needs. EU 
completions fell from 4.0 dwellings per 1,000 inhabitants in 2022 to around 3.1 in 2025, and 
building permits, which lead completions by around two years, remain about 16% below their 
2022 level. Spending on equipment increased in both 2026-Q1 and Q2, most likely driven by 
defence- and AI-related spending. Still, equipment investment fell short of the dynamics observed 
in 2025, despite limited signs of corporate financial distress. 
Export growth rebounded strongly. GDP 
growth in 2026-Q2 was largely driven by net 
exports (see Graphs I.5.1, I.5.2). Exports surprised 
on the upside, especially in Ireland (6), but 
importantly also in Germany and to a lesser 
extent also by Denmark, Sweden, Italy and 
France, among others. Excluding Ireland, trade 
within the EU accounted for the better part of 
aggregate growth, while trade outside the EU was 
also supportive (see Graph I.5.3). Exports boosted 
the turnovers in industry, above all in the sectors 
producing capital goods (notably electric and 
electronic 
equipment 
and 
other 
factory 
equipment), 
intermediate 
goods 
(notably 
chemicals) and consumer non-durables (notably 
pharmaceuticals)

Partial 
information suggests that the contribution of (net) exports varied significantly across reporting 
countries in the third quarter. It was positive in the Netherlands, related to the global AI investment 
cycle; Ireland, related to pharmaceuticals and technology exports; and Spain, related to tourism; 
but was weaker in Germany and negative in France and Italy. It can be expected to have been 
negative on aggregate.

=== PAGE 62 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
46 
The occurrence of extreme weather events in July and August is likely to have left a 
negative imprint on economic activity in specific sectors and regions. This summer, Europe 
experienced one of its most severe combined heat, drought and wildfire crises on record (7). These 
caused damage to forestry assets and reduced crop yields after several quarters of recovery from 
another summer of weather extremities across Europe in 2024-Q3. Low water levels disrupted 
waterway transport and strained supply chains in industry, while also constraining hydropower 
capacity and cooling for nuclear and thermoelectric plants, even as heatwaves pushed up cooling-
driven electricity demand, together raising energy prices (see Box I.5.1). 
Industrial and consumer confidence have decoupled. In early summer, consumer confidence 
partly recovered from the drop induced by the Middle East conflict. On the business side, 
confidence levels were largely restored in retail and services and improved further in industry. 
September and October readings indicate a relapse in confidence in retail and services. Consumer 
confidence also dropped significantly. Alongside higher inflation expectations, households reported 
reluctance to make major purchases in the following 12 months and a deterioration in their current 
and expected financial situation. Expectations regarding the general economic situation declined 
too. In contrast, the trend of rising confidence was confirmed in industry (see Graph I.5.7). Order 
book levels, including for export, have been steadily improving since last year (especially in 
Germany) and production expectations are currently trending up. 
Graph I.5.7: 
EC economic sentiment and sectoral confidence indicators and PMI, euro area 
   
EC confidence indicators have been centered along their average and standardized over 2000-2025 (Z-score) and expressed as 
100 + 10 * Z-score to align better with the Economic Sentiment Indicator (ESI). The ESI has broader coverage than the composite PMI. In 
addition to services and industry it includes retail, construction, and consumers. 
Source: S&P Global; European Commission. 
Investment is set to slow down this year and recover in the outer years, while remaining 
subdued. The high-rate environment is set to weigh on residential construction, which is expected 
to broadly stagnate this year and recover only modestly over the next two years. With high real 
construction costs, labour shortages and tighter financing conditions limiting the response of 
housing supply, a recovery in demand is likely to push up prices more than it raises new supply 
over the forecast horizon, especially in metropolitan areas where shortfalls are concentrated. By 
contrast, non-residential construction, including public investment, is projected to keep expanding 
this year under impulse from the RRF in its last year of implementation, so the momentum is set 
to weaken over the forecast horizon. Equipment investment, instead, is projected to keep 
expanding at a healthy pace this year, also supported by the rebound of manufacturing since 
spring and increased defence spending, but decelerate in 2027. Other investment, which largely 
includes intangible assets, is set to keep expanding steadily over the forecast horizon, though at a 
slower pace than in the US. Overall, investment is set to decelerate from a yearly growth rate of 
2.6% last year to an annual average of around 1.5% this year and next, before edging up towards 
the end forecast horizon.

Despite rising inflation, low unemployment and sustained wage growth should support a 
modest expansion in private consumption. Real disposable income is however projected to 
expand at a weaker pace over the forecast horizon than had been projected in spring, given the 
higher cost of living. Private consumption growth is set to soften to 1.3% this year, weaken 
marginally to 1.1% in 2027 and move back to 1.3% in 2028, as decelerating inflation allows for a 
stronger expansion of household purchasing power. The forecast for consumer spending is also 
conditional upon a small drop in the household saving rate, from 14.3% in 2025 to 14.1% in 
2028—still well above the pre-pandemic average of around 12%. In contrast, wealth effects play 
only a limited role in supporting consumption (see Box I.4.2.). In 2028, the grip of inflation is set to 
loosen, providing some relief to household budgets. After expanding at a projected rate of 1.9% 
this year, government consumption is expected to decelerate to just above 1% over the two years, 
as governments rein in expenditure growth.

year and 2.8% in 2027—well above the growth rates expected in spring. It would then decrease to

is now projected to expand by 1.4% in both 2026 and 2027, 0.3 pps. higher in 2026 than 
anticipated in the Spring 2026 Forecast and at the same rate as in 2025. Economic activity is 
expected to expand by 1.5% in 2028. GDP growth in the euro area is expected to attain 1.2% this 
year and next and move up to 1.3% in 2028. 
Graph I.5.8: 
Real growth dispersion and convergence 
   
Per capita GDP in % relative to EU average in 2028 in brackets; LU (238; -7.2) not depicted on panel b. 
While steady on aggregate, growth is taking a step down in 2027 in a majority of 
Member States. Most Member States are set to expand in 2026, though many by less than in 
2025. Exceptions are Ireland, due to large base effects from last year, and Romania, where the 
drag from fiscal consolidation is expected to ease. In 2027 growth is projected to slow down to 
varying degrees in seventeen Member States. Conversely, in 2028 it is set to continue accelerating 
in five Member States and rebound, if only partly, in another eleven (see Annex Table 1). Growth in 
the largest economies France, Italy and Germany is set to remain below average (see 
Graph I.5.8 a.). In general, the Member States with lower GDP per capita are expected to expand 
more than average (see Graph I.5.8 b.), supporting further convergence in the EU over the forecast 
horizon. Some mid-size and smaller high-income countries are also seen growing above average. 
EU GDP growth is estimated to emerge above potential in 2026. Potential growth is 
estimated to slow down slightly from 1.5% in 2025 to 1.4% in 2026-28 in the EU, and from 1.4% 
in 2025 to 1.3% in 2026 and 1.2% in 2027-28 in the euro area. The limiting factor is the 
slowdown in growth of the working-age population and of the participation rate, as well as to a 
further, if mild, decline in hours worked. Meanwhile, capital accumulation and productivity growth 
are set to be increasingly supportive (see Graph I.5.9, right). After widening to -0.6% and -0.5% for 
the EU and euro area respectively in 2025, the output gap is projected to narrow by 0.1 pps. by 
2028. Germany and the Netherlands, are projected to recover towards potential, Italy and Spain, 
are expected to converge down towards potential, Poland to slow below potential. By contrast, 
France is projected to fall further below potential (see Graph I.5.9).

(Continued on the next page) 
Box I.5.1: The scorching and dry summer 2026: potential economic impact
In the summer 2026, Europe experienced one of its most severe combined heat, drought, 
and wildfire crises on record. Average temperature over European land was 1.17°C above the 
1991–2020 average, making summer 2026 the third warmest on record after 2024 and 2022 
according to the Copernicus Climate Change Service. Record-high temperatures covered 22% of 
European land—hitting western and central Europe hardest—while exceptional warmth extended 
across Europe’s seas and persisted well beyond summer, with record-breaking daily temperatures also 
occurring in May and September. Meanwhile, below-average rainfall across much of southern and 
western Europe reduced soil moisture and triggered widespread droughts. In July and August, record 
or near-record low river flows hit major waterways including the Rhine, Danube, Po, Loire, Rhône, and 
Seine. These hot, dry conditions heightened wildfire risk, fuelling destructive blazes across 
Mediterranean countries, particularly in Spain and France. Recently, this prolonged dry spell gave way 
to the successive storms and torrential rainfall, which are triggering severe flooding across parts of 
western and southern Europe—including Portugal, Spain, France, and Italy.  
The extreme heat and drought disrupted European agriculture, energy systems, transport 
networks, and water-intensive industries. Droughts, heatwaves, and wildfires damaged crops and 
forestry, with major EU summer crop yields forecast to be up to 15% below their five-year average 
(European Commission, 2026), potentially driving up food prices. Simultaneously, extreme 
temperatures strained electricity systems. Cooling demand caused daily electricity consumption to 
spike—rising by 28% in Italy, 23% in Hungary, 14% in France, and 13% in Spain in late June (EMBER, 
2026)—while low water levels and warm rivers restricted hydropower and thermal generation. In turn, 
this pushed energy prices higher, although strong solar output helped ease price pressure in central 
hours (European Gasoline Market Outlook, August 2026). Record-low water levels on the Rhine severely 
constrained inland shipping, sending tanker-barge freight rates from Rotterdam to Karlsruhe surging 
from EUR 45 to EUR 150–155 per tonne by late July, and Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp to Basel 
rates from EUR 35 to EUR 277 per tonne by mid-August (European Gasoline Market Outlook, August 
2026). These supply chain bottlenecks hit chemical, metal, and fuel manufacturers hardest while 
posing risks to water-intensive activities, including data centres and food processing. In addition, 
heatwaves may have reduced workers’ productivity in a number of sectors, and in some cases, their 
ability to work, particularly in physically demanding or outdoor occupations. Finally, public finances are 
likely to have been impacted too, through lower tax revenues linked to disrupted production and trade, 
and, on the expenditure side, reconstruction efforts, disaster relief, alongside pressures on healthcare 
systems, with heatwaves linked to higher heat-related morbidity and mortality.  
The overall economic impact of weather- and 
climate-related 
extreme 
events 
remains 
difficult to measure. The indicator of Economic 
losses caused by weather- and climate-related 
extreme events in the EU Member States, compiled 
by the European Environment Agency (EEA), shows 
that such events caused an estimated EUR 822 billion 
in economic losses in the EU between 1980 and 
2024, of which more than EUR 208 billion (25%) 
occurred in 2021–2024 alone. In 2022, the year that 
recorded a previous temperature record, losses from 
extreme weather events amounted to an estimated 
EUR 58 billion (Graph 1). However, the EEA indicator 
captures primarily direct losses to physical assets, 
infrastructure and crops, leaving broader economic 
effects unaccounted for. These include disruptions to 
production capacity, lower productivity and damage 
to natural capital, as well as the economic costs of 
reconstruction. National accounts, which underpin conventional economic forecasts, including the 
European Economic Forecasts, provide a framework for capturing some of these wider effects, but

Box (continued) 
   
have important limitations. The destruction of fixed capital is recorded as a reduction in the capital 
stock, but the relevant data are published with considerable delay and do not identify climate-related 
causes. (1) Moreover, the loss of ecosystem services that are not traded in markets is not reflected in 
conventional measures of economic activity. The effect of extreme events on measured GDP is also 
not straightforward: while damage and disruption weigh on economic activity, subsequent 
reconstruction and repair can partly offset these losses. Over a typical short-term forecast horizon, 
GDP growth may even be boosted if reconstruction spending outweighs the initial disruption. Finally, 
the spatially and sectorially concentrated nature of weather shocks means that their effects may be 
more readily identified in regional and sectorial accounts than in aggregate national data, underlining 
the value of analysis at a more disaggregated level. 
Estimates based on evidence from past events suggest that the economic costs of extreme 
weather events can be sizeable and persist well beyond the initial shock. Their effects extend 
beyond immediate physical damage and disruption, weighing on productivity, investment, employment 
and population. Using data for 1,160 EU regions, Usman et al, (2024), in their Going NUTS analysis, 
find that regional output remains 1.5 pps. below its counterfactual level two years after a summer 
heatwave, and 3 pps. and 2.8 pps. lower four years after a drought and a flood, respectively. In Costa 
et al, (2024), firm-level evidence further highlights the productivity channel. (2) The effects of weather-
related disruptions can also accumulate over time and propagate across sectors. Mastropietro et al, 
(2026) find that the effects of droughts on production, investment, migration, and employment build 
up over several years, with particularly persistent effects on manufacturing and construction, whereas 
agricultural impacts are more pronounced in the short term. Finally, Ademmer et al, 2023 illustrate 
how localised weather-related supply disruptions can have wider economic consequences: a month of 
persistently low water levels on the Rhine is estimated to reduce German industrial production by 
around 1%, through disruptions to inland water transport. 
Preliminary estimates of the economic impact of the summer 2026 weather events vary, 
reflecting differences in geographical coverage, methodology and the economic channels 
considered. For France, both the statistical office INSEE and the Banque de France estimate that the 
summer heatwaves and associated drought will reduce annual GDP growth in 2026 by around 0.1 pps., 
with most of the impact stemming from lower agricultural output. Both estimates primarily capture 
short-term effects and draw on currently available sectoral data, with the Banque de France also using 
evidence from its monthly business survey of up to 8 500 firms. Private-sector assessments for Europe 
point to substantially larger losses. Schuiling and Texeira, (2026) estimate that the 2026 heatwaves, 
droughts and wildfires could reduce European GDP by 0.2–0.4% in the short term —with steeper losses 
in heavily affected countries like France (0.3–0.5%)—before reconstruction and recovery spending 
offsets part of the initial decline. 
The effects on prices are more ambiguous, reflecting opposing supply and demand effects. 
Heatwaves and droughts can push up food prices by reducing agricultural output. Kotz et al (2023) 
estimate that the exceptional heat of summer 2022 raised annual food inflation in Europe by around 
0.7 pps. and headline inflation by around 0.3 pps.. However, more destructive weather events can also 
depress household consumption and business investment, weakening aggregate demand. Costa and 
Hooley (2025) find that, following severe disasters, these demand effects can dominate over the 
medium term, putting downward pressure on the overall price level. By contrast, moderate disasters 
have little discernible effect on aggregate prices. 
 
(1) 
The destruction of fixed capital is recorded in the national accounts as 'other change in the volume of assets', 
reducing the capital stock. However, these adjustments are not regularly published separately by Eurostat or 
national statistical institutes, and the underlying capital stock data are typically available with a lag of around 
two years. Moreover, they do not identify the causes of capital destruction. Losses of non-capital goods, such 
as consumer durables, are also not captured. See Special Issue 4.3 Effects of extreme_weather_events on 
national accounts and the european economic forecast - European Commission (2023) 
(2) 
Firm-level evidence across 23 advanced economies suggests that ten additional days above 35°C reduce 
annual labour productivity by around 0.3%, while one additional heatwave lasting at least five days is 
associated with a reduction of around 0.2%. See Costa et al. (2024).

Box I.5.2: Firms’ financial position and investment in the euro area 
A sound corporate sector is essential for the economy, both as an engine for growth and as 
a source of resilience to shocks. Firms’ investment decisions are a key determinant of economic 
growth, while their financial strength shapes their ability to absorb adverse shocks without resorting 
to layoffs, cutting investment or defaulting on their obligations. High energy costs and rising interest 
rates, against a backdrop of subdued domestic demand, are putting both roles to the test. While firms 
have weathered earlier shocks relatively well, persistent cost pressures are weighing on profitability 
and could, if sustained, gradually erode their capacity to invest. This box assesses the financial situation 
of non-financial corporations, first as a buffer against shocks and then as a driver of growth. 
The financial position of euro area non-financial corporations remained sound in the first 
half of 2026, although the pace of deleveraging has slowed. Corporate debt stood at 193% of 
gross value added in the second quarter, its lowest level since 2008 and well below its 2010–19 
average. Debt-to-equity–another key gauge of corporates’ financial leverage–was also close to its 
lowest level in more than a decade (see Table 1). However, much of the decline in the two metrics 
since 2022 reflected the boost from inflation to nominal value added and increase in equity valuations 
rather than to a sustained reduction in borrowing. Debt growth actually picked up in the first half of 
2026, and so did the debt to value added ratio in both quarters. Liquidity buffers remain ample: 
deposits grew strongly and covered around 85% of short-term debt, close to the highest level since 
the series began. At the same time, debt-servicing capacity remains ensured. The interest burden has 
eased from its 2024 peak of 131/2% of gross operating surplus to around 111/3% in 2026-Q2, driven 
by the decline in interest rates. With bank lending rates rising again, interest payments have started to 
increase, but operating surpluses still comfortably cover interest costs by almost nine times.

0.3–1; rapidly increasing/decreasing = more than 1. 
(3) flat = change smaller than 0.25 standard deviations of quarterly changes in 2010–19.

valued added, self-financing ratio is internal funds over gross fixed capital formation, net lending-GVA ratio is net lending

Profitability, by contrast, remains under pressure, extending a decline that began after the 
2021-22 energy shock. The corporate profit share fell sharply in the first quarter of 2026 and
