reflects the unwinding of the margin gains made during the 2021–22 energy shock, with lower input 
costs being passed on to customers and wages catching up with past inflation. Manufacturing appears 
to experience most pressure, though manufacturing margins as a whole remain above pre-pandemic 
levels in the largest Member States. In 2024–25, manufacturers' value-added prices rose by only

around 1–1½% a year, while unit labour costs increased by around 3%. Margin compression was 
particularly pronounced in energy-intensive industries, notably in Germany. Labour-intensive business 
services have generally been better able to pass higher wage costs on to customers. Looking ahead, 
the renewed rise in energy and commodity prices adds to the pressure. While producers of energy and 
basic materials have raised their prices, manufacturers further along the value chain, such as motor 
vehicles and machinery, have so far passed on little of the increase in their input costs, weighing on 
profitability in the first half of 2026. Evidence from the Survey on the Access to Finance (SAFE) points 
to a further deterioration, with a net 21% of firms reporting lower profits in the third quarter, after 
16% in the second quarter. 
Overall, the corporate sector rests on sound financial position and retains substantial 
capacity to absorb adverse shocks. Low debt, ample liquidity and preserved debt-servicing capacity 
provide adequate financial buffers. Overall, the deterioration in profitability has so far not translated 
into a broad-based weakening of corporate balance sheets. The corporate vulnerability index with its 
contributing dimensions reflects these aspects of potential NFC financial vulnerabilities (see Graph 1). 
The index picked up in the first quarter of 2026 driven by the drop in profits and sales reported by 
firms. While both indicators recovered and contributed to the lower index in the second quarter, weaker 
sentiment weighed on business situations. Vulnerabilities were overall higher in the first half of 2026, 
compared to end 2025 but broadly similar to the same period the year before. The SAFE survey results 
also confirm that financial vulnerability remains contained: the share of firms simultaneously reporting 
lower turnover and profits, higher interest expenses and unchanged or rising indebtedness remained 
low, at around 4% in the third quarter, with SMEs showing, on average, slightly lower vulnerability than 
larger firms. 
 
 
 
 
Growth of insolvencies has also moderated, though developments have been uneven across 
sectors. Bankruptcy declarations in the euro area increased in the second quarter, following a decline 
in the first, and remained above their pre-pandemic average. However, their year-on-year growth 
moderated to around 7% in the first half of 2026, from 12% in 2025, suggesting that the unwinding 
of insolvencies deferred during the pandemic continues to play a role. Developments vary across 
sectors, reflecting differences in current economic conditions: bankruptcies continue to rise in transport, 
while remaining broadly stable in construction and industry and declining in accommodation and food 
services (see Graph 2). Meanwhile, business creation has broadly kept pace with insolvencies, leaving 
the ratio of bankruptcies to business registrations relatively stable. Available third-quarter data for

some countries also point to signs of stabilisation, with insolvencies easing slightly in Germany and 
business failures remaining broadly unchanged in France. 
Firms have ample capacity to finance investment, both internally and externally, so subdued 
investment does not reflect financing constraints. In the first half of 2026, firms' internal funds 
continued to exceed their investment needs, broadly in line with the post-2010 average, leaving the 
corporate sector a net lender to the rest of the economy. This financial surplus must be seen in the 
context of relatively subdued investment over the same period – with the investment rate hovering 
only marginally above the average of the previous decade notwithstanding the EU’s important 
transformational investment needs. Across Member States, investment growth has been more closely 
aligned with developments in economic activity than corporate financial conditions (i.e. profitability, 
internal funds or credit growth). Taken together, these developments suggest that subdued demand 
and limited investment opportunities, rather than financing constraints, have been weighing more 
heavily on corporate investment. Evidence from the ECB SAFE survey points in the same direction, with 
half of firms not applying for bank loans reporting that they have sufficient internal funds and only a 
small share citing obstacles when seeking a loan. SMEs are more exposed to external financing 
conditions, facing a widening financing gap and being more likely to be discouraged from applying for 
loans. 
Overall, the euro area corporate sector remains financially sound, but contributes less to 
growth than its financial position would allow. Sector accounts and surveys point to solid balance 
sheets across firm sizes, sectors and Member States, with little evidence of widespread financial 
distress. Weak investment reflects subdued activity rather than financing constraints. In 
manufacturing, firms have kept investment broadly in line with value added, but weak value-added 
growth, reflecting pressure on both prices and volumes amid technological change, has reduced the 
sector’s contribution to aggregate investment. Output in energy-intensive industries has contracted 
markedly and the automotive sector is under pressure, pointing to a price and non-price 
competitiveness challenge. A stronger contribution from manufacturing will depend on new sources of 
growth, including the ability to benefit from technological change such as the adoption of AI. SMEs 
remain more exposed to tighter financing conditions. At the euro area level, weak profitability appears 
largely cyclical, although in Germany and Austria it seems to reflect a longer-term decline. 

=== PAGE 71 ===
6. LABOUR MARKET 
  
55 
The EU economy continued to generate a moderate employment expansion in the first 
two quarters of the year. According to national accounts data, headcount employment in the EU 
increased by 0.2%—corresponding to 442 000 additional workers—in 2026-H1 with respect to 
2025-H2. In year-on-year terms, it rose by 0.5% in both aggregates. At the same time, hours 
worked per employee remained broadly stable in most sectors.  By sector, employment growth was 
driven by both market and non-market services, while employment declined slightly in 
manufacturing and agriculture (see Graph I.6.1). By nationality, the increase in employment among 
non-nationals more than offset the decline among nationals, linked to demographic ageing (see 
Graph I.6.2). For 2026 as a whole, the projection for employment growth in the Spring 2026 
Forecast—at 0.3% for the EU and 0.4% for the euro area—are well within reach, given an acquired 
growth entering the second half of the year for both the EU and the euro area slightly below 0.4%. 
Data for the third quarter, which were published after the forecast’s cut-off date, points to further 
slowdown in job growth, as employment broadly stagnated.  
Among the largest economies, Spain continued to record the strongest employment 
growth, and Germany the weakest. In Spain, headcount employment grew by 0.9% in the first 
semester compared to 2025-H2, and by 2.2% y-o-y (8). Foreign workers again contributed 
significantly, which may partly reflect the regularisation of immigrants carried out by the Spanish 
government during the second quarter. By contrast, compared to the last semester of 2025, 
employment stagnated in France and declined by 0.2% in Germany (and by 0.4% compared to 
2025-H1). In most countries, annual employment growth has been driven mainly by market and, to 
a lesser extent, non-market services. Germany stands out as a notable exception, with employment 
in market services declining since mid-2024 and the contraction intensifying. By contrast, 
employment in industry stagnated or declined in most Member States, with the downturn more 
pronounced in North-West Europe, while Spain stood out for its comparatively stronger 
performance. 
 
 
 
The unemployment rate increased marginally in the third quarter, but remained close to 
historically low levels. It stood at 6.2% in the EU and 6.5% in the euro area, 0.1 pps. above the 
rates observed in the second quarter, and 0.4 pps. and 0.3 pps. above the historical lows reached 
 
(8) 
Data for the third quarter, published before the cut-off date, shows that employment remained dynamic in Spain in the 
third quarter, when it grew by 2.6% with respect to 2025-Q3.

at the end of 2024 in the EU and in the euro area respectively. Eurostat’s broader measure of 
labour market slack, encompassing groups with an unmet need for employment (9), has remained 
broadly stable. It stood at 11.8% of the extended labour force aged 15-74 in the EU in 2026-Q2 
(12.6% in the euro area). Developments in unemployment rates differ substantially across 
countries. Malta and Luxembourg recorded relatively large increases in unemployment in 2026-Q3 
with respect to one year earlier (+0.9 and +0.7 pps., respectively), whereas Greece saw a 
significant decline of 1.2 pps. Among the largest EU Member States, unemployment edged down 
further in Spain (-0.3 pps.) and increased in France (0.5 pps.) and, less significantly, in Italy (0.3 
pps.) and Germany (0.2 pps.). 
There are signs of labour market weakening, particularly for younger cohorts. As young 
workers are generally more affected than older workers by softer labour demand, recent 
developments in their employment are consistent with the usual cyclical labour-market patterns, 
rather than reflecting, so far, any labour-reducing effect of AI adoption. Between early 2023 and 
2026-Q2, the unemployment rate among people under 25 increased by 1.2 pps., and the broader 
measure of labour market slack increased by 2.8 pps., up compared to 2026-Q2, and close to 30%. 
The share of young individuals facing other unmet supply of employment—in particular, those 
working fewer hours than wished—also increased. By contrast the older cohorts saw an 
improvement in these indicators (see Graph  I.6.3). These divergent trends, together with rising 
labour market participation rates among older cohorts, have translated into diverging employment 
rate developments: rates have declined by 1 pp. for younger cohorts and increased by up to 2.9 in 
the older ones. This temporarily reinforces the ageing trend of the labour workforce (see 
Graph  I.6.4). 
Net of the volatility associated to Ireland’s GDP, labour productivity growth picked up 
further. When Irish GDP is excluded from the EU aggregate to net out its high volatility, the 
upward trend in labour productivity growth is seen continuing into 2026-H1 in the EU. This took 
place against a backdrop of a gradual decline in the labour intensity of economic growth, following 
a period of exceptionally job-rich economic expansion between end-2022 and end-2024 (10). Cross-
country differences persisted. Among the largest EU Member States, France and Germany, with 
more subdued employment developments, led the productivity gains in 2026-H1, whereas 
productivity growth remained limited in Spain and Italy.

(10) Instead, the GDP decline in Ireland in the first half of 2026 implies a moderation in EU productivity growth when this 
country is included in the aggregate: labour productivity growth stood at 0.8% by mid-2026, 0.3 pps. below end-2025.

number of EU countries with a declining working-age population is projected to increase from nine 
in 2025 to twelve in 2027, limiting growth of labour supply and weighing on growth prospects. In 
aggregate terms, the EU and the euro area are still expected to see modest increases in the

as Croatia, Bulgaria and Slovenia, where high year-on-year increases remained high, above 7.5%.  
Growth in real wages has also moderated, 
more intensely than nominal ones. Continuing 
the moderation recorded in the previous two 
years, real wage growth eased to 0.4% in 2026-
Q2, 0.9 pps. below end-2025 rate. This reflected 
both the moderation in nominal wage growth and 
the rebound in inflation. In the euro area, real 
wage growth moderated by 1.1 pps., to 0.3%. 
Real wage growth turned negative in Romania, 
Bulgaria, Belgium, Austria, Italy, Slovakia and 
Estonia. 
 
Going forward, negotiated wages and survey 
data point to broadly stable wages growth 
rates in 2026-2027. With data available up to 
August, the ECB wage tracker with unsmoothed 
one-off payments shows a decline in wage growth from 3% in 2025 to 2.6% for the whole of 
2026, followed by an increase to 2.7% in the first quarter of 2027 and to 2.8% in the second 
quarter of 2027 (11). Similarly, according to the Corporate Telephone Survey carried out by the ECB 
at end June (12), companies continue to anticipate contained wage growth. On average, the 
quantitative indications provided by companies imply that wage growth is expected to slow from 
3.1% in 2025 to 2.5% in 2026 and stand at 2.4% in 2027 (13). Year-over-year percentage changes 
in wages advertised in online job postings on the Indeed website point to a more dynamic evolution 
of salaries between March and August in the euro area than suggested by negotiated wages, 
followed by some moderation in September.  
 
(11) Data up to September, published after the cut-off date of these projections, suggests slightly higher wage growth in 
first three quarters of 2027 compared with the full year of 2026.  
(12) Contacts with companies took place mainly between 22 June and 1 July 2026 (i.e. shortly after the signing of the US-
Iran Memorandum of Understanding in mid-June, which subsequently broke down). 
(13) Around 20% of contacted companies thought that the increase in inflation caused by the conflict in the Middle East 
would result in higher wage demands, and around 10% expected their firms to implement inflation compensation 
packages. Most companies in the sample, however, did not anticipate any notable impact on wage growth, citing the 
subdued economic conditions and employment outlook as supporting factors for this evolution. Upward pressures on 
wages seem somewhat higher according to perceptions of companies collaborating with the ECB Survey on Access to 
Finance. According to it, the share of companies reporting that their expectations regarding wage trends have been 
revised upwards is significant (around 40%), while at the same time the war’s impact on their expectations regarding 
number of employees or hours worked appears negligible.

Box I.6.1: Long-term labour market implications of demographic changes in the EU
Fast population ageing across the EU has major consequences for labour supply, 
employment and long-term growth. The 2027 Ageing Report projections (1) show that the EU 
population is set to decline from 2030 onwards due to the marked decline in fertility rates, though this 
will happen only gradually, given steady gains in life expectancy.  Higher labour market participation—
especially among women and older workers—is expected to mitigate part of the demographic drag 
but not fully offset the decline in the working-age population.  
The EU population is projected to rise marginally in the coming years, to a peak of 453 
million in 2029, before entering a period of sustained decline (see Graph 1). By 2050, the EU 
population is set to fall to 445 million (-2%), with a decrease by at least 10% in Bulgaria, Greece, 
Croatia, Latvia, Lithuania, Hungary, Poland and Romania. Simultaneously, the age structure in the EU 
is set to shift: the share of people aged 65 or over is projected to increase from 22% in 2025 to 29% 
in 2050, while the share of those aged 20-64 would fall from 58% to 53%.  
Graph 1: 
Total population growth and age-group contributions, EU (2025-2050) 
Source: 2027 Ageing Report. 
As a result, the EU goes from having 2.6 people aged between 20 and 64 for every person 
aged 65+ in 2025, to 1.8 people by 2050. This dynamic is captured by the old-age dependency 
ratio—the population aged 65+ relative to those aged 20-64—, which is projected to rise in the EU 
from 38% in 2025 to 55% in 2050. The old-age dependency ratio will range widely across countries, 
from 34% in Malta to 70% in Portugal and Greece.  
These trends reflect the combined effect of low fertility, rising longevity and positive net 
migration. Fertility in the EU has significantly fallen over recent decades, reaching a historical low of 
1.3 live births per woman in 2024. Member States’ fertility rates are assumed to converge, in the very 
long term, to rates currently observed among the EU frontrunners (those states with the highest fertility 
rates). This means that fertility rates would remain well below the natural replacement rate of 2.1 for 
all Member States, with an average EU fertility rate of 1.5 in 2050. After the interruption caused by 
the COVID-19 pandemic, life expectancy at birth has resumed its upward trend and is projected to 
increase further, by around three years between 2025 and 2050. Net migration is assumed to 
moderate gradually after the exceptionally high inflows of recent years and stabilise from the 2030s 
 
(1) 
The Ageing Report is a triennial report from the European Commission and the Economic Policy Committee 
(EPC). It provides long-term projections for public expenditure on pensions, health care, long-term care and 
education in the European Union and Norway. The first part of the 2027 Ageing Report, on which this box is 
based, was published on 4 November 2026. The second part, presenting the budgetary projections, will be 
published in spring 2027.

at around 1.4 million persons per year, or 0.3% of the EU population. This includes the assumptions 
that two thirds of displaced Ukrainians benefiting from temporary protection remain in their current 
host country, and that a future decline in the population aged 15-64 is partially offset by additional 
non-EU immigration for that Member State. 
Labour market participation is expected to continue rising, driven by structural increases 
in female participation and pension reforms that delay labour market exit. The participation 
rate of people aged 20-64 in the EU rose steadily, from 74% in 2005 to 81% in 2025. Female 
participation has been the main driver. Participation among older workers also increased strongly over 
the past two decades, reflecting pension reforms that raised statutory retirement ages and tightened 
access to early exit pathways. The participation rate among the EU population aged 20-64 is projected 
to rise further to 84% by 2050. The increase is expected to be strongest for the older ages, in particular 
in countries where the statutory and early retirement ages are automatically adjusted, fully or partially, 
to future gains in life expectancy (2). Eleven Member States currently have such mechanisms. More 
broadly, legislated pension reforms are projected to lift participation among people aged 55-64 by 
about 5 pps. by 2050 and by about 8 pps. among those aged 65-69, as people delay their labour 
market exit. 
Rising participation rates and a limited decline in the unemployment rate would allow for 
higher employment rates over the next decades. By 2050, the EU employment rate among people 
aged 20-64 is projected to rise by around 3 pps., to about 80%, with again the largest increases among 
older workers. The unemployment rate is set to decline gradually, to around 5% in 2050. 
Still, the shrinking working-age population implies that the total number of people 
employed in the EU will decline, thereby weighing on the economy’s growth potential. Total 
employment in the EU is projected to fall by 6% between 2025 and 2050, equivalent to almost 12 
million fewer people. In Bulgaria, Latvia, Greece, Slovakia, Poland, Lithuania, Romania, Hungary and 
Portugal, employment is projected to shrink by more than 15% by 2050. By contrast, employment is 
expected to increase by up to 9% in France, Finland, Belgium, Cyprus, the Netherlands and Denmark, 
with larger increases in Sweden (+11%), Ireland (+16%) and, especially, Luxembourg and Malta 
(around 30%). Aside from France and Finland, these countries’ working-age population is projected to 
grow by 2050 (see Graph 2).

people aged 20-64, the old-age economic dependency ratio serves as a key gauge for the expected 
macroeconomic repercussions of demographic ageing. Its projected increase implies that the EU would 
go from having 2.1 employed people aged between 20 and 64 for every inactive person aged 65+ in

Euro area inflation rose to a three-year high 
of 
4% 
in 
October, 
on 
the 
back 
of 
accelerating energy prices. Headline inflation 
was more than twice as high as at the onset of 
the conflict in Iran and the Middle East, while 
consumer energy inflation climbed to 20.8% 
year-on-year, up 24 pps. from February. By 
contrast, inflation excluding energy continued to 
show little underlying trend, remaining within the

*Estimate of inflation in fuels for personal transport in October based on DG ENER weekly oil bulleting https://energy.ec.europa.eu/data-and-

Transport fuels have so far accounted for the bulk of the increase on energy inflation. 
Transport fuel prices, which tend to quickly track movements in the underlying commodity price 
(Brent crude), rose by 31% between February and September (see Graph I.7.2b). Retail gas and 
electricity prices increased much less—by 11.8% and 3.7%—respectively, despite the surge in 
wholesale gas prices (TTF benchmark) and wholesale electricity prices, which climbed by 132% and 
80% respectively, over the period (see Graph I.7.2a). The weaker overall pass-through to retail gas 
and electricity prices reflects several factors, including a larger share of taxes and levies in these 
prices compared with than transport fuels, the prevalence of long-term consumer contracts, a high 
degree of administrative price regulation, and a rising overall share of renewable energy in 
electricity generation.

Food inflation fell to multi-year lows over the summer, but pressures in unprocessed 
food have begun to build in August. The moderation was especially pronounced for processed 
food. Over the summer, price momentum (3m-o-3m) fell to zero, while annual inflation dropped to 
0.4% y-o-y in September—its lowest level in more than 10 years—before edging up to 0.5% in 
October in the euro area (see Graph I.7.3.a). The slowdown was broad-based across all major food 
categories, with some—such as cereals, dairy, sugar, oils and fats—recording outright price 
declines on an annual basis. The current low readings for processed food inflation reflect 
favourable supply conditions, following last year’s bumper harvests in the EU, combined with 
strong competitive pressures from imported food products (15). Deflation in EU import prices of 
food (including both processed and unprocessed food) deepened to -4.0% y-o-y in July from -3.5% 
on average in the second quarter of 2026. However, price pressures in unprocessed foods appear 
to be firming (16) (see Graph I.7.3.b), reflecting the pass-through of higher energy and fertiliser 
prices, as well as the effects of the summer’s extreme weather on agricultural production in 
several regions. As a result, after falling to 2.4% y-o-y in July, inflation in unprocessed foods 
rebounded in subsequent months, reaching 4.6% in October.  
 
 
 
(15) Deflation in EU import prices of food deepened to -4.0% y-o-y in July from -3.5% and -3.2% on average in the second 
and first quarter of 2026 respectively. 
(16) Mainly reflecting inflation of vegetables included in the unprocessed foods which rose from 0.8% y-o-y in July to 6.8% 
in September in the EU.

Inflation of services has remained broadly 
stable since mid-2025. Since May 2025 
services inflation has fluctuated within a narrow 
range of 3.0%-3.5% in the euro area and 3.3%-
3.8% in the EU. There have been, however, 
important shifts across service categories. Prices 
have accelerated visibly for communication 
services and, to a smaller extent, for package 
holidays, accommodation and transport services, 
while 
easing 
in 
housing-related 
services, 
recreation and personal care (see Graph I.7.4.a). 
Clear patterns also emerge when services are 
grouped by main cost drivers. Notably, inflation in 
wage-sensitive services has continued to decline, 
falling to 2.7% y-o-y in September—its lowest 
rate in four years—suggesting that, so far, the 
energy shock has not triggered significant 
second-round effects (17). At the same time, the 
absence of a clear trend in energy-sensitive 
services inflation points to still limited indirect 
effects of the energy shock on services prices 
overall (Graph I.7.4.b).  
Inflation of industrial goods edged up over the summer. It rose from 0.5% in the first 
quarter of 2026 to 0.7%-0.9% between April and July, climbing further to 1.2% in August, where it 
stayed until October. The increase was driven by two factors: a one-off upward adjustment in 
medicine prices in Germany (18), and a steady rise in inflation of IT and communication equipment, 
notably computers and smartphones. Together with the visible acceleration in inflation of internet 
and information processing services (part of the Communication grouping in Graph I.7.4), the latter 
development likely points to rising demand pressures linked to the increased adoption of AI and 
expansion of data centres across the EU (see Graph I.7.5). 
 
(17) The inflationary impact of an energy shock is typically assessed through three channels: direct, indirect and second-
round effects. The direct effect captures the mechanical impact on headline inflation of a higher-priced product that is 
directly consumed by households (e.g. fuel). The indirect effect captures changes in firms’ production costs, which are 
passed on to final consumers (e.g. transport services). Second-round effects occur when a price shock raises inflation 
expectations and feeds into wage and price-setting behaviour, thereby causing further broad-based inflation pressures. 
(18) As part of the healthcare reform, pharmacy fees for prescription drugs were adjusted up in July 2026.

Core inflation (excl. energy and food) ticked 
up in May and has since broadly stabilised. 
The modest increase —driven by non-energy 
industrial goods prices— followed twelve months 
of core inflation hovering slightly above 2%.  A 
broader assessment of underlying price pressures 
(see Graph I.7.6) confirms a firming of these 
pressures over the course of 2026. Both the 
median and the range of 19 alternative measures 
of underlying inflation indicate a mild increase in 
the first half of the year, followed by broad 
stabilisation during the summer months.  
Energy inflation is set to remain high in 
2027 despite falling energy commodity 
prices. Given the assumed decline in Brent oil 
prices (see Section I.3. Energy and commodity 
markets) as of late October, transport fuels 
prices—petrol and diesel—are expected to decline 
gradually 
from 
their 
peaks, 
providing 
an 
immediate disinflationary impulse that is set to 
turn deflationary as from mid-2027 onwards. By 
contrast, the decline in wholesale prices of gas 
and electricity is expected to feed through to 
consumers 
much 
later, 
owing 
to 
longer 
transmission lags. Therefore, most household gas and electricity contracts in the EU are still likely 
to be adjusted upwards in the coming quarters, even as TTF prices embark on the expected 
declining path. Nevertheless, energy inflation is projected to moderate next year, as falling fuel 
prices more than offset the gradual rise in electricity and gas. Overall, energy inflation in the euro 
area is expected to peak at above 20% y-o-y, before coming down over the course of next year, 
averaging 7% for 2027 as a whole, and moderating to around 1% and 0% in the EU and euro 
area, respectively.  
The entry into operation of the second EU 
Emissions Trading System (ETS2) is forecast 
to impact energy inflation in 2028. The ETS2 
introduces carbon pricing for CO2 emissions from 
fossil fuels used in buildings, road transport and 
industries not covered by the ETS1 (19). The 
transmission of the carbon price to consumer 
prices for fossil transport and heating fuels is 
forecast to generate an impulse to consumer 
energy prices in 2028 (20), with its strength 
reflecting carbon intensity of domestic heating 
and transport, as well as existing climate policies 
and energy taxation. In some Member States, this 
impulse is expected to offset the deflationary 
effect of the decline in the energy commodity 
prices, keeping energy inflation positive in 2028. 
The energy shock is set to pass through to non-energy HICP components over the 
forecast horizon. Price pressures are expected to broaden progressively, as rising energy costs 
 
(19) For details see Special Issue 3. Trends in carbon intensity and the macroeconomic role of the EU Emissions Trading 
System in European Commission (2025).   
(20) This impact can be mitigated by the Social Climate Fund. 
Graph I.7.6: 
HICP excl. energy and food, and a range of 19 
alternative underlying inflation indicators in 
the euro area 
Median, interquartile range and range refer to a group of 19

feed through the production and distribution chains and are ultimately partially passed on to 
consumers (see Box I.7.1). Food prices are expected to be affected most strongly, as the impulse 
from higher prices of fertiliser and energy – particularly for gas – will be compounded by lower 
supply in many agricultural markets due to the extreme heatwaves and droughts in the summer of 
2026, as well as supply bottlenecks in specific markets (e.g. grain) caused by Russia’s war of 
aggression towards Ukraine and the conflicts in the Middle East. The expected rise in annual food 
inflation is set to be supported also by this year’s low base created by the deceleration in price 
momentum this summer. As a result, food inflation is expected to rise from just above 2% 
projected for 2026 to around 3% in 2027, before easing in 2028. Services inflation is expected to 
be affected mainly through higher input costs in transport, package holidays and other energy-
sensitive sectors. Slightly stronger-than-expected wage growth is set to strengthen the pass-
through, which has so far been limited. Consequently, services inflation is projected to persist 
above 3% this and next year and ease below 3% in 2028 in both areas. Finally, inflation in non-
energy industrial goods is projected to gradually increase from 0.5% in 2025 to 1.0% in 2026 and 
around 1.3% in 2027. This reflects several factors, including the healthcare reform in Germany, 
which will raise the cost of medicines and medical treatments further in January 2027; continued 
strengthening price pressures in IT-related goods and services; and the ongoing pass-through of 
higher energy prices via higher production and transport costs. Inflation is then projected to ease 
back in 2028 to around 1%, still considerably above the long-term average.   
Headline inflation is expected to peak at the turn of the year. The peak is expected at 
above 4% in 2026-Q4 in the euro area and 2027-Q1 in the EU. Inflation is then expected to 
decline to 2.1% and 2.3% in the fourth quarter of 2027 and remain unchanged throughout 2028. 
In both the euro area and the EU, headline inflation is thus projected to remain above 3%, on 
average in 2026 and 2027 and drop by approximately 1 pp. in 2028, averaging 2.1% in the euro 
area and 2.3% in the EU. Meanwhile, core inflation (excluding energy and food) is expected to rise 
in the coming months, peaking at around 3% in the first quarter of 2027 in both regions. It is then 
forecast to ease to roughly 2.5% in the final quarter of 2027, and further down to just above 2% 
in the final quarter of 2028. Euro area inflation projections using detailed information on selling 
price expectations from the Commission’s business surveys broadly confirm the near-term outlook 
for headline and core inflation. The median of headline inflation forecasts peaks in early 2027 and 
moderates from March onwards, while core inflation continues on a mild upward trend throughout 
the six-month forecast horizon (see Graph I.7.7). Separately, the magnitude and profile of revisions 
to euro area headline inflation over the forecast horizon appears consistent with financial market 
inflation expectations, which increased sharply at the short-term horizons but remain anchored at 
around 2% over the medium-term (see Section I.4, Graph I.4.2).

(Continued on the next page) 
Box I.7.1: The direct impact of imported energy price shocks on consumption prices
Input-output tables can be used to trace the propagation of the energy price shock to final 
domestic prices. The analysis in this Box estimates the impact of a stylised energy price shock on 
consumer prices in the EU. Using Eurostat’s input-output FIGARO tables, (1) it follows the shock along 
supply chains to show how it spreads through the economy. Specifically, it assesses the impact on the 
private consumption deflator of a 10% increase in the prices of crude oil, natural gas and coal imported 
from outside the EU. (2) Moreover, import prices of refined petroleum products are assumed to move in 
line with those of domestically produced refined products. The detailed industry breakdown of the 
FIGARO tables makes it possible to identify the sectors through which higher costs are ultimately 
passed on to goods and services.  
The results should be read as a stylised simulation. The exercise is static and linear; it captures 
the direct and indirect cost effects once retail contracts have fully adjusted, assuming that cost 
increases are fully passed on along the supply chain. It does not include second-round effects such as 
wage responses, changes in profit margins or demand adjustments, or changes in production efficiency. 
The simulated shock is not a global shock: prices of non-energy products imported from outside the 
EU, such as food or fertilisers, are held constant, although they would also rise as foreign producers 
face higher energy costs. The wholesale electricity price is typically set by the most expensive producer 
needed to meet demand, often gas-fired. Electricity from nuclear or renewable sources is then sold at 
a higher price although its costs do not change, so producers’ margins rise. The cost-based approach 
followed in the baseline simulation therefore understates the effect on electricity prices, which the 
sensitivity analysis on electricity prices addresses. For these reasons, the results may not be fully 
comparable with empirical estimates of pass-throughs of energy price shocks, but can help inform the 
forecast. 
Oil is the main conduit through which energy price shocks pass through to consumer 
inflation. The simulation results suggest that a 10% external shock to energy prices raises consumer 
prices in the EU by 0.22%. Crude oil accounts for half of this increase (0.11 pps.), while natural gas 
contributes around one third (0.08 pps.). Imported petrol, diesel and other refined fuels – the prices of 
which are assumed to rise by slightly more than half of the crude oil price shock, with a coefficient of 
0.55 – together account for the remainder (3), whereas the impact of coal is negligible (see the left-
most layer in Graph 1). 
The transmission of the shock through the domestic economy is concentrated in a limited 
number of channels. 
• 
Crude oil enters the economy primarily through refineries, which pass on higher costs to the rest 
of the economy via fuels (middle layer in Graph 1). Around two thirds of the total oil price impact 
reach households through higher prices of motor fuels and heating oil (right-most layer in Graph 
1). 
 
(1) 
A Leontief cost-push price model is applied to Eurostat’s 2024 FIGARO inter-country product-by-product input-
output table, covering the 27 Member States and the rest of the world. The shock is applied to import prices of 
mining and quarrying products (CPA B) and refined petroleum products (C19). Since FIGARO does not distinguish 
crude oil, natural gas and coal within CPA B, imports in that category are split using Comext trade data. Imported 
crude oil is allocated to refineries, while other users receive the remaining mix of gas, coal and other minerals.  
(2) 
Imported electricity is not considered, due to its negligible share in energy imports (around 1%). 
(3) 
The parameter is consistent with the EU-wide response implied by the FIGARO tables. A higher pass-through 
from oil to refined products changes the results only little. A pass-through of 0.8 instead of 0.55 adds 0.01 pps 
to the total impact.  

=== PAGE 87 ===
Economic outlook for EA and EU 
 
71 
 
Box (continued) 
    
(Continued on the next page) 
• 
Natural gas affects households mainly through higher prices of electricity  and gas used for 
heating (4). Together, these two items account for around two thirds of the total gas price effect of 
the energy price increase. The remaining third is transmitted through other goods and services in 
the consumption basket. Assuming a full pass-through from gas prices to wholesale electricity 
prices, following the estimates of Kosch et al. (2022), the impact would increase by about 0.06 
pps., raising the total impact on the consumption deflator to 0.28%. (5) 
• 
Imported refined petroleum products affect private consumption mainly directly (6) through higher 
prices of motor fuels. Indirect effects operating through higher prices of food, industrial goods and 
services account for around 40% of the total impact  
Graph 1: 
How a 10% increase in imported energy prices feeds into the consumption deflator in the EU 
Each column in this chart adds up to 0.22%, which is the total effect of a 10% shock to imported energy prices on the EU 
consumption deflator. The left column splits this effect by imported energy product, the middle column shows the importing sector 
in the EU, and the right column shows the prices of final goods and services in which the effect ends up, grouped into energy and 
other goods and services. 
 
(4) 
Input–output tables include flows of natural gas and refined products to consumers without the intermediation 
of the energy sector, suggesting direct imports of these products by consumers. This reflects a statistical 
convention: domestic suppliers that buy gas and resell it without transforming it are treated as intermediaries. 
Their contribution is recorded separately as a distribution or trade service, while the gas itself is attributed to 
the final user. The data therefore describe the origin of what households consume, rather than the actual 
importer. 
(5) 
The rise in electricity prices in the baseline simulation is proportional to the share of imported fuel in power 
companies’ costs, which is about a tenth across the EU. Most of the cost of supplying electricity comes from 
other factors: fuels sourced in Europe (renewables, nuclear…), maintenance of the grid, and the investment and 
labour cost behind power plants. However, wholesale electricity prices are set by the most expensive power 
plant needed to meet demand, which is frequently a gas plant. Assuming wholesale electricity prices rise by 
€1/MWh for each €1/MWh increase in the TTF gas price, electricity adds a further 0.06 pp. The pass-through 
parameter is an average of estimates for 13 EU countries weighted by electricity consumption. See M. Kosch, 
K. Blech and J. Abrell. “Rising Electricity Prices in Europe: The Impact of Fuel and Carbon Prices”, October 6, 
2022. Assuming a heat rate of 2 MWh of gas per MWh of electricity, this implies that gas sets the price in 
about half of all hours, in line with the Spring 2026 Forecast (Box I.6.1). 
(6) 
See the previous footnote.

Box (continued) 
    
Energy-related bills account for most of the hit on household budgets, but non-energy 
consumption also contributes noticeably. Higher prices of motor fuels and heating oil (0.09 pps.) 
and for electricity and gas (0.05 pps.) together account for around 60% of the total inflation effect 
(see the right-most layer in Graph 1). The rest is spread across other goods and services, whose prices 
have been pushed up indirectly through more expensive production and distribution. 
Across Member States, the effects of the energy price shock differ significantly. The impact 
on the consumption deflator ranges from around 0.1% to almost 0.4% (Graph 2). Consumer prices in 
Lithuania, Croatia, Slovakia, Cyprus, Poland and Italy appear to be hit the hardest, with an impact of 
between 0.3% and 0.4%. The smallest impacts, at around 0.1%, are estimated for Luxembourg, Ireland, 
Sweden and Denmark. Among the largest euro area economies, the impact ranges from slightly above 
0.3% for Italy, below 0.3% for France to around 0.2% for Spain and the Netherlands, and below 0.2% 
for Germany. 
Differences across countries are mainly driven by variation in energy consumption, import 
dependence and the energy intensity of domestic production.  The share of energy in household 
consumption matters: where fuel, heating and electricity account for a larger share of household 
spending, the same energy price increase has a stronger effect on the overall price level. In addition, 
exposure depends on the energy mix and on how much of each fuel is imported from outside the EU. 
Oil, almost entirely imported, is the main driver in all countries, mainly through motor fuels. The impact 
of gas varies considerably depending on its role in residential heating and electricity generation. 
Countries that generate power mainly from nuclear, renewables or other domestically sourced inputs 
are less exposed in this baseline simulation. (7) Finally, the amount of energy use in domestic production 
shapes the extent to which higher energy costs are passed through to the prices of food, manufactured 
goods and services. The impact through this channel is stronger in countries with more energy-intensive 
manufacturing, transport and agriculture.

8. EXTERNAL TRANSACTIONS 
  
73 
The EU current account surplus narrowed in 
the first half of 2026, with a pronounced 
decline in the second quarter, as expected. 
Against a backdrop of continued structural 
dependence on fossil fuel imports, the renewed 
surge in energy prices as from end February 
substantially increased import prices. The impact 
of this increase on the current account became 
apparent in 2026-Q2, when the energy deficit 
increased by almost 0.5 pps. of GDP compared 
with the previous quarter. On four-quarter 
cumulative basis, the increase was more modest, 
at 0.1 pps (see Graph I.8.1), as the widening of 
the energy deficit in the first half of 2026 was 
largely offset by its narrowing in the second half 
of 2025. Over the first half of the year, the 
deterioration in the energy balance was partially 
offset by a strong rebound in goods exports. Nevertheless, following significant market share 
losses in 2025, exports of non-energy goods remained below their 2025 average. As a result, the 
goods trade balance in 2026-Q2 stood around 0.3 pps. of GDP below its 2025 levels (see Graph 
I.8.1). Imports of services also increased, driven by continued growth in imports of intellectual 
property rights, as well as transport and insurance services, the latter likely reflecting higher 
energy-related freight and insurance costs. However, this increase was offset by robust growth in 
services exports. As a result, by mid-2026 the services balance remained unchanged at 0.8% of 
GDP. Together with slightly higher primary and secondary income balances, this cushioned the 
decline in the current account balance-to-GDP ratio, which stood 0.1 pps. below its 2025 levels. 
  
 
The EU merchandise trade balance is projected to deteriorate further in the second half 
of 2026, mainly reflecting continued worsening of the terms of trade. Based on energy 
futures at the forecast cut-off date, energy import prices are expected to be, on average, higher in 
the second half of the year than in the first. The energy deficit is thus projected to reach 1.9% of 
GDP in 2026 as a whole, 0.4 pps. above its 2025 level. The deterioration would nevertheless be 
considerably smaller than in 2022, following Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, primarily 
reflecting a less pronounced surge in energy prices and, to a lesser extent, lower import volumes of

fossil fuels. Beyond energy, a further, lower positive contribution of the terms of trade for non-
energy goods is also expected to weigh on the merchandise trade balance. Overall, the 
merchandise trade surplus is projected to narrow by 0.5 pps. of GDP in 2026, to 1.2% of GDP – its 
lowest level since 2011, apart from the exceptional low recorded in 2022. 
The merchandise trade balance is expected to improve only in 2028. Despite the expected 
moderation in energy prices, growth in goods import prices is still projected to slightly outpace 
export prices in 2027. The resulting further deterioration in the goods terms of trade will weigh on 
the merchandise trade balance, which is projected to decline to 1.1% of GDP in 2027 (see 
Graph I.8.2). The balance is projected to improve to 1.4% of GDP in 2028, largely driven by a 
narrowing of the energy deficit. The surplus in non-energy goods is expected to narrow slightly 
further over the forecast horizon, reflecting both somewhat faster import than export growth and a 
weakening of EU firms’ pricing and market power vis-à-vis international competitors. The latter 
reflects ongoing losses in external competitiveness, which have weighed on the non-energy goods 
surplus since 2015 and have more recently been compounded by factors such as US tariffs and 
the appreciation of the euro.  Differences across Member States in their exposure to energy 
imports and in cyclical conditions are expected to result in heterogeneous developments in the 
terms of trade, with corresponding implications for national goods trade balances (see Graph I.8.3).  
The EU’s services surplus, in contrast, is projected to further increase over the forecast 
horizon. Net services exports are expected to rise gradually, bringing the services surplus to 2.1% 
of GDP in 2028, 0.3 pps. above its 2025 level. The increase reflects both slightly improving terms 
of trade—even if heterogeneous across sectors—and an increase in services export volumes (see 
Graph I.8.2). 
Overall, the decline in the merchandise trade balance is expected to more than offsets 
the improvement in the services balance, resulting in the slight narrowing of the current 
account surplus as a share of GDP in 2027, before recovering 2028. The latter is projected 
to fall from 2.3% of GDP in 2025 to 1.9% in 2027 (see Table I.8.1). It is expected to recover only 
partially in 2028, supported by an improvement in the trade balance, particularly through a 
narrowing energy deficit and, to a lesser extent, a further increase in the services balance, which is 
set to remain above the goods surplus throughout the forecast horizon. The other current-account 
components — the primary and secondary income balances — are set to remain broadly stable 
over the forecast horizon.

75 
The aggregate EU general government 
deficit is expected to rise over the forecast 
horizon. The deficit ratio is set to widen to 3.6% 
of GDP in 2026, from 3.1% in 2025 (see 
Graph I.9.2), mostly reflecting the projected 
increase in interest expenditure, some revenue

and temporary measures to cushion the impact of 
high

in 2027, as still increasing interest expenditure 
and revenue shortfalls are largely offset by a 
mildly restrictive stance of national budgets. The 
phasing out of energy support measures contributes most to the budgetary restriction, while the 
planned further rise in defence spending works in the opposite direction. In 2028, the EU deficit is 
set to reach 3.8% of GDP on the basis of current policies. In the euro area, the government deficit 
is projected to increase from 3% of GDP in 2025 to 3.5% in 2026, 3.6% in 2027 and 3.8% in 
2028. 
An increasing number of Member States is expected to record deficits higher than 3% of 
GDP. In 2025, 10 countries reported deficit ratios exceeding 3%. This number is projected to rise 
to 12 in 2026, and 16 in 2027 and 2028 (see Graph I.9.2). In 2028, eight countries are projected 
to have deficits above 5% of GDP (Belgium, France, Slovakia, Hungary, Poland, Latvia, Romania 
and Lithuania).

The fiscal stance is computed by comparing the increase in primary expenditure—net of the impact of cyclical elements of unemployment 
benefit expenditure and one-offs—with an increase that would be in line with 10-year nominal potential output growth, with the difference 
expressed as % of GDP. The fiscal stance also takes into account the impact of discretionary revenue measures and includes expenditure 
financed by EU budget grants. For this purpose what matters is the time when this expenditure is recorded in national accounts irrespective 
of lags or leads in the cash flows from the EU budget. In the case of defence equipment, expenditure is recorded at the time of delivery, 
which is often different from the related payments.

10. RISKS 
  
79 
Risks to the outlook remain closely linked to the evolution of the conflict in the Middle 
East and elevated geopolitical tensions.  The standoff between US and Iran is lasting longer 
than initially expatiated. Nevertheless, global geopolitical risk index  has declined since its July peak 
(see Graph I.10.1). The decrease in geopolitical risk since the cut-off date of the Spring 2026 
Forecast increased the likelihood of upside surprises to GDP growth in 2027. While it increased the 
median of expected GDP growth by 0.2pps., it raised the 75th and 90th percentiles of the growth 
distribution by 0.4 and 0.5 pps. respectively. It also shifted the entire distribution of 2028 growth 
expectations higher by about 0.3 pps. (see Graph I.10.2). This provides further evidence of the 
upside risks to growth associated with a reduction in geopolitical risk.  
 
 
  
Energy price movements after the US mid-term 
elections suggest inflation might fall faster 
than projected. Oil prices have fallen sharply—
averaging USD 92 per barrel just prior to the 
publication of the Autumn 2026 Forecast (average 
from 9th to 11th November), nearly 20% below the 
assumption underpinning the baseline forecast 
(average from 15th to 29th October)—as the results of 
the US mid-term elections eased fears wider military 
escalations. Futures curves, however, have adjusted 
only partially so far, leaving oil prices at the end of 
the forecast horizon just 6% below the reference 
value at the cut-off date. Gas markets show a 
comparable pattern. Lower energy prices have also 
pulled down market-based inflation expectations and, 
through that channel, the projected path of short-
term interest rates and, to a lesser extent, long-term 
rates. Model-based simulations suggest that, while 
the projections for 2026 would hardly be affected, inflation in 2027 and 2028 could be 0.26 and 
0.06 pps. lower than projected, respectively, if recent market conditions were to persist. In turn,

Graph I.10.3: Changes in technical assumptions - model-based simulation 
  
The impact of recent changes in technical assumptions on EU inflation and GDP growth was quantified with the Commission's GM model. 
Against the backdrop of persistent tensions with Russia and increasing threats to 
Europe, a further deterioration in the security environment could weigh on economic 
activity through its effects on confidence, supply chains and firms’ operating costs. As 
tensions in the Middle East seem to ease, security threats closer to Europe remain acute. After 
nearly five years, Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine continues to cast a shadow on 
Europe’s prospects. State-sponsored or AI-enabled cyberattacks have emerged as a new threat to 
economic activity and supply chains, adding to inflationary pressures. The higher expenditure firms 
must incur to protect themselves against these risks raises operating costs and can divert spending 
away from more productive uses. Heightened security concerns could also weigh on business and 
household sentiment, dampening consumption and investment, especially on the EU’s Eastern 
Flank. 
Deeper trade relationships with like-minded partners could partly offset the negative 
impact of elevated trade policy uncertainty. Uncertainty remains over the future direction of 
US trade policy, including the expiry of the US-China tariff truce in early 2027 and the forthcoming 
review of the USMCA. Meanwhile, persistent EU-China trade imbalances, fuelled by China's state-
supported industrial overcapacity and the use of trade dependencies as leverage, remain a source 
of concern. On the upside, protracted global trade uncertainty increases the attractiveness of the 
EU as a stable, rules-abiding economic bloc. Improving trade relations with the UK and deeper 
cooperation with partners such as Canada could provide further impetus to economic activity. 
A disorderly correction in equity markets could weigh on economic activity and spill over 
onto broader financial markets. Volatility in financial markets remains elevated, especially in 
the United States but also in some East Asian markets, where high equity valuations and high 
concentration continue to be a source of concern. AI-related stock valuations appear vulnerable to 
correction if returns disappoint relative to the large scale of investment and associated external 
financing. While a correction in market valuations would ultimately remain contained to higher-
wealth households who can easily cushion the financial shocks, a substantial downward revision of 
global AI investment plans could dampen EU exports. The complexity and opacity of AI investment 
financing schemes, together with the involvement of highly leveraged investors, also increase the 
risk that a disorderly correction in equity markets could spill over into bond and credit markets, 
potentially resulting in a broader and more pronounced negative impact on EU economic activity. 
 
(29) Model-based simulations are done using the GM model, jointly developed The European Commission’s 
Directorate-General for Economic and Financial Affairs and the Joint Research Centre. For a recent research application, 
see e.g. Pataracchia et al. (2026). The version used here covers the EU (rather than the euro area) and incorporates 
additional model features.

81 
Renewed upward pressure on long-term interest rates could further increase pressures 
on public finances of several Member States and weigh on economic growth. Although 
yields have retreated from their summer highs, recent volatility highlights the importance markets 
attach to fiscal positions, medium-term debt trajectories and political developments. A further rise 
in borrowing costs could require more forceful fiscal adjustment, which would weigh on growth.   
Faster adoption of AI by European firms could provide a significant boost to 
productivity, but could also disrupt labour markets. European firms are increasingly 
embracing AI, but its integration into business operations remains limited in scope: adoption is 
often concentrated in individual applications rather than extending systematically across business 
functions and production processes (EIB, 2025). Faster and deeper adoption of AI by EU firms could 
raise productivity across the economy, increasing potential output (see Box I.10.1) and helping 
contain labour costs. AI adoption, however, could also bring labour-market dislocations, as 
changing skill requirements and the automation of certain tasks could weigh on employment in 
some occupations. Any resulting weakening of the labour market could dampen consumption, 
partly offsetting the gains from higher productivity. Finally, the rapid expansion of data centres 
could significantly increase electricity demand, partly counteracting the downward pressure on 
electricity prices from the growing supply of renewable energy.  
Further important upside risks to growth remain linked to several ongoing policy 
initiatives. Resolute policy action to address long-standing bottlenecks to investment and EU 
competitiveness could strengthen medium-term growth prospects and improve the economy’s 
capacity to absorb external shocks. Sustained progress in the energy transition, including 
investment in renewable energy and overall energy efficiency, would further reduce Europe’s 
vulnerability to fossil fuel price shocks. Stronger investment in European defence, infrastructure 
and industrial modernisation could also provide additional support to domestic demand and 
productivity growth over the forecast horizon. However, the forthcoming elections in several 
Member States in 2027 could result in political uncertainty and complicate policymaking. Further 
deployment of untargeted discretionary fiscal measures to shield households and firms from high 
energy prices could weaken incentives for decarbonisation and energy efficiency, while increasing 
medium-term fiscal consolidation needs. 
The increasing frequency of severe climate- and weather-related extreme events 
weighs on economic activity and exerts upward pressure on prices. This is a particular 
concern for Europe, the continent experiencing the fastest pace of warming. The summer 
heatwaves and droughts of 2026 illustrate the impact of extreme weather on agriculture, energy 
markets (through higher cooling demand and disrupted electricity generation), supply chains (due 
to low river levels) and labour productivity. Wildfires also destroyed productive and natural assets, 
with lasting economic consequences. While adaptation measures may benefit some sectors, their 
financing may add to medium-term fiscal challenges. 
Overall, in the near term, risks to growth remain two-sided, while risks to inflation 
appear tilted to the downside. Risks to growth reflect still-elevated geopolitical uncertainty, 
linked to the evolution of ongoing conflicts, including a further deterioration in Europe’s security 
environment. Financial market-related risks remain another important source of uncertainty. On 
the energy front, developments in energy markets since the cut-off date, if sustained, introduce 
material downside risks to the inflation projections, while providing some upside for growth. The 
most prominent upside risks to growth would stem from faster progress in implementing growth-
enhancing policies and structural reforms, as well as a faster-than-expected materialisation of 
productivity gains associated with the roll-out of AI, which might also provide additional relief from 
price pressures. 

=== PAGE 98 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
82 
 
   
(Continued on the next page) 
Box I.10.1: Incorporating AI into potential output estimates: challenges and practices
Artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping the economy and will have significant implications 
for its long-term productive capacity. The adoption of AI is stimulating dedicated investment while 
also transforming business models, work organisation and skill needs, with consequences for both 
short-term growth and potential output. The Commission estimates potential output using the EU’s 
Commonly Agreed Production Function Methodology (EUCAM), which combines the capital stock, trend 
labour input and total factor productivity (TFP) within a Cobb-Douglas production function (Arevalo et 
al., 2026). AI-related developments may affect potential output through each of these three channels: 
capital deepening, as firms accumulate AI-related assets; labour input, as AI influences hours worked 
and structural unemployment; and TFP, through efficiency gains stemming from production 
reorganisation, improved use of information, faster innovation, and changes in the workforce’s skill 
composition. This box reviews these transmission channels, discusses the challenges involved in 
measuring their effects, and explains how AI-related developments are captured in EUCAM. 
AI-related investment can raise potential output through capital deepening, but it is 
empirically difficult to isolate. In a standard production-function framework, investing in AI-related 
tangible and intangible assets expands the productive capital stock, raising potential output for a given 
level of labour input. However, evaluating the impact of AI-related investments presents two distinct 
empirical challenges. First, such investment is not separately identified in national accounts, making it 
difficult to isolate its contribution from that of other capital, such as broader ICT or software 
investment. Second, key complementary expenditures, notably on worker training and organisational 
restructuring, are typically expensed rather than capitalised. They are therefore missing from the 
measured capital stock, and their contribution to output ends up being captured in TFP instead. 
The impact of AI on labour input remains uncertain. AI can substitute for human labour in some 
tasks, complement it in others, and create new tasks and occupations, leaving its net effect on 
employment unclear. Although current AI is particularly capable of performing cognitive tasks, exposure 
alone does not determine employment outcomes: these also depend on the scope for automation and 
how work is reorganised around AI (World Bank, 2026). Where substitution dominates, AI may also 
shift income from labour to capital, affecting factor shares independently of its effect on output. So 
far, evidence points to uneven effects across workers rather than widespread aggregate job 
displacement. In the United States, there is evidence of adjustment occurring mainly through reduced 
hiring, in particular for young workers in AI-exposed occupations, rather than increased lay-offs 
(Brynjolfsson et al., 2025; Lichtinger and Hosseini Maasoum, 2025). In the euro area, by contrast, firms 
using AI report stronger demand for complementary skills and higher hiring (European Commission, 
DG Employment, 2025; Lebastard and Sondermann, 2026). A key adjustment risk is that workers 
displaced from some tasks, or young people entering the labour market, may lack the skills required in 
expanding occupations. Such mismatches could temporarily increase structural unemployment and 
delay labour market entry, effects that EUCAM would capture through the NAWRU and trend 
participation. Shifts in factor shares, however, are not captured, as EUCAM’s Cobb-Douglas 
specification holds them constant. (1) 
Total factor productivity is potentially the largest channel through which AI affects 
economic growth. Adoption and deployment of AI, even where not domestically produced, can 
improve the efficiency with which existing technologies and knowledge are used, by enhancing 
information processing and resource allocation across tasks and firms. It may also shift the 
technological frontier outward by acting as an “innovation in the method of innovation” (Bontadini et 
al., 2025), accelerating the creation of new ideas. While the first channel raises the level of TFP, the 
second could also raise its long-run growth rate if AI durably increases the efficiency of knowledge 
 
(1) 
The Cobb-Douglas specification on which EUCAM rests assumes a unit elasticity of substitution between capital 
and labour, thus holding factor income shares strictly constant. In contrast, general CES specifications would 
allow the elasticity parameter to deviate from unity, meaning factor shares shift dynamically as AI-driven 
automation transforms production and alter the relative demands for capital and labour. Given the high 
uncertainty surrounding AI’s empirical impact on the production factors and their interrelation, the fixed-share 
approximation remains the operational standard. 

=== PAGE 99 ===
Economic outlook for EA and EU 
 
83 
 
Box (continued) 
   
production. The magnitude of these effects is likely to vary across economies, depending on their 
sectoral and skill composition, as well as their capacity to innovate and adopt new technologies. 
However, estimates of AI’s contribution to productivity growth vary widely, reflecting 
differences in modelling approaches and underlying assumptions. Task-based approaches 
assess which tasks AI can automate cost-effectively, while macro-level approaches treat AI as a 
transformative technology that may also accelerate innovation. For a recent literature review, see 
European Commission (2026) (2) . The timing of the gains is also uncertain. Because the intangible 
investments that accompany AI adoption are largely unmeasured, measured TFP growth can follow a 
“J-curve” (Brynjolfsson et al., 2021): it is understated while firms invest in training and process 
redesign, and overstated once these investments pay off. This pattern reflects measurement issues 
rather than a genuine slowdown followed by a boom, so early productivity data are a poor guide to 
AI’s eventual impact. 
In the EUCAM framework, AI effects enter implicitly, as they materialise in the data and as 
projected in the Commission’s Economic Forecast. Over the forecast horizon, the impact of AI is 
captured through projected investment, employment and productivity, feeding into the capital stock, 
labour input and TFP respectively. Beyond the forecast horizon, the projected developments are carried 
forward through the extension of trends. Long-run TFP growth projections rest on an endogenous 
growth specification (Romer, 1990), which provides a structural channel through which AI could raise 
long-term TFP growth by increasing the productivity of R&D and the efficiency of knowledge 
production. Other growth model specifications would have implied more limited effects: in semi-
endogenous (Jones, 1995) and additive (Philippon, 2022) growth models, AI raises the level of TFP but 
does not permanently raise its growth rate.  
Other institutional potential output frameworks account for AI either implicitly or explicitly, 
the latter through an ad hoc adjustment to trend TFP growth. The implicit approach followed in 
the EUCAM also underpins the ECB’s unobserved-components model and the IMF reference forecast 
(IMF, 2026). The explicit approach is used by institutions such as the US Congressional Budget Office, 
the UK Office for Budget Responsibility, and the Bank of Canada. The adjustments to TFP growth 
typically draw on existing task-based estimates of AI’s automation potential. 
Overall, the effects of AI remain highly uncertain and therefore difficult to capture in the 
potential output estimates that underpin macroeconomic surveillance and fiscal policy. The 
pace of AI diffusion is uncertain, employment effects are ambiguous, AI-related investment is difficult 
to isolate and productivity effects are hard to assess. By incorporating AI only as its effects emerge in 
observed and projected investment, employment and productivity, EUCAM avoids building speculative 
gains into the fiscal benchmarks underpinning the EU’s fiscal surveillance framework, while its long-
term growth framework would carry forward any durable gains once they materialise. Risks for 
potential output estimates lie on both sides. On the upside, faster adoption and AI-driven gains in 
knowledge production could lift potential growth above current estimates. On the downside, 
overinvestment in rapidly obsolescing assets could leave little lasting addition to the capital stock, 
resource constraints could slow AI deployment, and AI adoption in the EU could continue to lag behind 
other major economies. Reducing this uncertainty calls for close monitoring of AI adoption and its 
returns, and assessment of alternative AI paths around the central potential growth projections. 
 
(2) 
European Commission (2026), 2027 European Macroeconomic Report, Institutional Paper Series Forthcoming.

Box I.10.2: Technical elements and general assumptions behind the forecast
This box details the technical and ad hoc assumptions underlying this forecast. The cut-off date for 
taking information into account in this Economic Forecast was 3 November 2026, and 29 October 
2026 for technical assumptions.  
1. Technical elements behind the forecast 
Exchange and interest rates 
Nominal exchange rates are kept constant over the forecast horizon at the level recorded during the 
reference period of 15-28 October 2026 (see Table 1 in this box and Table 31 in the Statistical Annex). 
All interest rate assumptions are derived from implicit market rates, thus fully reflecting market 
expectations at the time of the forecast. The assumptions for short-term interest rates for euro area 
Member States are derived from their average level, during the reference period, in three-month 
EURIBOR futures contracts over the forecast horizon. In the absence of future contracts, the 
assumptions for short-term rates of non-euro area Member States and countries outside the EU are 
derived from their average level, over the reference period, of the implicit forward three-month OIS 
(overnight indexed swap) rates, corrected for the average spread between the three-month EURIBOR 
rate and the OIS swap rate with three-month maturity. The assumptions for long-term interest rates 
for the euro area Member States are derived from the average forward sovereign rates over the 
reference period, when available. Forward sovereign rates are also used, when available, for the other 
EU Member States as well as of the countries outside EU examined in the forecast (1). 
Commodity prices 
Assumptions for Brent oil, gas and electricity prices are based on futures markets, using the average 
over the 10-day reference period between 15 and 28 October 2026 (see Section I.2). Given the 
unusually large gap between prices for immediate physical deliveries and financial futures, the 
assumptions for Brent oil prices in the current quarter factor in a linearly declining gap between the 
front month Brent price and Dated Brent, a key benchmark for near-term physical deliveries. 
Trade policies and assumptions 
In its judgement of 20 February 2026, the US Supreme Court struck down the tariffs imposed under 
the International Emergency Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) in 2025. In response, the US administration 
introduced a 10% ‘global tariff’ under a new legal basis (Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974), which 
allowed for across-the-board import restrictions for up to 150 days. Upon its expiration on 24 July 
2026, the US administration replaced it with indefinite Section 301 tariffs of 10% to 12.5% across 
major trading partners, alongside specific product exemptions and country-level tariff caps. This tariff 
is assumed to apply irrespective of existing trade agreements with the United States and to remain 
unchanged over the projection horizon. As a result, the trade-weighted average US tariff rate on 
imports declined from 16.8% to 13.5% (according to WTO data). The effective US tariff rate on EU 
imports is assumed to decline from 12.1% (tariffs of 14/10, AF25) to 11.4% (according to WTO data). 
Larger tariff reductions are assumed for several other US trading partners, slightly negatively affecting 
EU relative competitive position.  
Following the July 1, 2026 joint review—at which the United States declined to extend the agreement 
for a 16-year term—the USMCA has entered an annual review cycle, while the bilateral US-China trade 
truce is currently set to expire on January 10, 2027. In line with a no-policy-change assumption, the 
forecast holds existing tariff regimes unchanged across the projection horizon. Furthermore, the 
scheduled December 4, 2026 implementation of new Section 232 duties and minimum import prices 
on polysilicon derivatives used in solar panels and semiconductors—specifically polysilicon ingots,

euro area (i.e. the difference between the country short or long term rates and the three-month EURIBOR rate 
for the short-term rate and the 10-year German sovereign rate for the long-term rate), based on the monthly

ESA 2010 
The forecast is based on the ESA 2010 system of national accounts for all Member States, the EU and

Calendar effects on GDP growth and output gaps 
The Commission’s annual GDP forecasts are not adjusted for the number of working days, but quarterly 
forecasts are. The number of working days may differ from one year to another, and the difference 
between the calendar-adjusted and -unadjusted annual growth rates—that is, the “working-day 
effect”—may be significant in some Member States. However, it is estimated to be limited on 
aggregate, at about -0.1 pps. for the EU and the euro area in 2026, and marginally in 2027 and 2028 
(±0.1pps.).  
Net expenditure indicator and fiscal stance 
The net expenditure growth data used for fiscal surveillance will be published separately with the 
autumn and spring packages. For the purpose of quantifying the impulse provided by fiscal policy to 
the economy, i.e. the fiscal stance, the Commission services use the growth of a similar (though 
somewhat broader) net expenditure-based metric relative to medium-term potential GDP growth. (2)  
The inclusion of the Recovery and Resilience Facility in the forecast 
Transactions related to the RRF in the forecast are recorded in line with Eurostat’s ‘Guidance note on 
the statistical recording of the Recovery and Resilience Facility’ of 7 October 2021. In general, this 
implies that the budgetary impact of expenditure or other costs financed with non-repayable financial 
support (‘grants’) from the RRF is neutralised in revenue projections by matching transfers received 
from the EU. However, in some cases the appropriate time of recording is at the time of the adoption 
of Council Implementing Decisions. Expenditure financed by loans from the RRF are not neutralised 
and thus affect the government balance, while the loans by the RRF are recorded as Member States’ 
debt towards the EU.   
Budgetary data and forecasts 
The forecast incorporates validated public finance data up to 2025 as published in Eurostat’s news 
release of 21 October 2026 (3) (4) . In its press release, Eurostat expressed a reservation on the quality

In line with Eurostat’s press release, EU and euro area aggregates for general government balance and

through the European Financial Stability Facility), the projections in the forecast years 2026 and 2027 
are published on a non-consolidated basis. To ensure consistency in the time series, historical data are 
also published on the same basis. For 2025, this implies an aggregate debt-to-GDP ratio that is 
somewhat higher than the consolidated general government debt ratio published by Eurostat in its 
news release 21 October 2026 (by 1.3 pps. in the euro area 21 and by 1.1 pps. in the EU). 
 
2. Ad hoc technical assumptions underlying the forecast 
Russian war of aggression against Ukraine and geopolitical tensions  
The economic impact of Russia’s war against Ukraine remains highly uncertain and depends crucially 
on its evolution. The central scenario assumes that geopolitical tensions in the region and sanctions 
against Russia remain in place throughout the forecast horizon.  
People fleeing the war in Ukraine to the EU  
The number of beneficiaries of temporary protection in the EU was about 4.4 million by August 2026. (6) 
It is assumed that the number of active temporary protection registrations will decline to 4.3 million 
by the end of 2026 to 4.1 million by end-2027 and 3.9 million by end-2028. (7) Over 2026, new 
registrations are expected to continue declining at the average rate observed in the last three years, 
while downward revisions by Member States (i.e. data revisions by Member States reflecting people 
who returned to Ukraine, moved on to another country, or attained another status in their country of 
residence) are expected to continue at a rate reflecting the average over the same period. This results 
in the projection of an annual average of people seeking protection of about 4.4 million in 2026, 4.3 
million in 2027 and 4.0 million in 2028. Assumptions on the geographical distribution of people fleeing 
the war reflect their current distribution across Member States. Finally, as the labour market integration 
of people fleeing the war continues to make progress, related assumptions remain broadly in line with 
the previous projections. Based on the available information, it is estimated that the adult employment 
rate of temporary protection beneficiaries in working age may have been around 48% as of mid-2026, 
with significant heterogeneity across host countries. Going forward, this employment rate is projected 
to increase to 50% by end-2026, 55% by end-2027 and 60% by end-2028. 
Second EU Emissions Trading System (ETS2) 
ETS2 will become fully operational in 2028. The price of ETS2 allowances in 2028 is assumed to be 55 
EUR per ton CO2 in current prices, calculated as average of two elements (8) : (1) A 2025 Commission
