1. CYCLICAL AND STRUCTURAL DRIVERS BEHIND THE 
DYNAMICS OF THE EU MANUFACTURING SECTOR 
91 
The evolution of EU manufacturing activity reflects the interplay of structural and cyclical forces 
shaping its long-run trends and shorter-term fluctuations. This Special Issue disentangles the 
drivers behind the recent performance of EU manufacturing, based on statistical and econometric 
analysis. It analyses cycles of manufacturing activity at different frequencies, and uses a structural 
econometric model to uncover the drivers of manufacturing output in recent years.  
EU manufacturing has struggled to regain 
momentum following the Global Financial 
Crisis. In its aftermath, manufacturing output, as 
measured 
by 
gross 
value 
added 
(GVA),  
contracted sharply and took almost a decade to 
recover, as growth throughout the 2010s 
remained weaker than in the early 2000s. Growth 
in manufacturing GVA slowed further from 
around 2018–2019, well before the pandemic. 
The COVID-19 shock then caused a new sharp but 
short-lived drop in production in early 2020. 
Despite a strong rebound following the lifting of 
lockdowns, this recovery also proved short-lived, 
with manufacturing output broadly stagnating 
since 2022. (30) Industrial production volume, 
another key indicator of industrial performance, 
paints an even weaker picture, pointing to slower growth since the global financial crisis (GFC) and 
more recent signs of contraction, in contrast to the stagnation observed in manufacturing GVA (see 
Graph II.1.1). In addition to methodological differences (31), this divergence may reflect the fact that 
high-technology subsectors, which tend to generate higher value added per unit of production, 
have recently performed better than low- and medium-technology industries. The analysis that 
follows however, focuses on manufacturing GVA, as it better captures the sector’s contribution to 
the economy, discounting for the value of inputs imported or produced by other sectors. 
Exploring cycle lengths in the historical evolution of EU manufacturing  
Long-term trends and low frequency cyclical movements, rather than short-term 
volatility, have been the main drivers of EU manufacturing activity, making its 
developments a useful signal for economic forecasting. Since 2000, manufacturing gross 
value added has fluctuated around an upward trend  (see Graph II.1.2) that accounts for three 
quarters of the total variance of the series (see Graph II.1.3). (32) However, even after removing the 
trend and adjusting for the sharp downward spike in 2020-Q2 associated with the COVID-19 
pandemic, the share of variance attributable to low-frequency movements (cycles longer than 8 
years) remains around 40% in the full sample, with only minor differences between the pre- and 
post-COVID periods. Fluctuations at frequencies between 10 and 32 quarters account for most of 
 
(30) Most of the drivers behind the weakness in EU manufacturing have already been extensively analysed in recent studies, 
see e.g.  Banin et al. (2025), Chiacchio et al. (2023), De Santis (2020), Fidora et al (2024). 
(31) Industrial production volume (IP) of the EU aggregates the output of each industry in each country with fixed weights, 
while GVA reflects the constant changes of these weights. Moreover, in contrast to GVA, IP does not account for certain 
activities such as services provided by industrial companies or contract manufacturing carried out in other countries. 
(32) Cyclical components are identified using spectral analysis based on the periodogram (Hamilton, 1994, Chapter 6), 
which decomposes the variation in manufacturing activity across different frequencies. Cyclical components are 
extracted using the Christiano–Fitzgerald (2003) asymmetric band-pass filter, which isolates fluctuations within 
predefined frequency bands. The analysis distinguishes short-term fluctuations (2–10 quarters), business-cycle 
movements (10–32 quarters), and longer cycles (32–106 quarters).

corresponds to the typical horizon of the Commission’s forecast. (33) 
 
 
 
Identifying historical drivers of manufacturing output with an econometric model 
Identifying the relative importance of demand- and supply-side drivers is essential to 
understanding manufacturing activity over time. To address this, a structural econometric 
model is used to distinguish between drivers and assess their contributions to manufacturing 
developments over time. The model includes five variables: manufacturing gross value added, 
manufacturing producer prices, hours worked in manufacturing, industrial production outside the 
euro area and the cost of borrowing for euro area firms (with the latter two serving as proxies for 
extra-EU demand and EU-wide financing conditions, respectively). (34)  The drivers of manufacturing 
activity are identified into five demand and supply groups using a structural econometric model 
(their estimated contributions to the recent evolution of variables are presented on Graph II.1.4). 
On the demand side,  
i) Financial shocks leading to more favourable financing conditions are assumed to reduce interest 
rates, increase manufacturing production, producer prices and hours worked.  
 
 
(33) Results are also robust when using the manufacturing production index, albeit with a lower average growth rate in the 
estimated linear trend than for manufacturing GVA. Further analysis (not reported here) shows that manufacturing 
activity is more closely associated with demand indicators at business-cycle frequencies and with production inputs and 
energy costs over longer horizons. The co-movement of manufacturing GVA and producer prices at business-cycle 
frequencies (cycles of up to eight years), and their inverse relationship at lower frequencies, suggest a stronger role for 
demand-side factors in the short term and supply-side factors in the long term. 
(34) A vector error correction model (VECM) is estimated for the period 2003Q1-2026Q2 including the five variables 
mentioned in the text, all expressed in natural logarithms except for the cost of borrowing in the euro area. The model 
is fitted on quarterly differences of the variables and includes a constant intercept term. This can be interpreted as 
allowing for a linear trend in level terms capturing the long-term historical growth path in manufacturing and the other 
variables included in the model. Recent developments are assessed against the same historical benchmark rather than 
introducing statistical breaks, for example around the global financial crisis, that would change the reference growth 
path and complicate the economic interpretation. Instead, persistent changes in manufacturing dynamics are captured 
through the model's residuals and reflected in the estimated contributions of the structural drivers over time. Structural 
shocks are identified using sign restrictions imposed on their impulse responses in the levels of the variables after 20 
quarters. This way, identification focuses on the long-term impact of each shock instead of the short-term response 
that might be contaminated by noise. Results remain unchanged if the restrictions are instead imposed 4 quarters 
ahead. The restricted impulse responses and the identified shocks are available upon request.

=== PAGE 109 ===
Special Issues 
 
93 
ii) Global demand shocks raise EU manufacturing output, producer prices and industrial production 
outside the euro area, with corporate borrowing costs rising as monetary policy reacts to stronger 
demand and price pressures. This shock captures demand developments that are common to the 
EU and the rest of the world, essentially the global industrial cycle.  
iii) The third shock captures EU performance relative to global production: a positive change in this 
driver raises EU manufacturing output, producer prices and corporate borrowing costs while 
lowering industrial production outside the euro area, meaning production shifts from the rest of 
the world towards the EU. This driver captures EU-specific demand shocks, and various factors that 
change the competitiveness of EU producers relative to foreign counterparts, such as exchange 
rate developments or productivity gains by foreign competitors.  
On the supply side,  
iv) (Hicks-)neutral supply shocks raise manufacturing output and reduce prices, with an associated 
rise in labour demand which raises hours worked.  
v) Labour substitution shocks also raise output and lower prices, but they are also associated with 
falling hours. Such shocks may capture labour-saving technology and automation, or substitution 
away from labour due to a change in relative input prices. For example, negative changes reflect 
situations when firms retain workers despite weaker output, which, unless wages adjust 
sufficiently downwards, raises unit labour costs and prices.  (35)   
On the demand side, the support from financing conditions and global demand has 
weakened in recent quarters, but some EU-specific factors offset part of this decline 
Financing conditions acted as a drag on manufacturing activity around 2023, consistent with the 
transmission of interest rate hikes in 2022-23. Their contractionary effects faded by 2025 but 
headwinds from financing conditions re-emerged in 2026. Other demand drivers, reflecting global 
demand conditions, supported activity throughout much of the period, with their contribution 
accelerating in 2024 alongside global industrial demand, but fading from the end of 2025. EU-
specific demand was subdued in 2023–2024, potentially reflecting the repercussions of Russia’s 
war of aggression in Ukraine (36) and heightened uncertainty, but has strengthened more recently, 
supported by resilient labour markets and the implementation of the Recovery and Resilience 
Facility. This component can also reflect recent improvements in export competitiveness – indeed, 
the global export market share of EU producers is projected to stabilise in 2026 after several years 
of decline, as exporters benefitted from the strong global demand for investment goods related to 
AI and electrification (see Statistical annex, Table 51).  
Supply-side factors had a negative influence on manufacturing performance between 
2019-23, and have been broadly neutral more recently. During the energy crisis in 2022, the 
price of capital, energy and other materials increased relative to the cost of labour, leading to an 
increasing labour intensity of production that is captured in the model by negative labour 
substitution shocks.  (37)  
 
 
(35) For a theoretical derivation and empirical analysis of these supply shocks, see Mumtaz and Zanetti (2012). 
(36) See Special issue 1 (The cost of EU Member States’ proximity to the war in Ukraine) in European Economic Forecast,

Graph II.1.4: 
Historical decomposition, EU 
  
Estimated contributions of the identified drivers to the five variables included in the structural econometric model; see footnote  (34) for details.
Transformed levels are logarithms for all variables except cost of borrowing, which is measured in original units. The baseline represents the 
trend estimated by the model over the period 2000Q3–2026Q2. Large negative values at the beginning of the period shown indicate that 
realised values were substantially below the estimated trend. (38)  
GVA decompositions for the large EU Member States 
The post-2022 stagnation in EU manufacturing reflects divergent developments across 
Member States (see Graph II.1.5). On the supply side, in Germany and, to a lesser extent, Poland, 
input costs and other supply conditions shifted from supportive until 2019 to exerting a persistent 
drag on manufacturing activity in recent years. This may partly reflect higher energy costs and the 
greater weight of energy-intensive industries in their manufacturing sectors. In other countries, the 
same energy shock appears to have had a less negative and persistent impact, possibly reflecting 
differences in industrial structure and energy sources, such as the greater reliance on nuclear 
power in France.  
 
 
(38) The historical contributions capture the combined effects of current and past shocks. For variables that are largely 
exogenous from an economic perspective, namely global industrial production excluding the euro area vis-à-vis euro 
area economies and firms' borrowing costs vis-à-vis the manufacturing sector, these contributions reflect estimated 
interrelationships rather than necessarily implying causality. Moreover, according to the restricted impulse responses, 
the EU relative performance shock initially has a positive impact on global industrial production before turning negative. 
This may reflect initial positive spillovers through production chains, followed by a gradual reallocation of demand 
towards EU-produced goods. Conversely, the labour productivity/substitution shock initially has a negative impact on 
global industrial production before turning positive. These sign reversals warrant caution when interpreting quarter-to-
quarter changes in historical contributions, as they may reflect the evolving effects of past shocks rather than 
contemporaneous changes in the underlying economic conditions.

Conclusions 
The historical decomposition highlights some of the sustained pressures constraining 
European manufacturing. The analysis takes a short- to medium-term perspective – looking at 
the period starting in 2019 – to identify the contributions of demand and supply shocks around the 
variables' estimated deterministic trends. It reveals that during the observed period, the weak 
performance of the manufacturing sector was shaped by persistent supply-side weaknesses 
counteracting cyclical demand support. Moving to the current year, the manufacturing sector is 
again confronted with unfavourable financing conditions, higher input costs and adverse supply 
conditions, largely reflecting the outbreak of the Middle East conflict and the subsequent energy 
shock. Global demand conditions also remain unfavourable, albeit with a gradually normalising 
impact on EU GVA. At the same time, a positive relative performance of EU manufacturing vis-à-
vis the global industrial trend is evident, following several quarters of deterioration. Labour 
substitution shocks also appear to be gaining strength, pointing to potential tensions in labour and 
capital input markets from tighter financing conditions on the relative cost and availability of 
capital and the resulting substitution between production factors.  
Competitiveness, energy prices and security, and innovation including AI deployment, 
hold the key to breaking EU manufacturing's structural constraints. Stronger 
competitiveness could help European manufacturers regain ground relative to global trends, while 
improved financing conditions and stronger domestic and external demand could support 
investment and activity. AI adoption offers opportunities to raise productivity through more 
efficient use of labour and capital, addressing the limited historical contribution of labour-related 
supply improvements. At the same time, affordable and secure energy supplies, alongside 
regulatory reforms and deeper market integration, could help reverse persistent supply-side 
weaknesses. The challenge is to transform temporary demand support into a sustained 
improvement in manufacturing competitiveness. 

=== PAGE 113 ===
2. FROM PANDEMIC TO ENERGY CRISES : PRICE 
DEVELOPMENTS, CONSUMPTION ADJUSTMENT AND COST 
OF LIVING DIFFERENTIALS ACROSS INCOME GROUPS IN 
THE EU 
97 
This special issue examines how prices and consumption patterns have evolved across EU Member 
States since 2019, with focus on households’ adjustment to successive shocks, including the 
pandemic and the two energy crises (40). It first reviews shifts in relative prices and allocation of 
expenditure for various product groups at EU level, to then examine differences in expenditure 
shares across income quintiles. In most Member States, particularly in Central and Eastern Europe, 
purchasing power has eroded more among households in the bottom than in the top income 
quintile, largely reflecting the greater weight of food and energy utility bills in their budgets. By 
contrast, the first seven months of the conflict in the Middle East have narrowed the inter-quintile 
inflation gap somewhat, as the initial pass-through of higher energy commodity prices has been 
concentrated in transport fuels, which weigh more heavily in higher-income households’ budgets. 
However, further pass-through to household energy bills and food prices could widen the gap 
again.   
Graph II.2.1: 
Relative prices of selected ECOICOP divisions 
  
Relative price indices are calculated as a ratio of the seasonally adjusted HICP index for a given division to the seasonally adjusted HICP 
headline index. Ratios have been rebased to 2019=100. Excess volatility in the underlying ratios was removed by taking three-month centred 
moving averages. 
The pandemic and successive energy shocks have left a lasting imprint on the relative 
prices of consumer goods and services. Price developments have been far from uniform 
across consumption categories (41) (see Graph II.2.1). Over 2020-2021, the introduction and 
subsequent lifting of pandemic-related restrictions altered relative prices through abrupt shifts in 
supply and demand. These effects were particularly pronounced for food, transport (i.e. mainly 
transport fuels) and various services, such as restaurants and accommodation. The energy crisis

triggered a more significant wave of relative price adjustments. The energy price shock initially 
drove up the relative prices of housing, water, electricity, gas and other fuels (42) (via prices of gas 
and electricity) and transport (via transport fuels), to then gradually pass through to food prices in 
2022-2023 and, subsequently, to a broader range of services (43). The effects of the current energy 
shock have so far been most visible in the relative prices of transport, after they had retreated 
from their spring 2022 peak until early 2026, and, to a lesser extent, housing . The latter two 
nevertheless remain significantly above their pre-2022-energy-crisis levels. Relative food prices 
have also declined, but only after several years of sustained increases that brought them to an all-
time high in early 2026.  
Longer-term structural forces have continued to shape price trends. Relative prices of 
services, which largely include non-tradable activities shielded from external competition, have 
kept rising. At the same time, intense competitive import pressures have pushed down the relative 
prices of many non-energy industrial goods (e.g. clothing and footwear, household furnishings and 
especially information and communication). All in all, between 2019 and 2026, the increase in 
absolute prices in the EU as a whole ranged from 41% (44) for food and housing, 39% for 
restaurant and accommodation services and 33% for transport, to just 11% for clothing and 
footwear. Absolute prices in information and communication fell by nearly 2%. Meanwhile, the 
total headline index grew by 31%. 
Graph II.2.2: 
HICP consumption weights for ECOICOP divisions 
   
Household spending patterns have changed too, through both constraints on 
consumption choices and changes in relative prices. Graph II.2.2 takes a longer-term 
perspective, placing recent shifts in consumption patterns in the EU (45) within a context of broader 
trends of the past twenty five years. Restrictions on mobility and social interaction sharply 
curtailed spending on contact-intensive services, notably restaurants and accommodation, as well 
as transport, while redirecting expenditure towards goods and services consumed at home (e.g. 
food, furnishings and information and communication). This contributed to the temporary increase 
in the expenditure shares of food and household furnishings. The pandemic also coincided with a 
rise in the share of spending on housing, likely reflecting—to some extent—increased time spent at

home. As restrictions were lifted, these shifts partly unwound, but the subsequent energy crisis 
brought another significant change in expenditure patterns. Higher energy prices raised the 
expenditure shares of housing and transport, while their indirect effects also contributed to the 
increase in the share of food. 
Rising living standards and relative prices have shifted household spending towards 
services. Falling relative prices of many consumer durables have led to a gradual reduction of the 
expenditure shares of clothing and footwear, household furnishings and information and 
communication. Conversely, higher living standards and higher prices have lifted the shares of 
expenditure devoted to restaurants and accommodation, especially after the pandemic. The 
gradual rise in the shares health and education reflects a combination of demographic trends, 
namely ageing, higher living standards and policy choices regarding provision and financing 
arrangements particularly in health care (46). Meanwhile, the expenditure share of housing has 
fallen from the peak recorded during the first energy shock, partly owing to the correction in 
energy prices, but also consumption restraint and saving (discussed in more detail in the next 
para). Finally, the impact of the current energy shock on household spending patterns is still not 
reflected in Graph II.2.2. (47)  
Graph II.2.3: 
Evolution of relative prices and consumption weights in the two main ECOICOP energy groups 
   
Changes in the expenditure shares of energy utility bills and transport fuels also point 
to significant adjustments in consumption since the 2021-23 energy crisis. A closer 
examination of relative price and expenditure share changes across the two main consumer energy 
ECOICOP groups—(i) electricity, gas and other fuels and (ii) transport fuels (48)—reveals significant 
shifts since 2021. (49) The spending share of electricity, gas and other fuels (Graph II.2.3.a) followed 
a gradual upward trend until 2022, broadly in line with rising relative prices. Since then, it has 
declined sharply despite only a modest correction in relative prices, suggesting a role for energy 
consumption restraint and the accelerated uptake of energy-efficient appliances, heating systems 
and household renewable electricity generation (50). Developments in transport fuels (Graph II.2.3.b)

Graph II.2.4: 
Expenditure shares by income quintiles 
  
EU27 for ECOICOP divisions, EU22 for ECOICOP groups (excluding Italy, Czechia, Portugal, Romania and Sweden). 
Lower-income households spend relatively more on food and the cost of running their 
homes, while higher-income households allocate more to transport fuels and less-
essential items. Household Budget Surveys (HBS) (51) complement the aggregate analysis by 
showing how expenditure patterns vary across income groups. Graph II.2.5 confirms that, 
consistent with Engel’s law, the budget shares of essential goods and services in the EU (52) tend to 
decline with income, while those of other items generally increase. Notably, food, most housing-
related goods and services—including actual rentals, electricity, gas and other fuels, and water 
supply—as well as transport services account for larger shares of expenditure among lower-
income households. (53) Conversely, higher-income households allocate larger shares to clothing 
and footwear, household furnishings, vehicles and transport fuels, as well as services such as 
restaurants and accommodation and recreation and culture. The same pattern applies to dwelling 
maintenance and repair and, notably, imputed rentals (54). The latter item, which is covered by the 
HBS but falls outside the scope of the HICP, is particularly noteworthy: averaged across income 
quintiles, its expenditure share is close to that of food and twice that of actual rentals. Moreover, 
its share rises markedly with income, pointing to higher homeownership rates among better-off 
households, and therefore representing the reverse of the pattern observed for actual rentals (see 
Graph II.2.4.b). 
Income-related differences in consumption patterns vary considerably across Member 
States. For most ECOICOP divisions and groups shown in Graph II.2.5, the gap between the first 
and fifth income quintiles differs markedly across countries (55). In many cases this gap ranges

weight in some Central and Eastern European countries, notably Bulgaria and Latvia. The reverse

expenditure shares of these items, broadly associated with essential living costs, tend to decline

Graph II.2.5: 
Normalised cross-country variation in expenditure share gaps between the bottom and top income quintiles, by 
COICOP division/group 
   
The variation (difference between the bottom and top quintile) is normalised by dividing by the average expenditure share for all quintiles in 
a given division/group and country. Range and average for EU27 (divisions) and EU22 (groups). 
In most Member States, particularly in Central and Eastern Europe, purchasing power 
has eroded more for households in the bottom income quintile than for those in the top 
quintile. Goods and services that have experienced pronounced changes in relative prices —such 
as e.g. gas and electricity, transport fuels, food, restaurants and accommodation (see Graphs II.2.1, 
II.2.3)—also tend to account for markedly different expenditure shares across income groups (see 
Graph II.2.4). As a result, households in different income quintiles may be exposed to inflation 
developments to a different extent. Graph II.2.6, showing the gaps in cumulative inflation between 
the bottom and top quintile since 2019, (56) confirms that this has been the case for most Member 
States. While the average gap in the EU (EU22) amounted to 1.8pps over 2019-2016, cross-
country variation is considerable. Notably, lowest-income households in CEE countries, but also 
Ireland, have been among the most affected by the pandemic and energy shocks over 2019-2026. 
In Estonia, the cumulative inflation differential reached 8.3 pps., while in Ireland and Latvia it was 
7.4 pps. and 6.8 pps., respectively. In Slovenia, Croatia, Poland and Lithuania it ranged between 6 
and 4.5 pps. By contrast, in several Member States, including Germany, France and Spain, better-

Inflation differences across income groups have evolved unevenly over time, with the 
first energy crisis marking a clear turning point, as sharply rising energy bills weighed 
more heavily on poorer households. Graph II.2.6 decomposes the interquintile gaps in 
cumulative inflation into contributions accrued over four distinct periods: (i) the pandemic (2020 to 
mid-2021), (ii) the energy crisis (mid-2021 to end-2022), (iii) the disinflation phase (January 2023 
to February 2026) and (iv) the renewed energy shock (March-September 2026). Despite abrupt 
changes in consumption patterns and relative prices, inflation developments during the pandemic 
were remarkably similar across income quintiles, with the exception of a few Member States (e.g. 
Lithuania, Poland, and Denmark). The bulk of the cumulative inflation gap emerged, unsurprisingly,  
between mid-2021 and end-2022, when steep increases in the prices of gas, electricity and other 
fuels and food weighed more heavily on poorer households, particularly across CEE, but also in 
Ireland (57). The gap widened further during the subsequent disinflation phase from 2023 onwards. 
This reflected the fact that, even as the household energy bill fell, persistently high food inflation 
continued to weigh disproportionately on lower-income households. Moreover, falling prices of 
transport fuels and broad-based disinflation in services benefitted higher-income households 
more. The most recent period, covering the period since the onset of the conflict in the Middle East, 
has so far brought some narrowing of the inter-quintile inflation gap in most Member States. This 
largely owes to the fact that high energy commodity prices had up to July mostly passed through 
to transport fuels, which weigh more heavily on higher-income households, whereas the response 
of retail gas and electricity and food price had been rather muted so far. (see Section I.7. Inflation). 
Graph II.2.6: 
Differences in cumulative inflation between the first and the fifth income quintiles, 2019-2026 
   
Inflation corresponding to the consumption basket of the first income quintile minus the inflation corresponding to the consumption basket of 
the fifth income quintile, cumulated since 2019. The cumulative-inflation dot refers to value for September 2026 (2019=100), while 
contributions mark the value of the difference at the end of each identified period. EU22 excludes Italy, Romania, Sweden, Czechia and 
Portugal because of data availability issues. 
Cross-country differences in inflation gaps reflect the interaction between country-
specific household expenditure patterns and price developments, largely for energy and 
rentals. Several goods and services stand out as key drivers of these differences. In particular, 
developments in electricity and gas prices help explain the high position in the ranking of Estonia, 
Ireland, Poland, Lithuania and Slovenia. They also account for the low ranking of Finland and 
 
(57) Ireland recorded one of the EU highest increases in prices for electricity, gas and other fuels during the energy crisis 
(from mid-2021 to end-2022). The effect on inflation differentials was magnified by the largest gap in the 
corresponding expenditure weight between the poorest and richest households among non-CEE euro area economies.

Slovakia. (58) Transport fuels constitute another crucial category for explaining Member States’

gaps in expenditure shares between the highest and lowest income quintile, coincided with strong 
increases in relative prices. Finally, actual rentals—which have one of the biggest weights (see

differing effects on inflation gaps, depending on the direction of relative price movements. In 
Ireland and Slovenia, where relative prices of rents rose sharply, they contributed to widening 
inflation gaps for lower-income quintiles. By contrast, in most Member States at the lower end of 
the ranking, including Luxembourg, Germany and Finland, actual rentals had the opposite effect 
reflecting the decline in their relative prices.

phases; whereas in Finland relatively high inflation in this group interacted with a higher share of related expenditure 
among the top quintile compared than among the bottom quintile—the only EU country showing this pattern (see 
GraphII.2.5).

1. BELGIUM 
 
108 
Subdued growth in 2026-27 
Following a rise by 0.2% q-o-q in 2026-Q1 and 
0.1% in 2026-Q2, real GDP further increased by 
0.3% in 2026-Q3. This outturn was mainly driven 
by resilient private consumption and growing 
exports. Despite higher inflation and slower 
growth in social benefits, consumer spending is 
set to remain robust, supported by a moderating 
household saving rate in 2026 and 2027 and 
wage indexation particularly in 2027. Tighter 
financial conditions are expected to weigh on 
investment in both 2026 and 2027. While higher 
mortgage 
rates 
and 
depressed 
business 
confidence in the construction sector point to 
weak residential investment in 2026, a mild 
recovery is projected from 2027 onwards, in line 
with an increase in the number of building permits issued. Exports are forecast to rise markedly in 
2026, supported by improved manufacturing conditions among trading partners and dynamic 
global trade. However, imports are also projected to increase due to higher import of defence-
related goods, leaving the contribution of net exports to GDP broadly neutral in 2026-27. In 2028, 
higher GDP growth is expected to be driven by stronger domestic demand, supported by easing 
inflation and rising employment growth. Export growth is set to moderate, driven by the expected 
slowdown in external demand. 
Overall, the economic activity is expected to grow by 0.7% in 2026 and 0.8% in 2027, before 
accelerating to 1.2% in 2028.  
Employment is set to gain momentum as of 2027   
Employment is set to remain stable in 2026, before accelerating as of 2027, underpinned by 
measures encouraging longer careers, including those contained in the pension reform which 
introduced financial disincentives for early retirement and bonuses for postponed retirement. 
Unemployment is expected to increase in 2026-27 as the number of active job-seekers initially 
outpaces job creation. However, steady employment growth is projected to reduce the 
unemployment rate to 6.3% in 2028. Two expected wage indexations in the first half of 2027 and 
one early 2028 are set to support nominal wage growth, although the impact is tempered by the 
cap on inflation-linked indexation of certain wages. Productivity is projected to remain subdued 
over the forecast horizon.  
Inflation is set to surge in 2026, before gradually easing  
Headline inflation is projected to rise to 3.6% in 2026, primarily reflecting higher energy prices. In 
2027, energy inflation is expected to ease. However, food inflation is set to increase, driven by this 
Belgium’s economic growth is set to slow to 0.7% in 2026 and to remain subdued at 0.8% in 
2027, as decreasing purchasing power and tighter financing conditions are projected to weigh 
on private consumption and investment. GDP growth is forecast to accelerate in 2028, 
supported by stronger domestic demand. After a projected surge to 3.6% in 2026, driven by 
higher energy prices, inflation is set to ease as of 2027. The general government deficit is 
expected to remain stable at 5.2% of GDP in 2026. However, based on the information 
available at the cut-off date for this forecast, the deficit is projected to rise in 2027 and 2028, 
reflecting higher interest and defence expenditure and is expected to increase the already high 
government debt-to-GDP ratio further.

Euro Area Member States, Belgium

year’s extreme weather events and higher fertiliser costs, and services inflation is also projected to 
rise on the back of higher wage growth as well as increased voucher and transport prices. Overall, 
HICP inflation is estimated at 3.2% in 2027 and 2.5% in 2028.   
High deficits facing increasing interest expenses  
In 2026, the general government deficit is expected to remain stable at 5.2% of GDP compared to 
2025. A 0.3% of GDP decrease in primary expenditure is expected to be fully offset by an increase 
in interest expenses. 
Due to lengthy budgetary discussions, Belgium has not submitted a federal budget for 2027 
before the cut-off date of the forecast, and the forecast assumes unchanged policies. However, 
the 2027 fiscal projections include the expected impact from regional measures. The deficit is 
projected to increase to 5.5% of GDP in 2027 despite measures taken by the regional governments 
to contain current spending and increase revenue. Higher expenditure on interest payments and 
defence is only partly offset by reductions in social benefits and subsidies at all government levels. 
Expenditure in social benefits is expected to stabilise, reflecting earlier reforms to unemployment, 
long-term sickness and pensions. At the same time, revenue as a percentage of GDP is projected to 
remain stable, supported by higher receipts from VAT collection, the regional road vignette and the 
Flemish registration tax.  
In 2028, the deficit is forecast to widen to 5.7% of GDP, despite declining primary expenditure. 
Interest payments are projected to continue rising by 0.2% of GDP, partly offsetting savings made 
in public-sector wages and social benefits resulting from slower growth in the number of public-
sector employees and partial wage indexation. Defence spending is projected to increase gradually 
from 1.6% of GDP in 2026 to 1.9% in 2028, taking into account the delivery timelines for military 
equipment. Revenues are projected to decrease by 0.4% of GDP, due mainly to lower personal 
income taxes and slightly lower corporate income taxes. 
General government gross debt is expected to reach 111.2% of GDP at the end of 2026. The 
structurally high general government deficits are the main driver of the projected increase in the 
debt-to-GDP ratio to 115.8% by 2028.

2. BULGARIA 
 
110 
Domestic demand slows down as real income growth eases 
Real GDP growth remained strong in the first half 
of 2026, supported by increases in private 
consumption. Net exports contributed negatively 
to growth, mainly reflecting strong import growth 
driven by robust domestic demand. GDP growth is 
expected to moderate over the forecast horizon. 
Private consumption growth is expected to slow 
down in the second half of 2026. Over 2027-28, 
annual consumption growth is projected to be 
more moderate, as real disposable income eases 
on the back of weaker public and private wage 
growth and elevated inflation. Investment growth 
is projected to weaken in 2027 before recovering 
in 2028. This can be mostly attributed to the 
public investment profile, as the expiry of the RRF 
is set to temporarily weigh on public investment in 2027. Growth in exports of goods and services 
is projected to remain negative in 2026, due to subdued growth in the tourism sector, and a 
slowdown in the exports of goods such as vehicles and other investment goods. Exports are 
forecast to strengthen in 2027 and 2028, though subject to downside risks associated with the car 
manufacturing value chain. The current account deficit is projected to widen further in 2026 before 
narrowing over the forecast horizon. 
Wage growth to ease further despite tight labour market 
Wage growth showed signs of easing in 2025, with compensation per employee increasing by 
5.8%, down from 14.1% in 2024, mainly driven by strong increases in the public sector. Wage 
growth is expected to moderate further over the forecast horizon. This reflects weaker labour 
demand and thereby lower wage pressure in the private sector as well as lower public sector wage 
growth. Employment growth showed signs of weakening in the first half of 2026. This trend is 
expected to continue as growth slows down. The unemployment rate is set to remain broadly 
unchanged over the forecast horizon. Labour shortages are projected to persist in key sectors, 
amid demographic pressures and a shrinking working-age population. 
Inflation to remain elevated until 2027, driven by high energy and services prices 
HICP inflation is forecast to reach 4.6% in 2026 and 3.9% in 2027, reflecting higher energy prices 
following the conflict in the Middle East, which are projected to remain high in 2027. Services 
inflation has also been high, driven by rising input costs, notably for energy and labour. It is 
expected to remain elevated into 2027 before showing signs of easing towards the end of the 
forecast horizon.  Food inflation is expected to be positively influenced by second-round effects 
from higher energy prices, although base effects from high unprocessed food inflation in the first 
half of 2026 will dampen the overall impact in 2027. Inflation is projected to decline in 2028. 
Real GDP growth is expected to remain robust in 2026, supported by strong domestic demand. 
Growth is projected to moderate in 2027 and 2028 as higher prices weigh on consumer demand, 
wage growth decelerates and investment slows down following the peak in RRF-supported 
spending in 2026. Driven by high energy and services prices, inflation is projected to remain 
elevated in 2026 and in the first half of 2027, before moderating thereafter. The general 
government deficit is set to reach 5% of GDP in 2026 and to temporarily decrease to 4% of GDP 
in 2027, while the debt-to-GDP ratio is expected to increase and reach 37.7% in 2028.

Euro Area Member States, Bulgaria 
 
111 
While the factors currently driving inflation are expected to weaken, they are set to be partly offset 
by the introduction of ETS2.  
Deficit to reach 5% of GDP in 2026  
The general government deficit is expected to reach 5.0% of GDP in 2026, up from 4.0% of GDP in 
2025, driven by increases in expenditure due to a combination of sustained social spending—
largely driven by pensions—, and increases in public sector salaries. Strong increases are also 
expected in public investment on municipal projects, in expenditure on subsidies and on 
intermediate consumption. Discretionary revenue measures have a limited positive impact, which is 
largely offset by a 5% salary indexation on the expenditure side. There are downside risks to the 
deficit forecast linked to a possible statistical revision of 2026 deficit figures. 
In 2027, the deficit is set to temporarily decrease to 4% of GDP, mostly driven by the revenue side. 
It comes on the back of a windfall tax on selected sectors—currently planned for one year— of 
measures to increase revenue from VAT and excises, and of increases in the maximum and 
minimum thresholds for the payment of social security contributions. By contrast, a planned 
increase in the threshold for VAT registration and two new packages of tax relief measures have a 
revenue decreasing impact. On the expenditure side, compensation of employees is set to further 
moderate, also most other expenditure items are expected to grow less dynamically compared to 
2025. Defence expenditure is forecast to increase given planned deliveries of equipment, while 
pension spending is set to be driven by automatic indexations.  
Following the expiry of the temporary windfall tax, the deficit is projected to widen again. This 
reflects the persistent trend of revenue growth lagging behind expenditure dynamics, in the 
absence of structural measures to permanently strengthen revenue. As a result, the deficit is 
expected to return to 4.8% of GDP in 2028.  
Gross public debt is forecast to increase from 29.9% in 2025 to around 37.7% of GDP in 2028, 
largely driven by large and persistent primary deficits and rising interest costs.

=== PAGE 128 ===
3. GERMANY 
 
112 
Short-term growth supported by exports and fiscal policy  
Since the COVID-19 pandemic, Germany’s recovery has been among the weakest in advanced 
economies. In 2025, relatively strong consumption growth was offset by weak exports and private 
investments, partly reflecting competition from China and the effects of US tariffs. This left 
economic activity broadly stagnant. In late 2025, the economy began to regain momentum, 
supported by the ramp-up in public spending. This upswing has continued into 2026, despite the 
conflict in the Middle East. The composition of growth is, however, expected to change significantly. 
Higher energy bills are squeezing household 
budgets and confidence, restraining private 
consumption growth. Public spending on the other 
hand, that is public consumption, investment and 
transfers, is expected to support growth in both 
2026 and 2027. From 2027 onwards, planned 
consolidation measures are set to dampen this 
growth effect. Investment is expected to recover, 
driven by defence-related equipment investment, 
and to a lesser degree recovering private 
investment. Residential construction is likely to 
bottom out in 2027 and recover only slowly 
thereafter, as improving permits feed through 
while building and financing costs remain high. 
After contracting for three years, exports drove 
growth in 2026-H1. Energy-intensive products such as chemicals have been in high demand. This 
is likely related to inventory build-ups and trade diversion effects as some competing producers 
were more affected by the conflict in the Middle East. In addition, some industries are set to profit 
from the global AI investment boom, which should support exports. However, structural challenges 
are set to persist, and export market shares are expected to decline again in 2027 and 2028. Trade 
is forecast to contribute to growth in 2026, be neutral in 2027 and detract from growth in 2028.  
Real GDP is projected to grow by 1.3% in 2026, 1.0% in 2027 and 0.7% in 2028. 
Demographic ageing is accelerating and structural change is leaving its mark  
Low capacity utilisation dampens labour demand, leading to declining employment. Job losses in 
manufacturing are expected to continue and only be partially offset by gains in public services, 
such as health, care and defence. The unemployment rate is set to rise to 4.0% in 2026, and to 
remain broadly at that level until 2028. As the baby boomers increasingly reach retirement age 
and net migration decreases, the working-age population is expected to shrink, detracting from 
employment and GDP growth but also limiting the rise in unemployment. Rising inflation in 2026 
and 2027 is set to slow down real wage growth. As inflation subsides, it is expected to resume. 
After several years of stagnation, the economy is set to expand by 1.3% in 2026, 1.0% in 2027 
and 0.7% in 2028. This acceleration is supported mainly by a surge in exports in early 2026, 
which is set to further benefit in 2027 from the global AI boom, and from an expansion in 
defence and infrastructure expenditure. Meanwhile, increased uncertainty and high inflation are 
dampening private consumption, and rising labour, energy and financing costs are holding back 
private investment. As exports and the fiscal impulse weaken in 2028, growth is forecast to 
decelerate. The general government deficit is expected to widen over the forecast horizon, as 
public infrastructure investment and defence expenditure gain momentum, but the pace of the 
fiscal expansion will moderate due to consolidation measures, notably in social security. The debt-
to-GDP ratio is projected to increase from 62.8% in 2025 to 68.9% in 2028.

Euro Area Member States, Germany 
 
113 
High energy prices drive inflation 
Inflation is projected to pick up again, reaching 2.9% in 2026 and peaking at 3.2% in 2027 before 
dropping to 2.0% in 2028. The conflict in the Middle East caused a sharp increase in fuel prices, 
driving energy inflation. Wholesale gas and electricity prices also spiked; household prices are set 
to react mainly in 2027 as contracts adjust. Wage growth is set to keep services inflation elevated, 
while some policy measures, such as increases in certain taxes and in co-payments for medicines, 
are expected to further add to inflation. 
The degree of fiscal expansion moderates as of 2027 
Fiscal policy is expected to be expansionary in 2026. The general government deficit is projected to 
rise to 4.1% of GDP in 2026, up from 2.9% in 2025. The expansion is largely driven by the 
expenditure side through social benefits. Growth in pension expenditure reflects the strong wage 
growth of recent years and the increase in retirees, while cost increases in the health and long-
term care systems add to the pressure on public budgets. Infrastructure-related public investment 
in 2026 is set to be significantly below what was expected following the creation of the 
infrastructure and climate fund in 2025, largely due to implementation delays. At the same time, 
defence expenditure is growing steadily, benefiting from the reform of the national debt brake. 
Despite the cyclical upswing, revenue growth is slowing down in 2026. The slowdown can be partly 
attributed to smaller increases in health insurance contribution rates compared to 2025, to tax 
relief measures such as relaxed depreciation rules for firms, and a tax rebate on fuels. 
In 2027 and 2028, fiscal policy is expected to be less expansionary than in 2026. Deficits are 
expected to continue widening (to 4.4% and 4.5% of GDP respectively) but at a slowing pace. 
Infrastructure-related investment is set to gain momentum and social expenditure to stay 
elevated, partly due to new pension entitlements for mothers. Consolidation efforts are projected 
to partly offset this expansion. On the expenditure side, consolidation take place in the statutory 
social security system, particularly in healthcare. Announced higher excise tax rates in 2027 and 
pension contribution rates in 2028 are expected to raise revenues, as will the non-indexation of 
income tax brackets. The latter effect is not expected to be fully offset by the income tax reform. 
Public debt is forecast to rise from 62.8% of GDP in 2025 to 68.9% in 2028, driven by the deficits.

=== PAGE 130 ===
4. ESTONIA 
 
114 
Established recovery but moderate growth 
Estonia’s economy continued to expand in the 
first half of 2026, strongly driven by inventory 
accumulation, 
while 
other 
components 
of 
domestic demand made only an overall small 
contribution. Several growth-supporting policies 
are in place, such as a tax-free allowance, a 
higher minimum wage and substantial fiscal 
spending, yet their impact on economic activity 
has so far been contained. Higher loan servicing, 
fuel and energy costs started weighing on 
households spending in the second half of the 
year. Real wages are set to continue rising, 
supporting consumption growth in 2027 and 
2028, though an ageing population allocating a 
larger share of its income to savings constrains it. 
Investment remained weak in the first half of the year, despite robust corporate balance sheets 
and planned large-scale public investments, including in defence. An acceleration is expected in the 
second half of 2026, while some public projects, e.g. Rail Baltica, are extended to 2027-28 and 
beyond. Higher imports will accompany this expansion, as most defence products are sourced from 
abroad. 
Export performance was muted in the first half of 2026. Demand in traditional markets, such as 
the Nordic countries, remained weak, but businesses increased their exports towards Lithuania, 
Latvia, Poland and Germany over the last few years. Elevated oil and gas prices made locally 
produced shale fuel oil and natural gas profitable to export. Without a correction in energy 
markets, these exports are expected to continue. Meanwhile the exports in the ICT sector, a key 
services’ exporter, declined. In 2027 and 2028, the revival of the Finnish and Scandinavian 
economies is set to lift Estonia’s exports.  
Real GDP in 2026 is expected to grow by 2.2%, followed by 2.1% in 2027, and 2.3% in 2028.  
Inactivity and shrinking labour force reduce unemployment  
The unemployment rate declined to 6.6% in the second quarter of 2026 despite weak employment 
dynamics, as rising inactivity—particularly due to illness and disability—reduced the labour force. In 
third quarter, unemployment indicators diverged—the Eurostat seasonally adjusted rate was 
picking up while registered unemployment continued to decline. The unemployment rate is 
projected to decline from 7.1% in 2026 to 6.9% in 2027 and 6.8% in 2028, amid shrinking labour 
force. Nominal wage growth moderated to 5.6% in the second quarter of 2026 and is projected to 
remain below 5% in 2027 and 2028.  
Temporary factors lower inflation but energy pressures build 
HICP inflation decreased to 2.1% in the third quarter of 2026. This reflected the drop-out of last 
year's VAT rate increase from the calculations, a good regional harvest that lowered food prices, 
The Estonian economy is projected to grow steadily—by 2.2% in 2026, 2.1% in 2027 and 2.3% in 
2028. Consumption and investment are supported by favourable conditions, while export 
prospects are improving. While HICP inflation is set to moderate to 3.1% in 2026, it is expected to 
increase again in 2027 to 3.6% due to rising energy and services prices, before decelerating to 
2.4% in 2028. The government deficit is set to widen to 4.4% of GDP in 2026, reflecting tax cuts 
and spending increases, before narrowing to 3.9% in 2027 and 2028.

weaker services inflation (partly because the medical fee increase was no longer included in the

are projected to drive inflation up over the next quarters, until their impact dissipates by mid-2027. 
Inflation is projected at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.6% in 2027. In 2028, it is set to decline to 2.4%, as 
energy prices are expected to contribute negatively. Services prices are forecast to become the 
main driver amid a tighter labour market and growing wages.  
The government deficit is set to widen, reflecting tax cuts and spending increases 
The general government deficit is projected to increase from 2.0% of GDP in 2025 to 4.4% of GDP 
in 2026. The universal tax-free income allowance is expected to reduce personal income tax 
revenue by around 1.2% of GDP in 2026. Meanwhile, continued growth in labour incomes and 
recovering private consumption support tax receipts, alongside the carry-over effect of the VAT 
rate increase in July 2025. On the expenditure side, gross fixed capital formation is projected to 
rise by around 1.2% of GDP, driven by infrastructure projects such as Rail Baltica. Higher wages, 
notably for teachers and internal security personnel, are also set to increase the deficit. Total 
defence spending is forecast to increase by 0.8 pps. of GDP, reaching close to 5.0% of GDP in 
2026. 
In 2027, the deficit is forecast to narrow to 3.9% of GDP. Revenue growth is expected to pick up, 
supported by stronger private consumption and continued—albeit slower—growth in labour 
incomes. Higher energy excise revenue is expected to offset income tax refunds to taxpayers 
whose tax-free allowance was not fully applied during 2026. On the expenditure side, lower public 
investment is projected to support the deficit reduction, partly offset by a rise in defence spending 
and an increase in the public wage bill as a share of GDP. 
In 2028, the deficit is projected to remain at 3.9% of GDP, as higher interest expenditure offsets 
an improvement in the primary balance. Intermediate consumption is set to increase, while slower 
growth in the public wage bill is expected to help contain expenditure. Public investment is forecast 
to rise moderately, and defence spending is set to reach 5.2% of GDP.    
Public debt is projected to rise from 24.0% of GDP in 2025 to 32.1% in 2028, driven by high 
deficits.

=== PAGE 132 ===
5. IRELAND 
 
116 
Domestic economy maintains growth momentum, while GDP volatility persists 
Ireland’s real GDP fell by 7% y-o-y in the first 
half of 2026, mainly due to a decline in 2026-Q1 
driven by base effects from the pharmaceutical 
export surge in early 2025. GDP rebounded in the 
second quarter of the year as exports recovered, 
and preliminary GDP estimates point to strong 
growth in the third quarter. The domestic 
economy remained robust. It was underpinned by 
resilient household consumption despite higher 
inflation, and by strong modified investment 
(which excludes the more volatile intangible and 
aircraft leasing components), particularly in 
technology-related assets by multinational firms. 
Overall, following the surge seen in 2025, GDP is 
projected to contract by 0.4% in 2026, before 
growing again by 3.9% in 2027 and 3.6% in 2028. Modified domestic demand - a less volatile 
indicator of domestic economic activity in Ireland - is set to expand by 3.9% in 2026, 3.0% in 2027 
and 3.5% in 2028. 
Private consumption is expected to keep growing, underpinned by a resilient labour market and 
substantial household savings. Modified investment is also projected to remain strong, supported 
by continued technology and AI-related investment, residential construction growth and the 
government's National Development Plan. Headline investment fell sharply in the first half of 
2026, largely due to base effects from a spike in intellectual property imports a year earlier. The 
forecast for headline investment incorporates a technical assumption that volatile R&D-related 
intellectual property investment will remain broadly unchanged in the coming quarters. 
Exports are projected to support growth, driven by sustained demand for pharmaceuticals 
produced in Ireland, AI-related trade flows and robust computer service exports. Ireland’s high 
concentration of activity in a few multinational-dominated sectors leaves the economy exposed to 
firm and sector-specific shocks as well as adverse international developments. 
Employment growth moderates 
Employment continued to grow in the first half of 2026, albeit at a slower pace than in previous 
years, while the unemployment rate increased slightly from low levels. These developments point 
to an easing of labour market conditions. However, divergences between labour market data 
sources add uncertainty to the assessment of underlying conditions.  Employment is expected to 
keep growing over the forecast horizon, though at a slower pace, supported by sustained growth in 
the domestic economy, while the unemployment rate is projected to remain at around 5%. 
Inflationary pressures remain elevated amid high energy prices  
HICP inflation averaged 3.2% y-o-y in the first three quarters of 2026, partly driven by higher 
global energy prices. Inflation is expected to remain elevated, reflecting high energy prices, 
Ireland’s GDP is expected to contract mildly by 0.4% in 2026, mainly due to base effects from the 
pharmaceutical sector’s exceptional growth in early 2025. It is projected to grow again by 3.9% in 
2027 and 3.6% in 2028, supported by continued export growth. Domestic economic activity is set 
to keep expanding, driven by resilient household consumption and robust investment. Inflation is 
expected to remain elevated amid high energy prices, before easing in 2028. Public finances are 
set to stay in surplus, though the outlook is marked by continued risks to corporation tax revenue.

sustained services inflation and the pass-through of higher energy costs to other components of

prices. Overall, headline inflation is forecast to reach 3.4% in 2026 and 3.1% in 2027, before 
moderating to 2.2% in 2028 as energy price pressures are expected to gradually ease. 
Public finances are set to stay in surplus 
Ireland's general government is set to register a fiscal surplus of 1.4% of GDP in 2026. Robust 
domestic economic activity supported strong growth in tax receipts in the first three quarters of 
the year. While growth in corporate profits is projected to moderate this year, corporation tax 
revenue is expected to grow further thanks to first receipts of the domestic top-up tax, which 
applies a minimum effective tax rate of 15% for large corporate groups. 
The surplus is projected to remain at 1.4% of GDP in 2027 and decline to 0.6% of GDP in 2028, as 
expenditure growth is forecast to outpace growth in revenue. The strong growth in expenditure 
reflects projected increases in public sector pay, social transfers and capital investment, the latter 
contingent on reforms planned under the Accelerating Infrastructure Action Plan. On the revenue 
side, corporation tax revenue is set to benefit from additional top-up tax receipts of around EUR 3 
bn in 2027, of which around half is expected to unwind in 2028. This can be explained by a timing 
factor, as the top-up tax legislation allows for an extended filing deadline for first-time payers, and 
as a result in 2027 some companies are expected to file double tax returns for the two preceding 
fiscal years. Revenue from other tax categories is set to maintain a solid growth momentum on the 
back of an expanding domestic economy, while a slowdown is expected for VAT receipts as the 
reduced rate for the hospitality sector takes a full-year effect in 2027. Energy support measures, 
totalling around 0.2% of GDP in 2026, are assumed to be phased out throughout 2027.   
The general government debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to decrease from 33.5% in 2026 to 32.2% 
in 2027 and 32% in 2028. The debt ratio is expected to fall more gradually than implied by 
headline budget surpluses, mainly reflecting cash transfers to the Future Ireland Funds. 
The concentrated nature of Ireland's corporation tax revenue remains a risk and makes Ireland's 
public finances vulnerable to changes in US trade and tax policies as well as shifts in the 
international tax environment.

=== PAGE 134 ===
6. GREECE 
 
118 
GDP growth is set to moderate, but remain above the euro area average 
In the first half of 2026, the Greek economy expanded by 1.9% y-o-y, driven by broad-based 
growth of domestic demand. 
Investment activity is expected to remain buoyant 
in the second half of 2026, underpinned by a 
record-high inflow of EU funds through the RRF. 
However, the rise in energy prices is set to weigh 
on household consumption, even if this impact is 
mitigated by recent energy support measures and 
reductions in the tax wedge, introduced in 2026 
and scheduled for 2027. Although Greece 
remains highly dependent on energy imports, the 
trade balance is supported by the export of 
refined oil products and by the widening price 
difference between refined oil products and crude 
oil (crack spread). This improves the terms of 
trade and increases the GDP deflator in 2026. 
Looking beyond 2026, elevated energy costs, 
tighter financing conditions and the gradual winding down of the RRF are expected to dampen 
investment. Overall, GDP growth is forecast to ease to around 1.8% in 2026 and to 1.5% in both 
2027 and 2028, bringing Greece’s GDP growth closer to the euro-area average.  
Unemployment remains on a downward path 
The labour market kept expanding in early 2026, with the unemployment rate falling to 8.3% in 
the second quarter. The long-term unemployment rate also declined to 4.3%, although it remained 
the highest in the EU. Vacancy rates remained high, signalling a tight labour market, particularly in 
tourism. Employment is expected to grow, albeit at a modest pace due to structural impediments 
such as skill gaps and limited access to child and elderly care, as well as moderating economic 
activity. The unemployment rate is projected to decline further to 7.7% by 2028. Wage growth is 
set to moderate over the forecast horizon, as the inflationary shock fades away. 
Energy price shock drives inflationary developments 
The energy crisis triggered an increase in inflation, which averaged 3.8% in the third quarter of 
2026. In a context of strong demand and a tight labour market, the increase in energy prices is 
expected to pass through to non-energy goods and services prices, keeping inflation elevated in 
2027. As a result, inflation is forecast to increase to 4.1% in 2026 and then to moderate to 3.2% 
in 2027. In 2028, inflation is forecast to further decline to 2.4% as the effect of energy price 
shocks phases out and domestic demand moderates. 
 Economic growth in Greece is expected to moderate to 1.8% in 2026, as the energy price shock 
reduces households’ real income and restrains consumption growth. Investment, however, is 
expected to remain strong, bolstered by the absorption of EU funds. GDP growth is forecast to 
ease further to around 1.5% in 2027 and 2028 as the implementation of the RRF winds down. 
Driven by the surge in energy prices, inflation is expected to rise to 4.1% in 2026, and to decline 
gradually to 2.4% by 2028. Unemployment is set to fall, albeit more slowly than in the past, to 
7.7% in 2028. Greece’s fiscal position is projected to maintain sizeable primary surpluses, 
although continued expansionary measures are set to gradually reduce their size over the 
forecast horizon. The debt-to-GDP ratio is set to remain on a downward path, declining to 126.6% 
by the end of 2028.

=== PAGE 135 ===
Euro Area Member States, Greece 
 
119 
Fiscal surpluses moderate amid expansionary measures 
The general government surplus is projected to decline from 2.0% of GDP in 2025 to 0.9% of GDP 
in 2026, corresponding to a reduction in the primary surplus from 5.1% to 4.1% of GDP. The 
decrease in the headline surplus reflects the impact of expansionary fiscal measures, partly 
compensated by continued robust revenue growth. Fiscal measures excluding energy support are 
set to have an incremental budgetary cost of around 0.8% of GDP in 2026. These mainly comprise 
personal income tax cuts, higher public sector wages and enhanced support for pensioners and 
people with disabilities. Energy support measures to mitigate high energy prices for households 
and businesses amount to 0.3% of GDP in 2026, with most of them assumed to be discontinued in 
2027. 
In 2027, the general government surplus is forecast to moderate to 0.7% of GDP, while the 
primary surplus is set to decline to 3.7% of GDP. Fiscal measures are expected to have an 
additional net budgetary cost of around 0.6% of GDP. These include further public sector pay 
increases, a EUR 500 Christmas benefit for public sector employees and a 0.5 pps. reduction in 
social security contributions. Additional costs stem from tax relief for families with many children, 
farmers and the self-employed, as well as from a reduction in the business levy for legal entities. 
The forecast also includes a EUR 95 million subsidy to the manufacturing sector for increased 
electricity cost of 2026. These effects are set to be partly offset by favourable revenue 
developments, including gains from the extension of the digital labour card to new sectors and 
other smaller revenue increasing measures.  
In 2028, the headline surplus is projected to narrow further to 0.2% of GDP, while the primary 
surplus declines to 3.4%. Fiscal measures are expected to have a further net cost of around 0.1% 
of GDP, mainly reflecting the full-year impact of previously adopted measures while interest 
expenditure is set to increase by 0.2% of GDP. 
General government debt declined to 140.8% of GDP by June 2026, almost 50 pps. below its pre-
COVID-19 peak in 2018. The ratio is set to decline further, to 126.6% by 2028, supported by 
strong nominal GDP growth, budget surpluses, and the use of cash reserves for debt repayments.

=== PAGE 136 ===
7. SPAIN 
 
120 
Real GDP is expected to remain strong in 2026, at 2.6%, but to decelerate gradually over the 
forecast horizon, reaching 2.1% in 2027 and 1.9% in 2028, as employment growth loses 
momentum and the RRF support winds down. Economic activity is set to be driven by domestic 
demand. Private consumption, supported by a further decline in the household saving rate, and to a 
lesser extent investment, are expected to remain the main engines of growth. Led by the surge in 
energy prices, HICP inflation is projected to rise to 3.9% in 2026, before easing to 3.3% in 2027. 
The government deficit is set to increase in 2026 to 2.6% of GDP and fall to 2.2% in 2028. The 
debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to drop below 100% over the forecast horizon. 
Economic activity to remain robust, albeit decelerating over the forecast horizon 
In the third quarter of 2026, real GDP expanded 
by 0.6% q-o-q, supported by private consumption 
and by a marginally positive contribution from net 
exports. 
Despite 
the 
uncertain 
geopolitical 
environment and the drag exerted by elevated 
energy prices, economic activity is set to remain 
buoyant in 2026. Real GDP is forecast to grow by 
2.6% this year, also underpinned by strong carry-
over from 2025. Thereafter, it is set to moderate 
to 2.1% in 2027 and to 1.9% in 2028 as the pace 
of job creation loses momentum and RRP-
financed investment winds down. 
Domestic demand is set to lead economic growth 
over the next years, driven mainly by private 
consumption and, to a lesser extent, investment. Consumer spending is expected to be underpinned 
by continued, yet moderating, employment growth, in a context of inward migration, and by a 
further decline in the household saving rate. Similarly, the healthy financial position of non-
financial corporations and the execution of the RRP are projected to sustain gross fixed capital 
formation in 2026, particularly in construction and intangible assets. Investment is then set to 
decelerate in 2027 and 2028. The drag from net exports on GDP growth is set to fade gradually, 
from 0.3 pps. in 2026 to a neutral contribution in 2028. Export growth is expected to pick up on the 
back of a modest improvement in external demand, while imports slow in line with domestic 
demand. 
The main risks faced by the economy concern mainly the potential impact of persistent bottlenecks 
in the housing market. A continued shortfall in supply relative to household formation would keep 
price pressures elevated and could increasingly constrain labour mobility and the inflows of 
workers. At the same time, rising construction costs could further weigh on residential investment. 
In the labour market, nominal wage growth combined with weak productivity growth could keep 
unit labour costs rising faster than in the euro area, with a more lasting pass-through to services 
prices and an erosion of external competitiveness. On the upside, a faster decline in the household 
saving rate, currently above its historical average, could sustain consumption beyond the baseline. 
Dynamic labour market and declining unemployment 
Strong migration inflows, reinforced by the recent extraordinary regularisation of migrants, are set 
to support a further expansion of the workforce and uphold job creation in 2026. The labour 
market is projected to lose momentum over the forecast horizon, in line with the deceleration of 
economic growth. Employment is expected to grow by 2.3% in 2026 and 1.8% in 2027, while 
hours worked increase overall at a slower pace. The unemployment rate is projected to continue 
declining to 9.9% in 2026, 9.6% in 2027 and 9.4% 2028.

=== PAGE 137 ===
Euro Area Member States, Spain 
 
121 
Inflation to rise in 2026 due to higher energy prices 
HICP inflation is projected to rise in 2026, to 3.9%, driven by the increase in energy prices. This 
compounds persistent price pressures in services and the gradual pass-through of higher energy 
prices to food and industrial goods. As a result, HICP inflation excluding energy and food is set to 
keep rising in the coming quarters and to remain elevated in 2027. Headline inflation is expected 
to ease to 3.3% in 2027, as energy base effects unwind, and to 2.3% in 2028. The broad-based 
increase in prices could feed into higher-than-anticipated nominal wage growth over 2026-27. 
Government deficit to increase in 2026 due to cost of policy measures  
In 2026, the general government deficit is expected to rise slightly to 2.6% of GDP, driven by the 
fiscal cost (estimated at 0.5% of GDP) of measures to cushion the economic effects of the conflict 
in the Middle East. On the revenue side, these include VAT cuts on fuels, electricity and gas, a lower 
excise duty on hydrocarbons, and the suspension of the tax on electricity production. On the 
expenditure side, they comprise direct support for farmers, transport operators, electro-intensive 
firms, and vulnerable households. Temporary flood-relief measures for Andalusia and Extremadura 
add a further 0.3% of GDP. Revenue, particularly from direct taxation, remains strong. It is 
underpinned by high growth in capital gains and taxes on income driven by the deferred rise in 
public wages from 2025. However, the positive revenue developments are not expected to fully 
compensate for the different spending measures. 
Due to the snap election being called, the draft 2027 budget has not been presented, and the 
forecast assumes unchanged policies. In 2027, the deficit is forecast to decline to 2.2% of GDP, on 
the back of the lower projected cost of flood-related emergency measures—compared to 2026—
and the assumed expiry of energy-support measures. Along with the higher revenue from direct 
taxation and social security contributions, these factors will more than offset increases in defence 
spending, interest payments and pensions. After an expansionary fiscal stance in 2026, fiscal 
policy is set to become contractionary in 2027 following the end of the RRF implementation period. 
In 2028, the deficit is expected to stabilise, as higher revenue from social contributions and taxes 
on income and wealth are set to be offset by higher expenditure on pensions, interest and defence. 
The debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to keep falling to 99.2% in 2026, dropping below 100% for the 
first time since 2019, thanks to nominal GDP growth outpacing the cost of debt servicing. The debt 
is expected to keep narrowing, also supported by a projected primary surplus.

=== PAGE 138 ===
8. FRANCE 
 
122 
Rising energy prices and fiscal adjustment weigh on demand  
Real GDP growth is expected to decrease to 0.4% 
in 2026. In the first three quarters of 2026, 
economic activity was dragged down by a drop in 
residential investment, following the suspension 
of subsidies for renovation, and weaker public 
investment. In addition, exports were hit by supply 
bottlenecks in aeronautics in 2026-Q3. Still, net 
exports are projected to contribute 0.1 pps. to 
GDP growth in 2026. Higher energy prices and 
headline inflation are weighing on private 
consumption, which is expected to remain 
subdued over the year, despite a small decline in 
the saving rate to a still high rate of 17.3%. On 
the production side, lower agricultural output due 
to the summer exceptional heatwaves and 
drought also detracted from growth. 
In 2027, economic activity is projected to gain some momentum, despite the fiscal consolidation 
included in the draft budget. Real GDP growth is set to reach 0.6%. This improvement is supported 
by the dissipation of the temporary factors weighing on activity in 2026. Private consumption is 
expected to pick up and to contribute 0.3 pps. to GDP growth, leading to a further projected decline 
of the saving rate to 16.6%. Investment is set to detract 0.2 pps. from GDP growth, weighed down 
by residential and equipment investment. Net exports are expected to accelerate, driven by 
aeronautics and defence, as well as by accelerating economic activity in France’s trade partners. 
Unemployment set to increase 
The labour market weakened in 2026, with the unemployment rate rising to 8.3% in 2026-Q2, up 
0.7 pps. y-o-y, against a backdrop of an increased labour force. The activity rate of people aged 15 
to 64 was stable in 2026-Q2 at a record high of 75.4%. The labour force is projected to increase in 
2027 and to accelerate in 2028, following the assumed reinstatement of the 2023 pension 
reform. Payroll employment is expected to decline by 0.3% in 2026 and 0.5% in 2027, before 
picking up again in 2028 (+0.1%). The unemployment rate is set to increase from 8.4% in 2026 to 
9.2% in 2027 and 9.8% in 2028.  
Inflation expected to increase due to higher energy and food prices 
Inflation rose to 3.8% in October 2026, reflecting a 24.1% surge in energy prices and a 2.7% 
increase in services prices. Inflation is expected to peak at 4.3% in 2027-Q1 due to further 
increases in energy prices and their pass-through to food and services prices. It is then set to 
decline to 1.6% in 2027-Q4 as energy prices gradually decline. Headline inflation is projected at 
2.9% on average in 2027 and at 2.1% in 2028. 
Economic growth is set to decelerate to 0.4% in 2026, dragged down by weak investment, high 
energy prices and the adverse impact of the summer’s heatwaves. It is expected to increase to 
0.6% in 2027, driven by net exports and a rebound in agricultural production. In 2028, in the 
absence of further adjustment, economic activity is forecast to accelerate to 1.4%. High energy 
prices are set to lift inflation to 2.6% in 2026 and 2.9% in 2027. The government deficit is 
expected at 5.5% in 2026, before declining to 5.4% in 2027 and edging up again in 2028 under a 
no-policy-change assumption. Public debt is set to increase from 115.7% of GDP in 2025 to 
123.2% by 2028, driven by continuing sizeable deficits.

=== PAGE 139 ===
Euro Area Member States, France 
 
123 
Large primary deficits and rising interest payments leading to higher public debt  
The general government deficit is expected to rise to 5.5% of GDP in 2026, from 5.1% in 2025. 
This increase reflects the slowdown in economic activity and associated sluggish tax revenue, as 
well as a 0.4 pps. increase —to 2.6% of GDP —of interest payments on government debt. These 
factors are expected to more than offset the impact of the deficit-reducing measures implemented 
since spring, namely expenditure savings across ministries and public administrations of about 
0.3% of GDP and, on the revenue side, the freezing of social contribution exemptions. 
In 2027, the general government deficit is expected to decline only slightly to 5.4% of GDP, as the 
projected muted economic growth and increasing interest payments on public debt offset the 
significant consolidation effort embedded in the forecast. On the expenditure side, the announced 
measures in the draft budget, which underpin the forecast, are worth about 0.8% of GDP. They 
include the partial freezing of pensions, the freezing and reform of several social benefits and 
across-the-board efforts to contain expenditure at various levels of public administration. At the 
same time, interest payments are projected to increase by 0.4 pps., to 3% of GDP, driven by higher 
interest rates on new bond issuances and payments on inflation-indexed bonds. On the revenue 
side, while tax receipts are still expected to grow broadly in line with economic activity, newly-
announced and past revenue-increasing measures account for about 0.7% of GDP. They include, 
among others, the prolongation of the exceptional contribution by large enterprises, the renewed 
freezing of social contributions’ exemptions, the capping or reduction of tax exemptions, including 
for pensioners and on work-related social benefits, and an exceptional levy on dormant assets. 
In 2028, under a no-policy-change assumption, the general government deficit is expected to 
increase to 5.7% of GDP. While some revenue measures planned for 2027 are set to expire, tax 
revenue is overall projected to evolve broadly in line with economic activity. On the expenditure 
side, interest payments are expected to increase further, to 3.3% of GDP.  
The government debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to remain on an upward trend over the forecast 
horizon and to exceed 123% in 2028. This trend, assuming unchanged policies, is set to be mainly 
driven by high primary deficits and rising interest payments.
