9. CROATIA 
 
124 
Economic growth to moderate in 2026 and pick up thereafter  
Croatia’s real GDP growth is forecast to moderate from 3.5% in 2025 to 2.1% in 2026, before 
picking up to 2.4% in 2027 and maintaining a similar, albeit marginally lower, pace in 2028. 
Growth is expected to remain above the EU average throughout the forecast horizon, driven mainly 
by domestic demand, while the negative contribution of net exports is projected to diminish. 
Private consumption growth is expected to 
moderate in 2026, despite continued growth in 
real wages and employment, as households 
remain cautious about spending decisions due to 
persistent price pressures. Investment growth is 
expected to slow markedly, as rising costs, tighter 
financing conditions and heightened uncertainty 
weigh on firms’ investment decisions. The 
absorption of EU funds, particularly during the 
final phase of RRF implementation, is expected to 
provide some support to investment. Goods 
exports are forecast to expand steadily, albeit at 
a slower pace than in 2025, supported by 
demand from the main trading partners. By 
contrast, following two years of contraction, 
services exports are projected to grow modestly, as persistent price and cost competitiveness 
pressures, particularly in tourism, continue to constrain growth. Tourism activity in 2026 has 
remained comparable to 2025 but is less buoyant than during the post-pandemic recovery. Import 
growth is expected to moderate, reflecting slower domestic demand growth. As a result, net 
exports are projected to make a negative contribution to GDP growth in 2026, though less than in 
2025. In 2027 and 2028, economic activity is expected to expand steadily at 2.4% and 2.3%, 
respectively. Private consumption is set to grow above 2% in both years, in line with real 
disposable income growth. Investment growth is expected to rise in 2027, on the back of stronger 
activity in the second half of 2026, and to remain solid in 2028, as cost pressures ease, while the 
phase out of the RRF is expected to be partly offset by a pick-up in the absorption of other EU 
funds. Favourable external demand is set to support stronger growth in goods and services exports. 
Imports are forecast to continue to grow in line with domestic demand. The negative contribution 
of net exports to GDP growth is expected to diminish further. The current account deficit is 
projected to widen to 5.2% of GDP in 2027 and remain stable in 2028, reflecting a persistent 
goods trade deficit partly offset by the services surplus. Net current and capital transfers are 
expected to continue supporting Croatia’s external position, although their contribution is set to 
edge down gradually over the forecast horizon. 
Contained employment growth and weakening wage pressures 
Employment growth is set to continue but to remain below 1% over the forecast horizon as 
economic activity moderates. Labour shortages, which remained elevated in recent years, are 
expected to ease, due to lower labour demand and continued inflows of non-EU workers. At the 
Croatia’s GDP growth is expected to moderate in 2026 to 2.1%, before expanding steadily at 
2.4% and 2.3% in 2027 and 2028, remaining above the EU average. Employment growth is set to 
continue but to remain below 1% over the forecast horizon, with the unemployment rate staying 
at historically low levels. Inflation is expected to stay elevated at 4.2% in 2026, before easing to 
2.6% in 2028, as services and energy inflation decreases. The general government deficit is 
expected to remain close to 3% for the forecast horizon, while the decline in the debt to GDP ratio 
is foreseen to stop.

same time, wage pressures are set to abate gradually, in line with the expected softening of the 
labour market conditions. The unemployment rate is projected to remain at historically low level, at 
4.7% in 2026 and 4.6% in 2027 and 2028, mirroring still relatively tight labour market conditions.  
Inflation to moderate despite energy price increases 
Headline inflation is projected to stay elevated at 4.2% in 2026, down from 4.4% in 2025. Energy 
inflation is expected to offset the reduction in food inflation, as well as the falling prices for non-
energy industrial goods and the gradual easing of services inflation. As of 2027, energy and 
services inflation are expected to decelerate due to lower energy commodity prices and 
moderating wage pressures, with overall inflation set to decline to 3.0% in 2027 and 2.6% in 
2028. Nevertheless, energy inflation is expected to remain above headline inflation levels as 
government energy support measures expire in April 2027 and ETS2 becomes operational in 2028. 
Headline inflation excluding energy and food is projected to decline from 3.5% in 2026 to 2.5% by 
the end of the forecast period, reflecting moderating wage growth and easing domestic demand 
pressures. 
Government deficit remains high and debt adjustment to stop by end of the forecast period 
In 2026, the deficit is forecast to decrease to 2.8% of GDP. Revenue is supported by solid GDP 
growth and high employment levels. Public investments continue to grow, supported by the RRF. 
Social assistance expenditure is expected remain high following the recent increase due to pension 
reforms aimed at improving pension adequacy. Defence spending and energy measures are also 
expected to contribute to high levels of public expenditure. The deficit is expected to remain at 
2.8% of GDP in 2027, as the growth of expenditure with wages and social assistance remain high. 
Public investment is set to decrease, due to a reduction in EU funded investment (driven by the end 
of the RRF, partially compensated by a pick-up in EU structural funds) and a decrease in nationally 
financed investment. In 2028 deficit is expected to reach 2.9% of GDP as expenditure growth 
outpaces revenue collection.  
The debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to remain at 55.7% in 2026, as debt-increasing stock-flow 
adjustments compensate the debt ratio decline due to the denominator effect. It is then expected 
to decline to 55.5% in 2027 and 2028.

10. ITALY 
 
126 
Domestic demand holds up, driven by investment 
Real GDP growth picked up in the first half of 
2026 but slowed in the third quarter, and is 
overall forecast at 1.0% for 2026. Private 
consumption is expected to decelerate as inflation 
weighs on disposable income, while investment 
continues to expand, sustained by RRF-funded 
public works. With goods imports expected to rise 
faster than exports and tourism-driven services 
exports outpacing imports, net trade is set to 
provide a neutral contribution to GDP growth.  
In 2027, real GDP is forecast to increase by 0.7%, 
due to a more modest expansion in domestic 
demand. Household consumption is bound to be 
constrained by the persistent impact of higher 
energy prices. Although many firms maintain healthy balance sheets, less favourable financing 
conditions and uncertain economic prospects weigh on their investment decisions. Net exports are 
projected to make a positive contribution to growth on the back of more moderate import 
dynamics reflecting subdued domestic demand.  
In 2028, real output growth is forecast to rise marginally to 0.8%. Household consumption growth, 
helped by recovering purchasing power, is expected to increase gradually. Net exports are projected 
to continue supporting growth, while investment is set to slow, due to weaker government support 
and higher interest rates. 
Subdued growth in employment and real wages  
Labour demand is expected to moderate after a prolonged expansion until mid-2026. However, 
self-employment is set to increase, supported by a more favourable tax regime. Further small rises 
in labour participation are anticipated to stabilise the unemployment rate. Wage growth in 2026 
has been lagging behind inflation and is expected to allow recovering purchasing power only in 
2028, as negotiated wages incorporate the impact of past inflation.  
Inflation soars, fuelled by energy prices  
Fluctuations in energy commodity prices are anticipated to drive inflation dynamics, despite the 
temporary measures to reduce excise fuel duties and support road transport and farming. In 2026, 
HICP is forecast to rise to 3.4%, receding slightly to 3.1% in 2027, along with the gradual pass-
through to the prices of other products, and falling below 2% only in 2028.  
Government deficit falling below 3% of GDP in 2026 but public debt increasing further 
In 2026, the general government deficit is projected to narrow marginally to 2.9% of GDP, as the 
primary surplus rises to 1.2% of GDP and interest expenditure increases by 0.3 pps. of GDP, 
Real GDP growth is forecast to slow in 2027-28, after reaching 1% in 2026, as private 
consumption is held back by renewed inflationary pressures and investment decelerates. After 
increasing on the back of higher energy prices, inflation is expected to subside only gradually in 
2027. Government deficit is set to fall from 3.1% of GDP in 2025 to 2.9% in 2026. Over 2027-
28, extra expenditure on defence and energy security measures will push the deficit up again. 
Italy has recently activated the National Escape Clause. The combined effect of a reduction in the 
primary surplus and rising interest expenditure prevents a clear downward trend in the debt-to-
GDP ratio, which is projected to peak in 2027 and decrease to 138.2% by 2028.

reflecting rising yields, particularly on inflation-linked bonds. Tax revenues are set to stabilise as a 
share of GDP. The further cut to the personal income tax rate for middle-income earners and a 
temporary tax relief on selected wage components are fully compensated by increases in taxes for 
financial institutions and insurance companies. The temporary cuts in fuel excise duties restrain 
growth of indirect tax revenues. On the expenditure side, lower capital transfers for housing 
renovations are offset by rising public investment, also strengthened by RRF funds, while current 
primary expenditure remains stable as a share of GDP. 
In 2027, the deficit is forecast to increase to 3.5% of GDP, with the primary surplus declining to 
0.8% of GDP. The lagged effects of higher inflation are expected to lift current expenditure, 
particularly on pensions. Budgetary measures underpinning the forecast include the extension of 
the 33% tax rate bracket on personal incomes up to EUR 55,000 from the current EUR 50,000 and 
the increase of the turnover threshold of the flat-tax regime for the self-employed to EUR 100,000 
from EUR 85,000. They also include the waiver of the annual tax on motor vehicles, the extension 
to 2027 of the tax relief on wage increases and overtime pay for low- and middle-income earners, 
and tax credits for the Special Economic Zone. On the expenditure side, the budget increases 
funding for healthcare, infrastructure, defence and energy security, as well as subsidies to 
households, employment and business investment. Offsetting measures raise the tax burden 
through higher levies on the financial, energy and gambling sectors and an increase in the financial 
transaction tax. Expenditure savings include an adjustment to the pension indexation for 2027 and 
cuts to spending by ministries. Higher yields on new bond issuances push interest payments up by 
0.2 pps. to 4.4% of GDP. 
The headline deficit is projected to decline to 3.2% of GDP in 2028, under a no-policy-change 
assumption. Interest expenditure is set to increase further to 4.6% of GDP. At the same time, 
capital expenditure is set to decline, following the high levels recorded in previous years.  
The government debt-to-GDP ratio is set to decrease from the 2027 peak of 138.8% to 138.2% by 
the end of 2028. Interest-growth-rate differentials and large debt-increasing stock-flow 
adjustments related to the housing renovation tax credits which affected the deficit in previous 
years are expected to drive the increase in the government debt ratio in 2026-27, while projected 
primary surpluses remain limited.

11. CYPRUS 
 
128 
Growth to slow but recover quickly, amid ample buffers  
In the first half of 2026 real GDP growth reached 3.2%, driven by private consumption and 
construction activity. Service exports in ICT and royalties continued to expand, while tourism 
receipts initially declined amid conflict-related uncertainty. Higher ship registrations associated 
with certain trade routes distorted the external balance and investment growth figures, but this is 
expected to normalise during the year.  
Real GDP is projected to grow by 3.4% in 2026, 
3.0% in 2027 and 3.2% in 2028. The energy price 
shock is expected to temporarily slow growth, 
weighing on private consumption, as higher 
imported inflation is expected to erode disposable 
incomes. In the course of 2027, easing inflation 
combined with lagged wage adjustments through 
automatic indexation are projected to support 
disposable incomes and consumption. Investment 
excluding ship registrations is expected to slow 
this year as higher costs delay investment 
decisions, before recovering over the course of 
2027, 
supported 
by 
a 
solid 
outlook 
for 
construction activity. The net exports of services 
are forecast to return to its pre-conflict average 
as tourist arrivals recover in 2027. The relocation of export-oriented foreign corporates to Cyprus is 
expected to slow down, while companies in export-oriented sectors, notably ICT, become more 
established domestically and expand their export markets. The temporary reduction in the trade 
surplus due to the oil price shock and the continued increase in profit repatriation by foreign-
owned corporates are set to weigh on the external balance. As a result, the current-account deficit 
is projected to remain wide at 8.4% GDP in 2028.  
Risks to the outlook remain tilted to the downside. Global trade disruptions pose risks to Cyprus’s 
shipping sector outlook, while tourism arrivals are sensitive to geopolitical developments in the 
region. The sizeable import dependence, especially on energy goods, increases the country’s 
vulnerability to external shocks.  
Energy price surge to propel inflation amid a solid labour market  
Headline inflation is projected to rise to 3.9% in 2026, before easing to 2.9% in 2027 and 2.4% in 
2028. The surge in oil prices has raised energy costs, although VAT and excise duty reductions 
have cushioned the impact. Food inflation, which is sensitive to energy and transport costs, is also 
projected to increase.  
Labour market conditions remain robust, as shown by high employment and a historically low 
unemployment rate, projected at 3.7% in 2028. Rising household spending amid subdued wage 
Cyprus’s economic outlook remains robust, despite rising energy prices, which are expected to 
temporarily weigh on growth. Lagged wage adjustments are set to strengthen consumption. 
Tourism inflows, which were affected in the early stages of the conflict in the Middle East, are 
projected to fully recover in 2027. Nevertheless, the current-account deficit is forecast to remain 
wide, notably reflecting the repatriation of profits from foreign-owned corporates. Healthy public 
finances have given the government room for an expansionary personal income tax reform and 
for financial relief measures against the impact of rising energy prices. Fiscal surpluses are 
expected to remain high and will contribute to the fast decline of government debt, which had 
fallen below the 60% threshold by the end of 2025 after peaking at 114% in 2020.  
Graph III.11.1: Cyprus - Real GDP growth and contributions 
 

=== PAGE 145 ===
Euro Area Member States, Cyprus 
 
129 
adjustment is projected to temporarily weigh on household savings in 2026, before a pronounced 
increase later as incomes recover. Real wage growth is expected to accelerate in 2027 as salaries 
adjust through the automatic cost-of-living adjustment mechanism. However, this is set to raise 
unit labour costs in the short-term.  
Fiscal surpluses persist despite tax reform and energy support measures  
In 2025, the general government balance of Cyprus recorded a sizeable surplus of 3.6% of GDP, 
even though government expenditure grew more strongly than revenue. In 2026, the fiscal surplus 
is projected to remain at 2.9% of GDP, despite a general tax reform that reduced revenue by 0.7% 
of GDP and measures to mitigate the impact of high energy prices amounting to 0.4% of GDP.  
In 2027 and 2028, the budget surplus is projected at 3.2% and 3.3% of GDP, benefiting from the 
overall positive economic and labour market situation. Government expenditure in 2027 will be 
affected by the automatic indexation of wages to the cost of living, which takes full effect for the 
first time following its gradual introduction. On the revenue side, measures against the impact of 
high energy prices will continue to have a dampening effect on VAT and excise duties collection. 
Other financial support measures are targeted at families or farmers with livestock affected by 
foot-and-mouth disease. Additional defence spending financed through Security Action for Europe 
(SAFE) loans is expected to start to show its effect in 2027 and more markedly in 2028.  
Government debt is projected to continue its downward trend. After its peak in 2020, the debt-to-
GDP ratio dropped by more than 60 pps. within five years to 52.3% at the end of 2025, thus 
falling below the 60% threshold for the first time since 2009. This significant decrease in 
government debt is set to continue, with the debt level projected at 46.3% in 2026, 40.6% in 2027 
and 35.1% in 2028.

=== PAGE 146 ===
12. LATVIA 
 
130 
Growth set to continue in Latvia through 2028 
 Latvia’s 
economy 
recorded 
stronger-than-
expected GDP growth of over 3% y-o-y in the first 
half of 2026, underpinned by solid growth in 
domestic demand, notably investment, and a 
positive contribution from net exports. Real GDP 
growth is set to remain robust in 2026, at 2.6%, 
before easing to 1.8% in 2027 and picking up to 
2.2% in 2028. Private consumption is forecast to 
grow by 2.5% in 2026, supported by solid real 
wage growth. In 2027 and 2028, it is projected to 
moderate, as elevated inflation weighs on 
households’ purchasing power. Over the forecast 
horizon, public consumption is set to grow more 
slowly than GDP, reflecting restrained spending 
on the public administration. Private and public 
investment are forecast to grow at 5.2% in 2026, driven by strong construction activity, 
investment in machinery and equipment, alongside investment in defence. In 2027, public 
investment growth is projected to slow down but remain high, reflecting the phase-out of RRF 
support, before accelerating again in 2028 due to planned large military investments and 
equipment deliveries, and a modest expected pick up in spending on Rail Baltica project. Stronger 
lending growth to households and firms is set to support private investment in the near term, while 
housing construction is expected to benefit from an improving supply pipeline and rising building 
permits. However, higher interest rates and elevated uncertainty are likely to moderate investment 
gradually over 2027–28. 
 Exports of goods and services are projected to grow steadily over the forecast horizon supported 
by demand growth in Latvia’s export markets in Northern Europe, notably for wood products, 
construction materials, food, machinery, vehicles, other transport equipment, electrical and 
electronic products. Imports are expected to grow in line with final demand. The current account 
balance is set to decrease in 2026, reflecting an increase in prices of energy imports, in particular 
of diesel fuel and natural gas used for power and heat generation, before increasing gradually as 
energy prices normalise.  
Labour market to remain stable  
As the economy continues growing, the unemployment rate is projected to decline from 6.9% in 
2025 to 6.7% in 2028. Still, employment is set to decrease gradually, reflecting the broader trend 
of a shrinking working-age population. Nominal compensation per employee is expected to 
continue growing by around 6% each year, reflecting persistent labour and skills shortages across 
sectors, alongside geographical and occupational mismatches. Moderate increases in the statutory 
minimum wage are also expected to support wage growth.  
Following a return to growth in 2025, Latvia’s economy is expected to continue expanding despite 
heightened geopolitical uncertainty and rising energy prices. Real GDP is forecast to grow by 2.6% 
in 2026, before moderating to 1.8% in 2027 and picking up to 2.2% in 2028. Inflation is set to 
remain elevated at 3.2% in 2026 and 3.6% in 2027, before easing to 2.9% in 2028. The general 
government deficit is forecast to steadily increase and reach 5.4% of GDP in 2028, mainly due to 
defence expenditure, as well as interest payments and social benefits.

Euro Area Member States, Latvia 
 
131 
Elevated inflation due to higher energy prices 
HICP inflation is projected to remain elevated at 3.2% in 2026 and increase to 3.6% in 2027, 
mainly driven by energy inflation. In 2026, the rise in inflation is mitigated by temporary lower 
excise duties on motor fuels, as well as the temporary reduction in VAT on selected food products. 
In 2028, the introduction of the ETS2 is expected to slow the disinflation process, with HICP 
inflation decelerating only moderately to 2.9%. Services inflation is forecast to remain above 
headline inflation throughout the forecast horizon, driven by solid wage growth. 
Government deficit set to increase 
In 2026, the government deficit is forecast to increase to 2.9% of GDP, up from 2.5% of GDP in 
2025, due to revenue and expenditure factors. Lower revenue from income tax and indirect taxes, 
together with moderate property income, is forecast to weigh on overall revenue growth. Higher 
government expenditure reflects higher investment on defence, increasing interest costs, and 
continued growth in social benefits, largely due to pension indexation and a rising number of 
pension recipients. 
In 2027, the government deficit is forecast to rise to 4.4% of GDP, mostly driven by the continued 
broad-based increase in defence expenditure, alongside persistent growth in social transfers and 
interest expenditure. At the same time, the effects of the 2025 personal income tax reform, mainly 
via the introduction of a uniform tax-free income allowance and higher tax-free income allowance 
for pensioners, together with lower interest revenue and lower property income due to reduced 
dividends from state-owned companies in the energy and wood sector, is expected to weigh on 
revenues.  
In 2028, the government deficit is forecast to rise further to 5.4% of GDP, mainly due to the 
continued delivery of military investments and increasing social transfers, alongside lower indirect 
tax revenue and a declining contribution of property revenue.  
The debt-to-GDP ratio reached 46.2% in 2025 and is forecast to increase to 51.9% by 2028, due 
to high budget deficits and the pre-financing of upcoming debt redemptions.

13. LITHUANIA 
 
132 
Economic growth is set to continue 
In 2026, private consumption is forecast to be the 
main driver of GDP growth, expanding by 4.3% 
due to strong real wage growth and to a change 
in the pension system. The change, which makes 
the second pillar pension voluntary and allows the 
withdrawal of accumulated funds, is set to boost 
private consumption temporarily. While real 
wages continue to grow in 2027 and 2028, 
consumption growth is projected to slow down to 
2.1% in 2027 and, in 2028, return to medium-
term average at 3.1%. Investment is projected to 
increase by 5.5% in 2026 and by 4.9% in 2027 
and 2028, driven by increased public investment, 
including in defence and some public projects, e.g. 
Rail Baltica. In 2026, export growth is expected to 
be stronger than in 2025. This is primarily due to strong performance of exports in services 
(especially transportation services). High demand from the main trading partners, namely other EU 
countries, drove the buoyant growth of services exports in the first two quarters. Total export 
growth is projected at 6.0% in 2026, followed by 2.9% in 2027 and 2028. At the same time, 
imports are expected to continue to outpace exports, reflecting strong domestic demand. Net 
exports are therefore projected to provide a negative contribution to GDP growth: -0.8 pps. in 
2026, -0.1 pps. in 2027, and -0.5 pps. in 2028. Overall, real GDP is projected at 2.7% in 2026, 
decreasing at 2.2% in 2027 and 2.3% in 2028. 
Labour market set to gradually tighten 
High labour demand due to strong economic growth is forecast to reduce the unemployment rate 
from 6.9% in 2025 to 6.7% in 2026. While economic growth is set to slow down in 2027 and 
2028, moderating labour demand, the labour force is projected to contract as the population aged 
15-65 declines by between 0.2% and 0.3% annually. As a result, the overall unemployment rate is 
expected to stabilise in 2027 and 2028. The 11.1% increase in minimum wages in 2026, followed 
by an expected 8% rise in 2027, combined with persistent labour shortages and skills mismatches, 
is expected to support nominal wage growth, which is projected at 8.5% in 2026, 6.9% in 2027 
and 6.3% in 2028. 
Inflation expected to rise mainly due to energy price developments 
HICP inflation is projected to increase to 5.1% in 2026, up from 3.4% in 2025, following the surge 
in energy prices but also the abolition of VAT concessions for heating. Energy inflation is expected 
to decline to more moderate levels in 2027, while in 2028, the implementation of ETS2 is set to 
put upward pressure on prices. The introduction of a 10% security contribution levy on non-life 
insurance premia in 2026 to fund defence spending has also led to price increases in many 
Lithuania’s economy is expected to continue growing, supported by private consumption as real 
wages increase and second pillar pension savings are released. Real GDP is projected to grow by 
2.7% in 2026, before easing to 2.2% in 2027 and 2.3% in 2028. Despite geopolitical uncertainty, 
investment is set to grow strongly driven by increased public investment. Inflation is expected to 
rise to 5.1% in 2026, driven by energy and services prices. It is forecast to ease to 3.3% in 2027 
and 2.9% in 2028 as energy prices and wage growth moderate. The general government deficit is 
projected to gradually increase, from 1.8% in 2025 to 5.1% in 2028, reflecting higher defence 
and social spending, public investment and interest payments.

economic sectors. Food prices are expected to increase moderately driven by higher fuel and 
fertilisers costs. Reflecting high but moderating wage growth, services prices are projected to 
increase by 6.6% in 2026, before slowing down to 5.1% in 2027 and 4.6% in 2028. Overall HICP 
inflation is expected to be 3.3% in 2027 and 2.9% in 2028. 
General government deficit set to increase  
In 2026, the general government deficit is projected to increase to 3.5% of GDP, from 1.8% in 
2025. Nationally financed expenditure is expected to rise by 2.3 pps. of GDP, while revenue 
increases by 0.7 pps. The widening deficit reflects higher public investment, intermediate 
consumption, social benefits (including old-age pensions) and interest expenditure. Defence 
spending is projected to increase to 3.7% of GDP (from 2.7% in 2025). Revenue growth in 2026 is 
mainly driven by higher tax receipts, supported by increased corporate income tax rates; a new levy 
on non-life insurance premiums; the removal of reduced VAT rates for heating, hot water and 
firewood; higher excise duties on fuels and alcoholic beverages; and a new excise duty on 
sweetened drinks. Higher non-tax revenue, including sales, also makes a moderate contribution. 
In 2027, the deficit is projected at 4.3% of GDP, as nationally financed expenditure increases by 
1.4 pps. of GDP, outpacing revenue growth of 0.1 pps. Higher intermediate consumption and social 
benefits, alongside an increase in interest expenditure are the main contributors. Nationally 
financed public investment is also expected to increase slightly, while lower other current transfers 
partly offset these pressures. Defence expenditure is projected to reach 4.1% of GDP. Revenue 
growth in 2027 is driven by increases in direct tax receipts (partly reflecting greater personal 
income tax progressivity and higher commercial property tax rates) and social contributions. 
The deficit is forecast to increase further to 5.1% of GDP in 2028, driven by the expenditure side. 
This is due to additional defence spending, reaching up to 4.5% of GDP, higher social benefits, 
interest payments and the public wage bill. Lower non-defence investment and intermediate 
consumption are projected to partly offset the spending pressures. 
Public debt is projected to increase to 43.8% of GDP in 2026, 48.5% in 2027 and 51.5% in 2028. 
The increase is driven by widening deficits and substantial stock-flow adjustments. These 
adjustments mainly reflect social security fund surpluses accumulated as financial assets rather 
than used for debt repayments, and the pre-financing of defence expenditure.

=== PAGE 150 ===
14. LUXEMBOURG 
 
134 
Growth is set to weaken in 2026 
After an expansion of 1.4% in 2025, real GDP 
grew by 0.3% in 2026-Q1 before contracting by 
0.9% q-o-q 2026-Q2. Fixed investment fell after a 
rebound in 2026-Q1 and private consumption 
contracted also in 2026-Q2, held down by a more 
restrictive financing environment and the energy 
price 
shock. 
However, 
an 
automatic 
wage 
indexation that occurred in June, combined with 
government measures to improve disposable 
income, 
is 
expected 
to 
support 
private 
consumption during the second half of the year. 
Government consumption is expected to be the 
main contributor to GDP growth this year. Thanks 
to the exports of services offsetting the weak 
performance of goods exports, net exports are 
expected to provide a small positive contribution to 
growth this year. Overall, GDP is projected to grow by 1.3% in 2026. 
In 2027 and 2028, economic growth is expected to accelerate, on the back of private consumption 
that is expected to benefit from an expansionary fiscal stance. Automatic wage indexations are 
forecast to further improve disposable income. Private investment in satellites and planes is set to 
contribute to a recovery in gross capital formation over 2027 and 2028. Despite being still below 
historical averages, confidence indicator is improving in the construction sector. However, tighter 
financing conditions are expected to hinder its recovery. Likewise, higher financing costs may 
affect investment fund activities. The contribution of net exports is expected to be modest over the 
forecast horizon. 
Employment to pick up 
Domestic employment growth is expected to increase to 1.6% in 2026 from 1.1% in 2025, before 
accelerating further to 1.8% 2027 and 1.9% in 2028. Still, resident unemployment rate is 
projected to increase to 6.9% in 2026, reflecting in part the increasing role of cross-border workers 
in overall employment growth. The unemployment rate is set to decrease to 6.7% in 2027, before 
edging down to 6.3% in 2028 as employment growth accelerates further. 
Energy prices to fuel inflation in 2026 
Headline inflation is set to increase to 3.7% in 2026, up from 2.5% in 2025, driven by surging 
energy prices. Additionally, wage increases based on indexation in June 2026 pushed services 
prices up in the second half of 2026. In 2027, inflation is projected to decelerate to 2.5% as 
inflation in energy and services moderates gradually. Inflation is then expected to moderate 
further in 2028, reaching 1.9%. 
Real GDP growth in Luxembourg is projected to moderate to 1.3% in 2026, held back by weaker 
private consumption, that more than offsets the continued expansion in government consumption. 
Growth is expected to accelerate to 1.8% in 2027 and to 2.0% in 2028, benefiting from 
government measures and tax cuts which are forecast to stimulate private spending. Meanwhile, 
rising interest rates are set to weigh on exports of services. After accelerating in 2026 driven by 
surging energy prices, headline inflation is set to decrease in 2027, before slowing down to below 
2% in 2028. The government deficit is expected to decline to 1.0% of GDP in 2027, driven by 
buoyant income tax revenues, before widening in 2028 following the personal income tax reform.

=== PAGE 151 ===
Euro Area Member States, Luxembourg 
 
135 
General government deficit declines before widening again 
In 2026, the general government is projected to post a deficit of 1.1% of GDP, from 1.8% of GDP 
in 2025. In 2026, general government revenues are expected to increase at a faster pace than 
expenditure. In particular, revenues from personal income taxes are expected to benefit from the 
improvements in the labour market and the absence of adjustment to inflation of tax brackets. The 
increase in the social contribution rate from 24% to 25.5% is expected to drive up revenues. 
Indirect taxes, as a percentage of GDP, are set to decrease, reflecting a deceleration in private 
consumption and the impact of the government’s measures aiming to mitigate the impact of high 
energy prices on households and enterprises. Higher tobacco excise duties are expected to only 
partially offset this decline. Windfall revenues from inheritance taxes contribute to the decrease in 
the general government deficit.  
 
In 2027, the deficit is projected to decline to 1.0% of GDP. The projected improvement in the 
labour market and in the economic activity are expected to support personal and corporation 
income taxes revenues, partly offsetting the reduction of the standard corporate tax rate from 
16% to 15%. Furthermore, the expected acceleration in private consumption combined with the 
increase of tobacco excises are set to support indirect taxes. 
 
In 2028, despite the projected acceleration in economic activity, the deficit is set to widen to 1.7% 
of GDP, as a result of personal income tax reform, which is expected to reduce revenues by around 
0.8% of GDP. Furthermore, the entering into force of the ETS2 system, which replaces the national 
carbon tax, is expected to temporarily reduce indirect taxes. This reflects the lag between the 
application of ETS2 and the collection of revenues, which is expected to begin only from 2029 
onward. In turn, public investment is projected to remain high by recent standards over the 
forecast horizon, supporting the government’s social, digital and green agenda.  
 
The interest expenditure is expected to rise due to higher refinancing costs on newly issued debt, 
reaching 0.5% and 0.7% of GDP in 2026 and 2027, respectively. The debt-to-GDP ratio is 
projected to increase from 26.0% in 2025 to 29.3% in 2026, 29.6% in 2027 and to 30.5% of GDP 
in 2028, due to the persistent primary deficits and debt increasing stock-flow adjustments in 
relation to the social security fund.

=== PAGE 152 ===
15. MALTA 
 
136 
The growth is expected to remain robust in a challenging environment 
Malta recorded 4.7% real growth in 2025. In 2026 real GDP is projected to rise by 3.9%, 
underpinned by strong expansion in public consumption and robust private expenditures and 
investment. Global uncertainty does not seem to have materially affected Malta’s growth so far. 
The country’s resilient services sectors are the 
main 
factors 
behind 
the 
solid 
economic 
performance, notably driven by strong growth in 
tourism, 
recreational, 
IT, 
financial 
and 
professional services. The contribution of net 
exports to growth remains positive, albeit smaller 
than in the previous year, supported by the 
substantial surplus in services trade that more 
than offsets the deficit in goods trade. Tourism 
activity is forecast to continue expanding in 2026 
at a pace exceeding expectations. 
In 2027, global headwinds, some expected 
moderation in labour migration, as well as the 
gradual 
maturation 
of 
some 
fast-growing 
services sectors are expected to slow real GDP growth to 3.5%. The fiscal policy measures 
introduced by the government for 2027 are expected to contribute to higher private consumption 
expenditure, lower net exports and higher inflation, with an overall neutral effect on real GDP 
growth. The increase in tourism taxes is expected to have a moderate negative impact on the 
growth of export services, while higher private consumption from fiscal measures is set to increase 
imports, overall reducing the growth contribution of net exports. Private consumption is projected 
to grow by 4.0% in 2026 to 4.8% in 2027, boosted by an annual tax-free payment by government 
of EUR 1,000 for eligible Maltese workers. It is expected to slow to 4.0% in 2028, benefiting from 
rising real wages. Government consumption growth is expected to accelerate to 7.5% in 2026, 
before moderating to 2.7% in 2027 and rebounding to 5.4% in 2028, providing continuous support 
to GDP growth. Following a 0.4% increase in 2025, investment is set to expand by 3.0% in 2026, 
3.9% in 2027, and 3.0% in 2028, primarily driven by public investment. 
Overall, growth in 2028 is expected to rebound slightly to 3.8% on the back of continued strong 
domestic demand and an improved contribution from net exports.  
Strong employment growth reliant on foreign workers 
Employment growth is forecast to increase to 4.2% in 2026 from 3.9% in 2025, still relying 
heavily on foreign workers. Labour force growth is set to moderate to 3.2% in both 2027 and 
2028, in line with the evolution of economic activity, but also affected by expected increases in 
work permit fees that are set to slow the hiring of foreign workers. The unemployment rate is 
Malta’s economy continued to perform strongly, driven by solid domestic demand and a growing 
tourism sector. The fiscal policy measures introduced by the government for 2027 are expected 
to boost private consumption expenditure, but also to reduce net exports and contribute to higher 
inflation. Real GDP growth is forecast at 3.9% in 2026, moderating to 3.5% in 2027 amid a 
challenging external environment, and rising to 3.8% in 2028. Inflation is forecast at 2.5% in 
2026, and further increasing to 3.0% in 2027. The government energy subsidies are set to 
continue to mitigate the impact of higher international energy prices. Due to significant increases 
in government expenditure, the government deficit in 2026 is expected to increase to 2.8% of 
GDP and to remain at this level in 2027 and 2028. The debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to increase 
from 46.3% in 2025 to 47.2% by 2028.

Euro Area Member States, Malta 
 
137 
forecast to remain low at 3.1%, reflecting the tight labour market with elevated vacancy rates. 
Nominal wage growth averaged 4.3% in 2025 and is expected to slow gradually to 3.9% in 2026, 
3.0% in 2027, and 3.2% in 2028. It is projected to grow faster or at the same pace as inflation. 
Energy support measures continue to keep inflation moderate  
The steep rise in international fuel prices affects the Maltese economy indirectly through higher 
imported inflation, especially on transport, food and other services. The government subsidies 
keeping retail energy prices unchanged at low levels continue to compensate the direct impact of 
international shocks on domestic energy prices. However, the increase in work-permit fees for 
third-country nationals is expected to be partly passed through to higher consumer prices in 2027. 
Inflation is expected to edge up to 2.5% in 2026 after 2.4% in 2025. It is then projected to rise to 
3.0% in 2027, before easing to 2.6% in 2028. 
The deficit is expected to remain at slightly below 3% 
The general government deficit is forecast to increase from 2.1% of GDP in 2025 to 2.8% of GDP 
in 2026. This reflects the combined effects of income tax cuts, phasing out of revenue from 
residency and citizenship schemes, and significant increases of government expenditure on energy 
subsidies due to the higher energy prices, as well as on higher intermediate consumption and 
compensation of employees. 
The deficit is set to remain unchanged at 2.8% of GDP in 2027. Intermediate consumption and 
public sector wage growth are expected to remain significant, while other current expenditure is 
also projected to increase, partly reflecting a new EUR 1,000 annual bonus that the government 
will pay to workers. This increase is expected to be offset by higher revenues, notably from taxes 
on production and imports, reflecting higher tourism taxes and an increase in work permit fees for 
third country nationals. In 2028, under a no-policy-change assumption, the deficit is expected to 
remain at 2.8% of GDP, with the government expected to absorb the increase in fuel prices as a 
result of the coming into force of ETS2. The government’s commitment to keep retail energy prices 
unchanged poses a significant uncertainty for the public finance projections. The public debt-to-
GDP ratio is expected to increase steadily from 46.3% in 2025 to around 47.2% in 2028.

16. THE NETHERLANDS 
 
138 
Growth 
supported 
by 
resilient 
domestic 
demand 
Household consumption is supporting economic 
activity in 2026, driven by positive real wage 
growth. Consumer confidence has turned less 
negative, 
particularly 
regarding 
households’ 
expectations 
on 
their 
financial 
situation. 
Government consumption is also projected to 
grow strongly, notably due to more spending 
directed towards public sector wages and 
healthcare. Investment is forecast to pick up 
moderately, supported by improving business 
sentiment, strong profitability in parts of the 
corporate sector, notably in machinery production 
benefiting from the AI boom, and rising public 
investments in defence and infrastructure. Broader investment, including housing construction, is 
nevertheless constrained by structural bottlenecks. These include electricity grid congestion and 
permit-related constraints linked to excessive nitrogen deposition. Net exports are projected to 
make a positive contribution to GDP growth in 2026. Exports are projected to perform strongly, 
supported by the machinery, pharmaceutical and refining sectors. This is expected to outweigh 
robust import growth driven by domestic demand. 
Economic activity is projected to moderate in 2027 as elevated inflation, together with higher 
income taxation, is expected to weigh on household consumption growth. Households’ accumulated 
savings are expected to partly cushion this impact. Supported by the same favourable factors as in 
2026, investment is forecast to remain robust despite tighter financing conditions, while net 
exports are projected to continue contributing to GDP. In 2028, easing inflationary pressures are 
expected to support a pick-up in private consumption, while investment is forecast to continue 
growing at a stronger pace, as housing construction gradually recovers thanks to an increase in 
issued permits. This is projected to more than offset the lower government consumption growth. 
Real GDP is forecast to grow by 1.5% in 2027 and 1.6% in 2028.  
Labour market remains tight 
The Dutch labour market remains tight, with the number of vacancies still close to that of 
unemployed persons. Unemployment is projected to rise, from 4.0% in 2026 to 4.2% in 2028, as 
labour shortages persist even amid slower employment growth. Nominal wages are forecast to 
outpace HICP inflation until 2028, but higher taxes are expected to weigh on real disposable 
income, slowing growth to around zero.  
Elevated inflation only moderates by 2028 
HICP inflation is projected at 3.0% in 2026, broadly unchanged from 2025. After following a 
decelerating path until the first quarter of 2026, inflation picked up in the second quarter, driven 
by rising motor fuel prices. It is expected to reach its highest level in the fourth quarter of 2026 on 
Economic activity is projected to expand by 1.8% in 2026 and 1.5% in 2027, supported by 
resilient domestic demand despite the surge in energy prices. GDP growth is then projected at 
1.6% in 2028. Net exports are also expected to make a small positive contribution to GDP growth 
until 2028, with high-tech companies driving export growth. Driven by the surge in energy prices 
and sustained services inflation, inflation is projected to remain elevated in 2026 and 2027, 
before easing towards the end of 2027. Increased government spending is set to broadly offset 
adopted revenue measures, especially on income taxation, over the forecast horizon.

Euro Area Member States, The Netherlands

the back of higher gas and electricity prices. Nevertheless, services inflation is expected to remain 
the main contributor to headline inflation, supported by continued wage growth. In 2027, HICP 
inflation is forecast at around the same level of 2026. The energy price surge is expected to fade 
in the second half of 2027, while second-round effects on food prices and services inflation are 
projected to remain moderate. In 2028, inflation is forecast to drop to 2.2% thanks to falling 
energy prices and lower services inflation due to slower wage growth. 
Government deficit to widen despite higher revenues  
In 2026, the deficit is projected at 2.5% of GDP, up from 1.6% of GDP in 2025. This increase can 
largely be attributed to the reform of the military pension system, which required a transfer of 
approximately 0.7% of GDP from the government to a private pension fund.  
In 2027, the government deficit is set to decrease to 2.1% of GDP, as the one-off effect of the 
military pension system reform phases out. It is, however, projected to remain above its 2025 
level. On the revenue side, announced higher income taxes and social contributions paid by 
corporations are expected to partly be offset by the government’s plans to increase the labour tax 
credit and to extend the reduced excise duties on motor fuels until 2028. On the expenditure side, 
the partial postponement of the covered costs for the chronically ill and childcare benefits are set 
to reduce expenditure in 2027. The postponement of the increase in the healthcare deductible to 
2028 and additional humanitarian support to Ukraine are expected to have the opposite effect. 
In 2028, the deficit is projected to increase to 2.3%, as the plans on defence spending and 
investment related to nitrogen are not fully compensated by additional revenue measures. 
Debt-to-GDP is forecasted at 46.1% in 2026 and 46.5% in 2027. This reflects the lower-than-
anticipated take-up of loans by the electricity transmission system operator, reducing the stock-
flow adjustment to 1% of GDP in 2026 and 0.3% in 2027. Continued government deficits are 
expected to raise the debt-to-GDP level to 46.9% of GDP in 2028.

=== PAGE 156 ===
17. AUSTRIA 
 
140 
Recovery of growth despite headwinds 
After a temporary dip in the second quarter, 
Austrian GDP growth is expected to strengthen in 
the second half of 2026. Recent industry 
sentiment indicators are showing the strongest 
readings in years, and industry turnover in 2026 
has reached a new peak. The main driver of this 
momentum is strong international demand for 
industrial components, partly fuelled by the AI 
investment boom, from which Austria benefits 
indirectly. This supports solid export growth, while 
also 
strengthening 
imports. 
Equipment 
investment is set to expand further to satisfy 
rising demand in industry and higher defence 
expenditure. In contrast, higher interest rates and 
construction 
costs 
weigh 
on 
construction 
investment. Private consumption is held back by high uncertainty and real disposable incomes that 
decline in 2026 and stagnate in 2027. However, the saving rate is expected to decline further 
which should support modest consumption growth.  
The outlook is subject to risks, notably from energy markets. Moreover, the rebound in industrial 
demand could be short-lived should the global expansion lose momentum. Overall, real GDP is 
expected to grow by 0.8% in 2026, by 1.2% in 2027 and by 1.1% in 2028. 
Unemployment is increasing 
The unemployment rate has increased steadily since 2022 and is expected to reach 6.1% in 2026. 
The increase affects women more than men: in October, the number of unemployed women was 
up by 3.8% y-o-y, whereas the number of unemployed men was down by 2.6% y-o-y. Austria is 
implementing a major pension reform which raises the female statutory retirement age from 60 to 
65 years in stages, aligning it with that of men by 2033. This increases the labour force and 
supports employment growth in the medium term, but in the current transition phase, it also 
increases female unemployment. However, improving economic growth is expected to bring the 
unemployment rate down to 5.8% in 2027 and 5.5% in 2028. Compensation of employees is 
projected to grow below inflation in 2026. Already concluded collective bargaining agreements also 
indicate that wage growth will remain modest in 2027. 
High energy inflation persists  
Ongoing volatility in energy markets keeps HICP inflation high at an expected 3.1% in 2026. Energy 
inflation is expected to reach 7.9%, driven mainly by high motor fuel prices. The government has 
taken several measures to reduce inflation, including a price brake on petrol and diesel and a 
reduction in VAT on basic food items which took effect in July 2026. Electricity prices are 
contributing negatively to overall inflation, due to temporary policy measures introduced for the 
year 2026 and delayed pass-through of wholesale prices to households’ tariffs. In 2027, gas and 
The Austrian economy is recovering gradually despite headwinds from energy markets and 
rising borrowing costs with growth expected at 0.8% in 2026 and above 1% in 2027 and 
2028. Strong global demand is boosting exports and equipment investment, making up for 
lacklustre consumption growth over the forecast horizon. Inflation remains elevated due to 
high energy prices in 2026 and 2027 at 3.1% and 2.9% respectively. The general government 
deficit is projected to decrease from 4.0% of GDP in 2026 and to 3.6% in 2027 and 2028. The 
debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to continue to increase, reaching 84.0% in 2028.

retail electricity prices are expected to rise more strongly, whereas the effect of higher motor fuel 
prices is expected to fade from March. HICP inflation excluding energy and food is set to subside 
slowly, as the pass-through of higher energy costs is expected to be mitigated by modest wage 
growth. As a result, Austrian inflation is expected to stay elevated at 2.9% in 2027 and to decline 
to 2% in 2028, remaining slightly below the Euro Area average over the forecast horizon. 
Government deficit expected to remain high  
The general government deficit is projected to decline from 4.1% of GDP in 2025 to 4.0% in 2026 
and to 3.6% in 2027 and 2028. A contractionary fiscal stance is expected in 2026 and 2027, 
corresponding to 0.6% and 0.8% of GDP, while a neutral fiscal stance is projected for 2028.  
On the expenditure side, in 2026, savings from below-inflation adjustments to pensions and public 
wages, tighter rules on early retirement and lower climate-related capital expenditure are 
projected to contribute to budget consolidation. In 2027, changes in social security and 
unemployment insurance are projected to decrease the deficit. In 2028, additional savings stem 
from the reduction of investments in the national railway infrastructure. However, these savings 
are partially offset by rising costs related to an ageing population, particularly in healthcare and 
long-term care, higher interest expenditure and an increase in military investments.  
On the revenue side, in 2026, the partial retention of the tax bracket creep and the increase of the 
top earners’ tax rate are expected to contribute to a deficit reduction. In 2027, the parcel delivery 
tax and an extraordinary increase in the maximum base for social contributions are expected to 
boost revenues. In 2028, a progressive increase in the corporate income tax rate and restrictions 
on eligible assets under the investment-related profit exemption are projected to raise additional 
revenue. However, the higher revenues are expected to be partly offset by lower employers’ 
contributions to the Family Burdens Equalisation Fund, and by the switch from national carbon 
pricing to ETS2, which will temporarily reduce recorded revenues due to differences in accounting 
treatment. 
The government debt-to-GDP ratio is expected to continue rising over the forecast horizon. After 
reaching 80.7% in 2025, it is projected to further increase to 82.4% in 2026, 83.0% in 2027 and 
84.0% in 2028, mainly due to persistent primary deficits and subdued GDP growth.

=== PAGE 158 ===
18. PORTUGAL 
 
142 
Economic growth improves despite adverse shocks 
Despite higher energy prices and weaker economic sentiment, Portugal’s GDP growth picked up 
from 0.2% q-o-q in the first quarter of 2026 to 0.9% in the second quarter. Private consumption 
and exports of both goods and services contributed strongly to the rebound. Investment remained 
robust in the first half of the year but growth was concentrated in the first quarter where a data 
centre project had a substantial impact on both private investment and imports. Despite some 
recovery in economic sentiment during the summer, the flash estimate for the third quarter of 
2026 points to a moderation in GDP growth to 0.2% q-o-q. This was mainly driven by a decline in 
industrial activity while retail sales continued to grow at a solid pace. 
Overall, GDP growth is expected to rise from 1.9% 
in 2025 to 2.3% in 2026, reflecting the strong 
outcome in the first half of the year, the 
completion of RRF-funded projects and a further 
boost to domestic demand by the planned 
pension bonus and tax refunds in the last quarter 
of the year. In 2027, economic growth is set to 
moderate to 1.9% due to winding down in public 
investment linked to the end of the RRF cycle, 
partly offset by a slight acceleration in the use of 
EU cohesion funds and a further expansion of 
private investment in data centres. Growth is 
projected to improve again in 2028 against the 
backdrop of an assumed easing in energy prices 
and a more favourable external environment. In 
the external sector, the current account is forecast to move from a surplus of around 1% of GDP in 
2025 to a broadly balanced position over 2026-28. This is mainly due to the adverse energy price 
effects in 2026 which are assumed to unwind in the subsequent years. 
Labour market remains resilient amid continuous employment growth 
Labour indicators continued to improve at a sound pace until September 2026 albeit with some 
moderation in employment growth amid weaker migration inflows, as evidenced by data on the 
working age population. Unemployment dropped from an annual average of 6% in 2025 to 5.6% 
in September 2026. In year-average terms, the unemployment rate is forecast at 5.8% in 2026, 
5.7% in 2027, and 5.6% in 2028. Employment and wage growth are forecast to moderate 
somewhat over the forecast horizon. This trend, together with the recovery in productivity, is set to 
translate into a moderation of unit labour costs, which are nevertheless forecast to rise slightly 
faster than in Portugal’s main trading partners. 
Inflation set to recede after energy driven spike 
Headline inflation increased from 2.2% in 2025 to 3.7% y-o-y in the third quarter of 2026, mostly 
driven by energy prices. Services prices remained slightly above headline inflation but faced limited 
impact from energy markets. Food and non-energy industrial goods were also shielded from the 
energy shock until the third quarter of 2026 but are projected to face some lagged effects in the 
Portugal’s economy is set to accelerate to 2.3% in 2026 as strong domestic demand outweighs 
adverse external shocks. Growth is projected to moderate to 1.9% in 2027 and to pick up again in 
2028. Amid gradual moderation in wage and employment dynamics, headline inflation is 
expected to recede over the forecast horizon. The general government balance is projected to 
weaken gradually and record small deficits of 0.1% of GDP in 2027 and 0.3% of GDP in 2028. 
Public debt is set to continue its downward trajectory over the forecast horizon.

Euro Area Member States, Portugal 
 
143 
following months. However, energy transmission channels are forecast to remain relatively limited 
and inflation is set to moderate as of 2027. In year-average terms, inflation is forecast to reach 
3.2% in 2026 and to recede to 3.0% in 2027 and 2.2% in 2028. Core inflation excluding energy 
and food is expected to remain slightly below the headline rate in 2026 and 2027 before moving 
slightly above it in 2028. 
Budget balance projected to weaken gradually 
Portugal’s budget balance is forecast to reach 0.1% of GDP in 2026, following a 0.7% of GDP 
surplus in 2025. The projected decrease of the surplus in 2026 stems from the impact of 
government support measures taken in response to the January and February storms, higher RRF 
loan expenditure and an extraordinary pension supplement. Revenue remains strong on the back of 
resilient economic activity and a strong labour market although it is partly offset by the impact of 
revenue-decreasing fiscal policy measures. The fiscal stance in 2026 is expected to be 
expansionary amid a peak in support from EU funds, notably the RRF.  
In 2027 and 2028, the general government balance is projected to weaken gradually, turning into 
small deficits of 0.1% of GDP in 2027 and 0.3% of GDP in 2028. The weakening is set to be driven 
by lower tax revenues, notably direct tax revenues as a result of rate cuts in personal and 
corporate income taxation. At the same time, interest expenditure is projected to rise in view of an 
increase in bond yields. Nationally financed public investment is forecast to decline slightly to 2.3% 
of GDP, following a peak driven by RRF-loans in 2026. Risks to the fiscal outlook relate to financial 
weaknesses in state-owned enterprises and contingent liabilities from public-private partnerships. 
Portugal’s fiscal stance is set to turn contractionary in 2027, following the end of the RRF, before 
shifting toward a neutral stance in 2028.  
Following its decline to 89.2% in 2025, public debt-to-GDP is projected to continue its downward 
trend over the forecast horizon on the back of persisting primary balance surpluses and favourable 
growth-interest rate differentials. It is forecast to reach 86.5% of GDP in 2026, 83.2% in 2027 and 
80.1% in 2028.

=== PAGE 160 ===
19. SLOVENIA 
 
144 
Growth set to increase, supported by domestic demand and exports 
Real GDP growth in 2025 was revised up from 1.1% to 1.5% in 2025. Growth in the first half of 
2026 was rapid, driven by domestic demand. Private consumption was supported by wage growth 
and high employment, while public consumption also expanded. Investment growth was 
particularly strong in non-residential construction, reflecting the final RRF investments, and in 
machinery and equipment. Exports also rebounded after their weak performance in 2025, 
supported by the opening of important new production capacities in the automotive and 
pharmaceutical sectors. However, the contribution of net exports to GDP growth remained negative 
due to the faster growth of imports.  
GDP growth is forecast to reach 2.9% in 2027 
and 2.5% in 2028. In 2027, private consumption 
is expected to receive further support by the 
lower taxes and contributions. Employment 
remains stable at a high level, while wage growth 
stays robust. The saving rate is projected to 
further increase in 2027. Public investment is 
expected to remain high in 2027 and 2028, 
reflecting additional defence investments. Private 
investment is also forecast to continue the strong 
momentum observed since the end of 2025, 
underpinned by solid credit growth. Export growth 
is projected to continue in line with the foreign 
demand, although the growth contribution from 
net exports is expected to remain negative.  
Persistent labour market tightness 
Employment remains at historically high levels, rising only by 0.2% in 2026, and is expected to 
remain stable thereafter, supported mainly by the sustained inflow of foreign workers. The 
unemployment rate is projected to remain at around 3.8% throughout the forecast horizon. 
Nominal wage growth is set to remain high in 2026, at 7.4%, reflecting the continued impact of 
the public sector wage reform, the increase in the minimum wage and tight labour market 
conditions. It is expected to ease to 5.7% and 5.2% in 2027 and 2028 respectively. 
Inflation set to ease gradually from 2027 
Inflation is projected to rise from 2.5% in 2025 to 3.4% in 2026, driven by high energy prices as 
well as marked wage growth amid a tight labour market. It is then forecast to ease to 2.4% in 
2027, as energy-related price pressures gradually fade. However, services inflation is expected to 
remain elevated in 2027 and to ease only gradually thereafter, maintaining upward pressure on 
headline inflation. HICP inflation is projected to decline to 2.3% in 2028. 
Slovenia’s economy is set to grow by 3.9% in 2026 and 2.9% in 2027, driven by strong domestic 
demand, before slowing down to 2.5% in 2028. The labour market is expected to remain resilient, 
with employment broadly stable at a high level and unemployment rate remaining low. Inflation 
is forecast to peak at 3.4% in 2026, before falling to 2.4% in 2027 and 2.3% in 2028. The 
government deficit, however, is projected to reach 2.9% of GDP in 2026 and rapidly increase to 
4.5% in 2027, as tax revenues are decreasing and expenditure remains high. The debt-to-GDP 
ratio is projected to increase from 64.5% in 2026 to 65.3% by 2028.

Euro Area Member States, Slovenia 
 
145 
Persistent expenditure pressures and revenue measures widen the deficit 
In 2026, the general government deficit is set to increase to 2.9% of GDP, from 2.5% of GDP in 
2025. Revenue is supported by a strong economic performance and by the full-year impact of the 
long-term care contribution, introduced in July 2025. By contrast, measures to mitigate the impact 
of high energy prices are expected to reduce revenue by around 0.3% of GDP. On the expenditure 
side, current spending continues to increase, driven by social benefits and public wages. Public 
investment is projected to reach 5.7% of GDP, supported by the completion of RRF-funded projects. 
In 2027, the general government deficit is forecast to increase to 4.5% of GDP, mainly due to 
current expenditure remaining elevated and the impact of new measures. In line with the no-
policy-change assumption, the projections include measures that have been adopted or credibly 
announced and specified in sufficient detail. On the revenue side, these include a cap on social 
security contributions, lower VAT rates on food and, temporarily, on energy and changes to the 
personal income tax regime, which reduce revenues. On the expenditure side, these include full 
pensions for working pensioners, increasing expenditure. Overall, these measures, adopted in the 
Intervention Act (ZIURS), are estimated to increase the deficit by around 1.2% of GDP. Current 
spending is set to continue to increase due to social benefits and public wages, while expenditure-
containment measures are incorporated in the projections where they have been specified in 
sufficient detail. Despite the end of the RRF, public investment is projected to remain high at 5.5% 
of GDP thanks to the continued implementation of EU cohesion funds and strong nationally-
financed investment, including in defence.  
The fiscal outlook is subject to high uncertainty. On one hand, a further extension of the energy 
support measures into 2027 and reducing fuel excise duties below the EU minimum levels, would 
increase the deficit. On the other hand, the deficit could be lower than projected if specific 
expenditure-reducing measures are adopted or the ZIURS measures are revised or do not 
ultimately enter into force altogether. 
In 2028, the general government deficit is projected to decline slightly to 4.3% of GDP. Revenue is 
set to benefit from continued economic growth, while expenditure is projected to fall, mainly due 
to lower public investment. As a result of the high fiscal deficits, the debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast 
to increase from 64.5% in 2026 to 65.3% of GDP in 2028.

20. SLOVAKIA 
 
146 
Growth remains modest amid uncertainty and fiscal consolidation  
Following subdued growth of 0.9% in 2025 held back by uncertainty and fiscal consolidation, real 
GDP growth is expected to slow further to 0.8% in 2026. Private consumption has proven more 
resilient than expected but is set to remain modest, weighed down by fiscal consolidation and only 
slightly increasing real wages. Public investments are projected to support growth with a robust 
deployment of EU funds, while uncertainty and higher interest rates affect private investments. 
The contribution of net exports to growth is expected to be broadly neutral.  
In 2027, real GDP growth is projected at 1.6%, 
driven mainly by a growth impulse from net 
exports and accelerating private consumption. 
Export growth is expected to regain momentum in 
2027, supported by the launch of a new 
automotive production factory. Gross fixed capital 
formation is expected to contract in 2027, mainly 
reflecting a decline in public investments as the 
RRF is coming to an end. Private consumption is 
expected to accelerate with a pick-up in economic 
activity and higher real wage growth. In 2028, 
real GDP growth is forecast at 1.5%, slightly 
down from 2027, with investments rebounding 
but the export impulse fading and higher inflation 
weighing on household consumption. Additional 
impact of the fiscal stimulus in Germany represents a positive risk to growth due to close 
economic integration and positive spillovers. However, overall risks to growth remain tilted to the 
downside over the forecast horizon, mainly due to Slovakia’s external dependencies, particularly on 
energy commodities, and geopolitical tensions.  
Resilient labour market despite economic turmoil 
The unemployment rate is projected to increase from 5.4% in 2025 to 5.7% in 2026 as the labour 
market slightly loosens reflecting weaker economic activity.  However, the ongoing decline in 
employment is largely offset by a shrinking labour force. Overall, the labour market remains 
relatively tight due to robust labour demand in specific sectors, alongside a high influx of foreign 
workers. The unemployment rate is forecast to remain at 5.7% in 2027 and 2028. Growth in the 
employee compensation is expected to slow down in 2026, before regaining pace in 2027. 
Elevated inflation levels shape real wage dynamics, as growth in real compensation per employee 
in 2026 is expected to be only slightly positive, before picking up in 2027 and again slowing down 
in 2028. 
Real GDP growth is expected to remain modest at 0.8% in 2026, before picking up to 1.6% in 
2027 and 1.5% in 2028. EU funds continue to support public investment in 2026, while private 
consumption and investment remain subdued due to fiscal consolidation and uncertainty. Net 
exports are expected to pick up and support growth in 2027 and 2028. HICP inflation is projected 
to slow in 2026, as a sharp acceleration in energy prices is outweighed by the slowdown in food 
and services inflation. Inflation is set to moderate in 2027 before picking up in 2028 due to 
energy support removal and the introduction of ETS2. The public deficit is set to continue 
increasing from 4.4% of GDP in 2026 to 5.0% in 2027 and 5.5% in 2028, as fiscal consolidation 
comes to a halt. Rising deficits, together with the diminishing favourable effects of the interest 
rate-growth differential (as the difference between nominal GDP growth and the effective interest 
rate on government debt becomes less favourable), are expected to contribute to an increase in 
the debt-to-GDP ratio from 63.4% in 2026 to 66.3% in 2027 and 69.5% in 2028.

Euro Area Member States, Slovakia 
 
147 
Elevated inflationary pressures set to continue 
In 2026, HICP inflation is expected to ease to 3.8%, as effects of 2025 tax increases fade, and 
moderation in food and services inflation more than offset increasing energy prices. Energy 
inflation is set to pick up strongly, mainly driven by the energy support adjustment that included a 
removal of price caps for heating.  The impact of the conflict in the Middle East is reflected mainly 
through higher petrol and diesel prices, while the effect on gas and electricity prices is limited as 
they are regulated by the government.  
In 2027, HICP inflation is projected at 3.2% in view of the 2026 energy support adjustment effect 
fading. However, agricultural prices are forecast to increase, and commodity prices are set to 
remain elevated, with an expected pass-through to other components. In 2028, HICP is set to 
increase to 4.0% due to the removal of the energy support mechanism altogether, resulting in 
removed price caps on gas and electricity, as well as the implementation of ETS2.  
Deficits and debt to increase despite consolidation measures   
In 2026, the general government deficit is expected to remain at 4.4% of GDP. Despite a 
consolidation package for 2026, the deficit is projected to remain unchanged compared with 2025, 
reflecting the delayed delivery of military equipment initially expected in 2025. In 2027, the deficit 
is forecast to increase to 5.0% of GDP, as fiscal consolidation comes to a halt and additional policy 
measures increasing expenditure are set to be introduced, such as support to the agricultural 
sector in response to drought. The main factor contributing to the higher deficit is increased public 
investment in defence, the construction of a nuclear power plant in Jaslovské Bohunice and 
national co-financing of EU-funded projects. The deficit is forecast to increase further to 5.5% of 
GDP in 2028, driven by the expected delivery of military equipment.  
The government debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to continue rising, largely driven by the projected 
deficits and the waning favourable effects of the interest rate-growth differential, from 60.5% in 
2025 to 63.4% in 2026, then further to 66.3% in 2027 and 69.5% in 2028.

=== PAGE 164 ===
21. FINLAND 
 
148 
After growing by 0.8% in 2025, Finland’s real GDP growth is projected to gain steam with growth 
forecast at 1.6% in 2026, 1.6% in 2027, and 1.5% in 2028. Continued export strength, together 
with rising private consumption and investment are expected to keep the economy on a steady 
growth path. Inflation is set to ease from 2.5% in 2026 to 1.7% by 2028. Driven by expanding 
economic activity, the unemployment rate is forecast to decline from 10.6% in 2026 to 9.4% in 
2028. Public spending is set to grow modestly amid fiscal consolidation, though defence 
investment is expected to surge as from 2026. The general government deficit is forecast to widen 
from 3.8% of GDP in 2025 to 4.0% in 2026, and to remain elevated at around 4.5% in 2027-28. 
Public debt is projected to continue rising, reaching 93.7% of GDP by 2028. 
Domestic demand set to drive economic growth 
Following revisions to historical national accounts since the Spring 2026 Forecast, GDP growth 
figures have been substantially updated. Real GDP rose by 0.8% in 2025 – an upward revision of 
0.6 pps. compared with the data available for the Spring Forecast. The revised data also suggests 
that 2026 began with stronger growth momentum. In the first half of the year, the economy grew 
by 1.3% y‑o‑y, supported by stronger exports, private consumption and investment. Imports also 
rose significantly, driven by robust domestic demand, especially for military equipment, which 
turned the contribution of net trade to GDP negative. The expansion is expected to continue 
throughout 2026 as domestic and exports demand remain strong and surveys show rising activity 
in key sectors. Stronger domestic and external demand is projected to lift real GDP growth to 1.6% 
in 2026 – an upward revision of 0.8 pps. compared with the Spring Forecast. Real GDP growth is 
projected to remain at 1.6% in 2027 and to decline to 1.5% in 2028. 
Consumer confidence has improved, reaching 
close to its long-run average, and private 
consumption began to grow in 2026, with a 
gradually accelerating rise projected over the 
forecast horizon. Real disposable income for 
households is expected to be roughly flat in 2026, 
leading to a fall in the saving rate. Higher policy 
rates in the euro area are projected to increase 
loan-servicing costs for households with variable-
rate mortgages. Higher employment, wage 
growth and softer inflation should sustain 
consumption in 2027-28.  
Investment is projected to increase in 2026, 
driven by a rise in machinery and equipment 
installations and by construction investment: 
deliveries of new fighter jets have begun (to continue until 2030) and data‑centre construction is 
booming. In contrast, residential investment is forecast to decline in 2026 as weakness in the 
housing market continues. A gradual recovery is projected as from 2027, in line with recovering 
house prices and higher household income. As firms report increasing export orders and stronger 
confidence, exports are projected to grow over the forecast horizon. At the same time, domestic 
demand, including for defence and data‑centre equipment, is set to push import growth above 
export growth in 2026 and remain more dynamic in 2027-28. This would turn the net‑export 
contribution to growth negative in 2026-28. The current account balance is projected decline from 
0.8% of GDP in 2026 to 0.2% of GDP in 2028. 
Employment set to expand gradually 
The unemployment rate increased to 11.0% in September 2026, largely due to migration and 
rising labour force participation and persistently low labour demand. Despite economic growth,

Euro Area Member States, Finland 
 
149 
employment decreased during the first half of 2026 as output growth was supported by 
productivity gains while remaining relatively less employment intensive. More recently, 
employment has shown signs of recovery. Employment is expected to follow the economic activity 
with a lag and increase gradually, by 0.6% in 2027 and 0.9% in 2028, after a decline of 0.4% in 
2026. Unemployment is projected to average 10.6% in 2026 and to decrease to 9.4% by 2028. 
Inflation expected to ease over the forecast horizon 
Higher fuel prices are pushing up consumer prices, with average inflation projected at 2.5 % in 
2026. As the impact of the energy price shock wanes and otherwise price pressures are assessed 
to be modest, inflation is expected to ease gradually, falling to 1.7 % in 2028. 
Public finances continue to face significant strain 
Despite tax revenue gains from more upbeat economic activity, the general government deficit is 
expected to reach 4.0% of GDP in 2026, up from 3.8% in 2025. Expenditure growth is driven by a 
large delivery of F-35 fighter jets, rising interest payments and an increase in the compensation of 
employees. The freeze on the indexation of certain social transfers, such as the basic 
unemployment allowance, is expected to contain expenditure growth until 2027. Revenue is 
projected to grow, supported by nominal GDP growth and increases in social contribution rates. 
However, the weak labour market and personal income tax cuts are limiting income tax receipts.  
In 2027, the deficit is forecast to rise further to 4.5% of GDP. Expenditure pressures remain 
elevated due to investments in defence, solid growth in compensation of employees and 
continuing growth in debt servicing costs. On the revenue side, fiscal intake is set to be dampened 
driven in part by the implementation of a corporate income tax cut. In 2028, the deficit is projected 
to stay at 4.5% of GDP based on the no-policy-change assumption.  
The debt-to-GDP ratio is expected to be at 89.8% in 2026. Continued large deficits are projected to 
push the ratio higher over the forecast horizon, reaching 93.7% by 2028.
