22. CZECHIA 
 
152 
Solid growth, driven by private demand and public consumption 
Czechia’s real GDP growth is set to slow to 1.8% 
in 2026, reflecting the impact of the energy price 
shock and a negative contribution from net 
exports. Growth is projected to accelerate to 2.5% 
in 2027, supported by solid household and 
government 
consumption, 
investment, 
and 
improved contribution from net exports, before 
moderating 
to 
2.1% 
in 
2028. 
Household 
consumption is expected to remain the main 
driver of growth over the forecast horizon. 
Despite a decline in consumer confidence since 
March 2026, household consumption is forecast 
to expand by close to 2.5% in both 2026 and 
2027, supported by further growth in real wages, 
pent-up demand and a declining households’ 
saving rate. The latter are among the highest in the EU and are projected to moderate, remaining 
however well above their historic average, due to persisting consumer uncertainty and to larger 
asymmetries in the distribution of disposable income. Investment is expected to contribute 
positively to growth, expanding rapidly in 2026, supported by a higher absorption of EU funds, 
increased residential construction, as well as higher domestic and external demand in some 
industrial segments. Over 2027 and 2028 investment growth is set to decelerate, in a context of 
tighter financing conditions, but to remain positive.  
Despite the outstanding economic uncertainty, exports are expected to grow over the forecast 
horizon, driven also by higher defence and infrastructure spending in some key trading partners. 
The expansion in domestic demand is expected to lead to rapid imports growth, resulting in a 
negative contribution of net exports to economic growth in 2026, before turning slightly positive in 
2027 and 2028. Downside risks to exports remain, due to uncertainties in international trade and 
over the scope of Czechia’s export growth linked to the fiscal expansion of its trading partners. 
Labour market remains tight, despite higher participation rates 
Czechia is projected to record an increase in employment in 2026, reflecting higher participation 
rates in the labour force. The unemployment rate is set to broadly stabilise, narrowly increasing 
from 3.1% in 2026 to 3.3% in 2028, remaining among the lowest in the EU. Structural shifts 
continue to reshape the Czech labour market, with increasing female participation and higher 
employment in services offsetting a decline in the manufacturing sector. Nominal wage growth is 
projected to remain markedly above inflation but to decline gradually, from 4.9% in 2026 to 4.5% 
in 2027 and 4.0% in 2028. 
Inflation affected by energy price shocks 
HICP inflation is projected at 2.1% in 2026, before accelerating to 2.8% in 2027, and slowing to 
2.5% in 2028. Energy price shocks are pushing inflation higher, but their impact on consumer 
Real GDP growth in Czechia is forecast at 1.8% in 2026, accelerating to 2.5% in 2027 and then 
moderating at 2.1% in 2028. Private consumption is expected to remain the main driver of 
growth, supported also by expanding public consumption. However, higher energy prices and 
growing uncertainty are forecast to weigh on economic activity, especially in 2026. The energy 
price shock is expected to raise inflation and, despite a deceleration in services inflation, the 
headline indicator is projected to peak at 2.8% in 2027, before easing somewhat in 2028. Fiscal 
expansion is set to widen the public deficit to 3.3% in 2027 and 2028.

prices are expected to be mostly visible in 2027, due to deferred effects both on energy inflation 
and on the pass-through to the other HICP components. In 2026, the decline in electricity prices 
resulting from the government permanently taking over the payment of the renewable energy fee 
from consumers is forecast to offset most of the price increases in transport fuels and gas. Energy 
inflation is therefore expected to accelerate in 2027. Services inflation is projected to moderate in 
2026, reflecting the slowing wage growth, following the same trend in 2027 and 2028. HICP 
inflation excluding energy and food is forecast to exceed headline inflation in 2026, at 3.1%, 0.2 
pps. higher than in 2025, before decreasing to 2.8% in 2027 and 2028. 
Fiscal expansion set to increase deficit 
The budget deficit is set to increase to 2.7% in 2026 from 2.2% in 2025, driven by lower revenue 
after the full phase-out of the tax on energy companies’ windfall profits. The revenue foregone 
due to the reduction in diesel excise tax, introduced in response to the conflict in the Middle East, is 
expected to be partly compensated by the extraordinary sectoral tax on higher gross refining 
margins. Expenditure is set to increase, reflecting the takeover of the payment of the renewable 
energy levy from electricity consumers.  
The budget deficit is forecast to increase to 3.3% of GDP in 2027, keeping the fiscal impulse from 
the national budget expansionary. The revenue-to-GDP ratio is projected to decrease, as EU 
transfers decline after the expiry of the RRF, partly offset by higher tax compliance following the 
reintroduction of the EET 2.0, the electronic evidence of sales registration system. Expenditure is 
set to rise driven by increased defence spending, social benefits, compensation of employees, and 
growing interest expenditure. 
Under a no-policy-change assumption, the deficit is expected to stabilise at 3.3% in 2028, as the 
increasing negative government balance is offset by nominal GDP growth. Overall, this implies a 
broadly neutral fiscal stance for 2028. 
The public debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to rise from 44.2% in 2025 to 49.5% in 2028, driven by 
the negative primary balance, partly offset by nominal GDP growth.

=== PAGE 170 ===
23. DENMARK 
 
154 
Consumption and investment to drive growth in 2027 and 2028 
GDP expanded strongly in the first half of 2026, 
driven by strong pharmaceutical exports, while 
economic activity in the rest of the economy has 
expanded at a more moderate pace. Growth is 
expected to reach 4.5% in 2026 before 
moderating to around 2% in 2027 and 2028. 
Domestic demand is set to strengthen gradually 
over the forecast horizon. Projected expansion in 
household consumption is supported by real wage 
growth and a resilient labour market. It is also 
underpinned by a gradual unwinding of still-
elevated saving rate, even though consumer 
confidence remains subdued. Robust public 
consumption continues to contribute to demand, 
and investment is projected to recover gradually, 
supported by construction activity and public 
investment in defence, providing additional support to growth in 2027 and 2028. The growth 
contribution from net exports is set to fade and turn broadly neutral. 
Danish exports have continued to increase in 2026 despite global economic and geopolitical 
uncertainty. This trend is expected to continue over the forecast horizon, although growth is set to 
slow down. The pharmaceutical industry has been the main driver of export developments, 
recording exceptionally strong growth in the first half of 2026, with a substantial share of 
pharmaceutical production taking place abroad under Danish ownership. Developments in the US 
market have played an important role, amid an agreement to reduce prices for selected weight-
loss and diabetes treatments in exchange for expanded market access, alongside the introduction 
of a new pill-based weight-loss medication. Following a decline in 2025, service exports are 
expected to return to growth in 2026 and continue expanding over the forecast horizon as shipping 
exports are set to benefit from growth in global trade. However, international transport routes are 
likely to remain vulnerable to disruptions, bottlenecks and rising costs, posing risks to major Danish 
logistics and transport companies.  
Employment growth slows down 
Employment is expected to rise modestly over the forecast horizon, after several years of rapid 
expansion. The labour force is projected to grow broadly at the same rate, favoured by a net influx 
of international workers as well as by older workers staying active beyond retirement age. 
Additionally, the activity rate of migrants living in Denmark has increased. Going forward, the 
unemployment rate is expected to remain at around 7.0%. Real wages are set to continue growing 
over the forecast horizon as nominal wage growth is projected to outpace inflation. 
The Danish economy is projected to maintain strong growth rates in 2026, reaching 4.5%, 
driven mainly by net exports before falling to 2.0% and 2.1% in 2027 and 2028 respectively. 
Economic activity is expected to slow down in 2027 and 2028, with domestic demand taking 
over as its main driver. At 1.7% inflation is set to remain subdued in 2026, partially reflecting a 
temporary lowering of electricity levies, but to increase thereafter supported by energy price 
developments. Employment is set to continue increasing, but at a slower rate than in previous 
years. The general government budget balance is expected to remain in surplus over the 
forecast period, albeit falling from 1.1% in 2026 via 0.5% in 2027 to 0.3% in 2028.

Non-EA Member States, Denmark 
 
155 
Inflation expected to increase 
Inflation is projected to remain contained in 2026, with HICP inflation at 1.7%, as the upward 
pressure from rapidly rising energy prices is offset by a temporary reduction in the levy on 
electricity and by declines in food prices. In 2027, inflation is projected to increase to 2.7% before 
moderating to 2.5% in 2028. While energy prices are expected to decline over the forecast horizon, 
the reversal of the temporary reduction in the electricity levy from 1 January 2028 is expected to 
exert upward pressure on domestic energy charges and inflation. Headline inflation excluding 
energy and food is forecast at 2.0% in 2026, increasing to 2.3% in 2027, before declining to 2.1% 
in 2028. 
Diminishing government budget surplus 
Denmark's budget surplus is set to decline to 1.1% of GDP in 2026, before falling further to 0.5% 
in 2027 and 0.3% in 2028. This is mainly due to increased government expenditures, particularly 
on defence and support for Ukraine. In 2026 and 2027 the budget revenue is set to be negatively 
affected by cuts in excise duties. Moreover, the continued roll-out of previously decided tax 
reforms implies lower tax rates in 2026 and 2027. The fiscal stance is expected to be clearly 
expansionary in 2026 and close to neutral in 2027 and 2028.  
Continued government surpluses and nominal GDP growth are set to reduce the gross debt-to-GDP 
ratio from 27.2% in 2025 to 26.2% in 2026 and further down to 25.7% in 2027 and 25.2% in 
2028.

=== PAGE 172 ===
24. HUNGARY 
 
156 
The economy is recovering after a prolonged stagnation 
In 2026-Q3, real GDP increased by 1.5% compared with the same period of the previous year. 
Industry and services supported growth, while agriculture contracted due to the prolonged summer 
drought. Overall, real GDP is expected to grow by 1.8% in 2026, with consumption as the main 
driver, while investment is projected to decline owing to cuts in public investment. 
GDP growth is forecast to pick up to 2.8% in 
2027 and to 2.9% in 2028. Consumption growth 
is set to remain strong, supported by sustained 
employment and wage growth, tax cuts for low-
earners and an increase in social benefits. 
Investment is expected to pick up, helped by 
improved business sentiment, the decline in 
Hungary’s risk premia and the restoration of 
access to EU funds following rule of law reforms. 
New assembly facilities in the automotive 
industry are expected to sustain export growth, 
despite moderating external demand. In turn, the 
current account is expected to remain negative, 
partly due to the resulting profit outflows for 
foreign firms. 
The most significant downside risk to the outlook is a slowdown in exports, either due to weakness 
in the automotive and electronics sectors, or due to a loss of cost competitiveness amid high wage 
growth or currency appreciation. On the upside, better governance and a more predictable business 
environment could support stronger private investment and productivity growth.  
Strong wage growth fuelled by a tightening labour market 
Employment is forecast to further increase by 0.4% in 2026, driven by higher self-employment, 
while the unemployment rate will edge upwards, to around 4.6%, amid a declining labour force. 
Employment is expected to continue growing gradually as economic growth gains strength, while 
the unemployment rate is projected to decline to around 4.0% by 2028. At the same time, nominal 
wage growth is set to moderate from around 10% in 2026 to 6.8% in 2027, as the effect of public 
wage increases and the 11% minimum wage hike fade. Continued productivity gains in 2027 and 
2028 are set to support wage growth. Nevertheless, wage growth is projected to remain elevated 
due to the tight labour market.  
Currency appreciation moderated the inflationary impact of rising energy prices 
HICP inflation declined from 4.4% in 2025 to 2.1% in September 2026, due to currency 
appreciation and temporary government price regulations. Inflation is set to increase in 2027, 
driven by rising energy and food inflation and an increase in excise duties on tobacco products in 
late 2026. It is expected to moderate in 2028, on the back of easing services inflation. However, 
the introduction of the ETS2 scheme is set to lift energy inflation in 2028. 
GDP is projected to grow by 1.8% in 2026 and by around 3% in 2027 and 2028, supported by 
sustained consumption growth and the recovery of investment and exports. HICP inflation is set to 
pick up from 2.2% in 2026 to 3.4% in 2027, driven by higher energy prices before declining to 
3% in 2028. The general government deficit is projected to rise to 7.3% of GDP in 2026 and to 
decrease to 5.2% in 2027 on the back of a significant expenditure-based consolidation. The 
deficit is expected to remain above 5% in 2028 and the debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to increase 
to 78.2% by 2028.

Non-EA Member States, Hungary 
 
157 
Consolidation in 2027 to reduce the high budget deficit 
In 2026, the budget deficit is projected to increase significantly to 7.3% of GDP, from 4.7% in 
2025. The increase is driven by a 4pps. increase in expenditure to 51.6% of GDP. This is explained 
to some extent by the partial introduction of a 14th month pension, new housing support subsidies, 
and temporary measures totalling 1.5% of GDP, including a one-time bonus paid to military and 
law enforcement, tax repayments related to two EU Court of Justice decisions, and investments 
financed by RRF loans. Income tax revenue is projected to decline as a share of GDP due to the 
continued phase-in of the PIT exemption for mothers and an increase in the family tax allowance. 
Overall, the fiscal stance is expected to be strongly expansionary in 2026. 
In 2027, the deficit is projected to narrow to 5.2% of GDP, as the 2026 temporary expenditures no 
longer affect the deficit and expenditure cuts are implemented across all ministries. New measures 
targeting households and corporations are expected to increase revenue. These include a new 
wealth tax, an increase in CIT and an increase in excise duties on tobacco and spirits, totalling 0.6% 
of GDP. Revenues are also set to benefit from the extension into 2027 of several sectoral 
corporate taxes and the suspension of the gradual phase-in of the PIT exemption for mothers. 
These measures are projected to help offset the new deficit-increasing measures, including pay 
rises in the public sector, a higher family allowance, a PIT reduction for low earners and pension 
increases, amounting to 1.5% of GDP. Overall, current expenditure is expected to fall by 0.4% of 
GDP. Interest expenditure is projected to decline as a share of GDP, as sovereign yields fell in the 
run-up to the April 2026 elections and have remained at that lower level. The fiscal stance is 
expected to be contractionary in 2027.  
In 2028, the deficit is projected to remain elevated at 5.3% of GDP, in part due to the expiry of 
sectoral corporate taxes. The fiscal stance is expected to be expansionary in 2028.  
The debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to increase over the forecast horizon from 74.4% in 2025 to 
78.2% in 2028. The increase in 2026 mainly reflects the large deficit, alongside the RRF loans, 
despite a revaluation of foreign-denominated debt due to recent currency appreciation. In 2027 
and 2028, the debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to continue rising on the back of persistently high 
deficits.

=== PAGE 174 ===
25. POLAND 
 
158 
Growth to moderate over the forecast horizon 
In 2026, real GDP is forecast to grow by 3.5%, slightly lower than in 2025. Economic growth is set 
to be driven primarily by private consumption, supported by robust growth of real disposable 
incomes. Investment is projected to contribute positively, reflecting a high absorption of EU funds 
and a pick-up in construction investment in the first half of the year. The negative contribution 
from net exports is set to narrow substantially compared to the previous year as signalled by the 
strong export performance in the first half of 2026.  
In 2027, economic growth is projected to slow to 
2.7%. Higher inflation, lower growth of real 
disposable income and weaker confidence are set 
to weigh on private consumption. The positive 
contribution from investment is forecast to 
decrease due to lower public investment. The 
contribution from net exports, slightly negative in 
2026, is projected to become broadly neutral.  
GDP growth is set to remain unchanged at 2.7% 
in 2028. Private consumption is projected to 
remain a key driver of growth, supported by a 
decline in the saving rate. Investment and public 
consumption are expected to add less to growth, 
while the contribution from net exports is set to 
increase slightly. 
Labour market to remain robust 
Employment is projected to remain broadly stable over the forecast horizon. Demographic 
developments are expected to weigh on labour supply and to be only partly offset by an inflow of 
workers from abroad. The unemployment rate is expected to remain at around 3%. Growth of 
nominal compensation per employee is projected to edge down from 6.7% in 2026 to 6.3% in 
2028. This reflects assumed smaller increases of the minimum wage and public sector salaries 
compared to previous years. 
Inflation to increase amid energy price volatility 
HICP inflation is projected to rise from 3.3% in 2026 to 4.4% in 2027, and to decrease to 3.9% in 
2028. The immediate impact of the surge in global energy commodity prices in 2026 is mitigated 
by stable regulated household tariffs for electricity and gas and by a temporary reduction in fuel 
taxes. Inflation is set to rise in 2027 as energy support measures are assumed to end while gas 
and electricity tariffs are to be adjusted upwards. Inflation excluding energy and food is projected 
to remain broadly stable, at about 3% over the forecast horizon, as services inflation is forecast to 
moderate only very gradually amid continued robust wage growth and a limited pass-through from 
higher fuel prices. In 2028, an upward impulse to inflation comes again from the energy 
component with the entry into operation of ETS2.  
Economic growth in Poland is set to remain strong at 3.5% in 2026 and to moderate to 2.7% in 
2027 and 2028. In 2027, the positive contribution from investment growth, including from EU-
funded projects, is set to fade, alongside weaker private consumption growth in the context of 
renewed inflation pressures. Inflation is forecast to increase from 3.3% in 2026 to 4.4% in 2027, 
and to moderate to 3.9% in 2028. The general government deficit is projected to remain elevated 
at above 7% of GDP over the forecast horizon. The debt-to-GDP ratio is set to increase to 73.5% 
of GDP in 2028, driven by high government deficits.

Non-EA Member States, Poland 
 
159 
Deficit to stay elevated 
The general government deficit is projected to slightly decrease from 7.2% of GDP in 2025 to 
7.1% of GDP in 2026. On the expenditure side, this development reflects lower nationally financed 
public investment combined with higher social benefits and increased debt servicing costs. On the 
revenue side, stronger revenues from taxes on income, partly reflecting tax increases, are offset by 
lower VAT and excise duty collections, due to the fuel tax cuts (-0.3% of GDP) and other factors 
weighing on revenue performance. An earlier delivery of military equipment could lead to a higher 
government deficit in 2026.  
In 2027, the government deficit is forecast to remain unchanged at 7.1% of GDP. Nationally 
financed spending is set to increase largely due to rising debt servicing costs and defence 
expenditure. Government revenues are projected to increase due to the expiration of fuel tax cuts, 
increases in excise duties, and changes to the corporate income tax. Some of the revenue-
increasing measures included in the budget face uncertainty in the national legislative process and, 
together with a possible further extension of lower fuel taxes, the balance of risks points to a 
higher deficit in 2027 and 2028.  
In 2028, the government deficit is set to slightly increase to 7.2% of GDP, assuming unchanged 
policies. This mainly reflects a projected further growth of debt servicing costs and continued high 
defence expenditures.  
The public debt is projected to increase from 59.6% of GDP in 2025 to 73.5% of GDP by 2028, 
mainly driven by high deficits. The stock-flow adjustments are projected to increase debt in 2026 
and 2028, including due to the pre-financing of future defence equipment deliveries, and to reduce 
debt in 2027 as liquid cash reserves accumulated in the previous year are used.

=== PAGE 176 ===
26. ROMANIA 
 
160 
Economy set to exit a mild recession in 2027  
Following necessary fiscal consolidation measures, Romania’s economic activity declined in the last 
quarter of 2025 and in the first half of 2026. Private consumption fell by more than 2% on the 
back of frozen public wages and pensions, while inflation at almost 10% reduced disposable 
income. Private investment was dampened by a prolonged period of political instability, economic 
uncertainty and rising energy costs. In the second half of 2026, economic activity is expected to 
stabilise thanks to a gradual recovery in household demand, resilient exports and still strong gross 
fixed capital formation, in in part due to the finalisation of RRP projects. For the whole of 2026, 
real GDP is projected to contract by around 0.3%. 
In 2027, falling inflation and some increases in 
pensions and public wages are projected to 
support 
real 
disposable 
income, 
economic 
sentiment and private consumption. However, 
growth of gross fixed capital formation is 
forecast to decelerate as the effect of RRP 
investments subsides. At the same time, higher 
absorption of EU cohesion funds and an expected 
stabilisation and recovery of private investment, 
backed by increased confidence thanks to lower 
political uncertainty, are projected to support 
investment. Overall, real GDP is set to grow by 
1.8% in 2027 and accelerate further to 2.2% in 
2028. With inflation declining, monetary policy is 
expected to be eased over the forecast horizon, 
yielding more favourable financing conditions and supporting credit growth. The start of natural 
gas exploitation at the Neptun Deep project is expected to support real GDP growth while reducing 
the current account deficit, with a more pronounced effect in 2028. Over the forecast horizon, the 
current account deficit is projected to decline steadily to less than 6% of GDP in 2028.  
Risks to the growth forecast are tilted to the downside. A resurgence of political instability could 
hamper medium-term fiscal consolidation, erode confidence and private investment. In addition, 
uncertainty regarding external demand, in particular for the automotive sector, and concerns over 
high energy prices remain a challenge for the manufacturing sector. 
Rising unemployment and wage growth moderation  
Labour market conditions started to deteriorate already in 2025 amid subdued economic activity. 
The decline in employment which led to a pick-up in the unemployment rate is set to continue also 
in 2026. The unemployment rate is projected to peak at around 6.7% in 2026 and decline 
gradually over the forecast horizon in line with the rebound in economic activity. Following the 
two-year public wage freeze in 2025 and 2026, nominal wage growth decelerated rapidly, while 
After subdued growth in 2025, the Romanian economy is expected to contract somewhat in 2026 
as fiscal consolidation is weighing on private consumption. RRF-backed public investment has 
accelerated, partly offsetting weak consumer demand. A recovery in private and public 
consumption, together with a gradual pick-up in private investment is expected to lift real GDP 
growth to around 2% in 2027 and 2028. The offshore Neptun Deep gas project and increased 
cost competitiveness are set to support exports, reducing the external deficit further. The 
unemployment rate is forecast to decline again as the economic recovery picks up in 2027, while 
inflation is set to moderate after high levels recorded in 2026. The general government deficit is 
projected to narrow from 6% of GDP in 2026 to 5.6% of GDP in 2028, while the debt-to-GDP 
ratio is set to increase to 64% by 2028, as government deficits still outpace nominal GDP growth.

Non-EA Member States, Romania 
 
161 
real wages declined in these years. Wage growth moderation is expected to continue over the 
forecast horizon, keeping the growth of real unit labour costs low and supporting external 
competitiveness. 
Protracted decline in inflation  
Inflation accelerated in the second half of 2025, driven by the rise in VAT and excises, together 
with the elimination of the cap on electricity prices for households. Price increases were further 
exacerbated in 2026 by the surge in global oil prices and the severe drought impacting the 
electricity market. Average HICP inflation is projected to increase to 7.9% in 2026 after 6.8% in 
2025. Despite a gradual decline in global oil prices, underlying inflationary pressures are expected 
to dissipate slowly in 2027, keeping the HICP inflation rate at 4.4% on average, above the central 
bank target band (2.5% +/-1 pp). The introduction of the ETS2 in 2028 is expected to slow the 
disinflation process, with average HICP inflation only decelerating to 3.8%. 
Government deficit to remain high 
Romania’s government deficit is projected to decline to 6.0% of GDP in 2026, from 7.8% of GDP in 
2025. This reflects the impact of the fiscal consolidation measures undertaken in the second part 
of 2025 and strict control in budget execution so far this year. Current expenditure as a share of 
GDP is set to decline by around 1 pp. of GDP this year, driven by the nominal freeze in pensions 
and public wages. On the revenue side, the implementation of tax increases adopted in 2024 and 
2025 (VAT, property taxation, health contribution) is set to increase revenue by 1.2 pps. of GDP.  
In 2027, the deficit is projected to decline to 5.8% of GDP, driven by continued control over current 
spending and lower public capital expenditure after the end of the RRF, in particular its grant 
support. Defence expenditure is set to increase over the forecast horizon from 1.7% of GDP in 
2025 to 2.2% of GDP in 2028, supported by loans under the Security Action for Europe (SAFE) 
programme. Government debt is forecast to increase from about 59% of GDP in 2025 to around 
64% of GDP in 2028, mostly driven by high government primary deficits and interest payments.

=== PAGE 178 ===
27. SWEDEN 
 
162 
Real GDP growth is projected to reach 2.8% in 2026, reflecting a broad-based upturn in 
domestic demand. Growth is expected to moderate over the forecast horizon and reach 1.5% in 
2028 as higher inflation and interest rates, coupled with expiry of fiscal support measures, is set 
to dampen growth in household disposable income. Inflation is expected to increase to 2.8% in 
2027 and reach 2.4% in 2028 as tax cuts expire and energy and import prices rise. The labour 
market is set to follow the expansion in economic activity with a lag, although unemployment 
remains elevated. Driven by lower taxes and continued strong investment, notably in defence, 
the general government deficit is projected to widen to 2.1% of GDP in 2026, before falling to 
1.4% of GDP in 2027 and 0.9% of GDP in 2028. The gross debt-to-GDP ratio is forecast to 
stabilise at around 35% over the forecast horizon. 
 
 
Domestic-demand-driven expansion slows 
Real GDP growth is set to accelerate in 2026, 
driven by broad-based strength in domestic 
demand. Gains in real household income, 
supported by low inflation and substantial pro-
cyclical fiscal easing are expected to drive strong 
private consumption growth. Private investment, 
including in data centres, is underpinned by 
healthy profitability and muted wage costs. In 
2027 and 2028, the pace of the expansion is 
expected to moderate as higher inflation and 
increases in interest rates are set to dampen 
domestic demand. Defence expenditure, including 
on investment items, is projected to grow 
dynamically over the forecast horizon. Terms of trade losses and a challenging international 
environment are expected to limit the contribution of exports to economic activity. Overall, real 
GDP growth is forecast to moderate from 2.8% in 2026 to 2.2% in 2027 and 1.5% in 2028. 
Labour market to follow economic activity with a lag 
The recent economic recovery has largely been productivity-driven, allowing firms to expand 
production without markedly increasing employment. In 2026, employment gains are being mainly 
limited to government agencies and regional and local governments. Unemployment is expected to 
fall over the forecast horizon as the output gap closes and the economic recovery becomes more 
self-sustaining, prompting firms to expand employment. With employment strengthening, the 
unemployment rate is projected to fall gradually to 7.7% in 2028. Some of measured 
unemployment is due to students who are seeking part-time and holiday jobs while remaining in 
full-time education. Despite accounting for these measurement-related factors, structural 
unemployment is set to remain elevated, partly due to persistent skills mismatches and gaps in the 
education system.  
Inflation dynamics affected by interactions of fiscal factors and import prices 
Over the forecast period, consumer price inflation is shaped by changes in taxation, energy and 
import prices and their interaction. Temporary tax reductions, notably for liquid fuel, helped contain 
energy prices in 2026. The largest temporary reduction in taxes on liquid fuels is set to expire in 
December 2026. Temporary lower prices for monthly public transport passes are to end at the 
same time. The temporary decrease in VAT on food from 12% to 6% from April 2026 has led to 
appreciably lower food prices, but this measure is set to expire on 1 January 2028. The lagged 
impact of higher energy prices is also expected to add to inflation in 2027 and 2028. However, 
gains in negotiated wages are projected to remain stable at just above 3% throughout the forecast

and to increase to 2.8% in 2027 before falling to 2.4% in 2028 as input cost increases, including 
from energy, are projected to moderate. 
General government deficit to fall modestly  
The general government deficit is set to increase to 2.1% of GDP in 2026. The expansive fiscal 
stance is partly due to higher spending on defence and support to Ukraine, but also to sizeable cuts 
in tax rates on petrol and diesel. The increase in the deficit has further been driven by other 
measures on the revenue side, notably lower income taxes, the VAT reduction on food, and 
housing-related tax expenditures.  
In 2027, the general government deficit is projected to decrease to 1.4% of GDP. In the follow-up 
to the recent general elections, the draft 2027 budget has not been presented to the Swedish 
Parliament before the cut-off date of the forecast. Interest payments are set to rise in 2027 and 
2028 in view of higher interest rates. Against this backdrop and in the absence of additional 
spending plans and the expiry of the food VAT reduction, the deficit is forecast to fall to just below 
1% of GDP in 2028. The debt-to-GDP ratio is set to remain broadly stable over the forecast 
horizon and is projected to reach 35% by 2028.

=== PAGE 182 ===
28. ALBANIA 
 
166 
Growth set to moderate but remain solid  
Economic activity remained solid in 2025, with real GDP increasing by 3.7%. Despite global 
uncertainty, the economy stayed resilient in the first half of 2026, growing by around 3.6-3.7% y-
o-y. Domestic demand remained the main growth driver. Private consumption was robust, 
underpinned by marked real wage growth and credit expansion. Investment growth slowed but is 
expected to pick up in the second half of the year. Services exports continued to expand, while 
goods exports recovered after three years of decline, driven by metals and energy, the latter 
supported by favourable hydropower conditions. Construction and services expanded, but 
agriculture kept contracting and industry remained weak, while manufacturing continued to decline.  
Growth is projected at 3.4% in 2026 and is set to 
moderate to 3.3% in 2027 and 2028 amid higher 
inflation, slower growth in key EU partners and 
tightening labour supply. Private consumption is 
expected to grow steadily, driven by rising wages, 
low 
unemployment, 
and 
favourable 
credit 
conditions, although higher inflation is expected 
to weigh on incomes, particularly in 2027. 
Investment 
is 
forecast 
to 
remain 
robust, 
underpinned by credit expansion and inflows from 
the EU Reform and Growth Facility, though 
persistent under-execution of public investment 
remains a risk. Tourism is projected to remain a 
key driver, although its growth rate is set to slow 
as the sector matures. The current account deficit 
is projected to widen from its historical low in 2025, as weaker goods exports and a shrinking 
services surplus cause import growth to outpace exports. It is however expected to remain fully 
covered by FDI inflows. 
As a small open economy, Albania faces external risks from global uncertainty and geopolitical 
tensions, which are expected to affect growth through key trading partners in the EU. Domestic 
risks include the concentration of growth in tourism, construction and real estate, vulnerability to 
weather shocks, and contingent liabilities. Progress in EU accession negotiations is an upside risk to 
growth, notably through improved economic sentiment. 
Employment set to recover modestly amid labour supply constraints 
After two years of improvement, the employment rate declined slightly in the first half of 2026, 
amid continued emigration and population ageing. Labour is shifting away from agriculture 
towards services, while foreign workers are increasingly filling shortages. Employment is expected 
to recover moderately over 2027-28, with participation rising as a result of higher wage growth, 
although wage growth is expected to ease as the impact of the minimum wage increase fades. 
Albania’s economic growth is expected to moderate slightly, from 3.4% in 2026 to 3.3% in 
both 2027 and 2028. Higher inflation, slower growth in key EU partners and a tightening 
labour supply are set to weigh on activity, while private consumption and investment are 
expected to be the main drivers of growth. Inflation is projected to peak in early 2027, driven 
by higher food and import prices, before returning to the central bank’s target of 3% in 2028. 
Tourism-led services exports are forecast to keep growing, albeit at a slower pace, while the 
current account deficit is set to widen from its 2025 historical low as the services surplus 
narrows and goods exports weaken. The general government deficit is set to edge up to around 
2% of GDP before narrowing in 2028, while public debt continues to decline to around 49% of 
GDP in 2028.

Candidate Countries, Albania 
 
167 
After reaching a record low in 2025, the unemployment rate is expected to remain stable, although 
youth unemployment picked up again in the first half of 2026. 
Inflation temporarily above target 
After two years of contained inflation in 2024 and 2025, price pressures strengthened in 2026. 
Driven by rising food, transport and housing prices, consumer price inflation rose above the Bank 
of Albania’s 3% target, reaching 3.4% in July. Domestic pressures stemmed from strong wage 
growth and the rental market, where a shift from long- to short-term rentals pushed up rents and 
core inflation. The pass-through of higher energy prices linked to the Middle East conflict was 
partly contained by the continued appreciation of the lek and well-anchored inflation expectations. 
The Bank of Albania has kept its policy rate at 2.5% since July 2025. Inflation is expected to peak 
in early 2027 on higher food prices and imported inflation, before returning to target in 2028 as oil 
and food prices normalise. 
Fiscal deficit to edge up before narrowing in 2028 
In 2025, the general government deficit widened 
to 1.8% of GDP, while remaining below the target. 
In the first nine months of 2026, revenue grew 
strongly, in line with the plan, while expenditure 
was slightly below plan, with capital expenditure 
reaching 75.2% of the budgeted amount.  
Looking ahead, the deficit is projected to widen 
slightly to around 2% of GDP in 2026 and 2027, 
remaining below the authorities’ target of 2.3%, 
before narrowing in 2028. The primary balance is 
expected to remain neutral or in slight surplus 
over the forecast horizon, in line with the fiscal 
rule. Public debt fell below 53% of GDP in 2025 
and is projected to keep declining through 2028, 
supported by favourable nominal GDP growth.

=== PAGE 184 ===
29. BOSNIA AND HERZEGOVINA 
 
168 
Economic growth to remain moderate  
Economic output rose by 2.3% in the first half of 2026, mainly due to strong private consumption, 
which reflected strong real wage growth and higher public spending in the run-up to the October 
general elections. Investment growth remained subdued amid elevated uncertainties related to the 
current geopolitical situation and the outcome of the elections. Net financial inflows, largely in the 
form of transfers — in particular remittances —remained stable, at nearly 10% of GDP. The 
current-account deficit widened slightly, mainly due to lower export revenues. Net inflows of 
foreign direct investment were also slightly lower than a year earlier.  
Over the forecast period, economic growth is 
projected to remain muted. This reflects subdued 
external and domestic demand, as well as the 
impact of delayed structural reforms, which could 
gradually hamper the country’s international 
competitiveness. Private consumption is expected 
to expand modestly, supported by solid growth in 
real disposable income. Export growth is also 
expected to remain subdued, both due to 
moderate growth in the country’s key export 
markets 
and 
to 
deteriorating 
price 
competitiveness resulting from recent wage 
increases outpacing productivity growth. Overall, 
GDP growth is expected to remain stable in the 
second half of 2026, before picking up slightly in 
2027 and 2028 as inflationary pressures and 
geopolitical uncertainties are expected to ease.  
Short-term risks are tilted to the upside, while medium-term risks are on the downside. A swift 
formation of the new governments might improve business and consumer confidence and 
contribute to stronger growth in the near term. However, a lengthy process and delays in pursuing 
needed structural reforms could result in a growth trajectory which remains below the country’s 
potential. 
A shrinking labour supply affects the labour market  
Registered employment growth came to a standstill in the first half of 2026, reflecting declining 
employment in mining and agriculture, while trade and tourism still registered employment gains. 
The number of unemployed persons kept declining, partly due to continued labour market exits. 
Slow output growth is projected to accelerate labour shedding in some sectors, such as 
manufacturing. At the same time, a continued outflow of qualified workers is expected to lead to 
labour shortages in certain sectors such as construction and healthcare. This is set to fuel further 
wage increases, outpacing productivity growth. 
Economic output is forecast to grow by 2.3% in 2026, supported by solid domestic demand but 
constrained by weak external demand. In the first half of 2026, output growth remained stronger 
than expected, at 2.3% y-o-y, reflecting high real wage growth and higher public spending. 
However, inflation remained high. In 2027 and 2028, GDP growth is projected to pick up slightly, 
with domestic demand expected to benefit from declining inflation, but external demand 
projected to remain subdued. Labour shortages are expected to remain significant, keeping wage 
increases above productivity growth and thereby weakening the country’s price competitiveness.

Candidate Countries, Bosnia and Herzegovina 
 
169 
Inflation to benefit from lower import prices  
Inflation accelerated from 4% in 2025 to 4.6% in the first eight months of 2026, mainly driven by 
higher prices of food, restaurants, hotels, and health services. Meanwhile, nominal wages rose by 
8.6% in the first eight months of 2026, which translates into real wage growth of some 4%, still 
significantly above productivity growth. With labour market shortages in some sectors persisting, 
wage pressures are expected to remain high. Adding to inflationary pressures, the delayed effects 
of higher energy prices due to the conflict in the Middle East are set to keep consumer price 
inflation elevated in 2026. However, the expected decline in energy prices in 2027 and 2028 is set 
to help bringing down inflation in that period.   
Public finances to deteriorate in 2026 and 2027  
In 2026, the deficit as a percentage of GDP is 
forecast to increase markedly, from 2.0% in 2025 
to around 4% in 2026. In view of strong revenue 
increases resulting from higher wages and prices, 
spending plans for 2026 have been revised 
upwards even more, in particular in the Republika 
Srpska entity. Public sector wages, pensions and 
social transfers have increased even further, 
partly in connection with the general elections in 
October. Due to the permanent nature of many of 
the adopted spending measures and so far 
absent concrete plans for a sustainable financing 
of those measures, the general government 
deficit is expected to remain high. In 2027, lower 
nominal GDP growth is likely to lead to lower 
revenue growth, resulting in a further deficit 
increase. Those deficits will contribute to a rise in the country’s debt-to-GDP ratio well above 30% 
by 2028. Limited compliance with EU accounting standards undermines the accuracy and reliability 
of public sector data.

=== PAGE 186 ===
30. GEORGIA 
 
170 
Robust economic growth 
GDP growth remained strong, reaching 7.8% in the first half of 2026, compared with 7.5% in 
2025. Private consumption was the main driver of growth, supported by strong retail lending, 
higher remittance inflows and continued - albeit moderating - wage growth. Private investment 
also continued to expand. Merchandise exports increased markedly in the first half of 2026, driven 
largely by oil products, whose share rose from 1% to 12% following the opening of a new oil 
refinery. As imports grew at a slower pace over the same period, net exports contributed positively 
to GDP growth. On the supply side, growth was driven by services (mainly information technology, 
trade, transport and financial services) as well as by manufacturing, while output in agriculture and 
construction contracted. Overall, in 2026, GDP growth is projected to reach 7.3%. 
Looking ahead, growth is projected to moderate 
towards its long-term potential of 5.5%–6% in 
2027 and 2028. Growth is expected to remain 
broad-based across components, with private 
consumption making the largest contribution, 
supported by strong consumer lending and 
continued remittance inflows. Investment growth 
is also set to remain robust, underpinned by the 
expected recovery of delayed public projects and 
resilient private investment. The trade deficit is 
forecast to widen, as gradual improvements in 
export capacity are unlikely to offset the impact 
of rising imports driven by strong consumer and 
investment demand. However, the current account 
deficit is expected to remain below 3% of GDP, 
supported by increasing remittances and a surplus in services, particularly in ICT and tourism. The 
forecast is subject to both downside and upside risks resulting from both geopolitical 
developments and the domestic political situation. The duration and intensity of the Middle East 
conflict impact not only the energy prices, but also Georgia’s tourism revenues and financial 
inflows from Israel and the Gulf region. An end to Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine could 
potentially reverse some of the gains for the country from migration and transit trade, but the 
benefits of greater regional stability could offset these effects. 
Stagnant labour market  
Labour market indicators remained broadly unchanged since 2025 and in contrast to steady 
improvements seen in the years up to 2024. This points to weak job creation despite robust 
economic growth and persistent informality. In the second quarter of 2026, the number of people 
employed remained unchanged compared with a year earlier, and the unemployment rate 
remained high at 13.8%. Significant structural weaknesses persist in the labour market, including a 
mismatch between workers’ skills and business needs, as well as high youth unemployment. 
However, in the context of the ongoing reform of vocational education and employment services, 
Economic growth is expected to remain strong, exceeding 7% in 2026 and decelerating to 5.5%-
6% in 2027 and 2028. Growth is anticipated to remain broad-based, with the main contribution 
from private consumption. Despite robust growth, unemployment is projected to remain high, 
indicating that significant structural labour market weaknesses persist. Inflation is set to pick up 
in 2026 due to rising energy prices, but price pressures are expected to ease in 2027. The general 
government deficit is set to remain low over the forecast horizon, between 1% and 2% of GDP, 
while public debt is forecast to decline to 33% of GDP this year and continue decreasing gradually 
over the forecast horizon.

Candidate Countries, Georgia 
 
171 
labour market indicators are expected to improve gradually. Real wage growth slowed from 6.7% 
in 2025 to 3.1% y-o-y in the first half of 2026, which was below productivity growth in the 
Georgian economy. It is projected to stay moderate in 2027 and 2028. 
Temporary uptick in inflation  
Consumer price inflation rose to 5.9% y-o-y in October 2026, up from 3.9% in 2025. The increase 
was driven mainly by a surge in global energy prices following the outbreak of conflict in the 
Middle East. Core inflation, excluding energy and food, remained significantly lower than CPI, at 
4.0% in October 2026. In May 2026, the central bank raised its key policy rate by 25 bps. to 
8.25%, marking the first increase in two years. Overall, inflation is projected to average 5.4% in 
2026. As the current inflationary pressures are expected to ease gradually, inflation is forecast to 
return to the 3% target in the second half of 2027 and remain stable in 2028. 
Low fiscal deficit and public debt 
In the first eight months of 2026, general government revenue was 13% higher y-o-y, boosted by 
strong growth and higher inflation. Current expenditure rose by 10% over the same period, with 
the largest increase recorded in public sector wages. Capital expenditure fell by 3%, reflecting 
delays in public investment projects. The fiscal surplus in the first eight months of the year was 
higher y-o-y. Assuming the acceleration of public investment in the final months of 2026, the full-
year fiscal deficit is projected to reach around 1.3% of GDP in 2026. The deficit is then expected to 
widen, notably as public investment is expected to gather pace, yet to remain below 2% of GDP in 
both 2027 and 2028. General government debt is forecast to decline to 33% of GDP by the end of 
2026 and to continue on a slightly downward path in the following years, on the back of low fiscal 
deficits and robust nominal GDP growth.

=== PAGE 188 ===
31. MOLDOVA 
 
172 
Economic growth slowing amid increased geopolitical risks and inflationary pressures 
Economic growth slowed from 2.4% for the year 
2025 to 0.6% for the first half of 2026. Private 
consumption remained robust, supported by rising 
real 
wages 
and 
remittances. 
Net 
exports 
contributed positively to growth, mainly due to 
higher agricultural exports. This was offset by 
weaker investment, reflecting subdued business 
sentiment, delays in the Reform and Growth 
Facility and state aid-supported investment, and a 
scaled-back real estate support programme. 
Real GDP growth is forecast at 1.7% in 2026, as 
the impact of the Middle East conflict weighs on 
economic 
activity, 
notably 
through 
higher 
inflation, while a good harvest partly cushions the 
effect. Private consumption growth is set to 
remain robust, as high remittances, real wage growth and a one-off public salary increase for 
selected groups partly offset higher inflation. Private investment is projected to remain subdued 
amid tightening financing conditions. Exports are set to benefit from a good harvest, albeit 
dampened by the temporary reintroduction of import licensing for grains and seeds. In the first 
half of 2027, exports are expected to continue benefiting from the 2026 harvest, while 
persistently high inflation continues to weigh on domestic demand.  
As the impact of energy price pressures fades from the second half of 2027 onwards, growth is 
projected to accelerate gradually driven by domestic demand. The public salary reform and higher 
pensions are set to sustain consumption, partly offset by higher inflation stemming from VAT and 
excises increases under the tax reform. Investment is set to recover, supported by the extension of 
the zero-income tax rate on reinvested profits and by higher State aid. Investments under the 
Reform and Growth Facility are expected to accelerate markedly in 2028. Exports are projected to 
increasingly benefit from reform-driven diversification, particularly in 2028, while stronger 
domestic demand is set to drive higher imports. Net exports are, thus, expected to continue 
weighing on growth. The current-account deficit narrowed slightly due to a lower trade deficit 
during the first half of 2026. It is forecast to narrow further but to remain wide, reflecting the 
large structural trade deficit. Recorded remittances have strengthened and are projected to remain 
high, driven by sustained emigration and a probable shift towards formal transfer channels 
following Moldova's integration into the Single European Payments Area (SEPA). 
The forecast is subject to significant downside risks, including geopolitical risks linked to the Middle 
East conflict and Russia's war of aggression against Ukraine, as well as climate-related shocks, 
given the agriculture’s prominent role. On the upside, stronger absorption capacity could speed up 
investment and reforms under the Facility and reduce vulnerability to external shocks. 
Economic growth is projected to moderate to 1.7% in 2026, with a good harvest partly cushioning 
growing inflationary pressures fuelled by the Middle East conflict and lower-than-expected 
investment under the Reform and Growth Facility. Growth is expected to strengthen gradually to 
2.4% in 2027 and 3.3% in 2028, driven mainly by domestic demand. Private consumption is set 
to strengthen, reflecting rising real wages and remittances. Investment is forecast to accelerate, 
supported by tax incentives, other support measures and the Reform and Growth Facility. Net 
exports are expected to continue weighing on growth. The general government deficit is projected 
to further widen, reaching 5.1% of GDP in 2028, largely driven by the higher public sector wage 
bill and public investments. Government debt is set to increase to above 40% of GDP.

Candidate Countries, Moldova 
 
173 
Labour market to strengthen slowly amid gradual economic recovery 
Following the EU alignment of labour statistics in 2026, labour market indicators are no longer 
comparable with previous years. Despite these caveats, labour market participation remains low, 
reflecting significant seasonal employment abroad, while the female activity rate remained more 
than 10 pps. below the male rate in the first half of 2026. As growth strengthens, employment is 
expected to increase and unemployment to decline, supported by tight labour market conditions 
and efforts to tackle undeclared work. Real wage growth remained robust at 2.3% y-o-y in 2026-
Q2 and is expected to accelerate in 2027 on the back of the public wage reform. 
Inflation to accelerate largely driven by high imported energy prices  
Inflation has picked up markedly since the beginning of 2026, mainly reflecting price pressures 
stemming from the Middle East conflict and its impact on global energy markets. Inflation is 
forecast to increase to 8.2% in 2026, driven primarily by increases in regulated prices for energy 
and transport. The impact of higher energy costs for households is expected to be temporarily 
cushioned by energy in-bill support measures during winter 2026/27, while food price inflation is 
set to remain contained following a good harvest. Energy-related price pressures are projected to 
persist until mid-2027, with second-round effects lingering until the end of 2027. The tax reform 
and demand pressures from the public wage reform are set to add to inflationary pressures in 
2027. Inflation is set to return to the National Bank of Moldova’s target range of 5% ±1.5 pps. in 
2028, as energy price pressures unwind and base effects from the tax reform fade. 
Fiscal deficit set to widen due to higher personnel expenses and investments 
The general government deficit is projected to widen to 4.6% of GDP in 2026, from 4% in 2025, 
below the 6% target set in the September supplementary budget. In the first eight months of 
2026, revenue outperformed expectations, mainly due to higher-than-expected inflation, which 
offset lower international grants following exceptionally high energy related support from 
international partners in recent years. Expenditure remained under-executed, notably public 
investment under the Reform and Growth Facility. Over the forecast horizon, the budget deficit 
ratio is set to widen further as interest expenditure rises and personnel expenses linked to the 
public wage reform gradually increase. This is expected to be partly offset by net revenue gains 
from the tax reform and stronger growth, particularly in 2028. Public debt is projected to rise from 
40.7% of GDP in 2026 to 43.8% in 2028, driven by persistently high budget deficits.

=== PAGE 190 ===
32. MONTENEGRO 
 
174 
GDP growth to remain steady, with upside risks from policy measures  
Real GDP growth for 2025 was revised upwards from 2.7% to 3.3%. The economy expanded by 
3.1% y-o-y in the first half of 2026, driven by an acceleration in investment to 6.6% y-o-y. Growth 
in private consumption slowed to 2.6% y-o-y, as the positive impact on disposable income from 
the implementation of the Europe Now 2.0 programme in 2025 (which halved pension 
contributions and increased minimum wage) faded. Moreover, rising inflation constrained 
household purchasing power. Following a weak start into 2026, export growth turned positive in 
2026-Q2, while high-frequency tourism data point to further improvement during the summer. 
Meanwhile, import growth remained weak.   
Under a no-policy-change assumption, economic 
growth is expected to remain stable in 2026-28, 
hovering at around 3%. A deceleration in private 
consumption is projected to be offset by a 
recovery in exports and robust investment growth, 
driven by the implementation of major road and 
railway projects. The very large current account 
deficit is expected to narrow only marginally in 
2026-28, 
reflecting 
improving 
export 
performance, but to remain close to 20% of GDP.  
The economic outlook may change significantly 
as a result of the announced ‘Euro model’ reform, 
adopted by the government in September. This 
foresees the complete elimination of pension 
contributions and a substantial tiered increase in 
minimum wages based on education level. If adopted by the Parliament and implemented as 
currently planned, the reform would lead to a sharp rise in disposable income, boost private 
consumption, and trigger a surge in imports in 2027. In this case, GDP growth could exceed 6% in 
2027 but revert to some 3% in 2028. 
The balance of risks is tilted to the upside. While an uncertain global environment continues to 
pose downside risks, along with a slower-than-expected recovery in exports and volatile energy 
prices, the implementation of the ‘Euro Model’ measures presents a significant upside risk for GDP 
growth and inflation, potentially leading to overheating in the economy, high risk to fiscal 
sustainability and increased external vulnerabilities. Progress in EU accession negotiations is an 
upside risk to growth, notably through improved economic sentiment. 
Declining unemployment 
Employment gains, partly supported by the reduction of informal employment and sustained GDP 
growth, continued into 2026, with the unemployment rate declining to a new low of 9.4% in 2026-
Q2. Employment growth is expected to continue in 2027-28, albeit at a diminishing rate. 
Montenegro’s economic growth continued at around 3% in the first half of 2026, supported by 
increasing investment and, despite some deceleration, private consumption. Growth is set to 
remain broadly stable at around 3% between 2026 and 2028. The revised budget targets a 
deficit of 3.7% of GDP in 2026 and the deficit is projected to exceed 3% of GDP throughout the 
forecast period, while public debt is set to remain above 60% of GDP. The four-quarter current 
account deficit rose above 20% of GDP in the year to 2026-Q2 and it is set to remain broadly 
unchanged until 2028. If adopted by the Parliament, the announced increase in fiscal stimulus 
and minimum wage could lead to a higher fiscal deficit, faster GDP growth and higher inflation.

Candidate Countries, Montenegro 
 
175 
External prices drive inflation 
Inflation accelerated over the course of 2026, rising from 2.9% y-o-y in January to 5.2% y-o-y in 
September. The main drivers were prices of transport, accommodation, and alcoholic beverages. 
Inflation is expected to remain elevated in 2026 mainly due to external price pressures, although 
fuel price controls help to cushion the pass-through of higher oil prices to households, before 
moderating in 2027-28.  
Budget deficits to persist 
From January to August 2026, the budget 
recorded a deficit of 1.7% of projected GDP, up 
from 0.9% in the same period a year earlier and 
compared to a full-year deficit target of 3.7% of 
GDP. Revenue exceeded that target, driven by 
higher-than-expected receipts from personal 
income tax and social contributions, while VAT 
revenue increased in line with the plan. Spending 
was driven by a 50% y-o-y surge in public 
investment, as well as higher health spending and 
interest costs. In the absence of additional 
consolidation measures, the general government 
deficit is projected to decline only gradually and 
remain above 3% of GDP over 2026-28. Absent 
additional consolidation measures, public debt is 
projected to exceed 60% of GDP over the 
forecast horizon. Risks to the fiscal outlook are tilted strongly to the downside, notably due to the 
potential implementation of the ‘Euro Model’ reform, under which pension contributions, amounting 
to more than 4% of GDP in 2025, would be abolished, while the impact of the planned 
compensatory measures remains unclear.

=== PAGE 192 ===
33. NORTH MACEDONIA 
 
176 
Domestic demand to continue as growth driver 
Investment, supported by accelerated public infrastructure works, and household spending drove 
economic growth in the first half of 2026. Public consumption made only a limited contribution 
and net exports weighed on growth. Private consumption was bolstered by strong real wage 
growth, a further linear increase in pensions, buoyant credit growth, and the government’s 
temporary cuts to excise duties and VAT on fuel. The current account deficit widened, mainly as the 
trade balance worsened as a result of higher energy import costs and consumer goods imports. 
Looking ahead, GDP growth is projected to remain strong, with domestic demand as the sole driver 
and a gradually decelerating negative contribution from the external balance. Private consumption 
growth is expected to slow slightly, as the relief on fuel cost ran out in October, and the rise in 
pensions is projected to moderate with no further linear increases planned. Investment is set to be 
supported by the implementation of public infrastructure projects and by capacity expansions of 
established foreign firms. Given rising interest rates and stronger macroprudential measures to 
curb fast mortgage growth, residential investment is projected to be more muted. The current 
account is projected to gradually improve, as energy prices decline and the merchandise trade 
deficit narrows. 
 
Disinflation progresses slowly  
Driven by food and energy prices, headline 
inflation remained elevated in the first half of 
2026, before relenting over the summer, as food 
price rises slowed down. Core inflation also 
started to moderate. In June, the central bank 
raised the key policy rate by 25 bps. to 4.25%. 
Looking ahead, inflation is set to decline 
gradually, supported by prudent monetary policy, 
but to remain above the central bank’s target of 
2%. The growth of import prices is projected to 
slow down, but domestic demand-side pressures 
from rising disposable income, supported by 
expansionary fiscal policy, is expected to continue 
weighing on inflation at least this year and next.  
Structural challenges weigh on the labour market 
Headline indicators for the labour market continued to improve in the first half of 2026. The 
unemployment rate declined slightly year-on-year, while the labour force increased. Labour market 
participation rose but remains below EU average, at some 67%. The growth in gross nominal 
wages abated in the first half of the year. Yet, at 7.7% on average, it remains elevated, reflecting 
public sector collective agreements, labour shortages and a further rise in minimum wages taking 
effect in March 2026. Given persistent structural challenges in the labour market, it is expected 
that further improvements in the unemployment and the employment rate will be more moderate 
than in the past.  
Economic growth is projected to remain strong, driven by domestic demand. In the first half of 
2026, annual GDP growth accelerated to 3.7%, from 3.5% on average in 2025, supported by 
the implementation of public works and strong household spending. With food price inflation 
abating over the summer, headline inflation relented. It is projected to continue slowing, but to 
remain above the central bank’s target of 2%. The government postponed fiscal consolidation 
plans, raising the deficit target for 2026 from 3.5% of GDP to 4.1%. The deficit is projected to 
decrease over the forecast horizon, but to remain above the fiscal rule’s target.

Candidate Countries, North Macedonia 
 
177 
Fiscal consolidation is further postponed  
In the first nine months of 2026, strong revenue 
performance was outpaced by expenditure 
growth, in particular a large annual rise in capital 
spending, resulting in a sizeable increase in the 
budget deficit. In July, the government raised the 
deficit target for 2026 from 3.5% to 4.1% of GDP 
to cope with unforeseen spending pressures. The 
achievement of the deficit target of 3% of GDP, 
set in the fiscal rules, has been postponed to 
2028. The deficit is projected to decrease over 
the forecast horizon. On the revenue side, the 
government is implementing several reforms to 
boost tax compliance, such as e-invoicing and the 
digitalisation of the Public Revenue Office. On the 
expenditure side, it is committed to reign in 
operational 
expenditure 
and 
public 
wages. 
Regarding capital spending, it has recently shifted the financing of the remainder of the country’s 
part of the Pan-European Road Network (Corridor VIII and Xd) off-budget to the Public Enterprise 
for State Roads. The delayed fiscal consolidation is set to further increase the debt-to-GDP ratio.  
The growth outlook could be negatively impacted by a renewed rise in inflation, which would lower 
household and investment spending. Fiscal slippages, requiring higher than anticipated borrowing 
in an environment of rising interest rates, might have negative repercussions on private sector 
credit growth. The deferral of infrastructure projects may reduce the contribution of public 
investment to growth. On the positive side, the implementation of reforms to attract investment 
capital and to improve public investment management are set to support growth. These include a 
new framework for public-private partnerships and better control of fiscal risks. Implementing the 
structural reforms agreed in the EU’s Growth Plan is also expected to improve the growth outlook.

=== PAGE 194 ===
34. SERBIA 
 
178 
Domestic demand stimulated in the short run by several factors 
Economic growth is projected to strengthen in the short term, driven mainly by domestic demand, 
before moderating later in the forecast horizon. After slowing to 2.1% in 2025, GDP growth 
accelerated to 3.5% in the first half of 2026. This was supported mainly by strong private 
consumption, underpinned by rapid growth in wages, minimum wages and social transfers, 
household credit expansion of about 20% y-o-y, and slightly improving consumer confidence. 
Exports continued to grow solidly, led by a narrow set of sectors, notably electric cars, while 
imports grew even faster. 
Looking ahead, short-term indicators point to a 
continuation of this consumption-led growth 
pattern. Retail trade is expected to support 
growth, while most industrial sectors continue 
facing unfavourable global conditions. Household 
income is expected to be further boosted by the 
supplementary budget adopted in September, 
which provides significant one-off fiscal transfers 
to households in the second part of 2026. The 
economic effects of these transfers are set to 
carry over into 2027. Overall, GDP growth is 
forecast to reach 3.3% in 2026. In 2027, growth 
is expected to rise further to 3.8%, supported 
additionally by increased tourist inflows linked to 
the specialised EXPO 2027 in Belgrade (which is 
essentially a smaller version of World EXPO). In 2028, as these temporary stimulus factors fade, 
growth is forecast to slow to 2.9%, despite an assumed stabilisation in the global economic 
environment. In the short term, upside risks to the GDP prevail from strong momentum in private 
consumption. However, the outlook is subject to downside risks in the medium term, as the current 
state-administrated stimulus factors are inherently temporary and their unwinding could cool 
domestic demand more substantially than assumed. Structurally, rapid wage growth and the 
decline in foreign direct investment since 2025 could weaken external competitiveness and weigh 
on export performance.  
Wage growth has outpaced productivity growth for several years 
In the first half of 2026, Serbia’s labour market performance showed mixed developments. Both 
employment and unemployment declined, while the activity rate fell. This points to a shrinking 
labour supply due to population ageing. As a result, employment is expected to stagnate, while 
labour market remains tight. Wage growth was strong in the first half of 2026, with nominal 
wages up by 11% y-o-y. This was driven partly by rapid increases in the minimum wage, which 
rose by a cumulative 20% in October 2025 and January 2026, and are set to further rise by 9.2% 
in January 2027. Wage growth was notably rapid in some publicly funded sectors, like education 
In the first half of 2026, Serbia’s economy performed stronger than expected, driven by domestic 
demand. Real GDP growth is forecast to rise from 2.1% in 2025 to 3.3% in 2026 and 3.8% in 
2027, underpinned by household consumption and boosted by the specialised EXPO 2027 in 
Belgrade. These factors are also expected to add to inflationary pressures. As broad-based 
support to domestic demand fades in 2028, growth is projected to moderate to 2.9%, even if the 
external environment stabilises as assumed. The fiscal deficit is set to reach 3.5% of GDP in 
2026, reflecting a pre-election stimulus of around 1% of GDP and a cut to fuel excise duties. 
Thereafter, the deficit is projected to decline gradually to below 3% by 2028, while public debt is 
forecast to remain broadly stable at around 44% of GDP over the forecast horizon.

Candidate Countries, Serbia

and health, while it remained below average in the public administration as a whole. Wage growth 
is expected to remain strong and well above productivity growth in 2026 and 2027, decelerating 
by 2028 in line with the economy, assuming no additional administrative measures. 
Inflation has remained contained despite higher global energy prices 
Inflation remained relatively stable in the first half of 2026, despite the global energy price shock 
and the expiry of retail price margin caps, which limited margins for most food categories to 20% 
between September 2025 and February 2026. Inflation averaged only 2.7% between January and 
August 2026, contained by a good harvest for fruit and vegetables and substantial cuts to energy 
taxation. Inflation rose to 4.1% in September, reflecting base effects associated with the retail 
margin caps introduced a year earlier and higher global energy prices. For 2026, average inflation 
is forecast at 3.3%. However, core inflation, excluding energy and food, is significantly higher, 
reaching 4.5% around mid-2026, driven mainly by services prices. In 2027, inflation is projected to 
rise to 4.2%, as robust household demand is expected to offset the anticipated decline in global 
energy prices. By 2028, inflation is forecast to ease to 3.4%, assuming a continued decline in 
global fuel prices and some moderation in domestic demand. 
Deficit temporarily exceeds target 
Based on preliminary figures, general government revenue rose strongly by around 12% y-o-y in 
the first eight months of 2026, even with cuts to fuel excise duties. However, broad-based 
expenditure growth of nearly 14% exceeded revenue growth, and the deficit was 0.5 pps. higher 
than a year earlier. The supplementary budget adopted in September 2026 increased expenditure 
by EUR 1 bn (1% of GDP), mainly through transfers to households. This pushed the deficit to 3.5% 
of GDP in 2026, above the 3% fiscal ceiling. Strong projected economic growth and related tax 
revenue are expected to bring the deficit below the ceiling by 2027. It is expected to decline 
further to 2.7% by 2028, assuming a return to prudent fiscal policies. Serbia’s public debt is 
forecast to moderate slightly from 44.2% of GDP in 2025 to 43.6% by 2028, as robust nominal 
GDP growth offsets the impact of the accumulation of fiscal deficits. However, downside risks 
persist, as the time-critical EXPO 2027 projects may be subject to unforeseen cost overruns.

35. TÜRKIYE 
 
180 
The conflict in the Middle East weighs on economic growth 
Real GDP growth slowed in the first half of 2026 as the impact of the conflict in the Middle East 
affected all components of domestic demand. Higher energy prices and tight monetary policy 
weighed on private consumption. Regional instability hit tourism and exports. In addition, in 
September 2026, Türkiye’s equity market came under liquidity pressure as tighter regulation, 
introduced in response to alleged market manipulation, forced investment funds to unwind 
concentrated positions. Investors responded by increasing redemption requests, which some funds 
were unable to meet. The government acted swiftly to contain the impact of the financial market 
turmoil but could not prevent the BIST 100 index from falling. The turmoil adds to rising domestic 
political tensions and poses a downside risk to private consumption and investment in the near 
term. At the same time, the central bank cut its policy rate in October 2026, which could support 
domestic demand more than currently expected. 
Economic growth is expected to reach 2.8% in 
2026, before rebounding to 3.5% in 2027 and 
3.9% in 2028 as the effects of the conflict in the 
Middle East gradually fade. Domestic demand is 
expected to remain the main driver of growth 
over the forecast horizon. Private consumption 
and investment were held back in 2026 by high 
inflation, high interest rates, and elevated 
uncertainty, but are set to gradually rebound in 
the following years as the economic environment 
becomes more favourable.  
In 2026, net exports are expected to have a 
smaller negative contribution to growth than in 
2025, as imports have contracted as well. As of 
2027, net exports are projected to gradually recover alongside increases in both exports and 
imports. Supported by subdued domestic demand and favourable terms-of-trade effects, the 
current-account deficit is set to narrow in 2027 and 2028 after a marked widening in 2026. 
Türkiye’s external position remains sound, but fragile amid the ongoing financial market turmoil, 
continued exchange rate depreciation, and uncertainty over energy prices and trade disruptions 
linked to the conflict in the Middle East. If energy prices stay elevated for longer than assumed in 
the baseline, or if domestic demand picks up more strongly than projected, the narrowing of the 
current-account deficit could stall. Although the balance of risks to growth and external imbalances 
is on the downside, the recent track record of sound policies, and higher foreign exchange reserves 
have helped Türkiye weather the challenges of the conflict so far. 
The labour market remains resilient 
In the first half of 2026, the employment rate remained stable at around 55%, while the 
unemployment rate fell to 8%, reflecting a small decline in the participation rate. Over the forecast 
horizon, employment growth is projected to pick up modestly and the unemployment rate to 
Economic growth is expected to slow to just below 3% in 2026. Domestic demand is weakening in 
a context of tight monetary and fiscal policies, as well as persistently high inflation driven by 
elevated energy prices. GDP growth is then forecast to pick up to 3.5% in 2027 and 3.9% in 2028, 
as domestic demand strengthens amid disinflation and a more accommodative monetary and 
fiscal stance. Rising export demand and favourable terms-of-trade effects are set to reduce the 
current-account deficit. Government debt is projected to continue declining, reaching 22.4% in 
2028, due to strong nominal GDP growth.

decline further. Wage pressures are expected to remain contained, given continued elevated slack 
in the labour market, as underemployment remains high and the potential labour force large. 
The energy price shock has disrupted the disinflation process 
Disinflation stalled in the first half of 2026 as the conflict in the Middle East pushed up energy 
prices. It resumed gradually as of the third quarter of the year, with consumer price inflation falling 
to just below 30% y-o-y in September. Inflationary pressures remain high, reflecting elevated 
global energy prices and the gradual withdrawal of energy price support measures. Bringing 
inflation down remains a key policy priority, but the authorities face difficult trade-offs as they 
seek to avoid an excessive slowdown in economic activity. The recent policy rate cut and repeated 
revisions to intermediate inflation targets are proof to this. Although risks remain tilted to the 
upside, inflation is expected to decline gradually over the forecast horizon. Maintaining market 
confidence will require continued policy commitment to disinflation, while balancing other policy 
objectives ahead of the 2028 elections. 
The fiscal deficit is widening again 
In 2026, the general government budget deficit is 
set to widen to 2.5% of GDP reflecting higher 
spending and lower revenues, including losses 
from energy price support measures, as well as 
higher interest payments. Budgetary pressure is 
expected to ease as support measures are being 
phased out, and spending on post-earthquake 
reconstruction gradually decreases. As a result, 
the deficit is expected to remain elevated in 2027 
before narrowing somewhat in 2028. Despite the 
higher deficits, the government debt ratio is 
forecast to remain low and reach 22.4% in 2028.
