=== PAGE 198 ===
36. UKRAINE 
 
182 
Economic growth to decelerate in 2026 as the war takes a higher toll on the economy 
Economic growth slipped into negative territory in the first half of 2026, recording a 0.1% 
contraction compared to the same period of last year. Relentless Russian attacks since the 
summer on energy and logistics infrastructure, as well as industrial sites, severely disrupted 
economic activity. Strong private consumption and the growth impetus from the expanding 
localisation of defence manufacturing allowed for some relief. The shift of Russian attacks 
towards civilian infrastructure is projected to further weigh on confidence and growth in the 
second half of the year. A strong agricultural harvest is expected to lift the annual growth rate to 
0.6% for 2026, although exports are severely constrained by the blockade of Ukrainian Black Sea 
ports. 
Output 
is 
projected 
to 
remain 
severely 
constrained by the challenging security situation 
in 2027. With disruptions in production, supply 
chains and exports, economic growth is forecast 
to slow further to 0.3%. Private consumption is 
expected to hold up relatively well, given the scale 
of disruptions, benefiting from rising real wages. 
Public consumption and investment in defence 
production are set to also underpin economic 
activity. However, exports are expected to remain 
constrained by damage to production facilities 
and logistics infrastructures. In particular, the 
renewed blockade of Black Sea ports severely 
disrupts exports, while alternative routes cannot 
fully 
compensate 
for 
shipping 
capacity. 
Meanwhile, import demand for energy, defence-related goods and repair inputs is expected to 
remain high, with negative net exports weighing on GDP growth. Under the technical assumption 
that conditions for large-scale reconstruction are in place as from early 2028, growth is expected 
to accelerate to 3.5% in 2028. The current account balance is projected to remain negative at 
8.0% of GDP in 2026 and 7.9% in 2027. This is driven by a large trade deficit, which is only partly 
cushioned by international donor support. The outlook remains highly uncertain, with risks tilted to 
the downside as a further deterioration in the security situation could intensify disruptions to 
business operations. Conversely, a new Black Sea grain deal or earlier improvement in the security 
situation could facilitate an earlier and stronger recovery.  
War-related disruptions in the labour market persist 
Large-scale displacement, military mobilisation and emigration have continued to reduce the 
available labour force. This is contributing to persistent worker shortages and to widespread skills 
mismatches. Competition for workers has sustained nominal wage growth above inflation. 
Shortages of suitably qualified workers continue to limit productive capacity, while lasting effects 
of the war on the workforce are expected to continue disrupting the labour market, keeping 
unemployment elevated, throughout the forecast horizon. 
The intensification of Russia’s war of aggression since the summer, with relentless attacks on 
civilian and critical infrastructures as well as the blockade of the Black Sea ports, is having a 
significant impact on Ukraine’s economy. GDP contracted slightly in the first half of 2026 and is 
projected to remain subdued in 2027. Large defence and reconstruction needs, as well as 
continued reliance on external assistance, are set to keep the fiscal deficit and debt elevated over 
the forecast horizon.

Candidate Countries, Ukraine 
 
183 
Inflation set to rebound in 2026  
After easing in the first half of 2026, consumer price inflation has started to rise again. The 
renewed increases in fuel costs, exchange-rate pass-through and persistent labour-market 
tightness have fuelled inflationary pressures. The headline inflation rate rose to 10.0% y-o-y in 
September 2026, while core inflation reached 8.8%. Against this background, inflation is forecast 
to average 9.1% in 2026 and 9.3% in 2027, before gradually decelerating to 8.5% in 2028 as the 
effects of tightened monetary policy take hold and energy price pressures abate.  
Public deficit to remain high amid sizeable war-related expenditure needs 
Revenue excluding international assistance increased by 21% y-o-y in January–August 2026, 
supported by nominal wage growth boosting personal income tax receipts, efforts to reduce 
informality in customs, and higher excise rates. Nevertheless, expenditure rose at a faster pace, 
increasing by 27%, primarily owing to increased defence procurement and a larger public sector 
wage bill. Despite these pressures, the headline general government deficit is projected to narrow 
to 14.1% of GDP in 2026, reflecting the recording of inflows under the EU’s Ukraine Support Loan 
as revenue. The Loan is expected to provide the bulk of external financial assistance this year. 
Looking ahead, tax measures, including higher taxation of bank profits, taxation of income earned 
through digital platforms, removal of the VAT exemption for low-value imported parcels, and 
further excise increases are expected to yield sizeable additional revenues as from 2027. However, 
deteriorating economic activity is projected to weigh on tax receipts. At the same time, defence 
needs will remain elevated, with military remuneration reform adding substantially to personnel 
costs. Higher allocations for business support, pensions and assistance to vulnerable households 
are set to add to spending pressures. Consequently, the general government deficit (59) is projected 
to widen to around 17% of GDP in 2027. Public debt is expected to rise to 97.4% of GDP in 2027, 
driven by the large fiscal deficit, before gradually declining over the forecast period (60).

(59) The fiscal deficit projections account for the budget support part of the USL. Excluding international grants and USL, the 
fiscal deficit would reach 34.6% of GDP in 2026, 35.5% of GDP in 2027, and 9.4% of GDP in 2028. 
(60) Public debt projections exclude ERA loans and USL financing, in line with the treatment applied in the Debt 
Sustainability Analysis of the IMF programme. Were all ERA loans and USL financing included, public debt would 
increase to 135.9% of GDP in 2026, 160.0% of GDP in 2027, and 150.5% of GDP in 2028.

Other non-EU Countries 
 
 

=== PAGE 202 ===
37. THE UNITED KINGDOM 
 
186 
Stronger-than-expected growth in 2026 
Following weak growth in the second half of 2025, real GDP grew by 0.6% q-o-q in the first 
quarter of 2026 and by 0.5% in the second. The persistence of growth beyond the first quarter 
suggests that it does not merely reflect the residual seasonality that boosted first quarter outturns 
in previous years. Growth in the second quarter was driven mainly by net trade, as export volumes 
rose by 2.8% q-o-q while import volumes were flat, and by investment, with business investment 
up 1.8% q-o-q. Business investment has been broad–based, with strong spending on information 
technology, although the contribution of artificial intelligence to activity remains hard to identify in 
the data. Private consumption grew by 0.3%, despite the squeeze on real incomes, while public 
consumption fell by 0.5%. Monthly GDP rose by 0.4% in July and 0.1% in August, pointing to 
continued growth in the third quarter. 
Overall, real GDP growth is projected to pick up to 
1.3% in 2026, from 1.2% in 2025, before easing 
to 1.2% in 2027 and recovering to 1.4% in 2028. 
Higher regulated energy tariffs and a particularly 
large share of mortgages refinancing at higher 
rates are expected to weigh on household 
budgets from the fourth quarter of 2026—with 
the full effect felt in 2027—and private 
consumption is expected to slow markedly in 
2027. Consumption is projected to rebound in 
2028 as inflation recedes and real incomes 
recover. A gradual decline in the household saving 
rate is also set to help increase consumption. 
Investment is projected to be the fastest growing 
component of domestic demand, expanding 
robustly over the forecast horizon, supported 
partially by technology-related spending. Public consumption is expected to support growth in 
2026, before moderating in 2027 and 2028. Exports, led by services, are projected to grow in 
2026 and 2027. As weaker consumption holds back imports, net exports are set to contribute 
positively to growth in 2027, before turning slightly negative in 2028. 
The labour market is stabilising, but real wages are set to fall 
After a prolonged period of softening, the labour market is stabilising, although labour demand 
remains weak and youth unemployment is a structural concern. The unemployment rate edged 
down to 4.7% in the three months to August 2026, and regular pay growth eased to 3.7%. 
Employment is expected to grow marginally in 2026 and 2027, with the unemployment rate 
averaging 5.0% in both years before edging down to 4.9% in 2028. Growth in compensation per 
employee is projected to slow to 2.8% in 2026 and to remain below 3% thereafter, implying a fall 
in real wages in 2026 and broadly flat real wages in 2027. 
 
 
So far, UK real GDP has grown faster than expected in 2026, supported by strong business 
investment and resilient consumption. Higher energy prices linked to the conflict in the Middle 
East are pushing inflation up again, and the hit to household incomes is set to peak in early 2027. 
Real GDP is projected to grow by 1.3% in 2026 and 1.2% in 2027, before picking up to 1.4% in 
2028 as inflation recedes and consumption recovers. The general government deficit is expected 
to narrow gradually.

=== PAGE 203 ===
Other non-EU Countries, The United Kingdom 
 
187 
Energy prices are pushing inflation up again 
Headline CPI inflation rose to 3.5% in September from 3.1% in August, with motor fuels making 
the largest upward contribution. Core inflation excluding energy, food, alcohol and tobacco, picked 
up to 2.8%. Services inflation rose to 3.7%, after easing from 4.5% in March to 3.4% in August. 
There is little evidence so far of second-round effects. However, higher wholesale energy prices, 
which have already pushed up regulated household tariffs in July and October, are set to feed 
through further in early 2027, and the indirect effects on food prices have yet to materialise. The 
Bank of England kept the Bank Rate at 3.75% in September in a split decision, with three of the 
nine members of the Monetary Policy Committee voting for an increase. CPI inflation is projected 
to average 3.4% in 2026 and to peak in early 2027. It is forecast to fall back gradually to 3.0% in 
2027 and 2.3% in 2028, as energy price effects fade. 
Fiscal consolidation continues, but pressures are building 
The general government deficit is projected to narrow from 5.4% of GDP in 2025 to 4.6% in 2026, 
4.1% in 2027 and 3.9% in 2028, mainly as previously announced tax increases take effect. The 
budget announced on 28 October 2026 added a package of tax increases, including a temporary 
rise in the bank surcharge, partly offset by higher day-to-day departmental spending and 
temporary support for household energy bills. Nevertheless, general government gross debt is 
projected to rise from 102.0% of GDP in 2025 to 103.7% in 2028. 
Risks are tilted to the downside for growth and to the upside for inflation 
Risks to growth are tilted to the downside. More persistent inflation would extend the squeeze on 
real incomes and could require a more restrictive monetary policy stance, weighing further on 
domestic demand into 2027. Households are also exposed to higher interest rates, because most 
mortgages are fixed for only a few years and refinance relatively quickly. On the fiscal side, further 
increases in borrowing costs or spending overruns could require additional consolidation, weighing 
on growth. On the upside, the recent strength of business investment could prove more durable 
than expected. Given the weight of services in the UK economy and firms’ focus on adopting AI 
rather than building infrastructure, productivity gains could also materialise sooner, particularly in 
professional services.

=== PAGE 204 ===
38. THE UNITED STATES 
 
188 
Resilient growth in 2026 despite the energy shock 
After expanding by 2.3% in 2025, the US 
economy has maintained a solid pace so far in 
2026, growing by 0.6% q-o-q in each of the first 
two quarters and accelerating to 0.8% q-o-q in 
2026-Q3. As a net energy exporter, the US has 
been less exposed to the conflict in the Middle 
East than other advanced economies but rising 
retail energy prices (e.g. petrol and diesel) have 
been squeezing real household incomes. This has 
been partly cushioned by recent tax cuts and 
tariff refunds, which bolstered household and 
corporate cash flows. Private consumption growth 
has remained robust, supported by tax cuts and 
tax refunds as well as by wealth effects from 
rising equity prices. Moreover, domestic demand 
has benefited from continued robust technology-
related business investment, offsetting sluggish non-technology investment (especially residential) 
and subdued government spending. On the external side, exports increased moderately, driven by 
energy and AI-related trade, while imports rose strongly, despite tariffs, reflecting high foreign 
content of AI-related investment spending. Overall, real GDP is expected to grow by 2.3% in 2026.  
Growth to ease gradually in 2027-28 
Private consumption is projected to remain the main driver of the expansion, albeit growing more 
slowly than in recent years. Household spending is set to be supported by sound balance sheets 
and elevated asset valuations, although wealth gains are concentrated among higher-income 
households. The expected easing of energy prices as from 2027 is also expected to lower fuel 
costs and restore some purchasing power. However, subdued wage growth and a low saving rate 
limit the scale of private consumption growth. Moreover, job gains are set to remain modest amid 
continued immigration restrictions, which also curb labour-force growth. As a result, the 
unemployment rate is expected to stabilise at around 4.1% in 2027–28. 
Business investment is forecast to keep expanding as large technology firms carry out their multi-
year spending plans on AI infrastructure, but its contribution to growth is set to diminish gradually. 
At the same time, growth in other types of investment is likely to remain sluggish amid rising 
financing costs. Public consumption growth is set to increase in 2027-28, after subdued growth in 
2026. Exports are projected to keep expanding in the same period, supported by energy sales while 
imports are set to remain strong, driven by AI-related equipment. As a result, net exports are 
expected to weigh on growth. 
Macroeconomic policy is set to provide less support in 2027-28. The boost from the frontloaded 
tax cuts and the one-off tariff refunds are set to fade after 2026, while higher real interest rates 
weigh on interest-sensitive spending. Overall, real GDP growth is projected to slow down to 2.1% in 
2027 and 2.0% in 2028.  
US economic growth has remained robust but is projected to decelerate gradually over the 
forecast horizon and remain around 2%. Growth is set to be supported by resilient private 
consumption and AI-related investment. Higher energy prices are expected to temporarily lift 
inflation. The fiscal deficit is set to widen again to around 8% of GDP as of 2026, reflecting recent 
tax cuts and increasing interest rate burden.

Other non-EU Countries, The United States 
 
189 
Rising energy prices drive inflation  
Price pressures remain above the Federal Reserve’s 2% objective. Tariff pass-through has pushed 
up goods prices, while services inflation is proving persistent, reflecting increases in non-housing 
services prices and a renewed surge in housing costs. The energy shock is set to push CPI inflation 
up to 3.4% in 2026, from 2.6% in 2025. Disinflation is expected to resume in 2027, with headline 
inflation easing to 2.7% as oil prices retreat and the tariff effect drops out of annual rates. It is 
forecast to further drop to 2.2% in 2028.  
The fiscal deficit is projected to widen in 2026 and remain high through 2028 
After falling from 8.0% in 2024 to 7.2% of GDP in 2025, largely due to the surge in tariff receipts 
(close to 0.6% of GDP), the general government deficit is projected to widen to 8.1% of GDP in 
2026. The increase reflects the frontloaded tax relief, the refunding of tariff revenues and record-
high interest outlays. The deficit is expected to narrow to 7.9% of GDP in 2027 and in 2028 on the 
back of the reestablishment, in the summer of 2026, of stream of revenue from tariffs under a 
new legal basis (Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974) and as backloaded cuts in social benefits 
gradually kick in. The primary deficit is expected to surpass 3% of GDP in 2026, before subsiding 
to 2.5% of GDP in 2028, while interest expenses are set to continue increasing over the forecast 
horizon. General government debt is projected to keep rising, from 125.6% of GDP in 2026 to 
131.1% in 2028. 
Risks remain elevated  
On the downside, a sharp equity market correction, for instance triggered by a reassessment of the 
returns on AI investment, could affect investment and consumption via negative wealth effects, 
deterioration of company balance sheets and a change in the risk and investment appetite. Large 
AI and government financing needs could push long-term government bond yields even higher, 
raising borrowing costs for households and firms and adding to debt-servicing costs. Trade policy 
uncertainty, policy and governance uncertainties and mounting concerns over fiscal sustainability, 
with public debt on a steadily rising path, add to the downside risks. On the upside, higher military 
spending could boost government consumption and manufacturing, while faster adoption of AI 
could lift productivity and medium-term growth.

=== PAGE 206 ===
39. JAPAN 
 
190 
Growth set to moderate over the forecast horizon 
Japan’s economy maintained solid momentum in the first half of 2026, with real GDP growing by 
0.5% q-o-q in the first quarter and 0.4% in the second. Growth shifted from private consumption 
and exports towards public consumption in the second quarter, while private investment remained 
subdued. Net exports contributed positively as falling imports more than offset slowing exports. 
Activity is expected to soften in the second half of 
2026. Private consumption growth is set to 
contribute to annual growth mainly through carry-
over effects and strong first-quarter performance. 
Besides that, higher food and energy costs and 
the weak yen are projected to erode purchasing 
power despite recovering real wages. Investment 
is expected to remain broadly flat as uncertainty 
and high input costs weigh on spending, while 
inventory drawdowns add to the drag. Public 
consumption and net exports are set to be the 
main drivers of growth. Exports are set to benefit 
from global AI-related demand for semiconductor 
manufacturing equipment and electronics, while 
weak domestic demand is set to limit imports. 
GDP growth is forecast at 0.9% in 2026. 
Growth is projected to moderate to 0.7% in 2027 and to 0.6% in 2028. The planned food 
consumption tax cut improves households’ purchasing power and is expected to support spending. 
However, its impact is likely to be limited by a large retiree population and households' continued 
sensitivity to prices and entrenched propensity to save. Public consumption is expected to 
moderate as emergency energy subsidies are phased out with easing oil-price pressures. Private 
investment is projected to recover as firms are set to resume some delayed projects. Exports are 
expected to remain strong in 2027 before easing in 2028 as AI-related demand moderates and 
tourism growth seen in previous years loses momentum. Imports are projected to rebound in 2027, 
reflecting energy restocking, investment and the recovery in domestic demand, before normalising 
in 2028. Net exports are expected to make a small negative contribution over the forecast horizon. 
Risks remain tilted to the downside. A faster monetary tightening by the Bank of Japan in response 
to persistently high inflation could weigh on domestic demand through higher borrowing costs and 
tighter financial conditions. On the upside, additional fiscal support could cushion the impact on 
households and, together with public investment under the new growth strategy, support growth. 
Wage growth continues but gains remain uneven 
The unemployment rate averaged 2.5% over the first nine months of 2026. Persistent labour 
shortages, driven by structural factors including population ageing, limited scope to raise 
Japan’s economic growth is expected to moderate from 0.9% in 2026 to 0.7% in 2027 and 0.6% 
in 2028. In 2026, public consumption and net exports are expected to underpin activity, while 
subdued household spending and weak investment weigh on domestic demand. The planned tax 
cut on food is projected to improve households’ purchasing power and provide some support to 
consumption in 2027, although its impact is set to be limited by subdued consumer confidence. 
Inflation is expected to ease temporarily in 2027 due to the tax cut, before returning towards 2%. 
Meanwhile, fiscal policy is forecast to become more expansionary, with the deficit widening from 
0.7% of GDP in 2025 to 1.3% in 2026 and rising further thereafter. Public debt is projected to 
decline from above 200% of GDP in 2025 to around 194% in 2028.

Other non-EU Countries, Japan 
 
191 
participation and modest immigration inflows, are expected to keep unemployment at 2.5% 
through 2028. Wage growth is projected to remain firm over the forecast horizon. The 2026 spring 
wage negotiations (Shunto) delivered a third consecutive year of wage increases above 5% at 
major firms, and demands are expected to remain elevated, with trade unions targeting increases 
of up to 6% in 2027. However, negotiated wage increases are unlikely to fully translate into 
economy-wide wage growth. Nearly 40% of workers are in non-regular or part-time jobs, where 
pay rises tend to be smaller, while high import costs constrain SMEs’ capacity to raise wages.  
Inflation set to moderate as the food tax is cut 
The disinflationary trend that began in late 2025 continued into early 2026, with headline inflation 
falling to 1.3% in February, partly reflecting fuel subsidies. Inflation subsequently rebounded as 
higher global energy prices fed through to domestic costs, alongside persistent food and goods 
price pressures. Headline inflation reached 2.1% in September and is expected to remain elevated 
towards year-end, bringing the 2026 average to 1.8%, also due to a weakening yen. The planned 
food consumption tax cut is projected to exert a one-off negative effect on measured inflation in 
2027, with the possible reintroduction of electricity and gas subsidies from December 2026 adding 
to the downward pressure. Inflation is forecast to rise again as the tax cut’s statistical effect fades 
in 2028. The Bank of Japan is nevertheless expected to continue tightening its monetary policy 
gradually, as it examines price pressures, abstracting from the one-off effects of the tax cut. 
Fiscal policy set to become more expansionary, adding to fiscal pressures 
After narrowing to 0.7% of GDP in 2025, the general government deficit is projected to widen to 
1.3% in 2026. This partly reflects the July supplementary budget of around 0.5% of GDP aimed at 
shielding households and businesses against rising energy costs. The deficit is expected to widen 
further in 2027 and 2028 to 2.1% and 2.4% of GDP respectively, partly reflecting the reduction in 
the food consumption tax from 8% to 1% for two years as of April 2027, which is estimated to 
lower revenue by around 0.7% of GDP annually. The projections do not include potential extensions 
of energy subsidies in 2027 nor additional spending under the government’s growth strategy, as 
the budget for 2027 (for the fiscal year April – March) and related plans have yet to be finalised. 
The debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to decline from above 200% in 2025 to around 194% in 2028.

40. CHINA 
 
192 
Growth moderation exposes China’s widening economic divide 
China’s economy grew by 5.0% in 2025, meeting the official growth target for the second 
consecutive year, despite persistent domestic weaknesses. Growth was increasingly reliant on the 
external sector, as robust exports and successful market diversification helped offset weak 
domestic demand.  
Economic growth reached 4.6% y-o-y in the first three quarters of 2026. Activity remained 
supported by manufacturing, particularly high-technology and equipment industries, underpinned 
by industrial upgrading and policy support. However, growth continued to be uneven as domestic 
demand showed few signs of recovery. Household consumption stayed subdued, and investment 
remains a considerable drag, constrained by the ongoing property sector correction, declining 
private investment and softer infrastructure spending. Exports retained their strength, surging by 
36.3% y-o-y in September. However, with import growth also robust, the contribution of net 
exports to GDP growth is moderating. Inflationary pressures remained muted, reflecting persistent 
excess capacity and weak consumer demand. Producer prices have nevertheless been positive 
since March, largely driven by the energy price shock associated with the conflict in the Middle East 
rather than by a sustained recovery in domestic demand. 
External demand is expected to cushion an easing growth trajectory 
Over the forecast horizon, growth is expected to 
continue being constrained by weak domestic 
demand, despite resilient manufacturing and 
exports. Household consumption is projected to 
recover only gradually. It is held back by weak 
income expectations, linked to the persistent 
property crisis and labour-market uncertainty. The 
fading effects of consumer goods trade-in 
subsidies, aimed at promoting purchases of 
energy-efficient products, are also set to weigh 
on consumption. While services consumption is 
expected to remain more resilient than spending 
on goods, the overall contribution of consumption 
to growth is projected to remain limited. 
Investment is also expected to continue being 
subdued, as the prolonged property sector correction, corporate deleveraging and weak private-
sector confidence still weigh on capital formation. Real estate investment is projected to remain a 
significant drag, while the limited transmission of public investment to private activity continues to 
constrain a broader recovery. By contrast, investment in high-technology and AI-related industries 
is set to remain relatively strong, supported by policy priorities reaffirmed in the 15th Five-Year-
Plan, alongside sustained external demand. The resulting divergence between dynamic technology-
intensive sectors and weaker traditional industries is expected to persist, reflecting the continued 
imbalance between strong supply and subdued domestic demand. 
Net exports are projected to continue supporting growth, although their contribution will remain 
exposed to external risks. Chinese exports should continue to benefit from the country's 
While Chinese growth has been robust, meeting the official targets in 2024 and 2025, the 
expansion has become increasingly uneven. Resilient external demand and manufacturing offset 
subdued consumption, weak investment and persistent structural vulnerabilities. Despite targeted 
fiscal stimulus, growth is projected to slow to 4.5% in 2026 and ease further to 4.4% and 4.3% in 
2027 and 2028, respectively, as structural headwinds and downside external risks continue to 
weigh on the outlook.

competitiveness in manufacturing, the upgrading of its export base and strong demand for AI-
related products. Import growth is expected to remain uneven. Demand for semiconductors and 
other technology inputs is set to support imports linked to export-oriented manufacturing, while 
weak domestic demand continues to constrain broader import growth. The current account surplus 
is projected to decline marginally but remain above 3% of GDP over the forecast horizon. Against 
this backdrop, real GDP growth is expected to reach 4.5% in 2026, before moderating to 4.4% in 
2027 and 4.3% in 2028. 
Policy support remains targeted despite downside risks  
Fiscal policy is expected to remain moderately supportive over the forecast horizon. The authorities 
are maintaining a proactive stance, prioritising faster budget execution and bond issuance 
alongside targeted measures to support investment, consumption and the property market. 
However, the overall fiscal impulse will depend on the pace of implementation and the extent to 
which support translates into stronger domestic demand. On the monetary side, the authorities 
maintain a moderately accommodative stance, with a preference towards targeted lending 
facilities and lower policy-bank funding costs rather than towards broad-based interest-rate cuts.  
Risks to the growth forecast remain tilted to the downside. As domestic demand is set to continue 
to underperform, China’s growth model will remain dependent on external demand. While recent 
talks with the US and the EU have eased immediate trade tensions, the risk of renewed escalation 
remains. Domestically, policy support may prove insufficient or may come too late to offset 
persistent weakness in demand and investment, leaving growth at risk of falling short of the 
authorities’ target. The property sector remains a further source of risk: although prices have 
stabilised in major metropolitan markets, secondary urban markets continue to face substantial 
oversupply, prolonging the correction and weighing on household confidence. Over the medium 
term, high debt, weak productivity growth, diminishing returns on investment and adverse 
demographic trends are set to continue constraining China’s growth potential.

41. EFTA 
 
194 
Switzerland  
Economic growth was stronger than expected in the first half of 2026, at 1.7%, mainly thanks to a 
strong performance of chemical and pharmaceutical exports, but also due to solid domestic 
demand. Private consumption was the key growth driver, benefiting from a resilient labour market 
and low inflation, which remained at 0.5% in the first nine months of 2026, after 0.2% in 2025. 
Core inflation remained at some 0.3% y-o-y in the first nine months of the year. The low inflation 
was supported by moderate import price growth as well as the strength of the Swiss franc, 
reflecting its status as a safe-haven currency. In view of low inflationary pressures, the Swiss 
National Bank kept its policy rate at 0.0%, unchanged since June 2025. Employment growth 
remained moderate, and the increase was driven in particular by the service sector, notably 
tourism, transport, health and social services as well as in retail trade. Despite that increase, 
unemployment rose slightly, to 3.1% in July. Elevated global uncertainty contributed to subdued 
investment remained. Switzerland's fiscal policy centres on the debt brake, which aims to stabilise 
public debt by balancing the budget over the economic cycle. Following a slight fiscal surplus of 
0.5% of GDP in 2024, the federal budget was balanced in 2025.  
During the forecast period, private consumption is 
expected to remain the primary driver of growth, 
fuelled by a solid increase in real disposable 
income. Investment growth is projected to remain 
subdued in view of the uncertain international 
environment. In 2027 and 2028, the projected 
recovery of global demand is expected to help to 
raise GDP growth. In view of moderate output 
growth, employment increase is projected to 
remain subdued. In a context of moderate 
expansion of the labour force, unemployment 
rates are forecast to remain stable. Despite 
higher energy prices, inflationary pressures are 
expected to be contained, due to a strong 
currency and a low share of energy imports in 
total imports. The fiscal deficit is expected to 
remain close to balance, reflecting the country’s 
balanced budget rule. Thus, the debt ratio is set to continue declining. 
Among the country-specific risks to the outlook is the possibility of a continued currency 
appreciation, resulting from the currency’s safe-haven status, which could impede export growth.  
Norway 
Economic activity accelerated in the first half of 2026, with real GDP growing by 1.8% y-o-y, 
following a flat growth rate in the same period of the previous year. The main driver of the pick-up 
was a positive contribution of net exports to growth, as exports rose at a faster pace than imports. 
The EFTA economies registered solid, better than expected growth in the first half of 2026. This 
moderate growth momentum is forecast to continue in 2027 and 2028, driven by resilient 
domestic demand, which offsets a subdued external demand and high energy prices. Overall, GDP 
growth in 2027 and 2028 is expected to be in the range of 1.5%-1.8% in Switzerland and 
Norway, while output growth is set to be slightly stronger in Iceland, at 2.0%-2.3%. Inflation is 
projected to continue moderating in Iceland and Norway, to 2.7% and 2.2% respectively by 2028, 
while remaining close to 1% in Switzerland. This is projected to support disposable income and 
domestic demand. Public finances are expected to remain sound despite a challenging 
environment.

Other non-EU Countries, EFTA 
 
195 
Further positive contributions came from the continued, albeit slower expansion of private 
consumption and from a solid increase in government consumption. Investment dropped in the 
first half of 2026, compared with a modest rise in the same period a year before, mainly due to 
the decline in oil-sector investment and inventories. Annual average consumer price inflation stood 
at 3.2% in the first nine months of 2026 compared with 3% in the same period of the previous 
year, still well above the central bank’s target of 2%. On 23 September, the Central Bank raised 
the policy rate by 25 bps. to 4.5%, after a previous increase by 25 bps. in May. 
Economic growth is projected to strengthen 
slightly in 2026. Net exports are set to contribute 
positively to growth, supported by stronger 
external demand and slower import growth. 
Private consumption is expected to moderate as 
growth in households’ real disposable income 
decelerates, 
reflecting 
slower 
wage 
and 
employment growth and elevated inflation. Public 
consumption growth is set to accelerate, partly 
due to increased spending on healthcare, security 
and defence. Investment growth is projected to be 
subdued, with business investment set to grow at 
roughly the same pace as in 2025, while oil-
sector investment is projected to decline after 
three years of strong growth. The recovery in 
housing investment is expected to be sluggish 
due to high input prices and higher interest rates. 
Annual output growth is forecast to accelerate marginally in 2027 and 2028, mainly driven by a 
pick-up in private consumption due to stronger purchasing power and improved consumer 
confidence. Imports are projected to grow faster than exports, reflecting pent-up domestic demand 
and resulting in a slightly negative contribution of the external sector to growth. The government’s 
fiscal surplus, which stood at 10.5% of GDP in 2025, is projected to diminish during 2026-28. The 
2027 budget implies an approximately neutral fiscal stance. The structural non-oil fiscal deficit is 
expected to remain broadly unchanged at 12.6% of mainland GDP (which excludes oil and gas 
sector), while the overall balance is expected to remain in surplus, with oil revenue spending 
equivalent to 2.7% of the sovereign wealth fund’s assets. Domestic risks to the outlook are tilted 
to the downside. A depreciation of the krone could fuel inflationary pressures, subject mainly to the 
development of prices for hydrocarbons. This could also diminish household spending. Housing 
investment may continue to face uncertainty around interest rate developments and the rising 
debt service burden, given the high level of household indebtedness. Regarding the external 
environment, the uncertainty arising from geopolitical tensions is likely to weigh on export demand 
while the volatility of energy prices presents both upside and downside risks. 
Iceland 
In the first half of 2026, real GDP increased by 1.3% y-o-y, compared to 3.3% y-o-y in the same 
period of the year earlier. This slowdown was the result of softening private consumption and 
investment, while exports plunged due to a decline in marine and aluminium products. Export 
services contracted in winter season, but it was followed by a recovery in summer months.  
Following tax hikes on fuel and vehicles in early 2026 and the increase in fuel prices in spring, 
inflation was above 5% in the first half of 2026 and surged to 5.9% in September. In response to 
inflationary pressures, the central bank raised the key policy rate to 8%. 
The outlook for 2026 is for a mild recovery amid external headwinds. Private consumption is set to 
remain moderate, in line with high inflation and monetary tightening, while investment is forecast 
to stagnate after the completion of large-scale investments in data centres in 2025. Export growth 
is set to be weak due to a contraction in the country’s key export sectors, aluminium and fishing 
while a positive impact is expected from data centre services, high-tech and pharmaceuticals. The

2027-28 is expected to feature a continuous consolidation aiming at achieving a balanced budget 
and reducing inflationary pressures.

=== PAGE 213 ===
42. RUSSIAN FEDERATION 
 
197 
Despite high oil and gas prices, civilian investment is declining in the war economy 
Russian economic growth slowed to 0.6% y-o-y in the first half of 2026, from 1% on average in 
2025. While private and government demand provided limited support to growth, civilian industries 
are heavily affected by Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine, Ukrainian counterstrikes and 
western sanctions (61). Investment contracted sharply by 8.1% y-o-y in the first half of 2026, 
following a decline of 0.4% in 2025. While sectors linked to the military continue to benefit from 
government demand and preferential credit, high interest rates and international sanctions are still 
weighing heavily on civilian industries. Private consumption continues to benefit from robust real 
wage growth, underpinned by a persistently tight labour market reflecting war-related and 
demographic pressures. At the same time, the support of elevated oil and gas prices to the real 
economy has been relatively limited, as windfall gains have been absorbed by existing imbalances, 
such as high debt levels, and are used to rebuild buffers. 
High-frequency indicators point to continued slow 
growth. Real GDP growth slowed to 0.6% y-o-y in 
July, from 1.7% in June, before edging up slightly 
to 0.8% in August. The manufacturing PMI briefly 
returned to expansionary territory over the 
summer but fell back below the 50-point 
threshold in August and September. Business 
confidence remains weak. Growth in retail sales 
lost momentum after a strong second quarter, as 
consumer confidence fell to its lowest level since 
early 2023 in the third quarter. 
Continued military spending, capacity constraints 
and high interest rates are set to weigh on 
Russia’s economic activity over the forecast 
horizon. Investment is expected to contract 
markedly in 2026 and essentially stagnate thereafter. Hydrocarbon windfall gains are projected to 
be used largely for corporate deleveraging, providing limited support to new investment. Private 
consumption is set to remain the main driver of growth in 2026, but to lose momentum in 2027 
and 2028, due to slower real wage growth. Among other factors, Ukrainian counterstrikes on 
Russian fuel infrastructure are projected to cause exports to contract slightly in 2026. Exports are 
forecast to decline further in 2027 as the exceptional demand for sanctioned Russian oil linked to 
the Middle East conflict subsides. Overall, amid weaker domestic demand and more limited support 
from energy windfall revenue than previously expected, GDP growth is projected to slow to 0.6% in 
both 2026 and 2027, before edging up to 0.9% in 2028. 
 
(61) Some analysts have argued that Russia’s economic situation is worse than official data suggest. While not all economic 
arguments supporting this view are convincing, data manipulation cannot be fully ruled out in the context of Russia’s 
war of aggression. It is striking, however, that even the official data by now clearly indicate an economic deterioration. 
After slowing to 1% in 2025, real GDP growth in Russia is expected to drop further to 0.6% in 
2026 and stagnate in 2027 despite the elevated oil and gas prices. A slight pickup, to 0.9%, is 
projected in 2028. Inflation increased again in late 2026. While it is expected to continue its 
downward trajectory over the forecast horizon, it is projected to stay above the Central Bank’s 
target of 4%. As long as Russia continues its war of aggression against Ukraine, war-related 
spending is set to continue driving both federal and regional expenditure, resulting in continued 
general government deficits and rising government debt.

=== PAGE 214 ===
European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
198 
Inflation is expected to stay above target until 2028 
The disinflationary process has come to an end. After bottoming out at 5.3% in May, inflation 
picked up again, reaching 6.3% in September. Ukrainian counterstrikes on Russian fuel 
infrastructure have added to price pressures, compounding the effects of expansionary fiscal 
policy and robust wage growth. Further increases in fiscal spending are set to largely translate into 
inflation, as the economy is already operating at capacity. Wage growth is expected to slow 
somewhat over the forecast horizon, while the unemployment rate is projected to remain at its 
historic low of 2.2% in 2026 before edging up marginally to 2.4% by 2028. Against this 
background, the Central Bank has so far only cautiously cut its policy interest rate – it stood at 
14.0% in October. As a result, real interest rates remain high and weigh on economic activity. In 
sum, inflation is expected to be higher than forecast in spring, but to stay on its downward 
trajectory, averaging 6.2% in 2026, 5.5% in 2027 and 5.0% in 2028. 
Deficit to widen as military spending increases further 
Russia’s federal deficit is set to widen again in 2026 and to reach its highest level since the 
pandemic, as federal spending is projected to increase by 13% y-o-y. Despite the windfall 
hydrocarbon gains, oil and gas revenues net of fuel-related subsidy payments are projected to 
decline by 11% y-o-y, partly reflecting increases in subsidies. With non-energy revenue more than 
offsetting this shortfall, overall federal revenue is expected to grow by 10% in 2026. Although 
military spending for 2027 and 2028 has been revised upwards, planned expenditure still remains 
unrealistically low in the context of Russia’s ongoing war of aggression. Overall, general 
government deficits are projected at 3.6% of GDP in 2026, 3.9% in 2027 and 4.2% in 2028, 
translating into an increase in general government gross debt to some 25% of GDP by 2028. 
Considerable risks largely tilted to the downside 
Potential further sanctions and likely counterstrikes on strategic assets in response to Russia’s 
continued war of aggression could further hamper exports and fuel inflation, requiring a monetary 
policy response that would further constrain investment. Another risk to Russia’s current economic 
model is a sovereign funding crunch, a credit event or both. Sharply reduced demand for Russian 
sovereign debt, drastically higher-than-expected military spending and a further marked 
deterioration in corporate loan portfolios could trigger this. The clearest upside risk to growth is a 
prolonged conflict in the Middle East or a stronger impact thereof on oil prices, benefiting Russia.

=== PAGE 215 ===
43. INDIA 
 
199 
The economy remained resilient despite substantial exposure to the conflict in the Middle East 
Despite India’s large exposure as a major energy importer, the economy proved resilient to the 
shocks triggered by the Middle East conflict and expanded by 7.8% y-o-y in 2026-Q2 after 8.6% y-
o-y in 2026-Q1. Supply disruptions have been more limited than expected, in part due to the 
geographical diversification of energy imports. Domestic demand remained the main driver of 
growth in in the January-June period. In parallel to the continued healthy expansion in private 
consumption (+7.3% y-o-y in the first half of the year), investment strengthened markedly 
(+11.2% y-o-y). Public infrastructure spending continued to provide support, while signs emerged 
of a pick-up in capacity-expanding private-sector investment, supported by strong credit growth. At 
the same time, trade made a noticeable contribution to GDP growth, supported inter alia by the 
weakening rupee. Services exports remained strong while goods exports - including in engineering 
goods and electronics - picked up despite the challenging external environment. 
High-frequency 
indicators 
point 
to 
some 
moderation in the economic momentum in 2026-
Q3. The PMI remained in expansionary territory, 
although the pace of expansion slowed from 
2026-Q1, especially for (previously buoyant) 
services. Consumer confidence has softened and 
private consumption growth is expected to 
moderate in the coming months as rising inflation 
weighs on purchasing power. It is then set to firm 
up again starting next fiscal year (FY, April-March) 
as inflation eases. Sustained public investment in 
transport, energy and digital infrastructure is 
expected to continue. Solid corporate balance 
sheets and robust credit growth are projected to 
support private investment, despite the incipient 
limited monetary tightening implying some moderation in the near term. Overall, investment is 
expected to continue expanding over the forecast horizon. Net exports are set to make a broadly 
neutral contribution to growth over the forecast horizon, as sustained domestic demand fuels a 
strong import growth, offsetting the export dynamic, especially of services. Overall, real GDP 
growth is expected to reach 6.9% in FY 2026-27, moderate slightly to 6.7% in FY 2027-28 and 
pick up again to 6.9% in FY 2028-29. 
External position set to weaken amid terms-of-trade shock 
After a surplus of 0.6% of GDP in 2026-Q1 supported by higher services exports and buoyant 
remittances, the current account turned into a deficit in April-June 2026 (-0.5% of GDP). Since 
India imports around 90% of its crude oil needs, the merchandise trade deficit widened compared 
to April-June 2025, and net portfolio outflows continued, while strong services exports and 
remittances (+24% y-o-y) provided support. Some measures were taken to ease pressures from 
the Middle East conflict (such as incentives to attract capital inflows) and helped boost reserves 
substantially, but they did not end the pressure on the rupee. The current account deficit is 
expected to widen to 1.6% of GDP this FY (from 0.6% of GDP in FY 2025-26), driven by a widening 
Despite its heavy reliance on energy imports, India’s economy has so far proved resilient to the 
impacts of the Middle East conflict and its growth rates are set to remain strong. Growth is set 
to ease only slightly to 6.9% this fiscal year and to 6.7% in the next fiscal year, before picking 
up towards the end of the forecast horizon, with domestic demand remaining the main driver. 
The energy shock is temporarily raising inflation and widening the external and fiscal deficits, 
which are all nonetheless projected to gradually improve over the forecast horizon.

European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2026 
 
200 
trade deficit despite resilient services exports. This is expected to be followed by a narrowing to 
1.2% of GDP in FY 2028-29, supported by the gradually improving external environment.  
The Middle East conflict has triggered monetary tightening, while effects on the public 
finances remain manageable  
Consumer price inflation increased from 3.2% y-o-y in February to 5.5% in September 2026, close 
to the upper end of the 4% +/- 2pps. target range of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). Amid the 
continuing depreciation of the rupee, the increase in inflation has been driven by the direct and 
indirect effects of the energy shock triggered by the Middle East conflict despite policy measures 
limiting the pass-through, and increasingly by food prices. Core inflation (excluding food and fuel) 
increased to 4.6% in September. For the first time since early 2023, the RBI raised its main policy 
rate in October (by 25  bps. s to 5.5%) while changing its policy stance to calibrated tightening, 
citing elevated inflation risks and broadening price pressures. Amid the continuing price pressures 
from energy and food (including due to below-average monsoon rains), inflation is forecast to 
average 5.1% this FY. It is then projected to moderate to 4.5% in FY 2027-28 and 4.0% in 
FY 2028-29 on the back of a normalisation in both energy and food prices. 
The budget for FY 2026-27 adopted in February 2026 targeted a slight improvement in the 
federal balance. Since then, the Middle East conflict brought pressures for both expenditure (e.g. 
higher fertiliser and fuel subsidies) and revenues (e.g. lower excise duties on fuel), alleviated by the 
effect of dynamic domestic activity on revenues. Overall, the general government deficit is 
projected to reach 7.6% of GDP this FY (after 7.4% of GDP in FY 2025-26) before narrowing 
slightly to 7.4% of GDP by FY 2028-29. On the back of sustained nominal GDP growth, public debt 
is projected to see a reduction to 81.6% of GDP in FY2028-29. 
Risks to India's growth outlook remain tilted to the downside and include a prolonged energy shock, 
renewed supply-chain disruptions or accelerated rupee depreciation, which could further fuel 
inflation and weigh on demand, while a quicker-than-expected normalisation of oil prices would 
provide support. In addition, renewed tariff tensions would pose a significant risk to exports, while 
recently concluded free trade agreements could benefit India’s trade more than expected. 
Domestically, the effects of the El Niño phenomenon (especially in conjunction with another weak 
monsoon) could compound food inflation and weaken rural demand.

Note on concepts and sources
The directorate general for economic and financial affairs (DG ECFIN)
produces, under its own responsibility, short-term fully-fledged economic
forecasts in Spring and Autumn. These forecasts cover the principal
macroeconomic aggregates for the Member States, the candidate
countries, the European Union as a whole, the euro area and the
international environment.
Typically, intra-EU
imports
are
underestimated
compared
to intra-EU
exports, leading to an overestimation of the surplus. For the past the
"adjusted" balances are Eurostat estimates for EU and ECB estimates for the
euro area. For the future, they are ECFIN's forecasts based on the
extrapolation of the discrepancies observed in 2024.
Quarterly EU and euro-area GDP growth rates are aggregated using 
estimates for all Member States, including unpublished quarterly forecasts 
for IE, EL, CY, MT, PL and RO. 
Data for 2025 are based on outturns as far as available at the cut-off date 
of this forecast. Data for, 2026 and 2027 are forecasts. The source for all 
tables is the European Commission, unless otherwise stated. Historical data 
for the Member States are based on the European System of Accounts 
(ESA 2010). US national accounts and Japanese national accounts are 
based on SNA 2008. Due to differences in revision schedules of annual and 
quarterly national accounts, annual and quarterly figures may not be fully 
consistent for some Member States.  In order to avoid population census 
induced breaks in series and/or to treat Ukrainian refugees under 
temporary protection consistently across Member States, historical data for 
the population of working age of CZ, DE, EL, SE, PL and SK differ from 
published demographics data.
EA21 (BE, BG, DE, EE, IE, EL, ES, FR, HR, IT, CY, LV, LT, LU, MT, NL,
AT, PT, SI, SK, and FI)
   Emerging and developing Asia:
All countries in that region except the ones included in the
Advanced economies and the Asian MENA countries.
   Latin America :
All countries in that region.
Algeria, Tunisia, Morocco, Egypt, Israel, Jordan, Lebanon, Lybia,
Iraq, Iran, Yemen, Saudi Arabia, Bahrain, Oman, United Arab
Emirates, Kuwait, and Qatar.
   Sub-Saharan Africa : 
All countries in that region except the African MENA  countries.
   Eastern Neighbourhood and Central Asia:
 8.
Source: National Accounts, except for US current-account in tables 47, 49,
and 58 (Balance of Payments). Discrepancies with balance of payments
statistics may arise due to methodological
differences and revision
schedules.
7. 
EU and euro-area data are aggregated using exchange rates. World GDP 
is aggregated using Purchasing Power Standards (PPS). In the tables on 
world trade and international payments, the aggregation is carried out on 
the basis of current exchange rates. Tables 46 - 49 and 57-58 show also EU 
and euro-area "adjusted" balances. Theoretically, balances of EU and 
euro area vis-à-vis third countries should be identical to the sum of the 
balances of the individual countries in the EU or  the euro area. However, 
intra-EU or intra-euro-area balances are non-zero because of reporting 
errors. The creation of the internal market in 1993 reduced border controls  
and formalities, and accordingly the scope and precision of intra-EU trade 
coverage. 
   MENA (Middle East and Northern Africa) :
Armenia, Azerbaijan, Belarus, Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan, Tajikistan,
Turkmenistan.

European Economy Institutional Papers series can be accessed and downloaded free of charge from the 
following address: Publications (europa.eu).

•

(the main reports, e.g. Economic Forecasts)

(the Occasional Papers)

=== PAGE 263 ===
GETTING IN TOUCH WITH THE EU 
 In person 
All over the European Union there are hundreds of Europe Direct centres. You can find the address of the 
centre nearest you online (european-union.europa.eu/contact-eu/meet-us_en). 
On the phone or in writing 
Europe Direct is a service that answers your questions about the European Union. You can contact this 
service: 

by freephone: 00 800 6 7 8 9 10 11 (certain operators may charge for these calls),

at the following standard number: +32 22999696,

via the following form: european-union.europa.eu/contact-eu/write-us_en.
FINDING INFORMATION ABOUT THE EU 
Online 
Information about the European Union in all the official languages of the EU is available on the Europa 
website (european-union.europa.eu). 
EU Publications 
You can view or order EU publications at op.europa.eu/en/publications. Multiple copies of free 
publications can be obtained by contacting Europe Direct or your local documentation centre 
(european-union.europa .eu/contact-eu/meet-us_en). 
EU law and related documents 
For access to legal information from the EU, including all EU law since 1951 in all the official language 
versions, go to EUR-Lex (eur-lex.europa.eu). 
Open data from the EU 
The portal data.europa.eu provides access to open datasets from the EU institutions, bodies and 
agencies. These can be downloaded and reused for free, for both commercial and non-commercial 
purposes. The portal also provides access to a wealth of datasets from European countries.
